## B: black mudfish–bridle road

### black mudfish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The black mudfish occurs from the King Country to as far north as the Kaimaumau Swamp, north of Kaitaia. <i>(1998,Ecological Society Newsletter,88 : 13</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Black Mudfish <i>Neochanna diversa </i>Stokell <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,400</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 400]

>  Black mudfish:</i> Rare [in Whangamarino Swamp]. ... Wide-ranging in wetland <i>(Jan 22, 1989,Sunday Star (Auckland),A9</i>)

>  For a while there, I was worried two of New Zealand's iconic beers were going to join the black mudfish, gold-striped gecko and Three Kings skink on the endangered species list. <i>(Apr 29, 2004,Independent Herald (Wellington),12</i>)

### black nags
 
 <i>n.</i> the police [1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 24C BLACK NAGS. slang]

### black oil
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> the oil of black whales

>  <b>STRAUBEL</b> The oil of black whales was usually called `black oil', not because the oil was black, but because of the colour of the whales producing it. <i>(1954,Rhodes Whaling Journal,xvi [fn]</i>)

>  The following fishers are in Cloudy Bay:- The <i>Dragon</i>, full; <i>Courier</i>, 300 barrels <i>William Stoveld</i>, 300 barrels of black oil, and 400 of sperm; ... Mossman's shore whaling gangs have secured 170 barrels. <i>(Jul 27, 1831,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1831 July 27 Letter from Cloudy Bay in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 14]

>  <b>RUSSELL</b> It deserves to be mentioned, too, that nearly half of the black oil obtained by the adventurers of Nantucket and New Bedford is conveyed to England, ... <i>(1842,Polynesia,424</i>)

>  <b>MAJORIBANKS</b> [sc. from black whales] It is evident [78] that a great part of the black oil ... which was procured at New Zealand ... <i>(1845,Travels in NZ,77</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> [Table] SHORE FISHERIES DEPENDING ON THE PORT. ... Name of Station ... Tons of Black Oil ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,193</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> [Prices of] ... sperm oil, none; black oil, none; 3-bushel bags, 24s. ... per dozen. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),83</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> oil burned by careless trying-out in unclean or charred pots, (not the oil of the `black whale' in this instance).

>  <b>WELLER</b> There is a great deal of black oil in the cargo. <i>(Dec 1853,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Dec George Weller Letters from Weller Bros 1833-1840 in 1949 McLintock She History of Otago 69]

### black oreo dory
 
 <i>n.</i> = black dory, deepwater dory

>  THIS week's fish is the black oreo dory, also known as black dory, deepwater dory, and at my local suppliers - deepsea dory. The fish is taken by deepsea trawling ... Don't confuse it with the john dory. In spite of its name, the black oreo dory is not a member of the zeidae family of true dories ... <i>(Mar 20, 1983,NZ Times,9</i>)

### black oystercatcher
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) red-bill, torea

>  <b>FORSTER</b> Ducks, shags, black oyster-catchers, and some sorts of plovers were very numerous here [at Dusky Bay]. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/133</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 69. HAEMATOPUS UNICOLOR. Forst. Black Oyster-catcher. Red-bill. Torea. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,26</i>)

>  The following is a list of the birds shot ... Platycercus auriceps, yellow-top paroquet; Nestor meridionalis, brown parrot; Carpophaga Novae Zealandiae, wood pigeon; Haematopus unicolor, black oyster catcher, or red bill; Haematopus longirostris, pied oyster catcher; Ocydromus australis, Weka, or wood hen; Casarca variegata, Paradise duck; <i>(1872,Proceedings Otago Provincial Council,39</i>) [NOTES: Appendix. Black Stewart Island]

>  The Pied and Black Oyster-catchers, and Pied and Black Stilts, all occur on the main river beds; ... <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/209</i>) [NOTES: Travers NZ Birds]

>  <b>BULLER</b> hAEMOTOPUS UNICOLOR. (BLACK OYSTER-CATCHER.) ... Native name.- Torea-pango.HAEMOTOPUS UNICOLOR. (BLACK OYSTER-CATCHER.) ... Native name.- Torea-pango. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,225,1888 2nd edn 2/18 ed.</i>)

>  67. Haematopus unicolor, Forst. Black Oyster-catcher, Black Red-bill, Torea. - Breeds out on `the plains' in riverbeds . <i>(Sep 1885,NZ Journal of Science,51</i>) [NOTES: T.H. Potts]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Torea</i>, n. 1 <i>Haematopus ostralegus</i> and <i>H. longirostris, pied oyster catchers; . unicolor, black oyster catcher</i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,438</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> [T]here are two species [of oystercatcher], the pied (<i>Haematopus ostralegus finschi,</i> ...) common on South Island estuaries and shingle flats, and the black (<i>H. unicolor</i>, ...), common on Stewart Island and breeding also as far north as Cook Strait and up the East Coast of the North Island. Northern Hemisphere pied oyster-catchers are known as sea pies.) <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,260</i>)

>  <i>Chatham Island Oystercatcher Haematopus chathamensis</i> HARTERT, 1927 ... OTHER NAME: <i>redbill. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,176</i>)

>  The black oyster-catcher is not found outside of New Zealand. <i>(Nov 3, 1892,Otago Witness,24</i>)

### black perch
 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> [<i>Girella tricuspidata</i>, aka parore]

>  This station was named after the celebrated old chief Parore te Awha, who lived on the northern Wairoa and died in 1887, aged nearly a hundred years. He was always very friendly to the Europeans. One meaning of the word is gentle, soft, agreeable; it is also the name of a fish, bream, black perch. <i>(Sep 2, 1935,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.nzetc.org/tm/scholarly/tei-Gov10_06Rail-t1-body-d15.html]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> BLACK PERCH (<i>see</i> PERCH). <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,13</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> PARORE [<i>Girella tricuspidata</i>] ... In Australia blackfish, black perch, black bream are names in wide use. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,42</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] perch, black 1499-501 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  This station was named after the celebrated old chief Parore te Awha, who lived on the northern Wairoa and died in 1887, aged nearly a hundred years. He was always very friendly to the Europeans. One meaning of the word is gentle, soft, agreeable; it is also the name of a fish, bream, black perch. <i>(2008,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.nzetc.org/tm/scholarly/tei-Gov10_06Rail-t1-body-d15.html The New Zealand Railways Magazine, Volume 10, Issue 6 (September 2, 1935)]

### black petrel
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kuia</i> (ii) n. <i>Majaqueus parkinsonii, black petrel</i>, and <i>Priofinus cinereus, brown petrel</i>. Petrel. TOA-NUI. <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,180</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kuia,</i> (ii) n. <i>Procellaria cinerea, grey petrel</i>, and <i>P. parkinsoni, black petrel. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,154</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BLACK PETREL <i>Procellaria parkinsoni</i> <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,46</i>)

>  <i>Black Petrel Procellaria parkinsoni</i> GRAY, 1862 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Taiko, Parkinson's petrel.</i> ... This was the largest type of muttonbird available to North Island Maoris, ... <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,90</i>)

>  654. Procellaria parkinsoni Gray, 1862 <i>Black petrel (Taiko) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,263,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We saw a great Variety of bird this day viz. A white & sooty Albatross (<i>Diomedea exulans & palpebrata</i>). The black & sooty Petrel, (<i>Procellaria aequinoctialis & fuliginosa</i>) the black banded Petrel (<i>Procellaria vittata</i>); the small white rumped Petrel (<i>Proc. pelagica</i>) & we saw likewise three times a <i>Port Egmont Hen</i>, which is commonly thought a mark of the vicinity of Land. [ed. fn] The birds listed here are, respectively, the Wandering Albatross; Light-mantled Sooty Albatross; White-chinned Petrel; Great-winged Petrel; <i>Pachyptila</i> spp; Wilson's Storm Petrel and Antarctic Skua. <i>(Jan 28, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/220,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> <i>Majaqueus parkinsoni</i>, Gray. (Black petrel.) ... I am quite satisfied that this bird is the `Night Demon' of our diggers. I had a live one ... from Collingwood ... On windy nights the bird would become very excited, and then it would give vent to the hysterical laugh or scream from which it takes its name. <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/122</i>)

>  <i>Black Petrel Procellaria parkinsoni</i> GRAY, 1862 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Taiko, Parkinson's petrel.</i> ... This was the largest type of muttonbird available to North Island Maoris, ... <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,90</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 136. PROCELLARIA ATLANTICA. Gould. Black Petrel. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,47</i>)

>  I became personally acquainted with the black petrel <i>(Majaqueus parkinsoni)</i> and Cook's petrel (<i>Oestrelata cooki</i>) ... <i>(1908,TrNZI,40/503</i>)

>  .. there is a very pretty group including crested grebe, dabchick, grey duck, black teal, and flightless duck ; another taking in the mutton bird, whale bird, stormy petrel, night bird, blue billy, driving [sic] petrel, shearwater, and black petrel ... <i>(Sep 14, 1888,Otago Witness,15</i>)

### black physeter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the larger tribes that sport in shoals in the vicinity of the shore, are, sperm-whales, called <i>para</i> [as-acute] <i>paraua</i> [a3-acute] (physeter macrocephalus); black whale, <i>mungu nui</i> (black physeter); right whale, <i>tohora</i> (balaena mysticetus); finback, (balaena physalis); sun-fish. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202 fn</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the leviathans, who sport in shoals around these shores, the para paraua [a's-acute], or <i>sperm-whale</i> (Physeter macrocephalus); the tohora, or <i>right-whale</i> (Balaena bysticoetus); the mungu nui, or <i>black physeter</i> <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/323</i>)

### black pine
 
 <i>n.</i> . [aka matai <i>Prumnopitys taxifolia</i>, also miro <i>Prumnopiys ferruginea</i>, also rimu,possibly others]

>  <b>HAY</b> <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/195</i>) [NOTES: [Hay also uses this for rimu-->]]

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> `With hearts devout by the hill we climb/ To hear the Word by the old black pine' ... The verses refer to the little bush congregation by the clay cottage at Green Island, whose pastor was the Rev. W. Will. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,201</i>)

>  The timber on the western side consists of red- and white-pine, matai (or black-pine), totara, kawhaka (or cedar), rata, and occasional silver-pine, besides black- and red-birch (<i>Fagus fusca</i>). <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,19</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... the way is through the tall woods, chiefly <i>rimu</i> and <i>kahikatea</i> pines, with large <i>rata</i> and <i>matai</i> (black pine). <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 90</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Black pine (<i>P. spicatus</i>) leaves rather smaller in adult ... [Martin does not admit the name MATAI, except on a caption to fig. 37, page 175] <i>(1961,Flora NZ,161,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Podocarpus spicata</i>, Br. Matai, Mai, Black-pine of Otago. -A large tree, 80 feet high; trunk 2-4 feet in diameter. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,124</i>)

>  [Native name] Matai .. [Settlers' name] Black-pine .. [Family] Coniferae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 120]

>  <b>HAAST</b> Along the rather wet track appeared, besides the Totara pine, white pines (Kahikatea, <i>Podocarpus dacrydioides</i>), and black pines (<i>Rimu</i> or <i>Dacrydium cupressinum</i>), which with their regualarly-formed tops generally towered high above the other forest foliage; although here and there black beeches (<i>Fagus fusca</i>), 100 feet high, and 7 to 8 feet in diameter, were also to be seen. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,81</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> [=rimu] The Rimu (<i>Dacrydium cupressinum</i>) is a beautiful species of cypress; `Black Pine', as bushmen call it. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/90</i>)

>  <b>HETLEY & RAOUL</b> <i>Podocarpus spicatus</i> black pine [matai] <i>(1889,Fleurs Sauvages</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>P[odocarpus] ferrugineus, D.Don.</i> ... bark greyish-brown or almost black ... <i>Miro; Toromiro; Black-pine. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,650</i>)

>  8 [Lithograph] of matai (black pine) foliage. The Miro, `Black Pine' (<i>Podocarpus ferruginea. </i>), is, like the matai, a large-sized tree. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,16</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> I liked the berries ... of the black pine and of the supplejack. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,13</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Backlog: a dozey black pine (matai) or rata. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,60</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Podocarpus spicatus</i> R.Br. ex Mirb. Common name:<i>Black pine</i> Maori name: <i>Matai</i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,33</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Their wartime job was to drain ... the stunted manuka swamps Bill calls parkee (pakihis). It was once, he said, a great area for red pine, rimu, and the black pine, matai. <i>(1985,G'day Country,156</i>)

>  Matai (black pine) .. Podocarpus spicata .. A useful wood, heavy, close grained, easily worked, shrinking little; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,12</i>)

>  <b>DEWAR</b> Wood was plentiful [in the Southland bush c1900] - broadleaf, gummy, black pine, dry rimu, with easy access to the river. <i>(1953,Chaslands,76</i>)

>  Totara, or black pine, will be the wood you require for your stakes, ... <i>(Oct 11, 1849,Torlesse Papers,218,1958 ed.</i>)

>  Mr MACDONALD asked if Mr Williamson would give some explanation as to the strength of the girders of the proposed bridge over the Opihi, and whether provision had been made for anything in advance of the present time and whether black pine was the best kind of timber to use for the girders. <i>(Feb 16, 1870,Timaru Herald,5</i>)

>  No wood is to be seen elsewhere than on the mountains; here, however, rimu (red pine), totara, kahikitea (white pine), matae, (black pine), and towhai (or black birch) [a beech], are in abundance. <i>(Apr 24, 1850,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,3</i>)

>  RAILWAY SLEEPERS Wellington. November 11. Guthrie and Larnach's tender, for totara or black pine sleepers, delivered at Lyttelton, Timaru, or Oamaru,was accepted by Government at 2s 10d each. <i>(Nov 12, 1881,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  Hotel-keepers look pleased and jingle the loose silver in their pockets in a manner irritating to one's feelings. Firewood has risen; black pine, 27s per cord, in great demand. <i>(May 31, 1862,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  In an economic point of view, the chief trees of the Middle Island, are the Red and White Pine, respectively called by the Maoris: the former Mai or Matai (<i>Podicarpus spicata</i>),[fn] the latter the Kahikatea (Podocarpus dacrydioides).<i> [ed. fn] Black Rue of Otago, Hook., f.; Miro, Black Rue or Black Pine in Otago, Hector; Miro, often confounded with Black Pine, Balfour; Matai, Colenso, - Mai or Matai (Taylor). </i> <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/12</i>) [NOTES: (Monro)]

>  The most valuable for sawn timber are Black Pine, or Miro (<i>Podocarpus ferruginea</i>), having red, hard, durable wood. <i>Ibid.</i> 48 Black Pine. Podocarpus ferruginea, Don. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/31</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>KIRK</b> MIRO, also called BLACK PINE in Otago.- (<i>Podocarpus ferrugineus</i>.) <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,11</i>)

>  <i>matai</i>, a tree (Podocarpus spicatus), black-pine: often shortened to ma[a]i. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/145</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  The <i>miro </i>or black pine (<i>Podocarpus ferruginea</i>) yields a gum used for the same complaints. <i>(1905,TrNZI,37/68</i>)

>  Although the black pine towers above the surrounding bush, it has not been easily discernible from close up until the past week or two. <i>(Feb 6, 1940,Otago Daily Times,6</i>)

>  <b>6</b> [=MAHI] The timber in [the groves] is chiefly Kaikatea, (white pine), Mahi, (black pine), Remu, (red pine), Pokaka, Kowai, and Totara. ... That in the other forest [ie. not Harewood or Alford] is principally tawai, (black birch), the best timber for boat and ship building, but sometimes unsound: ... <i>(Jun 28, 1851,Lyttelton Times</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> Black pine - white pine - and some Totara, but that is scarce. <i>(1853,Journal,1/320,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TORLESSE</b> Kaikatea, (white pine), mahi, black pine, remu, (red pine). <i>(1851,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,6</i>)

>  Here [at Taeri] was the tall and graceful black pine; the manukau, a shrub like the [Victorian] tea-tree scrub, only more greenly leaved; a few broad leaved grass-trees; the mappo, the kolu, the red pine, and others too numerous for me to remember; but their leaves were ... delightful to one accustomed to the dingy foliage of Victorian forests. <i>(Sep 27, 1861,NZ Goldfields,23,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Sep 27 Tuapeka in NZ Goldfields 1861 (1976) 23]

### Black Power
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARRINER</b> I mean how many people've got the balls to whack even <i>one</i> Black Power member? <i>(2001,Stonedogs,348</i>)

>  Black Power, says Cathro, "are verbalising a real dissatisfaction about something." <i>(Dec 17, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> The predominant gang denominations in the jail varied. The Mongrel Mob was strong. After the Moerewa affair a lot of Black Power members came into the jail, anbd later the Stormtroopers arrived. But the other groups, like the Head Hunters, Sinn Fein, the Filthy Few, and Highway 61, were all present in smaller numbers from time to time. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,220</i>)

>  In a dark corner.. about 10 youths two patched Black Power members in their twenties are drinking a slab of beer, whisky and on assortment of other spirits. <i>(Jun 4, 1999,Dardanelles Driveller,11</i>)

>  At one end members of the Black Power gang stood about in bandanas and black T-shirts and tattoos and attitudes of lethal ease. <i>(Mar 23, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> 'Saves me the trouble,' says Puru, a young Black Power prospect, abused and bullied at home, expelled from school and, at only seventeen, already locked into a depressing cycle of crime, court and jail. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,44</i>)

>  When he was Prime Minister, Muldoon took a personal interest in Black Power by helping their work trusts to plug into the government lifeline of the PEP schemes. <i>(Sep 23, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

>  Robin once said to me: 'My life is Black Power, Harley Davidson, then you and the kids - in that order.' <i>(1995,Mana,No. 10 : 31</i>)

>  He had Helen Wong from his office working quietly with Black Power in Wellington finding them scrub-cutting contracts. <i>(Feb 1992,Metro,8</i>)

>  There will be a hangi lunch organised by Black Power (so no troublemakers) and live music featuring local bands the Pauas and Six Volts. <i>(Aug 10, 1992,Salient,20</i>)

>  <b>KOEA</b> She glimpses, briefly, a large red truck, a Porsche and four members of Black Power on motorbikes following a bus full of old ladies. <i>(1992,Staying Home & Being Rotten,131</i>)

### Black Prince
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a nickname of trade union leader Fintan Patrick Walsh

>  <b>FRASER</b> Walsh, known as 'The Black Prince', was a highly intelligent, iron-willed man. <i>(1990,Both Eyes Open,77</i>)

>  Although of medium height and stocky, with dark hair and features - he was dubbed 'The Black Prince' - Walsh was a physically dominating presence. <i>(1998,Dictionary of NZ Biography,533</i>)

>  Within 12 [years], [Walsh] was expelling opponents and a communist publication announced, "The Black Prince has emerged." ... Oh, Walsh wasn't just a Black Prince, he was <i>The</i> Prince, sitting up there in his Seamen's Union perch, watching the sun setting over Wellington, holding up Marx's <i>Wages, Prices and Profits</i> and declaring "This is my Bible." <i>(Nov 20, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  At times, even "The Black Prince" (union leader Fintan Patrick Walsh) sat up and took notice of Doug Morton. <i>(Oct 19, 2000,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  The "Black Prince", as he was dubbed by his one-time communist fellow travellers, famously managed to antagonise captains of industry and other Establishment types. <i>(Oct 9, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  <i>The Black Prince, The Biography of Fintan Patrick Walsh</i> is due to be published on Tuesday. It was the first successful biography of the union strongman, who ruled by fear, power and manipulation, Hunt said. <i>(Oct 2, 2004,Dominion Post,A17</i>)

### black pudding
 
 <i>n.</i> = black velvet. [>ca1955 Wellington]

### black robin
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a small black endangered bird, Petroica traversi whose numbers fell to five known individual birds in the 1970s, and of which there was only one female in the world, known as Old Blue. A small population now exists on at least two of the Chatham Islands.

>  Not once in all these years have I happened upon that dear little friend of former days, the black robin (sometimes called "the bushman's friend") of the southern bush ... <i>(Feb 6, 1901,Otago Witness,58</i>)

>  The land birds were a species of pigeon, which Mr. Travers then wrongly believed to be the same as the New- Zealand pigeon, the black robin (Miro Traversii) [sic] ... <i>(Jul 16, 1910,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  Too many species have been lost despite the amazing success stories such as black robins and takahe. <i>(Jun 1999,North & South,22</i>)

>  The black robin, whose fight back from the brink became legend in international wildlife lore, is now positively populous beside the black stilt. <i>(Oct 2, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  Who better placed to comment than Don Merton, the man credited with saving the Chatham Islands black robin and, thus far, the kakapo? <i>(Feb 1999,North & South,68</i>)

>  The black robin population, which was famously plucked from the brink of extinction in the 1980s, now numbers about 150 <i>(Feb 12, 2005,Daily News,A17</i>)

>  <b>SHEPHERD</b> This day returned to Albion cove saw ... a Parot of brownish colour and a large white bird as large as a turky and a small sort of brown teal duck, a large black and white Duck called a Paradise Duck a black bird called the organ bird shags a Black Robin with a white breast ... <i>(Mar 9, 1826,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Mar 9 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 359]

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> .. and the kingfisher, the chiming bell-bird, black robin, flycatcher, ... are common round every homestead. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/120</i>)

>  31. <i>Petroeca Traversi.</i> Black Robin. - Information concerning the breeding habits of this Chatham Island robin is as yet wanting. <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,288</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <i>Miro traversi</i>, Buller. (The Black Robin.) I have received several more specimens of this bird from the Snares. <i>(1893,TrNZI,25/57</i>) [NOTES: (Buller)]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Within one minute of landing [on the Snares] I had spotted the Black Robin (<i>Miro dannefordi</i>) and the Snares Fernbird (<i>Sphenaeacus fulvus</i>), each of them well differentiated from their mainland kith and kin. <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,193</i>)

### black sand
 
 <i>v.</i> [goldminng; blacksand, black-sand]

>  There were about forty men black-sanding, and they make a bare livelihood. <i>(1896,AJHR,D.-4/67</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [DNZE nested in black-sand v.intr. p.60](../D#dnze-nested-in-black-sand-v.intr.-p.60))

>  Te Maika has rich blacksand beaches, and is within walking distance of the very extensive blacksand deposit at Taharoa. <i>(Nov 1990,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,90/35-6</i>)

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> Generally the greater portion of the sand is removed, but in favourable spots, sheltered either by a slight indentation in the coast-line, or by some large pieces of drifted timber, its heavier particles remain; these consist [529] of black iron-sand (both magnetic and titaniferous oxide of iron) associated with small garnets and with gold. These black layers are often one or two inches thick, and repose upon coarse quartzose sands. As soon as a storm has subsided, the `beachers' or `surfers' as they are called, examine the coastline near their houses. When they come upon one of the rich spots, the fine particles of gold being often visible to the naked eye, they at once remove the black layer of sand out of the reach of the tide, and wash it when convenient. <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,528</i>) [NOTES: 1868 Von Haast in 1948 Von Haast Julius Von Haast 528-529]

>  Nearly the whole of the west coast of the Middle Island ... is auriferous ... a ship's boat disembarked you on the black sand of the sea shore ... Ten thousand diggers ... were delving in the sand of a long strip of beach sixty miles south of Hokitika. <i>(1895,NZ Reader,259</i>) [NOTES: 1885 A Forbes `Souvenirs of Some Continents' in 1895 NZ Reader 259]

>  <b>HEINZ</b> They give me a shovel and with it nearly full of sand, direct me to shake it gently in the water till all the sand has gone, then round its rim is a thin line of gold, fine as dust, enough to cover a threepenny bit. This is the easiest way of getting gold, so easy it is called `haymaking', but it will not last long, as the black sand brought down by the rivers, with its gold from the mountains and cast up on the beach where it has lain for centuries, is of limited extent. <i>(1974,Bright Fine Gold,10</i>) [NOTES: 18-- Harper Letters from NZ (1914) in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 10]

>  <b>SAMWELL</b> [with reference to the Aleutian Islands] ... we came to an anchor in about 30 fathms Water with bottom of black sand and shells. <i>(1778,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1778 D. Samwell Journal 27 June in Cook Journals (1967) 3/2/1120]

>  The gold is very fine, and accompanied by a great quantity of black sand, from which it is difficult to separate it. <i>(Aug 23, 1862,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  [goldfields] Sunk paddock ...<i> Saturday 24th.</i> Washed hard from very early to very late and brought home the blacksand. <i>(Jun 23, 1863,Diary of an Unsuccessful Digger</i>) [NOTES: 1863 June 23 Diary of an Unsuccessful Digger in 1931 Aug 27 Star (Christchurch) in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 96]

>  <b>HARPER</b> ... after heavy surf [on the West Coast] much black sand, with traces of gold, is thrown up on the beach, ... <i>(Oct 1866,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,105</i>) [NOTES: 1866 Oct Harper Letters from Canterbury (1914) 105]

>  Mr. Skey found the new mineral ... in a sample of heavy black sand, reported as saved by alluvial miners in Barn Bay ... <i>(1891,NZ Journal of Science,1/60</i>)

>  <b>KITTO</b> It is most useful for the final process [of gold recovery] when the black sand has travelled over the ordinary tables. <i>(1900,Practical Dredgeman's Manual,10</i>)

>  The common associate of alluvial gold is the <i>black sand </i>of the miner, principally composed of magnetite ... which from its great weight is left behind with the gold. <i>(1902,Brett's Colonists Guide,1030</i>)

>  ... the auriferous black sands ... The rich black-sands of Donoghue [sea] Beach have already been mentioned. <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.6 (NS) 23-24</i>) [NOTES: (Morgan, Mikonui)]

>  <b>BLYTH</b> <i>Black Sand</i>: These deposits occur all along the West Coast of the South Island and are composed of black iron and other metals sometimes carrying gold in alluvials also. <i>(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11</i>)

>  Such material in this [Shotover] district could hardly be anything else than the `black sand' derived from a dark rock containing magnetite ... which owing to its density, is commonly associated with gold in fluvial wash. <i>(1938,NZ Geol. Memoir,No.4/11</i>) [NOTES: (Modriniak and Marsden, Shotover, Otago)]

>  <b>HOWARD</b> Other seekers ... moved down ... to Codfish Bay, where payable gold was found in company with black sand occurring in wash-dirt three feet deep. <i>(1940,Rakiura,233</i>)

>  <b>HEINZ</b> ... all we got for our trouble with the tin dish were a few colours and black sand, which is always an indication that you are in gold-bearing wash. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,69</i>)

>  He shovels the black sand into the gold box, washes it through with water pumped from the lagoon on the other side of the sand hills. The gold falls on to the plush (`I just use an ordinary towel') which is then shaken into a baby's bath. <i>(Nov 27, 1977,Sunday Times (NZ),17</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Luring, encouraging, enticing, odd nuggets up to the size of a small fingernail bare their beauty in black sand in pan or sluicebox. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,113</i>)

>  <b>NOLAN</b> <i>Blacksand:</i> a dark, heavy sand composed chiefly of metallic minerals instead of quartz. <i>(1983,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,111</i>)

>  <i>black sand</i> black iron sand on some west coast beaches in NZ. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,71</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> In the descriptive part of this publication I have already alluded to the black-sand beachers, who watch the coast principally ... where small indentations favour the preservation of magnetic ... iron-sands. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,364</i>)

>  Black-sand beaches on the West Coast. [heading] <i>Ibid.</i> 136 Apart from other mining pursuits ... is the black-sand industry. <i>(1906,NZ Mines Record,122</i>)

>  Black sand, with its high specific gravity and well-rounded grains, tends to be precipitated with the gold ... <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/43</i>)

### black scrub
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shrub with dark bark and leaves [usually mingi or one of the Cyathodes family.]

>  ..the same dismal-looking vegetation - dry, withered looking tussock, intermingled with black scrub which always in its natural condition of living growth looks to the inexperienced eye quite withered and dead at a little distance. <i>(Feb 1894,NZ Farmer,54</i>)

### black scurf
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fungal disease of potatoes [indicated by small black spots on the skin].

>  These spots, more correctly termed a "black scurf" are the resting stage of the fungus Rhizoctonia, known also as Corticium during another stage of its life. In the black scurf stage it is never a serious blemish... <i>(Aug 15, 1939,NZ Journal of Agriculture,129</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (British) vest

### Black Sox
 
 <i>n.</i> the NZ men's softball team [no singular form?]

>  [Hdg] Black Sox reach softball's pinnacle. <i>(Dec 23, 2000,Dominion,41</i>)

>  Thus the men's softball team has become the Black Sox, which plays antonymously on the name of the celebrated Chicago White Sox baseball team which name has subsequently been applied in full circle to the New Zealand women's softball team (no concern in this sport about American associations!). <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,5</i>)

>  Our men's softball team, the Black Sox, created history by being the first country to have won back-to-back world series. <i>(Aug 5, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

### Black Stacks
 
 <i>n.</i> (Sport) New Zealand's speed-stacking team; (in singular) a member of that team.

>  The boys are members of the Black Stacks, our national representatives at a trend [speed-stacking] which has swept 300 schools across the past year. <i>(Apr 5, 2009,Sunday Star Times,A6</i>)

>  Black Stacks off to America [hdg] Ashhurst's Michael Sadlier heads to America as captain of the New Zealand Black Stacks in April 2009. <i>(Mar 2009,Internet - see Notes,1</i>)

>  Two city boys in Black Stacks team [hdg] New Zealand has a new national team - the Black Stacks. <i>(Mar 2, 2009,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.stuff.co.nz/manawatu-standard/news/1760196]

### Black Sticks
 
 <i>n.</i> New Zealand international women's (and more recently men's) hockey team (also in singular form)

>  "Black Sticks" is not the generic name for all New Zealand hockey teams. The Black Sticks are the New Zealand women's hockey team. <i>(Jul 11, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B10</i>)

>  And even the All Whites is OK , because they were the first to rip off the All Blacks. But then every man and his dog was doing it, and with each stupid title our sports team gave themselves, we sank deeper into the murky abyss, with the Tall Blacks, Tall Ferns, Black Caps and Black Sticks dragging us down at double speed. When I heard that one of our teams had actually named themselves "Wheel Blacks", I wondered whether we had finally settled in the sludge at the bottom. <i>(Oct 10, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B15</i>)

>  My dream is to play for the Black Sticks and eventually win a gold medal at an Olympic Games. <i>(Jun 1, 2004,Independent Herald (Wellington),20</i>)

>  Hockey officials entered uncharted territory yesterday by opting to call their nameless men's team the same as the women - the Black Sticks. <i>(May 11, 2005,Dominion Post,D6</i>)

### black stilt
 
 <i>n.</i> an endangered South Island wading bird, Himantopus novaezelandiae, also known as kaki. [aka kaki, torea-pango]

>  In <i>Pied</i>, a tight target-like knot of 50 black stilt heads is surrounded by a "widespread" (3nm in diameter) flock of pied stilts. This is a direct reference to the fact that in 1980 there were only 50 black stilts left in the world. One of the threats to the species was that it had begun inter-breeding with pied stilts. <i>(Aug 18, 2002,Sunday Star Times,F4</i>)

>  Wetlands - including a breeding area for endangered and rare black stilt - are now part of the conservation estate. <i>(Jul 12, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  HIMANTOPUS MELAS, Homb. et Jacq., `Ann. des Sci. Nat., 1841.' Black Stilt. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/70</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 80. HIMANTOPUS MELAS. Homb. et Jacq. Black Stilt. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,30</i>)

>  The Pied and Black Oyster-catchers, and Pied and Black Stilts, all occur on the main river beds; ... <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/209</i>) [NOTES: (Travers NZ Birds)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> HIMANTOPUS NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (BLACK STILT.) ... <i>Native name{s}</i>.- Kaki {and Tuarahia; Torea-pango (of Arawa tribe)}. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,205,1888 2nd edn 2/24 ed.</i>)

>  81. <i>Himantopus melas,</i> Hombr. et Jacq. Black Stilt. <i>(Nov 1885,NZ Journal of Science,559</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  Another excellent season at Twizel's black stilt aviary should see up to 82 of the endangered kaki/black stilt chicks released into the wild in 2002. <i>(Dec 29, 2001,Timaru Herald,B2</i>)

>  <b>LOCKLEY</b> New Zealand boasts four endemic waders not found elsewhere in the world. These are the black stilt, now limited to a few pairs nesting on high ground in the centre of the South Island; the shore plover, confined entirely to a small island off the Chathams, where its numbers are fairly strong; and the New Zealand dotterel, red-breasted in summer, rather curiously nesting in two discrete groups, one in the Hauraki Gulf, the other far away at and around Stewart Island - <i>(1980,House Above the Sea,181</i>)

>  <i>Black Stilt Himantopus novaezealandiae</i> GOULD, 1764 ... Now only 40 to 50 birds breed in inland river valleys of South Canterbury and North Otago. OTHER NAME: <i>Kaki</i>. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,214</i>)

>  HIMANTOPUS NOVAE-ZEALANDIAE, <i>Gould.</i> - Black Stilt. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/216</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  <i>H[imantopus novae-zealandiae]</i> (Black Stilt). <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/220</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Torea pango</i>, (Ar[awa]), <i>Himantopus melas, black stilt; </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,513</i>)

>  <b>MYERS</b> <i>Himantopus novaezealandiae Gould. Black Stilt. It is extraordinary that I have very little recent information concerning this species. </i> <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/76</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BLACK STILT <i>Himantopus novaezealandiae Maori name</i>: Kaki. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,148</i>)

>  <i>184. Himantopus novaezealandiae</i> Gould, 1841<i> Black Stilt (Kaki) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,51,1980 ed.</i>)

### black stilt plover
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A Gazette proclamation allows native game, consisting of wild duck of any species, bittern, pied stilt plover, wild geese, dotterel, native pigeon, teal, black stilt plover, curlew, and quail to be taken or killed from 1st April to 31st July in the counties of Waikouaiti, Maniototo, Vincent, lake, Peninsula, Taieri, Bruce, Clutha, and Tuapeka. <i>(Apr 1, 1881,Southland Times,2</i>)

>  Native game includes wild ducks of any species, bittern, pied stilt plover, wild geese, dotterel, tui, native pigeon, teal, black stilt plover, curlew, and native quail. <i>(Apr 4, 1891,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  PROTECTION OF ANIMALS ACT, 1867.- .. The following is a list of the birds and animals that are to come within the operation of the Act:- Pheasants .. black stilt plover, pied stilt plover ... <i>(Oct 5, 1867,Timaru Herald,2</i>)

>  The words 'native game' include wild duck of any species, bittern, pied stilt plover, wild geese, wood pigeon, teal, black stilt plover, ... <i>(May 2, 1868,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

### black stingray
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Stingaree; stingray, black .. .. oru, pakaurua, roha, whai repo .. .. <i>Dasyatis </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  [index to common names 283-293] stingray, black 333-4 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### black stump
 
 <i>n.</i> an imaginary marker at the limits of civilisation; remote country beyond this, the back of beyond. [also Australian]

>  It's located on a lovely green field over yonder hill. You walk to the other side of the black stump, down a garden path and into the fairy dale. <i>(Feb 7, 2004,Dominion Post,E6</i>)

>  Their strength was their family and an almost mystical dedication to Ohariu Valley, a part of the city somewhere beyond the black stump to just about everywhere. <i>(May 31, 2001,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  "It's the biggest styling and production job this side of the black stump." <i>(Apr 13, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),16</i>)

>  "I grew up in the country 30 miles from Dannevirke, which is about as far into the black stump as you can go ... where you didn't have rubbish collection ... and had to recycle things yourself." <i>(Nov 23, 2002,Dominion,F1</i>)

>  Anthony McCarten is positively laconic, drawling his tall tales of high hopes and shabby outcomes from somewhere back of the black stump. <i>(Nov 28, 1992,Dominion,11</i>)

### black sugar
 
 <i>n.</i> a grade of kauri gum (See [cf. Black Joe](../B#black-joe))

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Some of the classifications under which gum was exported give an indication of the appearance of some of the grades: bright, rescraped bush, bright chips, black flour dust, chalk, bright seeds and black sugar are some of the names. <i>(1956,Green Kiwi,70,1960 ed.</i>)

### black swan
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Once he and Cliff shot 100 black swans - a whole trailerload - on the wing. <i>(Apr 1998,North & South,125</i>)

>  Like one of Lake Pupuke's black swans taking off, the start is all splash and flurry above the water, precise fury below. <i>(Aug 14, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  [He] talks about spending his weekend "blasting black swan out of the sky". <i>(Jun 26, 2004,Dominion,E2</i>)

>  The black swan is not a native bird. They were introduced in the 1860s as a game bird from Tasmania and other parts of Australia. <i>(Aug 9, 2003,Dominion Post,F8</i>)

>  Further away still, black swans churned the water as their wings hauled their heavy bodies into the air. <i>(Oct 2, 2004,Dominion Post,E 16</i>)

### Black T-shirt Brigade
 
 <i>phr.</i> undercover police

>  We used to refer to the [undercover guys] as the Black T-shirt Brigade. <i>(Feb 8, 2004,Sunday Star (Auckland),A8</i>)

### black tea
 
 <i>n.</i> strong tea without milk, usu. brewed outdoors or for outdoors folk; cf. black billy tea.

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> Then with this eternal mutton, sodden brownie he will spread,/ An' black tea with a little sugar, ... <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,44</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> When the Boss called, `Smoke-oh,' the men scrambled down like baboons, sprawling ... their throats scalded by the black tea stewed up every few hours. <i>Ibid</i> 131 [In the cookhouse] They had supper - bacon and eggs, beans, huge flat scones fried in pig fat ..., black tea - ... <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,129,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> When the Boss called, `Smoke-oh,' the men scrambled down like baboons, sprawling ... their throats scalded by the black tea stewed up every few hours. <i>Ibid</i> 131 [In the cookhouse] They had supper - bacon and eggs, beans, huge flat scones fried in pig fat ..., black tea - ... <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,129,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAC</b> The workmen were having a spell ... The foreman nodded as we came near, squinting up at us from where he sat nursing a mug of black tea. `Goodday, mates. Like a mug of brew?' ... `Yeah, there's plenty in the billy.' <i>(1963,In the Sticks,84</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> That autumn I was given the packie's job. I was shown how to make an `oil drum' stew and, apart from a few slabs of toast and black tea, that was about the musterers' sole diet. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,22</i>)

### black teal
 
 <i>n.</i> black scaup, NZ scaup, papango

>  Pigeons, ground larks, and black teal were fairly numerous, but there were very few tuis. <i>(Jun 13, 1929,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  Black teal have been protected in the district for some years. <i>(Aug 20, 1931,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  This concentration of ducks affords a good opportunity of ascertaining if any of the rarer species are present in the district but among the concourse of water-fowl in this sanctuary not a single specimen of black teal, brown duck, or grey teal could be seen. <i>(May 25, 1940,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 102. FULIGULA NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. Gml. Black Teal. Papango. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,38</i>)

>  The Black Teal are usually associated in small flocks ... <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/208</i>) [NOTES: (Travers NZ Birds)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> FULIGULA NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (NEW-ZEALAND SCAUP.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Papango, Tetepango, Matapouri, Titiporangi, and Raipo; `Black Teal' and `Widgeon' of the Colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,259</i>) [NOTES: {1888 2nd edn 2/273}]

>  <b>PEACHE</b> Our bag was 4 brace of swamp hens, 11/2 brace of Red teal, 1 brace of Blue duck and 1 brace of Grey duck. ... we shot 2 brace of Black teal .. <i>(Apr 10, 1876,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1876 Apr 10 Peache Jnl in 1970 Gray Quiet with the Hills 43]

>  The brown duck [Anas chlorotis] ... is not so commonly seen as the grey duck and the `black teal' or pochard. <i>(1879,TrNZI,11/373</i>)

>  FULIGULA NOVAE-ZEALANDIAE, <i>Gml</i>. - Black Teal. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/127</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  <i>Fuligula novae-zealandiae</i>. - Papango, Scaup, Black Teal. Both Islands. Flight very feeble. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/113</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <b>PARKER</b> `There are eleven species of Native Ducks belonging to nine genera, all found elsewhere, except two--the little Flightless Duck of the Auckland Islands (genus <i>Nesonetta</i>) and the Blue Mountain Duck (<i>Hymenolamus</i>). Among the most interesting of the non-endemic forms, are the Paradise Duck or Sheldrake (<i>Casarca variegata</i>), the Brown Duck (<i>Anas chlorotis</i>), the Shoveller or Spoonbill duck (<i>Rhynchaspis variegata</i>), and the Scaup or Black Teal (<i>Fuligula Novae-Zealandiae</i>).' <i>(1889,Catalogue NZ Exhibition,117</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> [put to various varieties if need be] -->The following is Professor Parker's statement of the New Zealand <i>Ducks. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,128</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Papango, Fuligula novae-zealandiae, widgeon, black teal. </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 479 <i>tete. 1. n. Spatula rhyncotis, spoonbill duck. 2. Elasmonetta chlorotis, brown duck. ... 3. Nettion castaneum, grey teal. ... 4. Fuligula novae-zealandiae, black teal. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,300</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Kukupako - the black teal once common, now uncommon. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,64</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> ... and black teal were present in great numbers. <i>(1956,Pioneer Recollections,29</i>) [NOTES: 1947-1948 Beattie Pioneer Recollections (1956) 29]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>papango, 2.n. Aythya novaeseelandiae, New Zealand scaup, black teal. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,258</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> NEW ZEALAND SCAUP (BLACK TEAL) <i>Aythya novaeseelandiae Maori name:</i> Papango. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,94</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Fairly common on North and South Island lakes is the little black scaup, <i>Aythya novaeseelandiae </i>..., more familiarly known as the black teal. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,271</i>)

>  <i>Scaup Aythya novaeseelandiae</i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>New Zealand scaup, black teal, papango. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,150</i>)

>  The undermentioned contributions have been made to the society .. Master G. Gould, black teal ... <i>(Mar 8, 1867,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

### black tomato
 
 <i>n.</i> =black nightshade

### black tree fern
 
 <i>n.</i> mamaku

>  ... the Ponga Flat ... owes its name to the large grove of Black Tree-ferns with which it was formerly covered. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/90</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

>  <b>POTTS</b> Mamaku - this esculent appeared in junks of about a foot in length; it is the mucilaginous pith of the black tree-fern (<i>Cyathea medullaris</i>). <i>(1882,Out in the Open,18</i>)

>  That monarch is not as well known as its cousin, the mamuku korau or black tree-fern. <i>(May 11, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  No other members of the vegetable kingdom can produce a more telling effect than those noble specimens of Cyathea medullaris, the black punga or tree fern. <i>(Jun 15, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,60</i>)

### black udder
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an acute form of mastitis in sheep.

>  Chloromycetin solution injected daily for four days into the udder of a ewe suffering from 'black udder', an acute mastitis, is reported to have produced rapid improvement in the general symptoms .. This is only of academic importance, as 'black udder' seldom affects more than a few ewes. <i>(Jan 16, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,83</i>)

### black velvet
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori female or females, esp. as sexual objects; occas. sexual intercourse with a Maori woman (cf. Aust: of Aboriginal women). (See [also brown velvet](../A#also-brown-velvet))

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> `I'd like a nice piece of black velvet,' Clive said. `One of those quarter-castes, boy. They give you a good go for your money, they reckon.' <i>Ibid.</i> 263 I'd like a nice piece of black velvet. One of those quarter-castes, boy. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,214,1976 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FRANCES</b> All they want is to get you in the dark and then go off and boast they've had a bit of black velvet. That's what they call us, [Maori girls] Johnny. Black velvet! <i>(1964,Johnny Rapana,121</i>)

### black vine
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Rhipogonum scandens</i> = SUPPLEJACK (See [Maori vine](../M#maori-vine),[passion vine](../P#passion-vine))

>  And ... the <i>Rhipogonum scandens</i>, or Black vine,with its numerous smooth black stalks ... is also a very striking plant. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/III/50 Black vine. Rhipogonum scandens, Forst. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/25</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  R. scandens or Black Vine, with its numerous smooth black stalks, dark green shining leaves and bright scarlet berries. <i>(1868,TrNZI,25</i>) [NOTES: Buchanan, Botany Otago]

### black weka
 
 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Weka pango</i>, black weka, a South Island variety. <i>(1971,Dictionary of the Maori Language,481</i>)

>  <i>O. fuscus</i>. - Black Weka. West Coast of South Island. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/13</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <i>Ocydromus fuscus</i> (Black Weka) <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/220</i>)

>  In the Sanatorium grounds, and in the pond adjoining, there are a number of weka (including some black weka from the West Coast Sounds), kiwi, roa, wild ducks, paradise ducks (putangitangi), and a variety of other birds. <i>(Nov 29, 1902,Auckland  Star,4</i>)

>  The black weka is undoubtedly becoming more plentiful in the Fiord County Forest. <i>(1919,TrNZI,51/222</i>)

### black whale
 
 <i>n.</i> [indistinct classification, used variously, and not exclusive to NZ]

>  The baleen from our Southern "right whale," commonly known as the "black whale," is not so valuable as that procured from the northern animal. <i>(May 28, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  A black whale was stranded yesterday morning on the beach about ten miles south of New Plymouth. <i>(Dec 7, 1892,Star, The,3</i>)

>  A black whale, measuring between forty and fifty feet in length, has been washed up on the beach a few miles north of Timaru. <i>(Jul 23, 1907,Wanganui Herald,7</i>)

>  The southern whale fishery consists of three distinct branches .. second, that of the common black whale of the southern seas <i>(Jul 18, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> The black whale fishery was tried in New Zealand some years ago, but it was abandoned until last year, when it was renewed by one vessel and two shore parties from Sydney, and one vessel from Hobart Town. <i>(1831,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1831 Nov Bell Letter in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 3]

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> The black-whale fishery may be carried on to any extent in the bays ... <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,17,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> ... in Cloudy Bay are several large whaling establishments; as, in calving time, that large sheet of water is visited by immense numbers of the black whale, many of which are killed; .. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),27</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths collection).]

>  <b>POLACK</b> The Island of Victoria is peopled with a large number of Europeans, who have settled in the various bays frequented by the black whale, (balaena mysticaetus.) <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/205</i>)

>  <b>WALTON</b> From May to December, the bays of New Zealand are frequented by what is called the Black or Right Whale; ... <i>(1839,Twelve Months' Residence in NZ,20</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the larger tribes that sport in shoals in the vicinity of the shore, are, sperm-whales, called <i>para</i>[as-acute] <i>paraua</i>[a3-acute] (physeter macrocephalus); black whale, <i>mungu nui </i>(black physeter); right whale,<i> tohora</i> (balaena mysticetus); finback, (balaena physalis); sun-fish (diodon mola); large-lipped whale (balaena mysticetus); large-lipped whale (balaena musculus); hump-back (balaena gibbosa); shark, <i>mango</i> (squalus); hammer-head (zygaena); pilots, (scomber); <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202fn</i>)

>  <b>HODGSKIN</b> It had been for some time the principal rendezvous of the South Sea whale ships ... in search of the `black whale'. <i>(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,10</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The black whales of the southern hemisphere appear to be considerably inferior in size to those of the northern. <i>IBid.</i> 1/162 It is called the `black whale, or right whale' (Balaena Australis, or Antarctica). Sometimes chase is given to the finback and humpback whale, which, with the black whale, belong to the great division of the cetacea known by the sieve-like or screening apparatus (baleen) with which they are provided; ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/44</i>)

>  <b>MONRO</b> It [the finback] is distinguished from the black or right whale by the fin on its back, as well as several other peculiarities of structure. <i>(Jul 20, 1844,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson <i>Examiner</i> July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 248]

>  <b>MAJORIBANKS</b> The black whales have no teeth, consuming their food by suction. The sperm whales ... have teeth, ... <i>(1845,Travels in NZ,76</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> [=right whale] They [shore-stations] are fitted out for the capture of the black or <i>`right'</i> whale, [fn <i>Balaena Antipodum</i>] ...; but occasionally sperm [fn <i>Physeter macrocephalus</i>] or hump-back [fn <i>Balaena Gibbosa</i>] also near the land sufficiently to be taken by the shore-parties. <i>Ibid.</i> 153 Of <i>Whales</i>, there are the <i>Sperm (Physeter Macrocephalus)</i>, the <i>Humpback (Balaena gibbosa), </i>the <i>Fin-back (Balaena physalus)</i>, the <i>Pike-headed (Balaena Boops),</i> the <i>Large-lipped (Balaenopterus musculus)</i>, and the <i>Black</i> or <i>Right whale (Balaena Antipodum). </i> <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,129</i>)

>  He was authorised, should the prospects of black whaling not prove to be good to ... engage in sperm whaling. <i> Ibid.</i> xv/xvi Right whales, usually called black whales because of their colour ... <i>(1954,Rhodes Whaling Journal,xv,ed Straubel ed.</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### black wood robin
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) = black robin q.v.

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Miro Traversi </i>... Found now only on Little Mangere Island ... Black Wood Robin. TOU-TOU-WAI, MIRO. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,71</i>)

### black woodhen
 
 <i>n.</i> [weka]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 86. OCYDROMUS FUSCUS. Dubus. Black Wood Hen. <i>O. nigricaus.</i> BULLER. <i>O. bracypterus.</i> BULLER. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,32</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> OCYDROMUS FUSCUS. (BLACK WOODHEN.) ... <i>Native name</i>.- Weka-pango. ... referred to by Captain Cook ... `They are a sort of Rail, about the size and a good deal like a [175] dunghill hen.' <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,174,1888 2nd edn 2/112 ed.</i>)

>  (Reischek) OCYDROMUS FUSCUS,<i> Dubus</i>. - Black Wood-hen<i> (Weka). </i> <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/`105</i>)

>  <i> Ocydromus brachypterus</i>, Lafr. (Black Woodhen.) <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/194</i>) [NOTES: (Buller)]

>  <i>Gallirallus brachypterus</i> Lafres. Black Woodhen. Philpott (1919, 222) gives an illuminating account of the ups and downs of this species. In the mining days `many were killed for food, and many more were destroyed needlessly by the miners' dogs.' <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/80</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  NO. A.89. - OCYDROMUS FUSCUS, <i>Du Bus</i>. Kelp-hen, Blackwood hen. It abounds in the many inlets and sounds of the south-west coast of this island. The only place where we noticed that it seemed shy was in Milford Sound. As soon as the tide begins to recede these dusky Rails come out on the shore to feed amongst the kelp. <i>(1873,TrNZI,6/152</i>) [NOTES: (Potts)]

>  The black woodhen.. is found in the secluded forests of the west coast of Otago. <i>(Apr 3, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> ['blackwood hen', rails]

 <i>n.</i>

### black-backed gull
 
 <i>n.</i> a black and white gull, Larus dominicanus, the largest of three native species of gull, commonly seen inland as well as on the coast (also called karoro, southern black-backed gull).

>  LARUS DOMINICANUS, Licht. Kororo. Grey Gull, Black-backed Gull, Large Gull. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/75</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 108. LARUS DOMINICANUS. Licht. Black-backed Gull. Karoro. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,40</i>)

>  <i>On the Flight of the Black-backed Gull</i> (Larus dominicanus). By Captain F.W. Hutton <i>(1873,TrNZI,5/140</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> LARUS DOMINICANUS. (SOUTHERN BLACK-BACKED GULL.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Karoro; the young bird distinguished as Ngoiro, [Koiro, and Punua]. [271] This fine Gull, which ranges over the whole Southern Hemisphere, is extremely plentiful. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,270,[1888 2nd edn 2/47] ed.</i>)

>  ... the Karoro, or Black-backed Gull, in the wild and dreary solitudes of the Ocean Beach. ... named Larus Dominicanus (Dominican Gull) from his likeness to the mendicant brothers of that order ... <i>(Mar 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,482</i>)

>  karoro, sea-bird (Larus dominicanus), black-backed seagull. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/152</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>POWELL</b> BLACK-BACKED GULL (Larus dominicanus), Karoro of the Maoris, is easily from either the Red or the Black-billed gulls by its much larger size, ... <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,80</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Red bills, black-backed gulls and godwits ... now greeted us [at Farewell Spit] .... <i>(1951,Confessions,144</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Karoro ... Larus dominicanus, black-backed gull. <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,102</i>)

>  An odd black-backed gull calls from the lake ... and at night a boss grey duck will quack authoritatively to a few companions. <i>(1962,Landfall Country,327</i>) [NOTES: (Kidson `Annus Mirabilis')]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Black-backed gull .. karoro <i>Larus dominicanus </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> SOUTHERN BLACK-BACKED GULL <i>Larus dominicanus Maori name</i>: Karoro, Ngoiro (imm). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,154</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family LARIDAE (gulls). Of the three species here, the common red-billed gull (<i>Larus novaehollandiae scopulinus</i> ... ) breeds on islets in harbours and off shore; the black-billed (<i>L. bulleri, </i>... ) nests commonly on the wide shingly river flats of Canterbury; and the much larger black-backed (<i>L. dominicanus</i>, ... ) breeds along coasts, river banks and on outlying islands [and is] our biggest and commonest seagull. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,267</i>)

>  193. <i>Larus dominicanus </i> Lichtenstein, 1823 <i>thern Black-backed Gull (Karoro [Dominican Gull]</i>circumpolar ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,53,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Dominican Gull Larus dominicanus</i> LICHTENSTEIN, 1823 ...The New Zealand population has increased this century. ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Karoro, southern black-backed gull, kelp gull. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,221</i>)

>  <b>BROWN</b> A lone black-backed gull ... came to investigate, but Leaping Lena's back tray contained only a kerosine tin and a broken fishing grapnel. <i>(1986,Weaver's Apprentice, The,17</i>)

>  She wanted to commemorate what she considered ":the murder and genocide" of black-backed gulls in the area... Over the years in Island Bay she's fed bits of bread and meat fat to the black-backed gulls, and can pick out the young ones before they mature at three years. <i>(Jul 3, 2000,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>Crowe</b> Black-backed gulls can live to 28 years old. It is also sometimes called the Dominican gull from the colours worn by Dominican friars. <i>(2001,Which New Zealand Bird?,77</i>)

### black-billed gull
 
 <i>n.</i> an inland gull, Larus bulleri, with slender black bill and reddish-black legs and feet (superficially like the red-billed gull but with a black bill)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 110. LARUS BULLERI. Hutton. Black-billed Gull.<i> L. melanorhynchus</i>. BULLER nec TEMM. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,41</i>)

>  The Black-billed Gull breeds on the main river-bed ... <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/209</i>) [NOTES: (Travers NZ Birds)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> LARUS BULLERI. (BLACK-BILLED GULL.) ... [<i>Native name.</i>- Tarapunga.] <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,276</i>) [NOTES: {1888 2nd edn 2/58}]

>  <i>Bruchigavia melanorhyncha Buller. Black-billed Gull. This species bred in 1907 in a South Island river-bed. </i> <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/75</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> The birds breeding on [Bird Island, Nelson] were Caspian Tern (<i>Hydroprogne caspia</i>), Sea Swallows (<i>Sterna frontalis</i>) and Blackbilled Gulls (<i>Larus bulleri</i>), these in amity and friendship closely packed together; lastly ... half a dozen wicked pair of Blackbacked Gulls (<i>Larus dominicanus</i>). <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,51</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Black Billed Gull (Kittywake Gull) ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,241,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANNERING</b> [In Canterbury in the 1870's] Stilts were common on the river - blackbill gulls, black-backed gulls, shags, dotterel, native larks (pipits). <i>(1943,Eighty Years,22</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> A related species, the Black-billed Gull (<i>Larus bulleri</i>), is very similar [to the red-billed gull] except for the black bill and dark brown feet. It frequents the inland fresh waters [especially Lake Rotorua and Canterbury]. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,80</i>)

>  The black-billed gulls had already departed. <i>(1962,Landfall Country,326</i>) [NOTES: (Kidson `Annus Mirabilis')]

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BLACK-BILLED GULL <i>Larus bulleri Other name</i>: Buller's Gull <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,155</i>)

>  195. <i>Larus bulleri</i> Hutton, 1871 <i>Black-billed Gull New Zealand ... </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,54,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Black-billed Gull Larus bulleri</i> HUTTON, 1871 ... OTHER NAME: <i>Buller's gull. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,223</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BLACK-BILLED GULL <i>Larus bulleri Other name</i>: Buller's Gull <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,155</i>)

>  Less plentiful is the endemic black-billed gull, which is mainly a bird of the South Island. <i>(Oct 2000,NZ Wilderness,55</i>)

>  <b>Moon</b> The endemic black-billed gull is mainly an inland bird, frequenting South Island riverbeds and lakes. <i>(2002,Birds of New Zealand,89</i>)

>  The black-billed gull breeds mostly in Canterbury, Otago and Southland on shingle islands and on the shores of lakes and rivers. <i>(Oct 2000,NZ Wilderness,55</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  Immediately on arrival at Southland's capital, we went to Mr. McKenzie's Museum .. He had three New Zealand birds I had not before seen. They were a small yellow parroquet, a larger greenish blue one, and a black-crested penguin. <i>(Apr 4, 1874,Otago Witness,3</i>)

 <i>n.</i> now black-browed mollymawk <i>D. melanophrys.</i> [Note that smaller Diomedea called mollymawks in NZ.]

>  <b>BULLER</b> DIOMEDEA MELANOPHRYS. (BLACK-EYEBROWED ALBATROSS.) ... <i>Native name</i>.- Toroa. ... This species of Albatross is far more common in our seas than <i>Diomedea exulans</i>, ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,292,{1888 2nd edn 2/198} ed.</i>)

### black-eyed gecko
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a grey gecko which lives in the mountainous areas of Nelson and Marlborough at higher altitudes than any other NZ lizard. [<i>Hoplodactylus kahuturae</i>]

>  The black-eyed gecko was first discovered on the Seaward Kaikoura Range in the 1970s, though not formally described until 1984. It is New Zealand's only alpine lizard, inhabiting bluffs and outcrops between 1250 and 2200 metres above sea level. <i>(Aug 2002,Forest & Bird,305/33</i>)

>  Insects and lizards are particularly diverse in the new park, and include three species of giant weta and New Zealand's only alpine lizard - the black-eyed gecko - which has its stronghold in the area. <i>(Aug 2008,FMC - (Federated Mountain Clubs of NZ) Bulletin,30</i>)

>  "Three species of giant weta live in the park and it is a stronghold for New Zealand's only alpine lizard, the black-eyed gecko." <i>(Jun 7, 2008,Internet - see Notes,2</i>) [NOTES: www.doc.govt.nz/templates/news.aspx?id=49145]

>  <b>Jenny Jones</b> The black-eyed gecko was named after an inland region of Kaikoura called the Kahutara Saddle, in 1985. <i>(2002,Lizards,29</i>)

>  Black-eyed geckos have been recorded at 2200 metres above sea level, and Cascade geckos were discovered in the Sinbad Gully in fiordland clinging to rock ledges at 1600 metres above sea level, suffering from frost damage to their bodies. <i>(Sep 2010,NZ Geographic,52</i>)

### black-eyed Susan
 
 <i>n.</i> [Jim Cameron writes: NSOD defines this as "any of various plants having light-coloured flowers with dark centres esp. the yellow Rudbeckia hirta of North America". In NZ it is (or was) the common name for the quite different gazania esp. when growing wild. I recall them along the beachfront at Eastbourne in the late 30s.]

### black-fronted tern
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) the common South Island inland tern, also called tara [in 1888 only: =Grey T in 1873]

>  The black-fronted terns are unlike most terns in that they live and breed inland, only visiting the coast to feed in autumn and winter. <i>(Jun 2007,Conservation Action,29</i>)

>  <b>MYERS</b> <i>Chlidonias ablistriata </i>Gray. Black-fronted Tern. Oliver in 1907 found this beautiful tern breeding in at least two of the South Island river-beds. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/75</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BLACK-FRONTED TERN <i>Chlidonias albostriatus Other names</i>ra, Sea Martin, Ploughboy, Inland Tern, Riverbed Tern. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,157</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The somewhat stumpier black-fronted tern (<i>Chlidonias hybrida albostriatus, </i>... ) is common on South Island shingle flats . <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,267</i>)

>  <i>9.1 Sterna albostriata </i>y, 1845)] <i>Black-fronted Tern</i> New Zealand; breeding on riverbeds throughout the South Island east of the ranges . <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,54,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Black-fronted Tern Sterna albostriata</i> (GRAY, 1845) ... OTHER NAME: <i>Tara. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,227</i>)

>  Its vernacular name is black-fronted tern, and it closely resembles the white-fronted species, but lacks the white forehead and black bill. <i>(Apr 24, 1925,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  Its remarkable name, however, is applied on account of a white band separating the black crown from the bill, whereas in the black-fronted tern there are no white feathers over the bill. <i>(Apr 30, 1932,Evening Post,11</i>)

### black-heart
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  BLACK-HEART A heartwood discoloration in tawa and other timbers. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

### black-heart birch
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Nothofagus solandri</i>

>  The utmost confusion prevails among the common names of the birches. There are scarcely two districts, a few miles apart, in which the same name is applied to the same tree, and a similar result may be obtained by consulting two bushmen in the same bush. With a view to obviating this difficulty Mr Kirk suggests `the adoption of new names based of the obvious characteristics of foliage [e.g.] for Fagus fusca `tooth-leaved beech'; for Fagus solandri `entire-leaved beech' etc. [This is unsuitable as foliage varies] I would suggest the retention of the common Otago names Fagus fusca - Red birch ; F. solandri - Black-heart birch ; F Menziesii - Silver birch. <i>(1876,TrNZI,64</i>) [NOTES: (Blair)]

>  <i>Nothofagus Solanderi </i> <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/</i>) [NOTES: (3 examples)]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Nothofagus solandri</i>, Hook. White-birch of Nelson and Otago (from colour of bark), Black-heart Birch of Wellington.--A lofty, beautiful evergreen tree 100 feet high; trunk 4-5 feet in diameter. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,125,3rd ed.</i>)

>  <i>[Notho]fagus solandri,</i> Hook. f. ... <i>Myrtilloides cinerascens</i>, Banks & Sol., MS. <i>Cliffortiordes oblongata,</i> Dryander. Entire-leaved Beech. Black Birch of Wellington, Canterbury, and in part of Otago and Southland. White Birch of Nelson, and in part Otago. Black-heart Birch of Otago. White, Black, Red, and Brown Birch of Oxford and Alford Forests. A fine tree, sometimes attaining the height of 100 feet, but usually 60-80 feet, with a trunk from 2 to 4 feet or more in diameter. <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/301</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk, NZ Beeches)]

>  <b>KIRK</b> <i>Nothofagus solandri</i>, BLACK-BIRCH <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  35 Hand specimen, entire leaved beech (black heart birch). <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,16</i>)

>  <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/307</i>) [NOTES: (E. Phillips Turner) [Otago] see cit. 1 b. above.]

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

### black-sander
 
 <i>n.</i> a miner who works coastal black sand for gold; blacksanding; One who works sea-beach black-sand for gold.

>  Then - a teaspoonful of gold dust, for the two brothers were black-sanders, taking gold from the beach, but only occasionally. <i>(Aug 11, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),49</i>)

>  Two or three isolated settlers, together with one or two `black-sanders' along the sea-beach and a few bushmen complete the population of the area. ... [25] On the sea-beaches the `black-sanders' often have to wait many weeks or months before a payable patch of black-sand makes. <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.6 (NS) 20, 25</i>) [NOTES: (Morgan, Mikonui)]

>  <b>MAY</b> Eighteen weeks had been time enough for the black-sanders to extract the accumulated wealth of centuries. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,198,1962 ed.</i>)

>  Ken ... is a `blacksander', one of the few left on the Coast. His mine is the beach where the high tides wash away the top layers of white sand to reveal the gold-bearing black stuff. <i>(Nov 27, 1977,Sunday Times (NZ),17</i>)

>  The `blacksanders' reaped their harvest. <i>(1906,NZ Mining Record,124</i>)

### black-tracker
 
 <i>n.</i> transf. f. Aust. usage, one who cleans ?overhead tram-lines or ?tram-tracks

>  The latest boorish boss that deserves pillorying is one Francis, who is held [to be] of what are called the `black-trackers' who clean lines or something of that sort. [re: Tramway Dept] <i>(Jan 4, 1908,NZ Truth,133/5/8</i>)

### black-water rafting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Hdg] Zorbing not for the pink-knuckled... New Zealand has contributed the terms zorbing and black-water rafting. Zorbing is an entertainment where those taking part are suspended inside giant plastic balls and rolled down steep slopes. Black-water rafting is the pastime of floating along pitch-black underground streams in inflated inner tubes. <i>(Oct 3, 1998,Dominion,14</i>)

>  Making a splash is what black-water rafting is all about - for rafters and the new owners of the original Black Water Rafting Company. Tourism Holdings this month takes control of the 10-year old business which pioneered trips for tourists in inflatable rubber tubes along cold underwater caves of Waitomo. <i>(Nov 2, 2003,Sunday Star Times,D1</i>)

>  "We represent a company called Blackwater Rafting, who are involved in cave tubing," Mr Bassett said. "Unfortunately, they lost exclusive rights to their name when a judge ruled that Blackwater rafting had become a generic name for cave tubing and others in the industry could use it... For example, Blackwater Rafting was involved in cave tubing, but the media continued to describe the activity as blackwater rafting. The company should have corrected this the first time, as by not doing so the term eventually became generic." <i>(Feb 22, 2003,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (see citation) [also as verbal noun (see cit.)]

 <i>n.</i>
