### blackball
 
 <i>n.</i> black and white striped boiled sweet; a hard, round, black sweet with white stripes, similar to a `brandyball': in general English often called humbug. ; [ca1910 W.H.B. Orsman; ca1920s J. Winchester ;ca1930s-50s Havelock; [1930s-40s] I could've screamed sitting around the copshop like a half-sucked blackball]

>  <b>HOWE</b> But Mum had foresight. She stood at the finishing line with a blackball, a popular lolly [c1880] which was Johnny's favourite. <i>(1964,Stamper Battery,34</i>)

>  <b>SUMMERS</b> She said so to her father once, in a rather prickly mood because an old friend of his ... <i>would</i> produce a bag of blackballs and offer them round. <i>(1964,Smoke and the Fire,123</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> I always found my stocking bulging with presents of the simplest sort. Usually an orange, ... a bag of blackballs, ... <i>(1973,NZ Jack,64</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> The eyes of the young guests sparkled when they saw what was in the car - a case of walnuts, a tin of blackballs and a sack of oysters. <i>(1975,Eight Daughters,94</i>)

>  She brought a bag of blackballs for him. When he accepts them ... she coos and pats him ... <i>(1984,Some Other Country,184</i>) [NOTES: 1976 Hulme `Hooks and Feelers' in 1984 Some Other Country 184]

>  <b>HOLCROFT</b> As a schoolboy I had to go off early to bed ... Early evening ... I was given one boiled lolly - a blackball - from an old, curiously ornamented tin ... <i>(1984,Way of a Writer, The,6</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> She brought a bag of blackballs for him. <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,81</i>)

>  <i>blackball ... 2. n. NZ</i> black and white mint-flavoured sweet. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,72</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> I usually chose a blackball because I enjoyed rolling it around in my mouth ... <i>(1987,Swagger on Our Doorstep,46</i>)

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> <i>(1974,Predicament,37,1981 ed.</i>)

>  Suck that black-ball at your peril, my friend. <i>(Jan 11, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> "She used to share lollies with me. I think they were blackballs." <i>(1991,Always the Islands of Memory,150</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> The peppermints described in England as <i>humbugs</i> became <i>blackballs</i> in New Zealand. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,87</i>)

>  <b>MANDER</b> That night he added [to the store's stock] ju-jubes, liquorice, blackballs, butterscotch and sugar-candy sticks to his next order. <i>(1925,Allen Adair,50,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRIEVE</b> ... our latest `stunt' is to reward the tidiest with blackballs (in the case of infants), or for the seniors, an array of gold stars ... <i>(1939,Sketches from Maoriland,65,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> And one morning ... I went up to her and said I wanted to buy some blackballs. I always did like the taste of blackballs, he said. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,161,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> And Mrs Bedding, the lolly-shop lady, poured out all the wonders she had stored ... in the back of the shop. Black gelatinous bears ..., the blackballs, aniseed balls, ... Hundreds and thousands, musk lollies hard as bullets ... green, white, yellow conversation lollies with inscriptions of `Oh, you kid!' and `Is that so?' and sometimes, blushingly, `You're the cat's pyjamas.' <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,78</i>)

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> At the end of a day, when I sometimes gave him a hand, he would fish a blackball from his pocket, and offer it to me, saying: `I always pay my debts.' His unfailing supply of blackballs made him very popular with children during his many years at the Gorge. <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,29</i>) [NOTES: [f. c1869]

>  <b>FRAME</b> ... a sixpenny shout from her pay for blackballs or acid drops or ?.us; New Zealand where really woody Solanum spp. are common. <i>(1957,Owls Do Cry,47,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BATISTICH</b> She called us 'kids' collectively and never came without a brown paper poke of blackballs doled out to us from the bulging pocket of her check shirt. <i>(1987,Sing Vila in the Mountain,63</i>)

### blackberry
 
 <i>n.</i> The common term (rather than bramble) for the introduced <i>Rubus fruticosus</i> var., both the vine and the fruit since (in Britain,<i> bramble</i> is possibly applied more to the vine; <i>blackberry</i> to the fruit: see OED2 2.) Cook (Third Voyage) seems to prefer <i>bramble</i> [check]; also occas. (as was bramble) used for the native <i>R. cissoides, R. australis</i>, tataramoa, bush-lawyer (See [cf. also bramble](../A#also-bramble))

>  He had bought forty acres of blackberry and gorse. <i>(Dec 2003,NZ Memories,No. 45/26</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> BLACKBERRY (<i>Rubus fruticosus</i> and [<i>R.laciniatus</i>]), occasionally called bramble, is commonest in the middle and south of the North Island, and on the West Coast of Nelson. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,97,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>blackberry [=] blackberry: cut-leaved blackberry</i> [s](?) ... <i>blackberry</i> [s] <i>Rubus fruticosus</i> agg. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,6</i>)

>  <b>WADE</b> Tataramoa: the Bramble or Blackberry of New Zealand. <i>(1841,NZ Journey,196</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> one of the two main wild varieties of blackberry in New Zealand.



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> cut-leaved blackberry

>  <i>American blackberry [=] cut-leaved blackberry</i> [s] <i>Ibid.</> 6<i> blackberry, American [=] cut-leaved blackberry</i> [s] ... <i>blackberry, cut-leaved</i> [s] <i>Rubus laciniatus</i> Willd. <i>Ibid.</i> 19 <i>cut-leaved blackberry</i> [s] <i>Rubus laciniatus</i> Willd. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,1</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> CUT-LEAVED BLACKBERRY [bold] (<i>Rubus laciniatus</i>). A species of blackberry of unknown origin ... it is recognised [from ordinary <i>Rubus fruticosus</i>], by the distinctive shape of leaves. <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,17</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>blackberry, Himalaya</i> [s] <i>Rubus rugosus</i> Sm. var.<i> thwaitesii </i>Focke <i>Ibid.</i> 38 <i>Himalaya blackberry</i> [s] <i>Rubus rugosus</i> Sm. var.<i> thwaitesii</i> Focke <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,6</i>)

### blackberry man
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmhand employed to kill blackberry by poisoning, grubbing or slashing.

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> There was a full-time 'blackberry man' employed, and station hands whenever available were also put on grubbing out the bushes. <i>(1959,Guthrie-Smith of Tutira,138</i>)

### blackberry snow
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a poison used to destroy noxious blackberry.

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> Blackberries maintained the footing they had gained, not only on Tutira but throughout the district, and these he poisoned relentlessly with 'blackberry snow.' <i>(1959,Guthrie-Smith of Tutira,218</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Why, nowadays, there is not a squatter who does not know that blue stone is really blue stone at all ... but sulphate of ammonia, that blackberry snow is sodium chloride and sheep drench carbon tetrachloride. [Dr Falla speaks] <i>(1936,Sorrows and Joys,155</i>)

### blackbird
 
 <i>adj.</i> [Poss. transf. or omit; but cf n2. above 1849 Torlesse Maori servant or porter.]

>  Up Auckland way the crusade in favour of cheap domestic labor [sic] has resulted in a scheme for the importation of a number of black `boys' from the islands, said `boys' being men who are sent to the Dominion under contract by their relatives or owners with whom a regular slave or `blackbird' traffic will be initiated. <i>(Sep 9, 1911,NZ Truth,324/4/3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Maori (here Maori bearer or porter).

>  Captain Thomas, Wills and party arrived at Riccarton ... having bushed it last night at the edge of the big swamp, and leaving their blackbirds behind, who would not move on Sunday. <i>(Jan 21, 1849,Torlesse Papers,49,1958 ed.</i>)

### blackcap
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [white-fronted tern](../W#white-fronted-tern),[tern](../T#tern))

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> WHITE-FRONTED TERN<i> Sterna striata Other names:</i> Sea-swallow, Kahawai Bird, Tara, Blackcap, Grenadier, Swallowtail, Noddy (Chathams), Tikkitak (Stewart Is.) <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,164</i>)

>  <i>White-fronted Tern Sterna striata</i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Tara, sea-swallow, kahawai bird, black cap, grenadier, swallowtail, noddy. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,232</i>)

### blackfish
 
 <i>n.</i> mangrove fish, parore

>  GIRELLA TRICUSPIDATA. <i>Mangrove-fish</i>, or <i>Blackfish.</i> Common in Auckland throughout the year. Sold as `blackfish.' ... In December and January this species is often seen on the surface associated with school snapper, among which it appears to spawn. <i>(May 1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/92</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps and Hodgkinson Further Notes on Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Girella tricuspidata</i> (Quoy and Gaimard). BLACKFISH; PARORE. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,36,1971 ed.</i>)

>  Blackfish <i>Girella tricuspidata</i>(Quoy and Gaimard) <i>Syn</i>. Ctenolabrus knoxi <i>Hutton</i>, G. percoides <i>Hector, G. Multilineata</i> Clarke, and<i> Sparus hamiltoni</i> Phillips) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,407</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 407]

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE BLACKFISH <i>Girella tricuspidata (Quoy & Gaimard</i>) ... Maori name: PARORE. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,103</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> PARORE ... <i>Other names: Girella tricuspidata</i>; blackfish, black perch; black bream (Tasmania). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,242</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> PARORE [<i>Girella tricuspidata</i>] ... In Australia blackfish, black perch, black bream are names in wide use. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,42</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family GIRELLIDAE.<i>Girella tricuspidata</i>..., the blackfish or parore, is another seaweed eater; dark brown above, lighter beneath, striped in life, it inhabits warm northern waters. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,221</i>)

>  <i>black-fish n. </i>name of several varieties of salt and freshwater edible fish. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> BLACKFISH: <i>see Parore </i> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,25</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>PARORE</i> (Blackfish, luderick) <i>Girella tricuspidata</i> <i>(1982,Collins Guide,229,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [species name] parore .. [scientific name] <i>Girella tricuspidata</i>.. [other name(s)] black bream, blackfish, black snapper, old maids, mangrove fish, pacific bream <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Parore Girella tricuspidata</i> Known variously as blackfish, black snapper, black bream, and mangrove fish. Also present around southern Australia and known as luderick. ... Present around shallow reefs and mangrove estuaries of northern New Zealand ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,101</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] blackfish 1501, 1516, 2161-3 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> that's the HOOD of your car and you had better remember that, because hood won't be understood. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a ?rorqual, a ?dolphin

>  The other Morioris and the Maorified Maoris are early expecting a shoal of blackfish on the beach. <i>(Sep 3, 1910,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

>  He remembers "blackfish" strandings back to his early childhood. <i>(Feb 12, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  [Caption] Eighty-eight blackfish were recently stranded at Tokerau beach. <i>(Nov 25, 1935,NZ Free Lance,28</i>)

>  One sunny Friday morning someone looked out to sea and spied a big brown blob, lying prone on the shelly mudflats offshore from the Pakawau River mouth. Just another "blackfish" (the local name for pilot whales that regularly beach here), maybe? <i>(Jun 9, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  A shoal of 96 blackfish, a species of small whale, running from 15 to 30 feet in length, had been cast up on the beach at Te Raki. <i>(Dec 28, 1920,Otago Witness,55</i>)

>  <b>W.N. CHAPMAN</b> <i>(1793,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1793 W.N. Chapman's Letter from Norfolk Island in 1908 McNab History Records 1/186 --]

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> Some of the oil (black fish or bottle-nosed grampus) was still in the workshop in my time, until my brother, Reg tried it out on his mustering boots one day and was begged ... to leave them outside because they made her feel ill. <i>(1985,Last Entail Male,25</i>)

>  <b>GRADY</b> A haul of blackfish (pilot whales) caught in 1905 in Houhora Harbour, .. <i>(1986,Sealers and Whalers,136</i>)

>  blackfish - pai|ka [paikea] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,10,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 110 Also 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 283]

>  <b>RHODES</b> [?=Black whale] Rose a school of blackfish. Lowered 3 boats. ?I got fast but the iron drew. <i>Ibid.</i> 44 Saw blackfish and small fish ... <i>Ibid.</i> 46 Saw blackfish and porpoises. <i>(1836,Whaling Journal,33,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WEEKES</b> Saw a fine black-fish (whale). <i>(1840,Journal,7</i>)

>  The flats off Taupata Point ... are strewn with the bones of the Grampus, or black-fish of the whalers ... <i>(1846,Notes of an Expedition to Kawatiri and Araura</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Heaphy Exped to Kawatiri in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 204]

>  <b>SELWYN</b> In this bay [in Queen Charlotte Sound] shoals of black fish and porpoises were enjoying their evening gambols ... <i>(Aug 30, 1848,New Zealand (various authors/titles),Part V (Church in the Colonies XX) 70</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... the crew said they were killers a large fish of the cetacean family which however attack the right whale. They portend bad weather as last night we had a school of Black fish sporting around us and the gale followed. <i>(Nov 6, 1847,Journal,5/143</i>)

>  <b>STEPHENS</b> Grampuses, of black fish as they are called by the whalers. <i>(1851,Journal,488</i>)

>  <b>7/261</b> GLOBIOCEPHALUS MACRORHYNCHUS ... Blackfish. <i>(1875,TrNZI</i>)

>  <b>SHAND</b> ... the Rongomoana, or black-fish, and other kinds of whale ... <i>(1911,Moriori People of the Chatham Islands, The,6</i>)

>  The blackfish (<i>Globicephala melaena</i>) possesses 9 to 12 pairs of teeth, not nearly so large as those of the killer. <i>(1922,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,5/138</i>) [NOTES: (Oliver)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Tukuperu</i>, n. <i>1. <i>Ibid.</i>452 <i>Tukuperu ... Gobiocephala melaena, blackfish</i>. <i>Ibid.</i> 549 <i>Upokohue,</i> n.<i> Globicephalus melas, blackfi</i>, and <i>Cephalorhynchus hectori, porpoise. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,530</i>)

>  The blackfish (<i>Globicephalus melaena</i>) is usually found in schools ... <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/188</i>) [NOTES: (Oliver)]

>  One yet other flesh food [of the Moriori] was ... [that] classed under the generic term<i> rongo-moa n</i>' (whales) which might include every sea [365] mammal known to those meridians, but more exactly that commonly called `black-fish'. <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 364-365</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 364-365]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> The paraoa or sperm whale, the pakake or humpback whale, and the black fish (a relative of the dolphin and the common porpoise) were used as food. <i>(1966,Maori Life and Custom,31</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> Wonderful days those were, when we might be amongst the dolphins (cow-fish we called them) or a school of pilot whales (black fish to us), or to see a single thresher shark whacking the water with a tail as long as its body. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,78</i>)

>  [Caption] Nobody knows why these blackfish came to be lying dead on Ruakaka Beach near Whangarei. <i>(Jul 4, 1952,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  The blackfish (Globicephalus melas) is a gregarious and timid species of whale which feeds on cuttle fish. <i>(Jun 3, 1924,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  Recently a school of blackfish drove ashore at Maori Beach, Stewart Island, and were stranded. <i>(Jan 10, 1934,Feilding Star,4</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  [On the voyage out:] ... we saw several shoals of that kind of porpoise called by the sailors `black fish'; ... <i>(Jul 23, 1837,Journal of Ensign Best,84,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>A.C. BEGG</b> A whole shoal of porpoises are tumbling about us ... A number of Blackfish, each about 13 feet long, came alongside and were playing about for some time. <i>(Jun 17, 1859,Diary,2,1960 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Rudderfish </i>Centrolophus niger <i>Widespread in temperate seas of the world. In some areas known as blackfish. Mid to dark brown on the head ... </i> <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,139</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Bob McLurg tryed out blackfish oil, a nice job for a cold day. <i>(Jul 10, 1895,Chudleigh Diary,391,1950 ed.</i>)

### blackfoot
 
 <i>n.</i> paua

>  The most common paua species, blackfoot, needed three years to grow to sexual maturity. <i>(Feb 29, 2000,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  It is said that Maori traditionally would not take yellowfoots, believing them to be female blackfoots, hence their being named queen paua by Europeans. <i>(Jul 1998,NZ Geographic,No. 39/75</i>)

>  [species name] paua .. [scientific name] <i>Haliotis iris</i> .. [other name(s)] blackfoot. See also Haliotis australis <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### Blackguard Beach
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) Kororareka (Russell)

>  <b>WILKES</b> Kororarika is still the principal settlement, and contains about [296] twenty houses, ... and many shanties, besides tents. It is chiefly inhabited by the lowest order of vagabonds, mostly runaway sailors and convicts, and is appropriately named `Blackguard Beach'. <i>(1852,U.S. Exploring Expedition,1/295</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mar in 1852 Wilkes U.S. Exploring Expedition 1/295-296]

### blackie
 
 <i>n.</i> a black calf

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> There are five of them in the calf-pen, two brown ones, a blackie, and two that Jake calls the `map calves' because they are like white oceans covered with reddish continents and islands. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,32,1979 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1931 G. Turner Diary (Dannevirke) A blackie's egg. ca1935 Havelock]



 
 <i>n.</i> a steel helmet

>  <b>SLATTER</b> That's right, said the Major. Mine [sc an imitation steel helmet made of papier-mache] blew away in a gust of wind and the platoon commander nearly flipped a blackie with shock. What happened to Bluey? Went into the bag at Sidi Resegh. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,86</i>)

### blackman
 
 <i>n.</i> treacle

>  <b>McGILL</b> There were two old Irishmen, Vaizey and Paddy Brick, who were favourites with the children. `Would you like some bread and blackman?' Paddy would ask the kids, meaning bread and treacle. <i>(1980,Ghost Towns of NZ,93</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>blackman</i> treacle, in bread and blackman, favourite with children early this century; ref. Fred Smith, Southland, in author's <i>Ghost Towns of New Zealand. </i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> In the version [of Mother Mother the Pot Boils Over] played at Waikari, Dunedin, in 1920, a blackman comes to the door and says: `I want a match.' `Spit on the floor and take one,' says the maid. The blackman takes one of the children, whereupon the maid shouts, `Mother, Mother, the pot's boiling over.' <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,49</i>)

### Blackmore coulter
 
 <i>n.</i> an attachment to an ordinary seed-sower which spreads seeds in 30 rows, 3 inches apart instead of 15 rows of an ordinary coulter. Designed by L. Blackmore of the Department of Agriculture, Timaru. see 1952 Nov (1953). <i>Proceedings of the NZ Grasslands Assoc.</i> [(R. Mason)]

### blacksanding
 
 <i>n.</i> [black sanding, black-sanding]

>  This left dad with very little practical use from [his arm], although he made a lot of progress and eventually was able to use it for simple things like holding eating utensils and later was also able to do some shovelling when he went black sanding... <i>(2001,Good Bastards: the larrikin's guide to success,20</i>)

>  There were about forty men black-sanding, and they make a bare livelihood. <i>(1896,AJHR,D.-4/67</i>)

>  We used to go down there and go black sanding, a process where you scooped a thin layer of black sand from the beach and put it through a gold mining box which separted the sand from the heavier gold. <i>(2001,Good Bastards: the larrikin's guide to success,193</i>)

>  At present there are small parties who earn a good average living by what is known as `blacksanding' ... between Jackson's Bay and Karamea. <i>(1906,NZ Mining Handbook,122</i>)

>  The operation of working the beach sands on a small scale is known as `black-sanding', because the gold is associated with ironsand (magnetite) <i>(1906,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.1 (NS) 4</i>) [NOTES: (Bell, Hokitika)]

>  Ken Nolan started his blacksanding more than 25 years ago. <i>(Nov 27, 1977,Sunday Times (NZ),17</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [DNZE nested in black-sand v.intr. p.60](../D#dnze-nested-in-black-sand-v.intr.-p.60))

### blacksmith
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sticky sheep that is difficult to shear.

>  A sheep that is difficult to shear: a blacksmith. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an incompetent station cook

>  <i>Blacksmith</i>.-A term of contempt for a bad cook. <i>(Jan 13, 1934,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss]

>  <b>BAKER</b> Among other terms we have derived from farm life and and sheep stations are ...blacksmith, an incompetent station cook. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,40</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Blacksmith</i>. `An incompetent station cook' (B1941): New Zealand and Australian rural: late 19-20C. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,991</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>Blacksmith</i>.- A term of contempt for a bad cook. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,357</i>)

>  <i>blacksmith n.</i> incompetent cook on a N.Z. sheep station. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>blacksmith</i> contemptuous term for bad station cook.<i> [Acland] </i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,15</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an incompetent station cook

>  <b>BAKER</b> Among other terms .. blacksmith, an incompetent cook. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,40</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> If he is a bad cook he can also, and more safely, be termed a blacksmith. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,62</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> A bad cook was a "blacksmith" and the regular mutton stew was a "hash-me-gandy." <i>(1983,Other Side of Farming, The,1</i>)

>  They called a crook cook a 'blacksmith', perhaps because he used his stove like a forge and welded all the food to the pot. Though those whose noses scented out the menu, while clumping up the path, won't forget those days .. providing their cook wasn't a 'blacksmith' - on sabbatical from the local forge. <i>(Mar 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,19</i>)

### blacksmith bird
 
 <i>n.</i> [J. Commons suggests poss. echo of Handel's Harmonious Blacksmith]

>  <b>REED</b> On reaching Paieri we were met by Mr Dewe, who drove us to his pretty home amongst the hills, where we were delighted to hear the bellbirds or, as they called them, `blacksmith birds', ringing out their sweet notes. <i>(1973,Annals of Early Dunedin,15</i>) [NOTES: c1861 Mrs J.W.Stack in 1973 Reed Annals of Early Dunedin 15]

### blackwash
 
 <i>n.</i> two or more New Zealand victories in international contests

>  The 2005 British and Irish Lions tour officially ended with South African referee Jonathan Kaplan's final whistle on Saturday night. The result: 38-19 to the All Blacks, the final onslaught in what is becoming known as the 3-0 series Blackwash. <i>(Jul 11, 2005,Sunday Star Times,A7</i>)

>  [Caption] Good show, old boy ... Yeah Right: Sir Clive Woodward congratulates Graham Henry on the series Blackwash last night ... sort of. <i>(Jul 10, 2005,Sunday Star Times,Souvenir Wrap-up p. 8</i>)

>  If you were disposed to be optimistic, you might remember that after the Lions were "blackwashed" in 1966, they returned to New Zealand in 1971 and won the series. <i>(Jul 4, 2005,Times (London),63</i>)

>  The Australian media was reverberating with the awful prospect of a blackwash weekend. Two-zip to the Shaky Isles on top of the netball. <i>(Jul 27, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B4</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  It's for that reason that, although 12 members of the WElsh side were involved in this winter's losing British and Irish Lions tour of New Zealand, Jones insists the Six Nations champion will carry no scars from the 3-0 blackwash. <i>(Oct 30, 2005,Sunday Star Times,B5</i>)

>  IF YOU were disposed to be optimistic, you might remember that after the Lions were "blackwashed" in 1966, they returned to New Zealand in 1971 and won the series. <i>(Jul 4, 2005,Times (London)</i>)

### blackweed
 
 <i>n.</i> (Canadian) Pondweed. [a1954 (R. Mason) Black weed is used in Christchurch for <i>Anacharis canadensis </i> EDD 2(32) <i>Sparganium ramosum</i>i.e.Ang (Vocab 1830 & 1895); nf OED]

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> PONDWEED (<i>Elodea canadensis</i>), called also Canadian pondweed or black weed in Auckland and Canterbury. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,22,1967 ed.</i>)

### bladder fern
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cystopteris tasmanica</i>

>  Only the alpine shield fern (<i>Polystichum cystostegia</i>), bladder fern (<i>Cystopteris tasmanica</i>), mountain kiokio (<i>Blechnum montanum</i>), thousand-leaved fern (<i>Hypolepis millefolium</i>), finger ferns (<i>Grammitis poeppigiana</i> and <i>G. givenii</i>), one filmy fern (<i>Hymenophyllum villosum</i>) and two clubmosses (<i>Huperzia australiana</i> and <i>Lycopodium fastigiatum</i>) are found primarily in this habitat <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/78</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> The bladder fern, which, broadly speaking, has a home under every flag ...such, for instance, are some of the conditions under which the bladder fern <i>Cystopteris fragilis</i> is usually discovered here. <i>(1882,Out in the Open,71</i>)

### bladder kelp
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There are underwater forests of bladder kelp up to 15 m tall, and penguins nest under the floorboards, their morning calls an infallible alarm clock. <i>(Jul 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 64/30</i>)

>  ...my first success in an underwater photography competition was a grainy black-and-white photo of bladder kelp off the Otago coast. <i>(Aug 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 52/61</i>)

### blade
 
 <i>n.</i> a knife as a weapon

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> <i>Blade</i>: (n) Knife. Recorded as British criminal slang in 1929, but probably in use long before that. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,245</i>)

>  [Blue is a Mongrel Mob gang member] When he joined gang members used blades but today's weapon was the gun and 14-year-olds were not mature enough for this. `You do not want a 14-year-old boy with a gun in his hand. That is not the buzz,' he said. ... Blue said he thought the mobs' `buzz' was the best it had been for 20 years. `We have our own little buzz. You cannot describe it. It's living life to the hilt.' <i>(May 7, 1986,Evening Post,1</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>MILLS</b> Well, the calm, peaceful atmosphere of a blade shed is much more to my liking than the constant din of handpieces and machinery. <i>(1960,Sheep-O,40</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> He first went to the Muller [station] in the old blade days, ... got a stand again the next season and shore there for the next seventeen years. <i>(1973,Big Country,61,1977 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> In some parts there is a reaction in favour of blade shearing. <i>(1923,Sheep Farming in NZ,83</i>) [NOTES: [AND 1949 -shearing]]



 
 <i>n.</i> a street sign

>  Until June 30, the Council's staff looked after the street name "blades", while other road signs were contracted to AA Signs. But when the contract was retendered, the name blades were included in the package won by Horizon Holdings. <i>(Sep 22, 2000,Manawatu Evening Standard,1</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to remove earth, spoil, etc. with a bulldozer, etc. blade.

>  We bulldozed tracks and bladed the country that was safe to blade into windrows and let them rot down and put a match to them. <i>(Oct 28, 2004,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Had to take the scarfs out in sections and blade them out of the way with a dozer. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,32</i>)

### blade crutcher
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> With only two blade crutchers.. we had a fairly easy time. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,144</i>)

### blade gang
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a team of blade shearers.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> My last association with blade gangs was at Mt White in the '50s. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,44</i>)

>  <b>DOMINY</b> The sequence of shearing in the valley is determined by the order of lambing, and yearly the same blade 'gang' moves from property to property. <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,184</i>)

>  Blade gangs are conscious of the quality of their job, and there are fewer shearing cuts and fewer locks. <i>(2001,Wool Grower,Spring 23</i>)

### blade man
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shearer who prefers, and is expert at, shearing with blades.

>  To hear a human gurgle is contagious enough, but coming from a semi-bare-bellied sheep proved too much for the blademan and soon he was staggering around the boards convulsed with laughter himself. <i>(May 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,85</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Some great stories can be told by blade men ... <i>(1955,Wool Away!,8</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Machine men only stop a few times a run to change cutters, compared with blade men giving their shears several wipes in a run. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,9</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Like Charlie Obery I'm a blade man - for all their big tallies those characters who have to use machinery take second place with me .. I've run into most of the top blade men of my time in these parts. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,38</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A top blade-man in his day <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,20</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Hector Munro, a blade shearer himself, was one man down on his board and brought in his son Walter, a blade man, to help shear a mob. [blade-man] <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,195</i>)

### blade shear
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to shear sheep with blade shears.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Ivan blade-shore the whole wether flock... <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,180</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> .. it takes a reasonably adept shearer about five minutes to blade shear a sheep ... <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,29</i>)

>  <b>BARDSLEY</b> Six shearers would come to blade-shear and my job was mustering, penning-up and working on the board. <i>(2000,Land Girls, The,153</i>)

>  <b>PETER</b> .. we muster for the stragglers and blade-shear bellies and under neck so they can walk out. <i>(2000,Peter Glossary,2</i>)

>  We pre-lamb blade shear our 6000 Polwarths and Merino ewes in July over 12 days. We blade shear our 2000 replacement hoggets in September. We've been blade shearing our Merinos since I can remember. [blade-shear] <i>(2001,Wool Grower,Spring 23</i>)

### blade shearer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Bryce is a blade shearer; a fencer and territorial soldier in the off-season. <i>(May 11, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  <b>GIBBONS</b> They were both blade shearers. <i>(1988,Recollections (various authors/titles),30</i>)

>  Wanted, good blade shearer (one used to woolly sheep), willing to do mustering; must have dogs. Apply at once for position of barber to the Flying Corps. <i>(1918,Bubbles from the Thir(s)ty-Sevenths,10</i>)

>  <i>Blades</i>.- Hand shears as opposed to shearing machines. Hence also blade shearer. (C, since about 1897) <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  Until the outbreak of the Second World War all shearing at Glenaray was done with blades, but owing to the acute shortage of blade shearers ... the owners were forced to change to machine shearing. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,351</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Most of our good blade shearers today are veterans, and one foresees that this age-old art of blade shearing may die out. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,8</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Come shearing time, Donald, having no machines in the shed, engaged a couple of blade shearers. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,92</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> `Teviot' had a magnificent stone and steel woolshed [built 1880] ... with two complete drafting races, much wool storage room, two ancient screw presses, and stands for 26 blade shearers, for this was the era before the Ferrier Woolpress, ... <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,17</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> As blade shearing is slow, and the merino sheep ... are very difficult to shear, the back-country station is usually last on any shearer's itinerary. <i>Ibid.</i> 40 ... when one blade shearer starts on five thousand sheep the end is a long way ahead. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,35</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Twelve blade-shearers were required for shearing, besides anothe ten or so men in the shed. <i>(1975,Eight Daughters,30</i>)

>  Ivory Poi Rutene, aged 35, a blade shearer, ... was remanded to September 6. <i>(Aug 24, 1988,Press, The,18</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shearer who uses hand-blades rather than machines.

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> He was an exceptionally good blade shearer ... <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,109</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Boardwalkers have become unnecessary as the general standard of shearing among blade shearers has improved. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Hector Munro, a blade shearer himself was down on his board ... <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,195</i>)

>  Woolpro, it seems to me, doesn't recognise the existence of blade shearers and wish they would all just go away (or become machine shearers). <i>(2000,Wool Grower,Spring 5</i>)

### blade shearing
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) shearing of sheep with hand shears.

>  <b>PERRY</b> Nowadays shearing is mostly done with machines, although in some parts there is a reaction in favour of blade shearing. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,83</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> As blade shearing is slow .. the back-country station is usually last on any shearer's itinerary. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,35</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Despite the popularity of the faster machines blade shearing is still taught at special wool board shearing courses ... <i>(1983,Other Side of Farming, The,ii 2</i>)

>  <b>THORNTON</b> Many high country runs preferred to retain blade shearing for their Merino fleeces. <i>(1986,NZ Heritage of Farm Buildings,237</i>)

>  <b>NEWPORT</b> Machine shearing required a totally different style and some sheep suffered injury by men who only knew blade shearing. <i>(1989,Footprints Farewell,78</i>)

>  Not concerned with pin-drive or spline-drive shearing gear is Grant Anderson, one of the blade shearing gang operating at the Acland's Mount Peel woolshed ... <i>(Jul 27, 1995,NZ Farmer,12</i>)

>  We would like to look at whether blade shearing gives the same effect. <i>(Jun 11, 2002,Wairarapa Times-Age,5,Farming Supp. ed.</i>)

### blade shears
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) hand operated shears for blade shearing.

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> We did this with blade shears then ... <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,51</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> Jingling Johnnies : blade shears. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,46</i>)

>  <b>PEAT</b> The predominant sound, a very old sound, is the 'whisk-whisk, whisk-whisk' of four sets of blade shears. <i>(1992,Falcon and the Lark, The,47</i>)

### blade shed
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a woolshed in which only handshears are used.

 <i>n.</i>

### blades
 
 <i>n.</i>

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) handshears for shearing sheep.

### blanket
 
 <i>v.</i> to assault, using a blanket to blind or confuse the victim.

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> BLADE (v) Mode of assault, using a blanket to blind or confuse the victim. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,245</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [blanket board; blanketings](../B#blanket-board;-blanketings))

### blanket board
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The battery has three stampers in each box, double cams, quickslver placed in battery boxes, grating perpendicular, with round holes. The copper-plate tables are about 12ft. long by 5ft. wide, and raised about 14in. above the blanket boards; ... [The blankets] appear to be ordinary grey blankets of a very inferior description. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,93</i>)

 <i>n.</i> <i>Pleurosorus rutifolius</i>

This includes the rock fern (<i>)Cheilanthes sicheri</i>), woolly cloak fern (<i>C. distans</i>), blanket fern (<i>Pleurosorus rutifolius</i>), and <i>Pellaea callidirupium</i>. <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/73</i>)

### blanket man

### blanket piece
 
 <i>n.</i> [whaling]

>  <b>POLACK</b> [The whale] is then raised as high as the mast head, and another tackle is applied to a fresh piece of blubber, technically called blanket pieces, during which the preceding piece is lowered into an inclosed place below the deck, called the blubber room. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/420</i>)

### blanket table
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The constant outflow of crushed quartz and water from the stamp boxes is received on a table, having on it grooves or ripples, containing quicksilver and a large extent of amalgamated copper plates smoothly nailed on. This may be called the silver table; ... The flow then passes over blanket tables, which arrest mechanically all the heavier particles. <i>(1876,TrNZI,8/177</i>)

### blanket-bag
 
 <i>n.</i> a rolled blanket used as a bag for carrying, slung over the back; a blanket pack; ? an early version of sleeping-bag

>  <b>MALONE</b> About four miles from Pokatutu's pah, we stopped, opened our <i>blacket-bags</i>, and took a hearty lunch. <i>(1854,Three Years Cruise,17</i>) [NOTES: [refers to Maoris' packing everything in blanket-rolls and slinging them]]

>  <b>HARPER</b> ... on turning in we lie `heads and tails' in our blanket bags. <i>(1869,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,90</i>) [NOTES: [?fore-runner of sleeping-bag]]

### blanket-shirt
 
 <i>n.</i> a blanket-covering [merely], or ? b. a shirt made of blanket-material, or ? c. a shirt like a blanket in appearance (e.g. long Crimea shirt)

### blanket-tailings
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [blanketings](../B#blanketings))

### blanketing
 
 <i>n.</i> the bottom surface of a sluice-box etc. covered with ...

>  We see this liquid [sc. puddled ore] sent over `beds', and `floors', and `ladders', and `blanketings', and washed again and again. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/279</i>)

### blanketings
 
 <i>n.</i>



 
 <i>n.</i> [mining]

>  While on an official visit to the Thames Goldfield I ...[observed] the ... effect of Cyanide of Potassium, in preventing the flouring of mercury used in working the blanketings. These blanketings I found have ... a decidedly acid reaction, due ... to the presence of ferric and ferrous salts ..., and it is to the former ... that what is commonly known as [335] `flouring,' is mainly due, in the process cited above; such ferric salts being able to either oxidize or chloridize the surface of any mercury ..., thus enfilming it with a compound, which being practically insoluble in water ... prevents that metallic contact taking place between detached mercurial globules, which is necessary to amalgamation. <i>(1876,TrNZI,8/334</i>) [NOTES: (Skey)]

>  The blanket tailings or blanketings, as they are otherwise called, consist mostly of iron pyrites and other sulphides, cobines with quartz, and contain a fair proportion of gold ...that had escaped over the silver tables. These tailings are treated in berdans with extra quicksilver and ground up. The berdans now in use at the Thames ... are generally five feet in diameter ... At one time a couple of rotating balls were considered sufficient for the amount of crushing required; now, the general practice is to have a loose ball as well as a stationary one attached to a chain, and it is called a drag ball; this drag does more work than a loose ball ... for the drag grinds the bowl as much as the tailings. <i>(1876,TrNZI,8/179</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### Bledisloe Cup
 
 <i>n.</i> (presently) an annual international rugby union series between the All Blacks and the Wallabies [also the Bledisloe]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The third period of the history of Rugby games between New Zealand and Australia began with the first test match played for the Beldisloe Cup and this took place at Eden Park on September 12, 1931. <i>(1970,On the Ball,212</i>)

>  2ZB commentator, John McBeth has called all of the last 15 All Black tests as well as this year's Hong Kong and Australian seven-a-sides, the World Cup, the Bledisloe Cup and all of this season's Wellington games. <i>(Sep 26, 1987,Dominion,2</i>)

>  People talk about watching "the Bledisloe". It doesn't get the word Cup half the time. <i>(Aug 8, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

>  The All Blacks didn't lose Bledisloe II - the sequel which nearly lived up to its classic original - because the referee added a few lost minutes... Unfortunately, as much as they are taken on board, they will not bring back the Bledisloe. <i>(Aug 13, 2000,Sunday Star Times,B3</i>)

>  His Excellency, the Governor-General, Lord Bledisloe, has presented a cup for competition between New Zealand and Australian Rugby teams, and the first match will be the Test at Auckland today... The "Bledisloe Cup" will be the first trophy presented for matches between Australia and New Zealand. <i>(Sep 12, 1931,Dominion,9</i>)

### bleed
 
 <i>v.</i> [keep as a spec. ref. to tooted or ergotty sheep.]

>  One ram was tooted and we had to bleed him freely. <i>(Nov 19, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,110,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Often in that Autumn, belated lambs that failed to fatten in the earlier drafts, waxed fat. The seed ripened on the ryegrass, and with it came the legacy of `ergot'. Practical sheep men found that this complaint produced `staggers'. Too much blood obtained, although the patient was in splendid condition, used to keep spinning round, and the remedy was the ancient panacea of most diseases in England, centuries ago, and ere science interrupted their outlook, viz. `to bleed' and take from the patient the overflow of red corpuscles. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,145</i>)

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> After camping the night there, we had to cross Kerr's Hill which was covered with tutu, a poisonous plant for sheep, and the curse of drovers. ... Odd sheep started to get `tooted' and instead of pushing on the men started bleeding the sheep. This was done by cutting a vein under the eye. It was supposed to help the sheep, although I never thought it did any good. <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,19</i>)

### bleeder
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Gum climbers and bleeders scaled kauri using a weighted line and rope, and later, taking advantage of kauri's soft outer layer of bark and sapwood, hand spikes, climbing hooks, and boots equipped with toe spikes. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,32</i>)

### bleeding
 
 <i>n.</i> [Of gum]

>  It was found that if slashes were made in the trunk of the tree, it would bleed, and the resin could be harvested later. Initially, it was thought that bleeding did not harm the tree. <i>(Apr 1991,NZ Geographic,29</i>)

### blended household
 
 <i>phr.</i> a household comprising parents/partners and their children and/or children from previous marriages or partnerships. [Deputy Government Statistician, Radio NZ 10/6/2002 probably US]

>  Like in the old television show, <i>The Brady Bunch</i>, a blended family is formed when parents get divorced or widowed (when one parent dies) and then remarry. The parents bring their children together and create a new family. <i>(Jun 10, 2002,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: [See kidshealth.org/teen/your_mind/families/blended_families.html]]

>  Dr Fryor says despite the growing "normality" of blended families, not enough is known about what makes them tick and why some work better than others. <i>(Mar 13, 2003,Norwester,3</i>)

### Blenheim orange
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Amongst the varieties most suitable for London are the following: Sturmer Pippin.. Blenheim Orange.. Wellington Pippin. . Cox's Orange Pippin. <i>(1894,Ann. Rep. Dept of Agriculture,II/177</i>)

### Blenheimer
 
 <i>n.</i> a brand of wine

>  <b>OLDS</b> The Blenheimer was cooling my throat. <i>(1985,After Looking for Broadway,9</i>)

### Blenheimite
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  So the Blenheimites browbeat the poor farmers quite, - <i>(Sep 19, 1868,Marlborough Express,6</i>)

### blenny
 
 <i>n.</i> used mainly as a book-name (f. <i>Blennius</i>) for a widespread family (?genus), or in NZ a variety of genera, small spiny-finned fishes recorded earliest in NZ waters(OED2 1774); often (esp. by children) popularly called cockabully; (See [also triplefin](../A#also-triplefin))

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Blenny <i>Blennius maoricus</i> Kner ... Blenny <i>Ericentrus ruber</i> (Hutton) ... Blenny <i>Cologrammus flavescens </i>(Hutton) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,411</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 411]

>  <b>WILSON</b> BLENNY ... Small boys know them well, and catch them by hand in rock pools. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,28</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] blenny [TS] 1889-1985 BLENNIES 1880-1988 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> They are normally referred to as blennies in New Zealand but they are not true blennies ... and are best referred to as triplefins in reference to their three dorsal fins. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,278,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> BLENNIES OR COCKABULLIES. 1. Threepenny, <i>Gilloblennis</i> 2. Blenny, <i>Ericentrus</i>. 3. Cockabully, Forsterygion. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,228, [Caption] Fig. 72:</i>)

>  [species name] blenny .. [scientific name] <i>Tripterygiidae</i> .. [other name(s)] bully <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We got some other new fish, viz. a small Lumpfish [[ed. fn] <i>Cyclopterus pinnulatus</i> of <i>Descr. Anim</i>., p.30], a small Blenny, both caught under the rocks at low water mark; some Starfish, Coats of mail water spouts, Centipus, Muscles & Turbines, besides in the Evening a Lamprey ... [[ed. fn] <i>Petromyzon cirratus</i> of <i>Descr. Anim</i>. p.112] was caught. We described all this and drew a new Sole.[ed. fn] <i>Pleuronectes scapha</i> of <i>Descr. Anim</i>. p.130] <i>(Apr 8, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/250</i>)

>  <i>Trypterygium compressum. </i>Hutton. The Blenny ... This specimen of a blenny was caught off Otago Heads, ... <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/168</i>) [NOTES: (Arthur)]

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> More numerous still in both species and individual members are the Blennies, small spiny-rayed fishes in which the pelvic fins are placed far forward under the throat. Of the fifteen species in New Zealand five are recorded from Canterbury ... <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,200</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 200]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a fish

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] blenny, Maori 1889 ... [index to common names 283-293] Maori blenny [TS] 1889 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL</b> The mimic blenny, <i>Plagiotremus tapeinosoma </i>, is more aggressive, hiding amid schools of similar but harmless species and darting out to bite pieces from larger fish. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,135</i>)

>  <i>MIMIC BLENNY Plagiotremus tapeinosoma (Aspidontus maroubrae)</i> ... The name mimic blenny comes from this fish's habit of hiding amongst schools of somewhat similar but harmless oblique swimming triplefin in an attempt to get closer to potential prey without being detected. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,277,1984 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  [index to common names 283-293] blenny, threefin 1934, 1968 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> THREEFIN BLENNY ... <i>Other names: Tripterygion sp </i>.; cockabully, twister, weedfish. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,284</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] blenny, pool 1886 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] blenny, banded 1923-4, 1974 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Banded Blenny <i>Botoclinops bucknilli </i>(Griffin) ... Banded Blenny <i>Notoclinops segmentatus </i> (McCulloch and Phillipps) .. Banded Blenny <i>Petroscirtes rhinorhijnchos </i> Bleeker <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,411</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 411]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> BANDED BLENNY <i>Notoclinops segmentatus </i>(McCulloch and Phillipps) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,333</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] banded blenny [TS] 1923-4, 1974 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>CRESTED BLENNY Parablennius laticlavius (Blennius laticlavius </i>) ... The crested blenny is a small fish ... that like most blennies has a smooth scaleless skin and a blunt head. It has a long tapering body and the distinctive crest, made i of a large forked cirrus above each eye, that suggested the name crested blenny. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,277,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> Blennies <i>Family Blenniidae. The crested blenny, Parablennius laticlavius </i>, has a blunt head, tapering body, and a long dorsal fin behind a small crest above each eye. Yellowish brown with black stripe. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,135</i>)

### Blerta
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Bruno Lawrence's Electric Revelation and Travelling Apparition [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  After <i>Tankbusters</i> came Blerta, a combination of a band, a theatre group and a film company, which was started by Lawrence. <i>(Jan 16, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  According to the afficionados of New Zealand's musical history, Blerta stands for Bruno Lawrence's Electric Revelation and Travelling Apparition. For most New Zealanders over 50, however, Blerta is best remembered as a colourful troupe of artists, anarchists and musicians who travelled the highways and byways of New Zealand in a vivdly decorated omnibus of indeterminate age and intermittent reliability. ... Religious allusions aside, Blerta's over-riding message was fun. <i>(Oct 28, 2005,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  The ultimate road trip lasted for a good part of the 1970s when the Blerta (Bruno Lawrence's Electric Travelling Apparition) bus led a convoy of performers and hangers-on across the back blocks of Australia and New Zealand. <i>(Jul 2, 2000,Sunday Star Times,F3</i>)

>  During its five-year run in the 1970s, Blerta attracted such an extraordinary collection of movers, shakers and far-out characters, Bruno was just one wild man among many... "It's possible to trace the beginings of the New Zealand film industry from Blerta," says Murphy, whose documentary <i>Blerta Revisited</i>, features in the naton's film festivbals this month... Blerta began in October, 1971, when Bruno was 30 years old and restless as a bear stung by a swarm of bees... Blerta was an acronym for Bruno Lawrence's Electric Revelation and Travelling Apparition... "Blurter" was also a rude term for genitals in '70s slang. <i>(Jul 22, 2001,Sunday Star Times,F1</i>)

>  Dropping out was in then, and so was Bruno Lawrence's Electric Revelation and Travelling Apparition. The family hooked up with the heady Blerta roadshow for a while, and seven-year-old Karl was one of many kids with happy memories of racing on stage during performances. <i>(Apr 28, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)

### blight-bird
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Zosterops halmaturina</i> also called blighty, silvereye, blighty, white-eye, wax-eye, tauhou (See [silvereye](../S#silvereye))

>  ZOSTEROPS LATERALIS, <i>Lath</i>. - White-eye, Blight Bird. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/125</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  <b>BARLOW</b> We have a beautiful little bird called the Blight bird ... <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,191</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> The birds I noticed were the bell-bird, the blight-bird, the yellow-headed tit, the ground lark, the little parakeet, the banded dotterel, and the native snipe. <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,275,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANNERING</b> In and around the garden [in Canterbury in the 1870s] we had robins (rare) waxeyes (blight birds), tomtits,. ... <i>(1943,Eighty Years,21</i>)

>  <i>tauhou</i>, a wandering bird (Zosterops halmaturina), blight-bird; twinkie; button-eye; wax-eye; ring-eye. Tauhou means stranger and this name was given by the Maori when the bird appeared, in 1856, in the central part of Newzealand, gradually spreading north and south till the islands were occupied. It is an Australian bird, and was blown across the Tasman sea in the year mentioned. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/159</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kanohi-mowhiti, Zosterops lateralis, silver-eye</i> or <i>blight-bird </i>(mod.). <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,94</i>) [NOTES: [this is the first (?only) use of this term in any Williams.]]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Zosterops (ring eye) cerulescens</i> ... The white-eye feeds on insects and fruit besides honey. ... [74] White-eye, Blight Bird. POTEREKAI. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,73</i>)

>  [Caption] Silver-eyes are also known as "Blight Birds", being particularly valuable in removing aphids from orchards and rose bushes. <i>(Dec 18, 1935,NZ Free Lance,36</i>)

>  The blightie is a very tame little bird. <i>(Apr 22, 1924,Otago Witness,63</i>)

>  The silver-eye or `blight bird' frequented the gardens in the settled districts [of the South Island]. <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/207</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> The history of the little <i>Zosterops</i>, or Blight-bird, is a case in point. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/82 By the settlers it [sc <i>Zosterops</i>] has been variously designated as Ring-eye, Wax-eye, White-eye, or Silver-eye, in allusion to the beautiful circlet of satiny-white feathers which surrounds the eyes; and quite as commonly as the `Blight-bird', or `Winter Migrant'. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/xli</i>)

>  To-day my son was dissecting a common bittern (<i>Botaurus poecilopilus</i>) ... We found the `bill of fare' for that day to have been a silver-eye (<i>Zosterops caerulescens</i>), ... It is well known that the bittern varies its feeding ... adding still another enemy to our useful little migrant blight-bird. <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/285</i>) [NOTES: (Drew)]

>  <b>FIRTH</b> blight bird <i>(1890,Nation Making,208</i>)

>  <i>Blight&#769;.bird&#769;.</i> (bl&imacr.t&#769;.b&etilde.rd&#769;.), <i>n. (Zoo.l.) </i>The New Zealand name for the white-eye (<i>Zosterops coerulescens</i>), whichclears trees of insect blight; - called also <i>ring-eye, silver-eye</i>, and <i>wax-eye</i>. See WHITE-EYE in Dictionary. The bird was originally confined to eastern Australia and Tasmania, but it settled in flocks in New Zealand in 1851, making its escape from the great bush fire of Black Thursday (Feb. 6, 1851). <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2018</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Blight-bird</i> ... a bird-name in New Zealand for the <i>Zosterops</i> (q.v.). .us; <i>Called also</i> Silver-eye <i>(q.v.)</i>, Wax-eye, <i>and </i>White-eye (q.v.). It is called <i>Blight-bird </i>because it eats the blight on trees. <i>(1898,Austral English,36</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> The white-eye or blight-bird, with cheerful note, in crowded flocks, sweeps over the face of the country, and in its progress clears away multitudes of small insect pests.' <i>(1882,Out in the Open,130</i>)

>  A notable exception is the silver-eye, white-eye or blight-bird (zosterops) which was blown across the Tasman Sea by a storm some fifty or sixty years ago. <i>(Jun 18, 1904,Press, The,8</i>)

>  [<i>Zosterops</i>] subsists almost entirely [in Wellington orchards and gardens] on the destructive little <i>Aphis</i> known as `American Blight'. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/10</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  ZOSTEROPS LATERALIS, Lath. Tauhou. Blight-bird. We first noticed this bird ... in the Rockwood Valley, Malvern Hills, July 28th, 1856. ... It very soon obtained the name of Blight-bird ... from its habit of feeding on the American blight, with which apple trees in this colony are so generally infested ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/61</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  By the settlers it has been variously designated as Ring-eye, Wax-eye, White-eye or Silver-eye in allusion to the beautiful circlet of satiny-white feathers which surrounds the eyes.; and quite as commonly as the `Blight-bird' or `Winter Migrant.' <i>(1871,TrNZI,3/21</i>) [NOTES: (Buller)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 14. ZOSTEROPS LATERALIS. Lath. White-eye. Wax-eye. Blight-bird. Tauhou. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,6</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Tauhau (<i>Zosterops lateralis</i>) is a beautiful little green bird, much like a wren. ... It is much seen about gardens and clearings, and settlers know it as the `blight-bird'. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/226</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> SILVEREYE <i>Zosterops lateralis Other names: </i>Tauhou, Waxeye, White-eye, Blightbird, Blightie. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,223</i>)

>  <i>~ n</i>. New Zealand bird, the white-eye, or silver-eye. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),611</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> After their arrival in New Zealand in 1865, silvereyes soon became popular because of their attack on the orchard pest woolly aphis. This earned them an alternative name of `blight bird'. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/255</i>)

>  <i>blight bird ... n.</i> N.Z. bird, the white-eye, or silver-eye, or or blight-eye. Maori name <i>tauhou.</i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  ~ One of the New Zealand names for a small bird, also called `white-eye' and `silver-eye'. It is an Australian bird but established itself in New Zealand about a hundred years ago. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),42</i>)

>  <i>Silvereye Zosterops lateralis lateralis</i> (LATHAM, 1801) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>White-eye, waxeye, blightbird, tauhou. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,288</i>)

>  <i>blight-bird n. NZ & Austral.</i> silver-eye (which feeds on tree-blight). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,73</i>) [NOTES: [see BLIGHTY, Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 1332 & 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 51]]

>  `<i>Zosterops lateralis</i>, white-eye, blight-bird. One of our best friends, and abundant in all parts of the district.' <i>(1887,TrNZI,18/125</i>) [NOTES: check yearA. Hamilton, `Native Birds of Petane, Hawke's Bay']

>  The little green white-eyes, or blight birds, are moving from place to place ... <i>(Oct 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,43</i>)

>  <b>WHITE</b> The little `Silver Eye' or `Blight Bird,' so called from its very useful propensity of eating various blights, has a silvery circle round its eyes, composed of tiny white feathers which are sweetly becoming. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,71</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>pihipihi</i>, n. <i>1. Zosterops caerulescens, wax-eye, blight-bird. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,325</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small bird <i>Zosterops lateralis</i>, valued by horticulturists for pest control. [also known as ring-eye, wax-eye, white-eye or silver-eye].

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Kanohimowhiti, or Tauhau, white eye or blight bird <i>(Zosterops lateralis)</i> was first observed July 1856 in the South, and about Auckland. <i>(1870,Maori & English Dictionary,17/2</i>)

>  The little 'wax-eye' or as it is commonly known, the 'blight-bird' is reported to be doing good work in the orchards and gardens this year. <i>(Jun 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,522</i>)

### blighty
 
 <i>n.</i> a blight-bird, silvereye

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> SILVEREYE <i>Zosterops lateralis Other names</i>: Tauhou, Waxeye, White-eye, Blightbird, Blightie. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,223</i>)

>  mainly Auckland or North Island <i>(1979,Heinemann Dictionary of NZ Quotations</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>blighty</i> blight bird, now better known as waxeye, white-eye or silver-eye. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,15</i>)

>  <b>GREY</b> The trees were full of the little yellow blighties, named from their very good habit of eating the insect blight. <i>(1926,Angler's Eldorado,170</i>)

### blimey Charley
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  <b>BAKER</b> Need to find some outlet for pent-up feelings resulted in the concoction of <i>blimey charley!</i> (why charley?); <i>by Christchurch. </i> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,50</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Blimey Charley!</i> A c.p. used to blow off pent-up emotions: New Zealand and Australian: 20C. B1941. In Australian, also <i>blimey Teddy! </i> <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,992</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Some expressions that have been claimed for New Zealand are <i>at the rate of knots</i> `very fast', <i>hook your mutton</i> `clear out', <i>have the wood on</i> `have an advantage over' and the oaths <i>Blimey Charlie</i> and<i> (By) Christchurch </i>(obviously a neat save suggested by the place name). <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,177</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>blimey, Charlie! </i>indicating friendly relief; obs.; derived from mostly Cockney `blimey' abbreviation of `Gorblimey', abbreviation of `God blind me'. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,15</i>)

### blind
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  ... both stentons and bords should be blinded so as to make the air full. <i>(Nov 10, 1911,Maoriland Worker,36/6/2</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> as an epithet in the names of fish see: brotulid, cucumberfish, eel; ray, electric; numbfish



 
 <i>adj.</i> having no channel or opening for milk; thus, useless, dud.

>  <b>DAVIN</b> ... the only cow we bought ... turned out afterwards to have one blind teat. [sc a dud dug] <i>(1959,No Remittance,93</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> leading nowhere, terminating abruptly. esp. blind creek, blind gully.

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> Teddy and W. to lambs to drive them to pen, smothered 10 in a blind gully. <i>(Jan 15, 1848,Journal,97</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Jan 15 Pharazyn Journal (ATB:MS) 97 (also 152 chk)]

>  <b>HAAST</b> ... we descended as fast as we could, but soon discovered that we had selected a so-called blind spur, which fell abruptly into a steep [OED2 deep: check] gully, ... <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,8</i>)

>  The principal workings here are blind gullies and streams. <i>Ibid.</i> 24 With an immense number of blind gullies, all more or less auriferous, and a creek that paid and is promising to pay for a considerable distance yet ... who can predict the future of the Woolshed ... <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and ReSources,23</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> He's in one of them blind gullies. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,127</i>)

>  <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: see prec.Acland Glossary]

>  <b>McLEOD</b> A clearing completely surrounded by bush will be called a `blind Parkee'. <i>Ibid.</i> 255 <i>Pakihi</i>: A clearing in the bush, perhaps only a bay. If totally enclosed, a `blind' pakihi. <i>(1951,High Country,43</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> hard to see; with entrance covered

>  <i>Blind</i>.- (1) Unseen, hard to see (creek, gully, etc.) (2) Leading nowhere (spur, creek, gully, etc.) I think (1) is the commoner usage. (C.) <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary (ca1890-1910)]

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Gradually the [dog's command] distance is lengthened until you hunt him as far as you can see. Then he must be taught to run `on the blind' - that is, to go up or down hill as directed, looking for sheep he hasn't seen. <i>(1951,High Country,22</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> BOTAURUS POECILOPTILUS. (BLACK-BACKED BITTERN.) ... <i>Native name.</i>- Matuku-hurepo. ... [142] The Common Bittern is very generally distributed ... in places suited to its habits of life, such as raupo swamps, sedgy lagoons, and those `blind creeks', covered over with a growth of reeds ands tangle, which are so numerous in all the low districts. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,141</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) 1. ground easily overlooked when inspecting a flock. 2. a feature such as a gully or a spur that ends abruptly and denies access. (See [on the blind](../O#on-the-blind))

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> blind: i. Ground easily overlooked unintentionally even when quietly 'looking' sheep. ii. A feature - a gully or spur which ends abruptly and so denies access beyond. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,7</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> ?not leading to a richer seam or lode of ore; esp. as blind leader, blind reef, blind vein.

>  At present the diggings are believed to be confined to Tuapeka ... where Gabriel's Gully ... and a few blind leaders have been opened. <i>Ibid.</i> 45 Some of the many prospecting parties who are now ransacking the mountains are meeting with temporary success, but it is only in small ravines or blind leaders. <i>(Sep 20, 1861,NZ Goldfields,12</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> Eventually it was proved that the find was but a `blind reef', a `pocket', a mere isolated dribble from the main continuous vein we had at first supposed we had struck. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/299</i>)

>  These particular veins are `blind', or feather out in the enclosing dacites. <i>(1912,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.15 (NS) 126</i>) [NOTES: (Bell and Fraser, Waihi)]

### blind creek
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dried up creek-head, partly hidden.

>  Poor and mediocre benefits have resulted from the failure to clear all roots and stumps, to give the surface of fields some preliminary grading, to avoid drawing the mole across depressions and blind creeks. <i>(Jan 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,11</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> ..soft spots, localities mined with under-runners, blind oozy creeks, cliffs and so forth are avoided. <i>(1921,Tutira,142</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> .. the ground became terribly broken and cut up by swamps, quicksands, blind creeks, and all sorts of snares and pitfalls. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,68</i>)

### blind dipping
 
 <i>n.</i> dipping a gold-pan indiscriminately or blindly into a stream-bed on the chance of washing out gold.

>  <b>PYKE</b> When matters had settled down a bit the miners spread themselves along the river, getting gold almost everywhere on the beaches, and following it in until they stood up to their thighs in the stream `blind dipping'. <i>(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries  in Otago,104,1962 ed.</i>)

>  The plan of what, we believe, was called at the Nelson diggings "blind dipping" has, we learn, been successfully practised at the Dunstan. this is done by the miner standing in the water and taking up shovelfuls of stuff from the bottom. <i>(Nov 8, 1862,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

### blind eel
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Eptatratus cirratus</i>, a sea-eel exuding plentiful slime when hooked; =hagfish, tuere; (See [tuere](../T#tuere))

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>kanakana, ... 3. Blind eel or lamprey</i>. [so 1957/93 <i>Geotria australis, lamprey. = korokoro.</i>] <i>Ibid.</i> 324 <i>Pia ... 2. Blind eel. = napia. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,110</i>)

>  <i>Eptatretus cirrhatus</i> Forster. Blind-eel or Hag. <i>(1929,TrNZI,60/139</i>) [NOTES: (Young) 2]

>  <b>MORELAND</b> HAGFISH [<i>Eptatretus cirrhatus</i>] ... Hagfish and blind eel are widely used alternative names, while the Maori tuere and napia are still in use. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,30</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The above list of fish to be caught along the Canterbury coast has some notable omissions, as hoka (red cod) ... though I am not so sure about the rawaru (blue cod). Others omitted are the tarakihi, or terakihi, the moki, the komutumutu (trevalli), the puhaiwhakarua (red-jacket), koiro (conger eel), tuere (blind eel), whai (skate), and the koekohe [sic] (trumpeter). <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> HAGFISH (TUERE) <i>Eptatretus cirrhatus </i>(Bloch and Schneider) The Hagfish, or Blind-eel as the fishermen call it, is one of the most unpleasant-looking creatures of the sea. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,51,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Pia ... Heptatretus cirratus, hagfish</i> or <i>blind eel. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,279</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> the common hag fish or `blind eel', <i>Eptatretus cirrhatus ... Eptatretus</i> exudes quantities of thick slime. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,193</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> HAGFISH ... <i>Other names: Heptatretus cirrhatus</i>; blind eel; tuere, napia (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,297</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> BLIND EEL: see <i>Hagfish </i> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,28</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Hagfish Eptatretus cirratus also known as blind eel; it is eel-like, but not a true eel. Hagfishes occur in cool waters throughout the world, but this species is restricted to New Zealand.</i> <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,23</i>)

>  [species name] hagfish .. [scientific name] <i>Eptatretus cirrhatus</i> .. [other name(s)] blind eel <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] eel, blind 5-6, 10, 17 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  The repulsive blind eel is very much in evidence. <i>(Mar 9, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

### blind gully
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a steep-sided valley that ends suddenly.

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> Teddy and W to lambs to drive them to pen, smother'd 10 in a blind gully. <i>(Jan 15, 1848,Pharazyn Diary</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> .. taking short cuts across the lake to reach a stray heifer or explore a blind gully. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,72</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> 'He's in one of them blind gullies.' <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,127</i>)

>  About two miles from the river, on the Lammerlaw Creek, in a blind gully, christened by Constable Garvey, at the request of the miners, Johnson's gully, who was the first to prospect the place ... <i>(Jan 7, 1862,Otago Daily Times,2</i>)

>  A party of Maories from Jacob's River are doing well [mining] in a blind gully leading into Victoria Gully. The day I was there they found a nugget over an ounce in weight. <i>(Oct 2, 1862,Otago Daily Times,5</i>)

### blind off
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>DAVIS</b> After I come home at night the jargon runs round and round my head. ... On meat - bit of a jockey here, boys ; give us legs, legs; bring out your burton; blind her off; we'll need rollers and staging; turn off the blowers, mister mate, or we don't go in the freezer. <i>(1964,Watersiders,105</i>)

### blind roller
 
 <i>n.</i> a large wave which does not break

>  <b>PRESHAW</b> A few of us landed in one of the ship's boats; not a safe thing to do as a rule on the Coast, what are known as `blind rollers' often rising and swamping a boat. <i>(1888,Banking Under Difficulties,155</i>)

### blind tiger
 
 <i>n.</i> a sly-grogger; a seller or provider of illegal liquor

>  The man who enters a boarding or `temperance' house is immediately accused of having rolled a beer barrel through the maze of the Licensing Act. He is suspected of designs of all descriptions. He is dubbed `a dropper', or `a blind tiger.' <i>(Oct 26, 1912,NZ Truth,383/6/5</i>)

### blind-drag
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>HULME</b> And every beginning spring, Coasters in their hundreds flock to the rivers and streams to swoop and scoop and blind-drag as many [whitebait] as possible out of sanctuary. <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,37</i>) [NOTES: (King Bait)]

>  <b>HULME</b> And every beginning spring, Coasters in their hundreds flock to the rivers and streams to swoop and scoop and blind-drag as many [whitebait] as possible out of sanctuary <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,119</i>) [NOTES: 1974 Hulme in 1977 Listener Short Stories (King Bait) 119]

### blind-dragging
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HULME</b> The haiku was just a pleasant poetic fancy, blind- dragging was peculiar terminology, and the great [whitebait] runs - well, myths of the past ... <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,38</i>) [NOTES: 1986 Hulme Te Kaihau (King Bait) 38]

### blind-shotted
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>R GILBERD</b> `Nothing between us of course - only you knock around with a girl, it saves a man being blind-shotted' ... a beautiful descriptive word for the `tomo' or forced marriage. <i>(Oct 20, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1951 Oct 20 R. Gilberd Okaihau]

### blind-stabbing
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining] (See [cf. blind dip](../B#blind-dip))

>  <b>CLEVELAND</b> <i> blind-stabbing</i>: Used by Jack Sweeney, prospector, to describe traditional method of getting gold-bearing wash out of glacial rivers. The prospector is anchored by a rope which is controlled by a mate on the bank. He wheels a wheelbarrow as far into the torrent as is possible, shovels the alluvial wash into it blindly, because the water contains glacial silts and is milky white, and returns to the bank with as much as he can shift. This is processed at leisure for gold <i>(1950,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: South Westland]

### blinded with science
 
 <i>phr.</i> [ca1945 Wellington students': as a fairly common expression in forms of to blind one with science' rather than a c.p. as Partridge]

>  ... `blinded with science' comes from boxing, as does `well gone.' <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

### blister
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <i>blister v.</i> charge exhorbitantly for goods;<i>n</i>. exhorbitant charge for such. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

The charge was laid on the stone and covered with damp clay to make it airtight. Frequently used by truckers when the ore passes or chutes became blocked with large boulders. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),313</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a strong, usu. official written reprimand.

>  Note how the Labour Department always like to get your `goat.' When you refuse the overtime ... how they chase you round with `blisters'. <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book,44</i>)

I was sanguine enough to believe that a ,us; parental application of hot water and soap, administered with the added impetus of an official `blister' delivered in an envelope bearing the awe-inspiring legend `On His Majesty's Service', might have the desired effect. <i>(1939,Sketches from Maoriland,68,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <i>blister ... 3. a. N.Z. sl.</i> a rebuke. <i>b. N.Z. & Austral. sl.</i> a summons to court. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,113</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD but cf. 74 (as verb); 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 51]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

 <i>v.</i> blizzing (ppl a) coinage formed from blizzard occas. used esp. by trampers. [ca1952,and 1953 (R. Mason) `Heard among trampers in Tararuas: `still blizzing', `weather blizzy']

### blizzard
 
 <i>n.</i> a furious blast of frost-wind and blinding snow.....; a snow-squall. [??Omit.]

>  ... on the long stretch between Cape Egmont and Wellington, a new trouble has arrived in the shape of the `blizzard', the name signifying in America a snow-blast, but which has in the locality under mention been applied to the salt-storms that drive in from Cook Strait. <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/439</i>) [NOTES: (Walsh)]

### bloat
 
 <i>n.</i>

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

### bloater
 
 <i>n.</i> pilchard, esp.as bait.

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Pilchard Sardinops neopilchardus</i> ... Sometimes called the sardine. Also occurs around southern Australia. ... In the 1880s a fishery in the Marlborough Sounds produced salted and smoked `Picton Herring', and small fisheries have developed briefly elsewhere. ... Pilchard are caught for groper bait (`bloater'), and have been used also for tuna. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,40</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bloated dead animal.

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an animal that blows or bloats.

<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of spring weather conditions] capable of producing bloat in stock.

 <i>n.</i> [ca1985 March 16; so called at MAF Fisheries Research Unit's open day at Greta Point, Wellington]
