### block
 
 <i>n.</i> a pulley

>  <b>MAHONEY</b> A block, the common bush term for a pulley, was attached to a strong tree at each point where the change of direction was required. <i>(1998,Era of the Bush Trams in NZ, The,19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a wristwatch (USA, for any kind of watch, from 1914).

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> BLOCK (n) 1. Wristwatch. USA, for any kind of watch, from 1914. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,245</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a parcel of land (suitable eventually) for farming; a farm, or part of a farm, run, station [?should this be 2.], cf backblock @n.

>  <b>NAIRN & STEPHEN</b> Our day's travelling was over some high undulating land with flax tutu high grass and several blocks of bush land with very good timber. <i>(Jan 18, 1852,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Jan 18 Nairn and Stephen Journal in 1947 Beattie Pioneers Explore Otago 27]

>  [forest land] In the neighbourhood of Kaikoura, along the base of Mount Fyffe, and in the Hapuka Valley, there is another small block of forest land, in which three small sawmills have been erected. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,17</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> The block of bush, which belonged to an absentee, contained two or three hundred acres, ... <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,158</i>)

>  <b>OWEN</b> He considered it a luxury for a man who was often reduced to <i>rou-rou</i> on a back country block. <i>(1909,Philip Loveluck,82</i>)

>  On going over a block about a mile long and a quarter wide we found close on three hundred dead sheep. <i>(1907,TrNZI,39/292</i>) [NOTES: (a high-country block)]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> There are many things to be taken into consideration when burning. Climate - is your country early or late? Are you carrying ewes or wethers on the block that you propose to burn? In what state is your neighbour's country? If your country is warm and early, with a small rainfall, it is a good rule to burn early in the season ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,96,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> ... there was a wedge-shaped block of ninety thousand acres of high tops, mostly bush-bound, in the middle ... <i>(1946,Early Canterbury Runs,210</i>)

>  <i>Block:</i> an area of land, e.g.: Development block - an area being developed from bush or scrub to farmland. Settlement block - an area made (settled) into farms. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,5</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> He was ... able to prolong the toughness by taking on a new block in the sparse pumice land of New Zealand's North Island. <i>(1986,Change Agent,19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a chops block; the block of wood to be cut, not that which it stands on

>  <b>BACON</b> At intervals, Mac would call for competitors to draw their blocks; the selected blocks, trimmed to a uniform diameter, would be set on the ring of upturned logs and securely nailed into position ready for the next event. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,135</i>)

>  <i>block ... 6. do one's block. Austral. sl.</i> to become angry. ... <i>10. Austral. & N.Z. </i>an area of land, usually extensive, taken up for farming, settlement, etc. <i>11. N.Z.</i> an area of bush reserved by licence for a trapper or hunter. ... <i>13. Austral. & N.Z.</i> a log, usually of willow, fastened to a timber base and used in a wood-chopping competition. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,114</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 74; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 52]]



 
 <i>n.</i> a self-contained off-job training or study section of a trainee's or apprentice's training period, esp. a block course, an off-job course of study or training done in a `block' of time (say a fortnight) as distinct from a course given while working on the job.

>  <b>R MASON</b> Esp. on block. ca1939 R. Mason (nurses) In early days nurses trained and nursed at the same time, a system replaced by a `block' of nursing followed by a `block' of study. ON BLOCK signifies the whole time is devoted to study; on a period. <i>(Jun 7, 1952,Letter,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> sexual activity

>  <b>MARRINER</b> 'Yeah, but ... but before he'd let anyone take 'er up, 'e made 'er go on the block.' <i>(2001,Stonedogs,102</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> also a mustering block, the area the responsibility of a single musterer. [[see 4.below and move here ?];]

>  <b>KIERNAN</b> Put X-breds in Newton block, and T.S. took the Merion [sic] wethers over Papa creek. <i>(Feb 1, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Feb 1 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 125]

>  <b>PERRY</b> These sheep [sc. Merinos] will thrive far better on one block with plenty of room to move about than they will if constantly moved from one block to another. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,90</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> [station] There are many things to be taken into consideration when burning. Climate - is your country early or late? Are you carrying ewes or wethers on the block that you propose to burn? In what state is your neighbour's country? If your country is warm and early, with a small rainfall, it is a good rule to burn early in the season ... <i>Ibid.</i> 97 The hill lambs were weaned onto a block of country which had been spelled since shearing-time. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,96,1954 ed.</i>)

>  (`Central Otago') [Regulations] legalized at that time [1856], allowed land outside the original Otago Block to be bought for grazing-runs at a low cost. (Runholders were allowed 80 acres for the homestead block with 10 acres for each outstation.) <i>(1948,Our Own Country,135</i>)

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> Leaving the homestead at daybreak we mustered half the block, hunting the sheep across a small creek by dark. <i>(1969,Wild Irishman,107</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> [station] A greater number of stations have snowline fences on their winter blocks, ... . <i>(1981,Both Sides of the River,8</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to gang-rape, to group rape or group sodomise

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> BLOCK (v) Group rape or group sodomy. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,245</i>)

>  `The Black Power boys will block you if you struggle ...' That was a threat allegedly made ... to a 16-year-old girl ... She said he [the rapist] told her she would be put on the block if she struggled ... `He told me not to say anything to anyone or I would get done.' <i>(Feb 14, 1978,NZ Truth,3</i>)

>  Mr Weeks said Doyle told him: `I do not think the boys would have blocked her if they had known she was only 14 years-old.' <i>(Oct 29, 1978,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  Bloat (whose real name is Stuart David Burns), Peanut (Wayne Michael Fitzgerald), L.T. (Aloysuis [sic] Antonius Achterberg) and Gab (Timoti Rangiera Flavell) all pleaded not guilty to sexual violation of a 17-year-old who confessed to smoking several joints before being `blocked'. <i>(Aug 10, 1986,Sunday News,9</i>)

>  In the old footage they talk about 'blocking' [gang-raping] women and they play with knives. <i>(Dec 17, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  <b>MARRINER</b> If they ain't rapin' chicks, then they bashing or blocking 'em. <i>(2001,Stonedogs,101</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Then for three weeks they dug post and strainer-holes, ran out wires, footed posts, blocked and stayed the angles, strained up, [etc.] <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,126</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... Home had told the girl: `You've got between now and the time I finish this cigarette until you got into the bedroom and go on the block. <i>(Dec 4, 1973,NZ Truth,5/3</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> `Possible. What did that stupid goon say in that Auckland trial? Blocking was ceremonial. There was no way the pigs were going to stop it.' ... The block. Multiple rape. Gang rape. <i>(1980,Murder and Chips,103</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Members of the Hells Angels' motorcycle gang put two teenage girls `on the block', Auckland Supreme Court was told. <i>(Oct 9, 1973,NZ Truth,51/1</i>)

>  Later she heard the boys talking about `putting a girl on the block'. Mr Rowan: What does that expression mean to you. Witness: It's when a whole heap of jokers have intercourse with one girl. <i>(Dec 4, 1973,NZ Truth,5/2</i>)

>  A girl was told: `you have got your head on the chopping block.' Then she was raped by several youths, ... <i>(Jun 18, 1974,NZ Truth,1`1/6</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> One time a bunch of gang members from the other side of the compound decided they were going to put her on the block. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,131</i>)

>  <i>on the block</i>: about to be excluded; about to be raped <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]



 
 <i>n.</i> 1. one of the parcels in which a town site is available for settlement ... 1833 2. a parcel of land which is entire and of a piece, esp. one taken up for settlement 1835 3. a variously defined unit of measurement used to limit the size of a piece of land taken up for settlement or to determine rent 1843 ... the runs would be divided into blocks of a square mile ...4. a building allotment 1874 5. mining 1858 a block claim is a fixed area, with bounds ascertained from the first.]

>  <i>~ n</i>. a surveyed piece of land; a section of a town bounded by streets.- doing the block, on the block, promenading in a popular or fashionable place in a large town. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

>  <b>G T ARCUS</b> A few years ago I travelled to Britain via Australia and on the boat an Englishman was telling me that while in Australia he bought a block of land and built his own house. To me the word block suggested a large piece of land, so with visions of Sid Kidman I asked him how big. He replied, `Oh, just fifth of an acre,' I said that in NZ we would call that a section. An American put in that in his country a section was a square mile and the word was used with that meaning in parts of Australia, but in the States they used the word `lot' to describe a building block. I replied that in NZ building blocks were the piles that a house stood on and the Englishman said that in Australia they called them stumps. <i>(Nov 19, 1957,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <i>block ... 6. do one's block. Austral. sl</i>. to become angry. ... <i>10. Austral. & N.Z.</i> an area of land, usually extensive, taken up for farming, settlement, etc. <i>11. N.Z.</i> an area of bush reserved by licence for a trapper or hunter. ... <i>13. Austral. & N.Z</i>. a log, usually of willow, fastened to a timber base and used in a wood-chopping competition. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,114</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 74; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 52]]



 
 <i>n.</i> watersiders'

>  <b>JACKSON</b> There was seldom a day when I didn't get work on the block. With bonuses of dirt money and danger money, I calculated I'd easily save enough for a year's studies. <i>(1988,Rainshadow</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [shepherding and mustering] to ride round sheep or to muster a large unfenced area.

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> When I was blocking, it was just a case of catching and grooming a horse, saddling up, and getting on with the job. <i>(1981,Sunrise on Hikurangi,113</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> During the following weeks most of my time was spent blocking or riding around the ewes looking for cast ones. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,58</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> gang rape

 <i>n.</i> woman of easy virtue

 <i>n.</i> the head

 <i>n.</i> maximum security cell block

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> BLOCK (n) 2. Maximum security cell block. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,245</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> large area of land bought or leased for sheepfarming (only occas. for subdivision) cf backblocks.

>  The boundaries of the block are now pretty well defined ... <i>(Mar 25, 1841,Deans' Letters,31,1937 ed.</i>)

>  Several settlers are ready to cultivate land in the new `block' south of the Sugar Loaves [New Plymouth]. <i>(1849,Handbook to Suburban and Rural Dists. Otago</i>) [NOTES: The northern portions of the `block' ...]

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) 1. an area of land, usually separately surveyed off a larger piece 2. an extensive hilly paddock in hill and high country areas. 3. a complete farm property, occasionally one of many under the same ownership. (See [lifestyle -; ewe -; hogget -; summer -; winter -;](../L#lifestyle--;-ewe--;-hogget--;-summer--;-winter--;))

>  <b>DEANS</b> We are to take a lease of a block of land from the natives for 99 years ... <i>(1843,Pioneers on Port Cooper Plains,18</i>) [NOTES: Letter in DEANS (1964)]

>  <b>MCLEAN</b> With Messrs. Park, Rich, Wi Tako (of Wellington) Queen, Puhara and others, we rode to the Mimi and Jauru, thence to the northern point of the boundary of the new block. <i>(Dec 17, 1851,McLean Papers</i>)

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> The land is held in large blocks by speculators. <i>(1876,Colonial Travel,235</i>)

>  Following the coming of the pakeha the district had been farmed in large blocks, but the value of the land for dairying was realised early. <i>(Jun 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,573</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> They came to the district to take up their block. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,104</i>)

>  Their neighbours were farming a block of 700 acres. 2. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,114</i>)

>  Sheep and cattle should now be put on their winter blocks, keeping any animals not up to standard in a separate paddock. <i>(Apr 20, 1922,NZ Journal of Agriculture,220</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> When drafting was finished, my mate and myself helped the drovers take 5000 'fats' four miles across country to the main road, while the rest of the shepherds returned the rejects to their block. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,119</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Ridgemount now comprises four soldier settlement blocks ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,189</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> They were running in the Gully Block, fairly near the station. We picked up the coat and as we came out of the gate of the block, I suggested we swap horses. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,53</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> block: may be any specified portion of land within a property, eg Cabbage-tree block, Dipping block, Hogget block. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,7</i>)

>  When John bought the run six years ago, the hill had been split into 14 blocks...today there are 34 hill blocks and the carrying capacity has been lifted to 15,500 stock units. <i>(Sep 1985,NZ Journal of Agriculture,32</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> At that time there were only seven fenced-off blocks on the whole place, little different from any other similar run. Today there are 49, plus some holding paddocks near the yards. In the past sheep were put out on large blocks and wouldn't be seen for some months, till the next muster. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,42</i>)

>  The scrub blocks contained within the property boundaries shelter deer and the adjacent streams are the home of willy browns. 3. <i>(Aug 1, 1996,NZ Farmer,38</i>)

>  Owner is prepared to cut up into 500 acres, or more, but would rather sell the whole block. <i>(Feb 3, 1906,Farmers' Union Advocate,7</i>)

>  Land for sale by tender: 69.5097 ha. flat to rolling, fattening block at Makotuku ... <i>(Dec 8, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,24</i>)

>  Block stock manager for 5000 stock unit farm. <i>(Jun 15, 2002,Fencepost.com,Situations Vacant</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> land

>  The stud ewes were grazed from 1936 to 1941 ... on one block of pastures - `The Terrace'; then they were shifted for one year to another called the `Pahiatua Block.' <i>(1946,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,27/174</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> When Dr. F.F. Ormond purchased the Orangitirohia block on the north side of the river, not all the Natives had been satisfactorily dealt with, and a `Native difficulty' of the first grade soon arose. <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,20</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  The colonists, since they arrived in this country, have represented that great advantages would be gained by increasing the block so as to include all the plains of the district. They found that the Canterbury block was part of the plain lying in the vast segment of the wide circle between the sea and a range of mountains, entirely cut off from all other settlements by natural boundaries, and formed, as it were by nature, to consist of one district in itself. <i>(Jul 26, 1851,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  DESCRIPTION of the Country comprised with the Canterbury Block; ... <i>(1854,Canterbury Almanac, The,53</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> In 1853 the Canterbury block was enlarged and became known as the Canterbury Province. <i>(1947,Story of Canterbury, The,112</i>)

>  2. in land-subdivision, a major subdivision of a Survey District. ... A BLOCK is usually 1/16 of a Survey District and is square, with sides 250 chains long. Block numbers are indicated by roman numerals 3. in land-subdivision, a term relating to a defined area of Maori land and bearing the original Maori tribal name. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The New Zealand Company has received a Crown grant, under the seal of the territory, dated 13th of April, 1846, of the portion of this District [of Otago] <i>Ibid.</i> 300 ... the harbour of Otago, which is situated ... at the Northern extremity of the Block. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,297</i>)

>  <i>(1849,Handbook Otago Settlement</i>) [NOTES: [both block and `block'] (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> This Otago block contains 400,000 acres, and extends from Taiaroa Head down the coast to Tokata Point or the Nuggets, ... <i>(1899,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,58</i>) [NOTES: [both block and `block']]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> and official Government land purchase and partly a technical surveyors' term for a large surveyed parcel of land, which in some cases has been incorporated into place-names, some of which survive (e.g. Bell Block, Taranaki); A large parcel or lot of Maori land officially surveyed for purchase for subdivision and settlement; a survey block: possibly spec. of Ma. land defined and bearing the orig. tribal name (AND 2 1835 seems to fit with this last sense). - [ca1953 In a few cases `block', in this sense, has been incorporated into place names. e.g. Bell Block (Taranaki)]

>  <b>AYLMER</b> He had travelled through most of the settlement looking at the different `blocks' or divisions of land the Government buys from the natives to sell to the settlers. ... his proposed journey to look at a `block' or `lot' of land he had been advised to see. <i>(1862,Distant Homes,44</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Large areas of land were purchased in the North Island. Almost the entire South Island was bought in a few huge `blocks'. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,81</i>)

>  1. in forest management, a major subdivision of a forest, generally bounded by natural features and bearing a local name. In some of the first established exotic forests, BLOCK was used in place of <i>Compartment. </i> 2. in land-subdivision, a major subdivision of a Survey District. ... A BLOCK is usually 1/16 of a Survey District and is square, with sides 250 chains long. Block numbers are indicated by roman numerals 3. in land-subdivision, a term relating to a defined area of Maori land and bearing the original Maori tribal name. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  <b>CAREY</b> The word <i>block</i> is used in New Zealand to designate a portion of land purchased at a particular time, and opened to settlement; thus the Omata block, the Bell block, the Grey block etc. <i>(1863,Late War in NZ , The</i>)

>  To enforce this<i> tapu</i> they have constructed a pa on Rawiri's section on the Bell Block ... <i>(Sep 20, 1854,Taranaki Herald</i>) [NOTES: 1854 Sep 20 Taranaki Herald in 1856 Fitton NZ 153]

>  ... some large capitalists arrived here for the purpose of making extensive speculative purchases, even outside the original Canterbury block ... <i>(Jul 1, 1857,Lyttelton Times,2</i>)

>  (Wicksteed to Wakefield) The Company's claim to a `block' of 60,000 acres ... <i>(Jun 30, 1844,NZ Company Reports,17/113</i>)

>  ... a desire on the part of the Canterbury settlers to extend their block; ... <i>(Aug 16, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  [sc settlement] Some ...[sic] who wished to stop, intend proceeding to Auckland as they are frightened of bush land, and the block containing fern, which will be next opened, is hardly likely to be in the market for 6 months to come. <i>(Sep 24, 1853,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/131,1960 ed.</i>)

>  Thus a great opportunity for investment will be opened in a few months when the Hua Block and the Waiwakaiho Block will be proclaimed as open for selection ... <i>(Mar 13, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/145,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W.Richmond)]

>  Owing to the great delay in the surveys we have had no opportunity of making any purchase on the new blocks. <i>(May 29, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/146,1960 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (See [dead man](../D#dead-man))



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> as a unit of rural land subdivision, comprising sections.

>  ... that desirable BLOCK OF LAND, Number 114 on the Surveyors' Map. <i>(Sep 27, 1851,Lyttelton Times,4</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `I've a section m'self in this Block, and I'm tellin' you plain that I'll knock any man down who as much as offers to put match to twig before the right time [for burning felled bush]. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,25</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The holdings were surveyed into approximately 400 acre blocks to be allocated by ballot. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,11</i>)

>  About [pound]1.3/- per acre for fencing if done in blocks of not less than 640 acres. <i>(Aug 2, 1875,Chudleigh Diary,237,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> Roads at right angles to each other cut up the cultivated land into `blocks', and ... there are many landowners in Canterbury who farm several of these huge divisions. <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,65</i>)

>  On the line of railway ... blocks of Government land were laid off into sections, varying in area from one quarter to five acres. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,206</i>)

>  He has had a rough time of it surveying a block on the ranges up the Wai-o-hine in the Wairarapa ...[sic] <i>(Mar 22, 1894,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/597,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  2. in land-subdivision, a major subdivision of a Survey District. ... A BLOCK is usually 1/16 of a Survey District and is square, with sides 250 chains long. Block numbers are indicated by roman numerals <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  A stopbank protecting twenty farms in a block west of Kerepehi broke this afternoon and a number of settlers were forced to leave. <i>(Jun 29, 1960,Dominion,14</i>)

>  <b>BROWN</b> Those [sections surveyed in the township of Hampden] sold were bought by residents of Nelson as a spec., and several country blocks had absentee owners. <i>(1976,Difficult Country,47</i>)

>  <b>BROWN</b> Those [sections surveyed in the township of Hampden] sold were bought by residents of Nelson as a spec., and several country blocks had absentee owners. <i>(1976,Difficult Country,47</i>)

>  <i>Block</i> (bl&obreve.k),<i> n. [Add.] 13.</i> A rectangular lot of land; specifically, one of the large lots which Australian public land, when opened to settlers, is divided by the government surveyors. <i>Back blocks</i>, Australian pastoral country remote from the seacoast or from a river. "In the <i>back blocks</i> of Queensland, far removed from towns and civilization." <i>Melburnian. - Back blocker</i>, a bushman from the back blocks. <i> [Colloq.] - Mallee block</i>, an area of land marked out by government in the Mallee district. See MALLEE. <i>- The Block,</i> fashionable promenade in Melbourne and in Sydney. - <i>Do the Block</i>, to promenade in<i> the Block</i>. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2018</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> 1. A large area of rural land. 2. An area over which a hunter is licensed to kill animals. 3. (informal) The head: `I'll knock your block off',



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a session of group intercourse

>  Dick was alleged to have said he was at the party and was aware `a block was going on' but he repeatedly denied having intercourse with either of the females. ... Spence told the court he heard a discussion about one or two girls `going on the block'. <i>(Oct 9, 1973,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> <i>block</i> group rape or group sodomy <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,95</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  see jugged; block, known by the boys as a big <i>(Sep 6, 1977,NZ Truth,11/2</i>)

>  He said he knew her as `the block,' and he had sex with her. `She looked 17 or 18. I thought she was `easy,' he added. <i>(Apr 2, 1974,NZ Truth,18/1</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> an area of land, often part of a sub-division of 2 above, being developed from bush or scrub to farmland (development block) [define separately?], or being made available (`settled') into farms

>  [title] [sc for settlement] `It'll be jest an ordinary sod whare.' `But you made it, dearest.' `And you won't have no folk anywheres near yer.' ... `Yer don't know the life of a woman out-back. ...' He ... went away ... to the home that he had built her. It stood on a new-leased Government block, ... <i>(1930,NZ Short Stories,164</i>) [NOTES: G.B.Lancaster `In the Down-Country']

>  <i>Block</i>: An area of land, e.g.: Development block - an area being developed from bush or scrub to farmland. Settlement block - an area made (settled) into farms. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,5</i>)

>  [development block] On arrival at the Maori Affairs block shed the two women who'd been in the car took me aside. <i>(Mar 1988,More Magazine,30</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Besides the buildings there have to be many miles of fencing dividing the country into `blocks' for the various classes of sheep and seasons of the year. <i>(1951,High Country,11</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> an area allocated for prospecting for minerals

>  The blocks of land [at Thames] in which prospecting was allowed by the Maoris were limited; ... <i>(1948,Our Own Country,23</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> an area assigned to a rabbiter to trap over.

>  <i>Take up a block</i>.- To kill <i>rabbits,</i> to be employed as a <i>rabbiter. </i>(C.) He may be a permanent or a temporary hand, or may <i>take a block</i> for the skins. He may use dog, gun, and spade, poison, or traps. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> also of State Forest, allocated by an authority, over which a rabbit- or opossum-trapper is licensed to operate, or over which an official culler or a private sportsman is permitted to shoot.

>  <b>MILLER</b> ... they once saw a couple of poachers working their rabbiting block across the gorge. <i>Ibid.</i> 129 Rabbiters guard their rights with a jealous eye, and indeed a man moving over a block with a gun can upset all the work on a poison line. <i>Ibid.</i> One professional poacher I heard of used to watch a rabbiter `feed' his block - <i>(1946,There Was Gold,82</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Jim ... sent me down to a possum block in the Ruahines for the winter. <i>Ibid.</i> 91 ... before buying myself a bit of [culler's] gear and thumbing a ride towards my deer block at Lake Waikaremoana. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,31</i>)

>  <b>HELMER</b> It reproduces the atmosphere of a deer block in the Urerewa very nicely. <i>Ibid.</i> 24 deer control block `I thought I'd like to get into the forest for a couple of weeks and, take some movies of your deer shooters ...' Movies, eh? ... Civilians aren't allowed in most of the deer-control block, you know.' <i>(1964,Stag Party,6</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> George Hopp called for Abungus the next morning to introduce him to the dogs and show him round the block. He would be one of four rabbiters. <i>(1965,Stand in the Rain,145</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> [for hunting] He'd spent the last five years as a professional hunter. Now another season lay ahead ...This would be his second in a row here. So he now knew the block reasonably well. <i>(1980,Stag,20</i>)

>  <i>block</i> ... 11. N.Z.<i> an</i> area of bush reserved by licence for a trapper or hunter. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,114</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 74; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 52]]

>  <b>MANTELL</b> A cast antler caught in a bush and he had to barge right into it. Halfway through shooting out a block so his [88] mate patched him up and he carried on. <i>(1978,Murder in Fancydress,87</i>)

>  <b>KNOX</b> But where, oh where are all the game/I think the block is sort of lean/ The Deer and Chamois rather mean/ <i>(1945,Thousand Mountains Shining,172</i>) [NOTES: 1945 Dingle Two Against the Alps in Knox Thousand Mountains Shining 172 (deercullers')]



 
<b>6</b> <i>n.</i>

>  1. in forest management, a major subdivision of a forest, generally bounded by natural features and bearing a local name. In some of the first established exotic forests, BLOCK was used in place of <i>Compartment</i>. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)



 
<b>6</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NAIRN & STEPHEN</b> [timber block (early)] Our day's travelling was over some high undulating land with flax tutu and high grass and several blocks of bush land with very good timber. <i>(Jan 1852,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Jan 18 Nairn and Stephen Journal in 1947 Beattie The Pioneers Explore Otago 27]

>  <b>NAIRN & STEPHEN</b> [timber block (early)] Our day's travelling was over some high undulating land with flax tutu and high grass and several blocks of bush land with very good timber. <i>(Jan 18, 1852,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Jan 18 Nairn and Stephen Journal in 1947 Beattie The Pioneers Explore Otago 27]



 
<b>6</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BELL</b> [sc. of bush] ... on Robinson's section the last remaining block of standing [43] bush had already been felled earlier in the season. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,42</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Each year after a new block of fallen bush had been burned off, we would have a wonderful harvest of cape gooseberries ... <i>Ibid.</i> 59 A bush gang must always work as a team and work a block accordingly, ... A bushman has two meals during the day, one at 10 a.m. and the next at 2 p.m. They are always referred to as `smoko'. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,45</i>)

### block beef
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a part of a beef carcase used for steaks and roasts.

>  <b>BARTON</b> There are two main end-uses for beef. One is for table purposes where the beef is eaten as steaks and roasts - this beef is known as 'table beef' or 'block beef.' <i>(1971,Sheepfarming Annual 1971,141</i>)

### block committee
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>KING</b> ... Native Land Court sittings were held there in the late 1890s and early 1900s.* [fn] These sittings were not of the court proper, but of `block committees' of chiefs responsible for parcelling out shares in land allocated to particular hapu in earlier court sittings presided over by a judge. The block committees' decisions were subsequently ratified by court. <i>(1983,Whina,36</i>)

### block dray
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large heavy cart without springs.

>  <b>ADDENBROOKE</b> When I got to the end of the road, men with block drays, shovels and wheelbarrows were forming the road to the Wanganui River. <i>(1992,Home from the Hill,51</i>)

>  <b>LEA</b> There were shepherd hacks (up to quarter-draught), half and three-quarter draught for 'spring-cart' work and full draught for the big 'block drays' and other heavy work. <i>(1992,Draught Horse Power,12</i>)

>  <b>EWERS</b> Around 1928 there was a glut in the hops and quotas were cut. Dad bought 2 heavy draught horses and a block dray. <i>(1993,Lifetime on the Land, A,13</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> Most of the family went to Kekerengu by service car but Dad and I went in a block dray with one ancient horse. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,29</i>)

>  <b>WASWO</b> The Block Dray, a much heavier version of a two-wheeled cart had no springs, though like most spring carts it was a tip cart. <i>(1996,Farming Progress in NZ 1814-1995,79</i>)

### block graze
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to run a herd or flock on land in blocks or part-paddocks using back fencing.

>  If you block graze your yearling replacements and beef, they may need dividing into good and poor mobs, also. <i>(Jun 1970,NZ Dairy Exporter,7</i>)

>  His eventual aim is to subdivide the whole property into 4-chain-wide strips with a view to block-grazing the lot. <i>(Oct 10, 1974,NZ Farmer,44</i>)

>  Chris block grazes during the winter. <i>(Nov 1975,NZ Dairy Exporter,5</i>)

>  The second winter after sowing, we block-graze with cows, and also give them hay. <i>(Mar 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter,26</i>)

>  Topics include .. Taranaki research into the effects on pasture and cows when stock are block grazed or paddock grazed through winter ... <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,37</i>)

>  Harrowing is definitely indicated when cows have been slowly block grazed across the paddock. [block-graze] <i>(Jun 1, 2001,Fencepost.com,Expert Farming</i>)

### block grazing
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) stocking of pasture using front and back fencing and portable troughs.

>  If you are highly stocked or low on winter feed reserves, then block grazing, or wintering between the wires using a front and back fence, is a good way to get through until spring growth. <i>(Jun 1970,NZ Dairy Exporter,7</i>)

>  He says he has 'tried a whole lot' of wintering systems over the years but has decided that block grazing with a back fence is the best for his farm. <i>(Nov 1975,NZ Dairy Exporter,11</i>)

>  Mr Parks runs an all-grass system with winter ewe management a mixture of strip and block grazing. <i>(Jun 15, 1994,NZ Farmer,31</i>)

>  <b>WHITE & HODGSON</b> Block grazing differs from strip grazing to the extent that the herd/flock is offered pasture on a daily basis with both a front and back temporary electric fence, and daily blocks are rectangular in shape. <i>(1999,NZ Pasture and Crop Science,156</i>)

>  ..the cows are back fenced with another electric fence so that they cannot go back and cause further hoof damage on the pasture already eaten. Often, doing this will require the provision and connection of a portable water trough which will also be required to be shifted daily. This system has become known as block grazing. <i>(Jun 1, 2001,Fencepost.com,Expert Farming</i>)

### block lick
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a stock salt-lick brick.

>  Block or brick licks of various sizes can easily be made where phosphates are absent from the mixture. <i>(Apr 20, 1932,NZ Journal of Agriculture,262</i>)

### block manager
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person responsible for management of a large area of a station or farm.

>  <b>BACON</b> `But surely it'd take some time before this block was ready for settlement?' I asked. `Yeah, mate, but there's blokes got to come in here to get it ready. They [Lands and Survey Department] usually send in a block manager with half a dozen other jokers, shepherds, drivers and so on, to clear it up and get the place a bit straight for the permanent settlers. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,87</i>)

>  Farm Staff: Block manager, Puketotara Station. Be one of three Block Managers. Intensive and progressive. .. Block Manager: There is currently a vacancy for an experienced stockman to fill the above position at Huiarau Station. <i>(Feb 23, 2002,Dominion,43</i>)

>  Block manager: Position 1 is for a block manager. This particular job will entail being responsible for a block within Huiarau. <i>(Apr 13, 2002,Dominion,33</i>)

>  Huiarau Station: Block manager required. <i>(May 25, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,25</i>)

>  Erewhon Station. Finishing Block Manager: Reporting to the Farm Manager, a Block Manager is required to take responsibility for finishing 25,000 lambs, 2,500 cattle and supporting stock. <i>(Oct 19, 2002,Dominion Post,C19</i>)

### block out
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to deliver stock on to grazing areas.

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> We were forced to take in later mobs by the same route. However we struck no further snowstorms and were able to get them all blocked out. <i>(1962,Life's Roundabout, A,63</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>HEINZ</b> Under many of the mining camps there were tunnels from which the miners were `blocking out' the rich wash dirt clear to bedrock ... [ibid 30] ... or where a party were `blocking out' (that is tunnelling and following a gold lead on the bottom under a terrace). <i>(1952,Prospecting for Gold,11</i>)

>  <b>McCLENAGHAN</b> `Your blood's worth bottling, boy,' said Sam appreciatively. `I know a sort of a job if you fellas will try anything.' <i>(1976,Travelling Man,18</i>)

### block settler
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pioneering farmer allotted blocks of bush for development.

>  Meeting of Block Settlers: The Mangamingi block settlers further contended that their land was already loaded to the extent of about 17s an acre for roading purposes. <i>(Apr 22, 1896,Hawera Star,4</i>)

>  It is pleasing to notice that the Mangamingi and Ngaire block settlers are alive to their interests in the matter of roads. <i>(Apr 28, 1896,Hawera Star,2</i>)

### block wintering
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the grazing of stock on differentiated areas over winter.

>  Block wintering rotations are starting to be increased by shifting dairy herds to new blocks more than once a day. <i>(Jun 11, 1981,NZ Farmer,65</i>)

>  Preparation for winter has been good with most dairy farms now on either 40 day rotation or have 20% of farm closed since early April to commence block wintering. <i>(May 13, 1982,NZ Farmer,68</i>)

### block-bash
 
 <i>v.</i> (Sport) [cricket. Used also in Australia and UK.]

>  Aberhart said he always felt McMillan was at his best when he played cricket strokes down the ground and was not looking to "block-bash". <i>(Dec 17, 2005,Dominion Post,D2</i>)

>  But what matters most is..his acceptance-willingness to ease balls through the field rather than to block-bash. <i>(Dec 2, 2005,Dominion Post,Cricket  p3</i>)

### block-grazed
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of pasture and stock] restricted in feed allocation.

>  The block grazed herd were given 1/5th of the paddock each day, with a constant daily ration of silage, and were back fenced. <i>(Jun 1, 2001,Fencepost.com,Expert Farming</i>)

 <i>n.</i> ?

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hobby farmer or a townsperson living in the country.

 <i>n.</i> wicket

>  "The whole blockhead [wicket] is just like a billiard table," Blain says. <i>(Jan 18, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)

### blockhouse
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>VOGEL</b> ...highways have been opened into the interior ... which but a short time ago was accessible only by the roughest horse-tracks. <i>(1875,Official Handbook of NZ,31</i>)

### blockiness
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [stock conformation] soundness and stockiness.

>  There are many points in the conformation of sheep that denote sound constitution and generally they may be summed up in one word 'blockiness.' <i>(May 1916,NZ Farmer,618</i>)

### blocking
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) 'riding round' sheep paddocks or blocks to search for cast ewes.

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> The blocking on Puketiti was carried out by station hands as well as shepherds. <i>(1981,Sunrise on Hikurangi,112</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> While these busy seasonal activities were in progress Mike and I often went out blocking as it was known then. Our job was to ride the ewe blocks or paddocks checking to see if any ewes were cast. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,28</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRADY</b> In fact, it is not at all unlikely that the Peranos accelerated the closure of [opposition] Baldick whaling by engaging in the tactic known as `blocking'. This is achieved by `blocking' off whales from the opposition boats by using boats from your own team as a shield. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,43</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the inspection of stock on a farm block n1. 4 [f. block n??].

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> But early in July Mr Knox set Bill the job of blocking - daily riding round the ewes. <i>(1981,Sunrise,83</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> gang rape

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>blocking</i> group rape; aka `gang rape' or `gangbang'; from the English word `block', to have intercourse with a woman. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,16</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> `Possible. What did that stupid goon say in that Auckland trial? Blocking was ceremonial. There was no way the pigs were going to stop it.' ... The block. Multiple rape. Gang rape. <i>(1980,Murder and Chips,103</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sheepdogs] preventing a break in a mob of sheep.

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> 'Blocking' by a dog is to stop breaks in sheep. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,3</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Huntaways or barking dogs work with noise: chasing, blocking and pushing to herd sheep into position. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,135</i>)

### blocking out
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BLYTH</b> <i>Blocking out:</i> Describes the process of exposing, on at least three sides, any ore vein, also describes the working of auriferous alluvial deposits in square blocks. <i>(1935,Gold Mining Year Book,11</i>)

### blocklayer
 
 <i>n.</i> [The common NZ term - see Yellow Pages]

### blocky
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of fleecewool] thick, with a square tipped staple.

>  Blocky: Square tipped staple. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,52</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The wool should be lustrous, soft to touch, free from kemp with a blocky tip ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,44</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> It is important that the sire should have a blocky tip to the wool as the crossbred is apt to develop a wasty tip in the fleece. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,67</i>)

>  A hill country farmer wants tight blocky wool to keep out the rain and vegetable matter. <i>(1995,NZ Sheep Farmer,Autumn 27</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farmer on a lifestyle block.

>  These are not just the 10-acre blockies or lifestyle farmers who don't understand or don't care about the economics of calf-rearing. <i>(Apr 6, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,19</i>)

>  hobby farmer: a blocky. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person who does a lambing beat.

>  blocky - one who does a lambing beat. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a thick compact-woolled sheep.

>  blocky - a sheep with thick, dense wool. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep and cattle] square and thickset.

>  In choosing a fat lamb sire no matter the breed, the object should be a sheep of 'blocky' shape... <i>(Mar 1916,NZ Farmer,323</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> The Ryeland is gaining in popularity as a ram for getting fat lambs. It produces a nice blocky lamb, something like the Southdown cross. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,68</i>)

>  The owner's aim has been to breed blocky, low-set, fine-woolled Downs ... <i>(Jan 1, 1929,NZ Farmer,63</i>)

>  Whereas dairy stock are generally angular, clean cut and wedge-shaped, beef cattle are more blocky, well-covered and rectangular in shape. <i>(Oct 13, 1934,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  A short cannon [bone] is associated with a desirable blocky type of carcase. <i>(Dec 6, 1951,NZ Farmer,13</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> Romney rams from Wairongomai and then from Penrose, producing low-set 'blocky' sheep, were used for many years. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,17</i>)

>  <b>BARTON</b> ..in traditional beef animals selection has tended to produce shorter bones. The muscles attached to these bones are also short but wide, giving a 'blocky' look. <i>(1971,Sheepfarming Annual 1971,194</i>)

### blokal
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Hopoate, an allegedly god-fearing Mormon, will now and forever be sentenced to an annus horribilis after these terrible crimes against blokal dignity. <i>(Mar 31, 2001,Evening Post,2</i>)

### blokart
 
 <i>n.</i> a wind-driven three-wheeled land yacht, created and designed in New Zealand by Paul Beckett.

>  Speed freaks in blokarts prefer low tide, an offshore wind and a beach empty of people. These three-wheeled land yachts pack down to a lightweight, suitcase-sized parcel and can be carried to the beach and assembled without tools. <i>(Jul 2004,AA Directions,29</i>)

>  Weighing in at at only 27kg the blokart is portable enough to fit in the boot/trunk of most cars. It can be assembled from its unique carry bag in a few minutes with no tools required. <i>(2004,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.blokart.com./]

>  You may have noticed we have dropped the words blokarting and blokarter from our lingo coming out of HQ. Unfortunately new IP (Intellectual Property) laws mean we now have to use generic terms like blokart sailing, blokart sailor etc. <i>(Apr 2004,blokart Newsletter,1</i>) [NOTES: www.blokart.com]

>  Created and designed by Mount Maunganui's Paul Beckett, the blokart's back en masse in the Bay of Plenty for the debut New Zealand racing championships. <i>(Nov 2005,North & South,33</i>)

>  Over six thousand karts have been sold in twenty-eight countries, making blokart the world's fastest growing land-based sailing sport. <i>(Jun 2007,Tearaway,14</i>)

>  During his visit [to the Auckland Boat Show] he spotted a "Blocart", a small land yacht of about 2.5 metres long, and suitable for the smaller younger person to get an introduction to the sport. <i>(Nov 2003,Blind Sailing,7</i>)

>  [Hdg] lack-bluster HB weather leaves blokarts becalmed... "Basically blokarting is like windsurfing on wheels - a three-wheeled cart with a sail about 4.5m high," Mr Lansdown said... "Blokarts are spectacular when you see them up and running." <i>(Mar 19, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,11</i>)

>  Designed by New Zealander Paul Beckett seven years ago, Blokarts are the Formula One of land yachts. <i>(Dec 2006,Air New Zealand inflight magazine,73</i>)

>  All schools entering for the NZ Youth blokart champs go in the draw to win a blokart. <i>(Jul 2007,Tearaway,20</i>)

### blokarter
 
 <i>n.</i> (Sport, trademark) a blokart land yacht driver.

>  You may have noticed that we have dropped the words blokarting and blokarter from our lingo coming out of HQ. Unfortunately, new IP (Intellectual Property) laws mean we now have to use generic terms like blokart sailing, blokart sailor etc. <i>(Apr 2004,blokart Newsletter,1</i>) [NOTES: www.blokart.com]

>  Little physical strength is required (it's all skill) and some of New Zealand champion Blokarters are paraplegic. <i>(Dec 2006,Air New Zealand inflight magazine,73</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (Sport, trademark) land yachting in a blokart.

>  .. it's worth noting that Blokarting is one of the few sports in which men and women can compete on an equal basis .. "We're the capital of the Blokarting world!" <i>(Dec 2006,Air New Zealand inflight magazine,73</i>)

### blood alley
 
 <i>n.</i> a white marble streaked with red

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates, aggies, aggotties, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cat's eyes, chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, molly-bars, mulley-bars [mullibars: large marbles], peelies, peewees, piss-wees, pretties, Scotties, Scottish alleys, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers, white alleys, and in more recent years ball-bearings, chippies, plastics, and woodies. <i>Ibid.</i> 176 [Waitara, Taranaki, 1899] A `taw' was usually an `alley' or `blood alley' made of agate. We used to take them home and burn them in a fire to convert them into `blood alleys'. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

>  <b>KEITH & MAIN</b> Marbles were the currency of childhood and there were an astonishing number of names for them, some referring to the material or colour, others to value or function in the game. There were agates, aggies, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cats-eye, [sic] chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, mollies, peelies, peewees, pisswees, pretties, Scotties, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers and whities. The march of technology added ball bearings - ballies or steelies - which were unbeatable when it came to smashing an opponent's miserable glassie. World War II[roman] brought `clayeys' often imperfectly spherical they were pathetic terracotta substitutes for real marbles. <i>(1984,NZ Yesterdays,288</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>blood alley</i>. A white, or whitish, marble streaked with red: Aus. and NZ children's: latye C.19-20. Adopted ex. common English dialect. <i>(1984,Dictionary of Slang,97</i>)

### blood and bone
 
 <i>n.</i> a soil-enriching fertiliser, dried blood mixed with bonemeal [ca1920]

>  The Western Packing and Canning Co., Ltd., Manures. BB Blood and Bone ...L5 per ton. <i>(Nov 25, 1899,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  Waitara Freezing Works and Coal Storage Company has blood and bone manure for sale at L5 10s per ton. <i>(Sep 2, 1901,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  In addition to this, there is the loss of the refuse blood and bone manures, which must some day be returned to the soil if the fertility of the pastures is to be maintained. <i>(Aug 22, 1887,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  Waitara Freezing Works. Manures. Blood and Bone ...L5 15/- per ton. <i>(Sep 21, 1906,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

### blood and treasure
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>GREY in CARLETON</b> His Majesty's Government may also rest assured that these individuals cannot be put in possession of these tracts of land without a large expenditure of British blood and money... The confidential despatch to Mr Gladstone, known in the colony by the name of "Blood and Treasure," is of historical interest. <i>(1877,Life of Henry Williams,II Appendix D-1</i>)

### blood and treasure despatch
 
 <i>phr.</i> (also blood and money despatch) Grey's despatch accusing missionaries (and the Williams family in particular) of obtaining excessive Maori land. [Frances Porter in a p.c. 4 November 1987 drew HWO's attention to a use of the collocation `blood and treasure' by Jane Maria Atkinson: checks in biblical concordances and main dictionaries of quotations did not turn up any evidence of a general use or source outside this NZ one]

>  I have such a dread of that horrid long-headed Louis Napoleon that I rather expect after all the the misery is gone thro', all the `blood & treasure' spent, it will turn out that France alone is the gainer ... <i>(May 4, 1866,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/213</i>) [NOTES: (Jane Maria Atkinson) [reference Austro-Prussian War, and manoeuvrings of France under Louis Napoleon]]

>  There were several despatches attacking these [northern landholding] missionaries], but the `celebrated one' [mentioned by W. Williams, Journal 1847 August 19] was that of Grey to Gladstone, 25 June 1846, which came to be known as the `Blood and Money' or `Blood and Treasure' despatch. ... (Carleton ... Vol. 2, Appendix D.) <i>(1974,Turanga Journals,441fn</i>) [NOTES: 1974 Frances Porter in Turanga Journals]

>  As the editor [of the New Zealander] says if he [Governor Grey] has compounded many such documents as the blood & Treasure despatch, he is a ruined man ... <i>(May 5, 1848,Turanga Journals,487</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Apr 5 William Williams in Turanga Journals (1974) 487]

### blood blister
 
 <i>n.</i> small blister containing blood, often caused by a pinch or a smart blow; [ca1920 (R. Mason) -->ca1935 at least (Havelock)] (See [stone bruise](../S#stone-bruise))

### blood nose

>  <b>IRELAND</b> My brother and I told him to bugger off smartly and warned him he was going the right way to earn himself a blood-nose. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,125</i>)

### blood plum
 
 <i>n.</i> a variety of plum with a deep-red flesh (e.g. satsuma). ca1940 Havelock: also <i>blood-red plum</i> [ca1955 Auckland]

### blood scours
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>blood scours n</i> pl but sing. or pl in constr, <i>Austral & New Zeal</i> : a bloody diarrhea of calves prob. due to intestinal infection following malnutrition and lowered resistance <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,237</i>)

### blood-poison
 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1935 Havelock, and fairly frequently elsewhere. e.g. You'll get blood-poison off rusty nails.]

### bloody
 
 <i>adj.</i> a colloquial intensifier, used, so EDD, by the `lower classes' `indifferently as a term of depreciation or appreciation'; frequent in NZ speech as it is elsewhere, but not so much as to be regarded as a New Zealandism (contrast <i>great Australian adjective</i>: no doubt used off the New Zealand coast from earliest English rediscovery, but not much recorded in print before WW1 (See [cf. also bloody oath; my oath](../A#also-bloody-oath;-my-oath))

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> Miss Bloody Haigat. <i>(1944,Troop Target,26</i>)

>  [Third Mate - whaler] ... amongst other things said that all who said so were bloody liars. <i>(1836,Journal Barque 'Australian'</i>)

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> When the heathen Maories see their men made drunk, their women debauched, and their chiefs called `bloody Maories', no wonder our religion shall appear to them but little better than the empty profession of a barren creed. <i>(1859,New Zealand and Its Colonisation,37</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> I was telling them at the school [for painting] I go to ... about your man and his [mate][sic] saying `bloody' 72 times in 10 minutes; only I did not mention names. <i>(1867,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1867 Samuel Butler letter to Haast in 1919 Jones Jones Samuel Butler, A Memoir 1/128]



 
 <i>adv.</i> as an intensifier, very

>  You'll find out, too, that your New Zealand buddy is no Puritan when it comes to swearing. You hear the word `bloody' - bloody this and bloody that - all over the place, except in mixed company. <i>(1944,US War Dept. Short Guide to NZ,34</i>)

>  <b>ASHCROFT</b> [Against the over use of bugger, bloody and hell] ... One of [these], the word b-y, is simply coarse and nasty, but it may have had a perfectly harmless, even pious, origin. ... Its absurd uses now ... `That's a high building, Tom.' ... `Not so bloody high.' <i>(1900,Public Morals,13</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>DAVIN</b> ... no bloody fear <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,196</i>)

>  <b>6/114</b> Like bloody hell it will! <i>(1948,Landfall</i>) [NOTES: (Ballantyne)]

>  You mind your own bloody business. <i>(1948,Landfall,13/23</i>) [NOTES: (Gaskell)]

>  <b>WILSON</b> Hell's bloody bells. <i>(1950,Brave Company,83</i>)

>  Not bloody likely. You buggers started all this. <i>(1950,Landfall,15/213</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> `Eight pounds of bug down his [sc. the cat's] furry cakehole! Shell and bloody all. ...' <i>(1981,Fish Factory,78</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>adj.</i> immediately after an [ ] sound, perhaps to suggest the indef. art. of phrases as <i>not a bloody bit</i>

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> abso-bloody-lutely <i>(1944,Troop Target,99</i>)

>  <b>BRUNO</b> `Who goes there?' `Santa Bloody Claus.' `Pass Kiwi.' <i>(1948,Maleesh George,24</i>)

### bloody good
 
 <i>exclam.</i> in full: OH, BLOODY GOOD, WHACKO, PUP!

>  oh, bloody good, whacko, Pup! Kiwi war cry after downing Japanese plane WWII; Partridge, A Dictionary of Catch Phrases. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,78</i>)

### bloody hell
 
 <i>exclam.</i> [1984 15 F E1 Pakuranga Coll. 5 BLOODY HELL [of annoyance F6M6] -->1984 14 F E133Wgton Girls C. 5 BLOODY HELL [F5] -->1984 14 F E140Wgton Girls C. 35B BLOODY HELL [of disapproval] -->1984 16-17 F E16 Pakuranga Coll. 35C BLOODY HELL [of surprise F5M3] -->1984 14 F E143Wgton Girls C. 35C BLOODY HELL -->1984 14 F E144Wgton Girls C. 5 BLOODY HELL]

### bloody oath
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  `Bloody oath, Mat what good luck!' ... `Shout you eh?' `Too right you can. Double whisky.' <i>(1984,Some Other Country,182</i>) [NOTES: 1976 Hulme `Hooks and Feelers' in 1984 Some Other Country 182]

>  <b>STEWART</b> `No, that's just good sound business I suppose?' `My bloody oath it is,' answered Owen with emphasis. <i>(1985,Gumboots and Goalposts,153</i>)

>  "My bloomin' oath, won't I though," said the boy. <i>(Jun 28, 1902,New Zealand Free Lance,18</i>)

>  And Bill sings out: "Come over, mate, and have a cup of tea." / And middling quick he turns about: My blooming oath, that's me." <i>(Aug 13, 1896,Otago Witness,41</i>)

### bloody Spaniard
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [giant Spaniard](../G#giant-spaniard),[wild Spaniard](../W#wild-spaniard))

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> The larger species [of <i>Aciphylla</i>] are familiar to the settlers as `Spear-grass', or `Bayonette-grass', `Wild' or `Bloody-Spaniard', [<i>bloody</i> =?] in allusion to their very rigid, strong, poniard-like, sharp-pointed leaves ... Hence a `Spear-grass Scrub' on the hill ranges is frequently most formidable ... to men and horses, though it forms one of the favourite hunting grounds of the Wild Pig. In the Matukituki Valley, `Bayonette-grass' forms an undergrowth in a dense scrub of prickly thorn ... [50] Familiar to the shepherds of Canterbury as `Bloody Spaniard'. <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,49</i>) [NOTES: [A.Colensoi: alpine plant]]

>  That unpleasant kind of spear-grass which grows on the ranges in Taranaki and which is popularly known by the sobriquet of Wild Irishman, seems to be in bad repute wherever it is found. Dr. Von Haast says that in the South Island it is called Bloody Spaniard. <i>(Jul 25, 1885,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  When first walking along steep mountain sides, catching hold of friendly tussocks for support, sometimes by mistake you catch a young Spaniard, and then you know why our "rude forefathers," the pioneers, often called it the "bloody Spaniard"; it really and truly was; I have proved it. <i>(Dec 21, 1926,Evening Post,17</i>)

### bloody well
 
 <i>phr.</i> really

>  <b>BAUME</b> ... the kind [of woman] that shouldn't bloody well exist <i>(1993,Half Caste,14</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Dan Freel's conversation [as an Irish settler] was plentifully interlarded with oaths and obscurities that are - or would have been in the [18]80's - quite unprintable. At first it seemed an arresting, if not an interesting idiosyncracy, but one sickened of it. ... The sanguinary adjective too that sounds so daring but really has no significance whatever, he used between every second word, even between syllables as `I bloody well told the bloody mongrel that he was too ego-bloody-istical.' After the first few sentences this gave no gain in force and much loss in time. I noticed that the Irishmen, though they obviously bowed to Dan's smallest word, did not give him the sincerest flattery of imitation in this respect. <i>(1944,Moonshine,87</i>)

>  Yeh. Bloodywell need them. <i>(1950,Landfall,15/213</i>)

### Bloody-slow Cup
 
 <i>n.</i> a play on Bledisloe Cup

>  Locals unmoved by last year's stop-start affair - dubbed the "Bloody-slow Cup" - have little interest in paying A$99.75.. to watch it all again. <i>(Jul 19, 1998,Dominion,1</i>)

### bloom
 
 <i>n.</i> an attractive appearance of freshly-shorn wool which disappears with storage.

>  <i>Bloom:</i> Attractive appearance of freshly-shorn wool which disappears with storage. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,52</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a grey or grey-yellow tint of wool, suggestive of excellent health in an animal.

>  His sheep were blocky and low set and were well brought out. He had his sheep in great bloom. <i>(Dec 1, 1928,NZ Farmer,1565</i>)

>  The effect of too dark a bloom detracted from their appearance to a certain extent. <i>(Nov 8, 1951,NZ Farmer,27</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> bloom: Grey to yellow-grey tint produced by the presence of the abundant wool grease typical of a very healthy sheep. Though this practice is now disallowed, such colour was sometimes sprayed on when preparing sheep for the show-ring. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,8</i>)

### bloom dip
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dipping fluid that gives a golden gloss to wool.

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> Following Tapita's advice, he hung the blankets on a fence, beat them with a stick, washed them out in bloom dip, and let them hang out to dry. <i>(1981,Sunrise on Hikurangi,59</i>)

### bloom dipping
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the pre-sale washing of stud sheep in a greasy substance that gives a sparkly, golden glow to the fleece.

>  The Department is continuing publicity against the practice of bloom dipping which the Wool Board now seeks to have banned completely. <i>(Nov 6, 1963,Straight Furrow,38</i>)

>  <b>BARTON</b> Bloom dipping of sheep is outlawed today, as is the overtrimming of the fleece. <i>(1971,Sheepfarming Annual 1971,202</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> Next came the bloom dipping. This involved plunging the sheep in a dipping solution thick with grease and an unscourable gold dye. <i>(1994,Lab Coats to Gumboots,158</i>)

### blooming
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [stud stock for exhibition or sales] polishing of wool using a special dye or blooming powder.

>  <b>BARDSLEY</b> We had the Okirae Romney Stud to look after as well and that included clipping, dagging with hand-shears, blooming and foot-trimming heavy rams for sales. <i>(2000,Land Girls, The,149</i>)

### blouse
 
 <i>n.</i> short for (big) girl's blouse (Brit. according to ODE) [>198? [ref. overtyped] Listener 30 [McEwan, All Black of 1961 writes of his schooldays ?c1950s] Boys [at secondary school] who would not take part in the [rugby union] ritual were ridiculed for being a `softie', a `poofta', a `blouse' or, worst of all, a `girl'.]

### blow
 
 <i>n.</i> an isolated outcrop of mineral (esp. quartz). [Check whether date is 1897 or 1898]

>  <b>BARRY</b> I came to the conclusion that the lode was not a permanent one, but only what is called a `blow' of quartz. <i>(1879,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,224</i>)

>  The molybdenite deposits ... are described ... as roughly circular or oval-shaped outcrops of [24] quartz, or `blows' carry wolfram and native bismuth. The `blows' when followed down develop into irregular pipe-shaped masses ... <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/23</i>)

>  So far as one could judge from a surface examination, the outcrops appear to be isolated blocks of quartz or `blows' that follow the foliation planes of the schist. <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.5 (NS) 65</i>)

>  In places [scheelite] occurs as blows in the quartz in sufficient quantity to be sorted by hand-picking. <i>(1939,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.39 (NS) 104</i>) [NOTES: (Williamson, Naseby)]

>  An immense `blow' of quartz 150ft by 100ft on the surface, and standing 30ft above the surrounding ground was located in 1872 on the Rainy Creek property. <i>(1948,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.42 (NS) 54</i>) [NOTES: (Gage, Reefton)]

>  <b>NOLAN</b> <i>Blow</i>: a large outcropping reef, usually of barren quartz. <i>(1983,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,111</i>)

>  This great "blow" of quartz, as the miners term it, strikes to the north-west, and therefore crosses the auriferous belt nearly at right-angles. <i>(1897,AJHR,C9/9</i>)

>  The great "blow" or "buck-reef" of the Tokatea is found in the slates at the western end of the lower or No. 7 adit. <i>(1898,AJHR,C-9/51</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to open with explosives (See [peter](../P#peter),[tank](../T#tank))



 
 <i>v.</i> to clean a mineral (esp. gold) by blowing air over it to remove the particles of the material in which it was found; to dry-blow [mining] (See [cf. dry-blowing](../D#dry-blowing))

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> [Bank clerk] `By the time I had cleaned, or `blown' the gold, it was too late to think of returning. <i>Ibid.</i> 220 The gold is placed on a flat pear-shaped metal dish, with a perpendicular rim or edge except at the point of the pear, where there is no rim. This is moved in such a manner that the gold is tossed up, and all the finer particles and dirt are worked forward towards the opening, out by which the operator blows the sand. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,217</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> of a fly, to deposit eggs on (food, blankets, clothing, etc.), (See [also fly-blown](../A#also-fly-blown))

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Red blankets are usual; white show fly-blows. The blue-bottle flies blow among blankets that are left lying untidily about ... Fly-blown blankets are all very well, provided they have been quite dry ever since they were blown; <i>(Mar 24, 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iv/52</i>)

>  [The blankets] would then not be `blown' [by flies]. <i>(1877,TrNZI,x/308</i>) [NOTES: (Gillies)]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> In the summer months, with the countless number of `blue-bottles' (blowflies) the `blows' would hatch out in a few hours, and by the end of the day a [72] carcass would be a seething mass of maggots. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,71</i>)

>  If you `blew in' at the races,/ Then you `whip the flaming cat.' <i>(Mar 31, 1906,NZ Truth,41/6/4</i>) [NOTES: (Bloke's Patter: Smart slang)]



 
 <i>n.</i> to have a sniff of glue

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>blow</i> phr.<i> have a blow</i>, streetkid for sniffing glue. <i>[Alison Gray] </i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a rock-blasting explosion.

>  <b>McCARTHY</b> They were all good miners ... I was bogging the stone, Herbert was getting ready to bore the face again while Jimmy and Jack were putting up the set, that is, were timbering the walls and roof, which was necessary to prevent the walls and roof collapsing when the next `blow' took place. <i>(1973,Listen...!,52</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sheep when driven] to pant.

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> You will then have a hard slogging drive ahead. On arriving at your destination the sheep will be 'blowing', the dogs will drive into the nearest waterhole or trough and you will also have had it and be in need of refreshment. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,87</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to smoke marijuana

>  `What do you reckon we blow a joint.' Bobby had an ounce of green for the week-end ... <i>(1973,Islands,5/263</i>) [NOTES: Gifkins (Winter Riders)]



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Blow your hand</i>. Even the most experienced shearer can sometimes suffer a swelling of the tendons and muscle tissue in the cutting hand. The only remedy is rest and long soaking in cold water. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to squander: `he blew the whole inheritance at the races'.



 
 <i>v.</i> to boast; skite.

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> Found my mate ... had been on the spree ever since I left and had also been blowing about the country we had been to. <i>(1864,West Coast Prosp Party</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Barrington West Coast Prosp Party in Taylor Early Travellers 398]

>  <b>BARKER</b> I only mention this, not out of any desire to `blow' about our sheep, but because I want to account for my tender-heartedness. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,237</i>)

>  <b>THATCHER</b> He blows about some speculation, / That will soon make a man of him quite; / And he blinds you with this exclamation, / `I assure you, my boy, it's all right.' <i>(1875,NZ Songster,No. 3/69</i>) [NOTES: It's All Right]

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> [Colonialisms] To <i>`blow</i>' is, in the colonies, to brag or boast; this is graphic if not elegant; but one of the most curious expressions, based on an entirely errroneous derivation, is `an old identity', used for a person long `identified with a place' - in fact an old inhabitant, or one whose interests have long been bound up in it. This expression is unfortunately now universal even in newspapers. <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,171</i>)

>  I don't care about blowin' about it. <i>(Sep 5, 1917,NZEF Chronicles,35</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> The following New Zealand expressions derive from British dialects, ... <i>blow</i>: to boast <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),116</i>)

>  <i>blow v.</i> boast. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>NIXON</b> <i> blow</i> to boast. Seems to be replacing <i>skite. </i> <i>(Oct 21, 1983,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1983 Oct 21 Letter M.A. Nixon (Palmerston North)]

>  <i>blow ... NZ & Austral. colloq</i>. boast. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,75</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
 <i>n.</i> boasting; skite. [1953 15 M A28 Thames DHS 17 BLOW [boasting]]

>  <i>They'll</i> give that cruising Russian lot,/ Some real Colonial `blow';/ <i>(May 4, 1885,Lyttelton Times</i>) [NOTES: 1885 May 4 Lyttelton Times in 1967 Bailey and Roth Shanties 74]

>  <b>FIRTH</b> His merry laughter at our `blow.' <i>(1890,Nation Making,307</i>)

>  Blow, pure blow; much more of it and you'd bust. <i>(Mar 14, 1908,NZ Truth,143/3/4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> shearing: a stroke with the shears, a stroke of the shears (esp. long blow).

>  <i>blow</i>: (1) Any stroke the shearer takes; e.g. `a blow over the tail'. (2) `To strike a blow' means to begin work. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/21</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

>  <b>BURDON</b> The sheep they shore were not so well woolled on the belly and carried less wool on the points than they do now, so that fewer short blows were required to take off the trimmings; but the shears they used were one inch and a half shorter in the blade and, being joined by a single instead of a double bow, required a wrist of iron to drive them continuously throughout a summer's day. A piece of raw hide known as a knocker is now used to prevent the shears clashing when closed, but before this was introduced the clack and snap of steel meeting steel was a noise inseparable from any busy shearing shed. <i>(1938,High Country,84</i>)

>  <i>Blow.</i>- The stroke of the shearer in handshearing. He drives the shears forward and makes a cut. <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <i>blow n</i>. in shearing, a single cut with the shears. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> With a few deft `blows' the ringer had cleaned out the crutch. <i>(1949,High Country Days,49</i>)

>  A minimum of blows applied on the sheep should be aimed at. ... On good average-shearing crossbred sheep anything from fifty to sixty blows is satisfactory. <i>Ibid.</i> 65 The best way of working when crutching is to go at a good smart pace for half an hour and then have a five minutes' blow. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,6</i>)

>  <i>Blow</i>: A shearing stroke with the handpiece of shears. <i>(Feb 26, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>) [NOTES: (H.A.L. Laing)]

>  BLOW: A shearing stroke with a handpiece, or shears. <i>(Feb 21, 1968,Straight Furrow,20</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> On the board the noise changed. There was the high whirr of the shearing hand-pieces. Ten stands, ten bodies bent over the sheep, bring off the fleeces with deft, sweeping blows. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,3</i>)

>  A shearing stroke with a handpiece or shears. <i>(Feb 24, 1977,Dominion,11</i>)

>  <i>blow ... 6. Austral & N.Z.</i> a stroke of the shears in sheep-shearing. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,116</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 75; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 53]]

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Blow</i>. The cut made by the blade shearer between the fleece and the half inch of wool left on the sheep's skin. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94</i>)

>  You laugh as you remember the green rousie following the shearer's `blows' on a black sheep with a torch, at his command. <i>(Mar 1988,More Magazine,31</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a windstorm; a strong wind of some duration. [1940 Havelock There was a bit of a blow last night. The macrocarpa came down.]



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [stock] to bloat.

>  He has found that, unlike other clovers, subterranean, even although pure, does not blow sheep. <i>(Oct 6, 1938,NZ Farmer,26</i>)

>  It has been generally assumed that where there is comparatively little clover, cows are unlikely to 'blow' but swards dominant in ryegrass have proved dangerous. <i>(Oct 9, 1952,NZ Farmer,22</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> On occasions when we've been using the minimum amount of drench, we've had quite a few cows blow. When we do get them blowing, we generally get 50. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,86</i>)

### blow a tank
 
 <i>phr.</i> to dynamite a safe (cf. tank).

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Blow a tank.</i> To dynamite a safe: post-G.W. New Zealand c. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,67</i>)

>  [In local criminal slang] ... `to blow a tank' is to break open a safe, ... <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>BAKER</b> We had also acquired some underworld slang of our own : <i>blow a tank</i>, to break open a safe with explosives. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,52</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>blow a tank.</i> To dynamite a safe: New Zealand and by 1935, Australia: 1932, Nelson Baylis (private letter); 1941, Sidney J. Baker, New Zealand Slang; 1942 Baker, Australian Slang. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,52</i>)

>  Currently being circulated in Auckland is the tale of the craftsman who, with "jelly".. went off to "the sticks" to "blow a tank". <i>(Apr 1, 1953,NZ Truth,10</i>)

### blow around
 
 <i>v.</i> [1953 15 M A21 Thames DHS 14 BLOW AROUND [waste time]]

### blow down
 
 <i>n.</i> forest destruction caused by wind

>  She said the forest was at risk from a big "blow down". because the trees were predominantly all the same age... <i>(Oct 1, 2003,NZ Education Review,3</i>)

### blow in
 
 <i>v.</i> to spend recklessly, squander (also absol.).

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> It appeared that from October to Christmas he had made [pounds]2000 and he was now engaged in `blowing it in'. He was reeling down the street ...and when he saw me he called out, `Hey, Tom, I'm flyblown just now but come with me in a day or two and I will make your fortune for you.' [171] ... You ask what the term `flyblown' meant. It was a word used in those days [c1860s] and meant a man had no money, but once I saw a man really flyblown. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,170</i>)

>  He'd go to town and blow his money in. <i>(1946,That Summer,34</i>) [NOTES: (`White Man's Burden')]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> blow in (money): spend. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,298</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> We'll blow in the cash for what it's worth. <i>(1983,Brown Man's Burden,25</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  John Richard Bourke got his wages last Saturday, ..., and at once started to blow in for a lot of it on booze ... - the remarkable things happened afterwards, when John Richard was comfortably muzzied. <i>(Mar 5, 1910,NZ Truth,245/7/3</i>)

### blow off
 
 <i>v.</i> to depart, esp. hurriedly

>  And Sol `blew' off, happy in the thought that the `bull-ring' was dodged for that day, anyhow. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,9</i>)

### blow one's weights up
 
 <i>phr.</i> to inform on; variant of put one's weights up

>  You couldn't get away with it [insurance fraud] today. Somebody would blow your weights up. <i>(Nov 10, 1988,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Spectrum Documentary (Buller-West Coast)]

### blow out
 
 <i>v.</i> to leave; clear out, blow through, to leave suddenly.

>  <b>HYDE</b> But that night ... Starkie packed his bags and blew out. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,213</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>McARA</b> <i>Blasting</i>: ... [The fuses,] about five feet long, were each cut about half-an-inch longer than the one before it, thus ensuring that none of the holes would be over-burdened and `blow [313] out' in the wrong direction, as any explosion must follow the line of least resistance, which is the shortest distance to a `free face.' <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),312</i>)

### blow over
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [hunting or culling noxious animals] to kill by shooting.

>  <b>CURTIS</b> 'Aha' you say, I'll just stalk up through the bush, get nice and close, and blow those deer over. <i>(1997,Murphy's Law and the Hunter,68</i>)

### blow someone's light out
 
 <i>phr.</i> to give someone a hiding [(1910 citation only tangential)]

>  [`... He sez: `What yer lookin' for?' I sez: `Nothing. Sez he: `well, yer don't want both eyes ter see that.' An' with that he ups with his fist an' forward and makes a cut.] <i>(Apr 30, 1910,NZ Truth,253/1/6</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> blows out me off-side light.' [check] <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,245</i>)

>  <b>BEATON</b> SANDY: ... An' Ginny yelled, ... an' up an' rushes this chick, like she was gunna blow her [sc. the chick's] lights out. <i>Ibid.</i> Glossary 109 <i>blow out (someone's) light</i>: to give someone a good hiding <i>(1984,Outside In,23</i>)

### blow that for a joke
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>blow that for a joke! </i>emphatic rejection; `blow' euphemistic curse; eg `Me referee that lot? Blow that for a joke! <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,16</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> An acid dismissal of a proposition in which the speaker has been asked to participate; in the daily round of work, or in social life. .in 4;`What! Mac and I've done all the organizing and you others won't do a little job like this. Blow that for a joke!' .in 4;'I've done the late shift for three weeks, because none of you other jokers was willing. I wont do it continuously. Blow that for a joke!' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],75</i>) [NOTES: (1962-63)]

>  Knock it off, pack a sad, have a nice one Get stuck in, boots and all, have a coke, Half a shake, how ya doin', have a drop, of mother's ruin Hell's bells, blow that for a joke. <i>(Dec 2004,NZ Memories,p19,No 51 ed.</i>)

### blow through
 
 <i>v.</i> to clear out; leave (See [cf. blow out](../B#blow-out),[shoot through](../S#shoot-through))

>  <b>D. SINGE</b> I'll just blow through now. <i>(May 1941,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: Wellington, ca1941 Air Force]

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> To depart quickly, in other to avoid trouble of his own making. .There was a lot of money unaccounted for in the ledgers and he was the book-keeper. So he blew through. He hasn't been heard of from that day to this.' .in 4;b. To depart quickly with very little announcement. `Oh, he blew through - he went off up North somewhere, I think.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],76</i>)

### Blowhard

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> blowfly, ?bluebottle

>  <b>McGILL</b> We stopped. You could hear two sounds, a blowie and a bellbird. <i>(1985,G'day Country,98</i>)
