### blowing
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [stock] swelling with bloat [caused by an abundance of green feed.]

>  <b>WALLACE</b> Cattle not accustomed to an abundant supply of green food are liable to suffer from 'hoven' or 'blowing' after eating it. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,301</i>)

>  The cow should be on clean ground and kept on her brisket, propped up with sacks of hay or chaff, to prevent blowing. <i>(Nov 20, 1915,NZ Journal of Agriculture,437</i>)

>  ..the death-rate from other causes, principally blowing or bloat, has always been higher on lucerne than on pasture ... <i>(Dec 20, 1928,NZ Journal of Agriculture,387</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAKER</b> Boasting P8 (B1941) quots Money Knocking About NZ 1871 <i>(1941,NZ Slang,31</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>blowing/blowing off </i>boasting or talking too much; eg `Bill's blowing off again about his prowess with the cue.' NZA mostly. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,16</i>)

>  In 1871 ... <i>blowing</i>, for boasting .. amongst other terms, were current in New Zealand at the time. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,BAKER</i>)

>  They compare Auckland with Naples, Nice, Genoa, and Constantinople, and Auckland surpasses all. This is what they call in the colonies "blowing". <i>(Jul 23, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the fly-blowing of sheep

>  <b>PERRY</b> The blowing of sheep is most common during the spring, ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,145</i>)

### blowing down
 
 <i>n.</i> a system of sluicing

>  A system of sluicing in which strips of ground about 3 yards wide, on each side of a gutter, are sluiced into a gutter which runs down the slope. The work begins at the top of the slope and works down to a tail-race already constructed at the bottom ... <i>The Blowing-Down System of Sluicing</i>. This is a novel system of sluicing that has been successfully used for the working of wide stretches of poor alluvial ground in Central Otage. Its greatest application has been in the Cromwell District where it has been in use for over thirty years. It was first introduced by a Russian miner, and, although long in use, is almost unknown outside of Central Otago. <i>(1908,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.5 (NS) 47</i>) [NOTES: (Park, Cromwell)]

### blowings
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) leftover chaff blown out from under the elevators in the process of harvesting oats.

>  <b>BUCKINGHAM</b> The chaffie was the man who took away the blowings or flight that was blown out from under the elevators. <i>(1969,From Hethersett to Quarry Hills,38</i>)

### blown
 
 <i>adj.</i> In NZ often transf. f. ruminant animals to human beings: full of food or wind, replete (after a meal). [ca1972 Blenheim I feel blown after that big feed.]

>  in the past both sheep and lambs have been badly blown. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Agriculture,331</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> fly-blown

>  <b>PERRY</b> A blown sheep is generally noticed to stamp its feet, ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,146</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> When I was able to make an examination I found a nest of maggots from an earlier strike at work under the skin, and am still asking myself what made the second sheep give this rude assistance to the first. For they were not even standing side by side. To reach the blown ewe the other one had to stretch across an intervening back, and she bit so savagely that it was clearly a purposeful assault. <i>(Jan 30, 1951,Shepherd's Calendar,17,1961 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>MAY</b> Bush flies, mosquitoes and sandflies were numbered among the lesser scourges. Food, blankets and clothes were quickly `blown'; ... <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,277,1962 ed.</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of stock] 1. bloated. 2. flyblown.

>  <b>SEWELL</b> If not immediately relieved they soon die and swell as if blown. <i>(1856,Sewell Journal 1853-1857,213</i>) [NOTES: in McIntyre (1980)]

>  In the early stages the temperature and pulse are increased, and in many cases, the animal is somewhat "blown". <i>(Dec 20, 1916,NZ Journal of Agriculture,468</i>)

>  It is generally at this time of the year that blown cows are met with. <i>(Dec 20, 1928,NZ Dairy Exporter,34</i>)

>  I have been having trouble with cows being blown. They are running on normal pasture.. <i>(Oct 25, 1951,NZ Farmer,22</i>)

>  .. some of her fellows being used in the project would make a very thorough job of getting blown on the ration of pure clover that was being fed to them. <i>(Dec 1, 1952,NZ Dairy Exporter,13</i>)

>  <b>WATERS</b> If you had a cow blown, calving trouble ... <i>(1976,Mostly a Farmer,15</i>)

>  <b>BODKIN</b> When there was fresh new grass about, we had to be careful not to let the cows eat too much or they would get 'blown'...if a cow did get blown through carelessness on our part, it could be serious. 2. <i>(1998,Account of Monte Christo, An,79</i>)

>  The sheep rub themselves on the pumice banks and then get blown where they've rubbed. We had a string of seven quite severe frosts, and still had a sheep blown after that. <i>(Aug 9, 1984,NZ Farmer,116</i>)

>  Shift the mob one day and they could all look all right; two days later I'd pick up a couple badly blown. So it would be out with the kitchen scissors, off with the maggoty wool, slap on a bit of engine oil and hope for the best. Every one of the big rams had been blown. <i>(2001,Growing Today,64</i>)

### blucher boots
 
 <i>n.</i>

### bluchers
 
 <i>n.</i> strong leather half-boots

>  ... bluchers of good hard leather, flat and thick in the soles, ... <i>(1875,Otago Guardian</i>) [NOTES: c1875 Otago Guardian in 1980 Tonkin Dunedin Gaol 17]

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> ... her front teeth were knocked out, she had red pulpy hands, and she wore on her feet a pair of dirty `Bluchers'. <i>(1912,Stories,111,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> Everything was in that shop. Bluchers and sand shoes, straw hats and onions were strung across the ceiling, ... <i>(1916,Aloe, The,45,1982 O'Sullivan ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> A pair of old bluchers was on one side of the door, and a large red watering-can on the other. <i>(1922,Stories,475,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> ... bluchers - they were lace-up working boots with thin soles. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,29</i>)

>  <b>CHI</b> Working in the wet was hard on footwear, and boots were a heavy item in clothing expenditure. Some wore `bluchers', a short boot; gumboots do not seem to have been in general use until the twentieth century; ... <i>(1982,Diary,x</i>) [NOTES: 1982 Child Introduction to Ayton Diary x]

>  <b>FITTON</b> BOOTS AND SHOES.- ... Napoleons, 35s. ... per pair; Wellingtons, English, 30s. ...; colonial, 35s. ... per pair; Bluchers and Oxford, ... 12s; ... <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),83</i>)

>  <b>PICARD</b> ... the waster that has a bad word for him isn't fit for you to wipe your hob-nailed bloody bluchers on. <i>(1906,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,7</i>)

### bludge
 
 <i>v.</i> to live off the generosity of others, to sponge;often with on; to cadge, scrounge; sponge (on). [From <i>N.Z. English to U.S. English Dictionary</i> at http://nz.com/NZ/Culture/NZDic.html]

>  We bludged our way into a bus and went down to the Franz Joseph. <i>(Aug 1905,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,5/19/93</i>)

>  Those oysters you bludged from me. <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in the Middle East,43</i>)

>  The writer goes midnight like a lag/ Who knows that his pals will twist/ And tell a pernicious different tale/ Though they bludged him his last fag. <i>(1952,Here & Now,26</i>) [NOTES: (K. Smithyman)]

>  `What in hell are ya doin in Christchurch anyway? Bludgin on social security, I spose.' `No. Dicken on that.' <i>Ibid.</i> 47 I was nearly bowed over by some officer being thrown out. Trying to bludge in on their party he was. <i>Ibid.</i> 227 ... I felt sick. Like the time guarding the boob back at Advance Base and there were New Zealanders behind the wire. New Zealanders bludging cigarettes. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,44</i>)

>  <b>HELMER</b> ... we strolled up to see Russ Tullock. `Maybe bludge a drink,' Crump said. `Haven't a drop in the house, lads,' said Russ. `You'd be welcome to it if I had. ...' <i>(1964,Stag Party,103</i>)

>  v.int.(slang) impose on, avoid hard work at expense of others, whence : <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),611</i>)

>  <i>bludge ... v. (Sl.)</i> impose on, avoid hard work at expense of others, whence :<i> bludger ...n</i>. hanger on, parasite, `scrounger'. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>AGNEW</b> He had bludged a smoke from me and had asked why I refused to go to war. <i>(1974,Loner, The,127</i>)

>  <i>bludge Austral. & N.Z. inf. ~ vb. 1.</i> (when <i>intr</i>., often foll. by <i>on</i>) to scrounge from (someone). 2.<i> (intr.)</i> to skive. <i>~n. 3.</i> a very easy task. [C19: back formation from<i> bludger</i> pimp, < BLUDGEON] - <i>bludger n. </i> <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 76; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 53]]

>  <b>ROBINSON</b> I sometimes joke about what I call `the worldwide freemasonry of runners' which makes it possible by just putting on a pair of road shoes and shorts to bludge a spare bed in any city in the world. Derogatory. To continually ask for help in money, goods or services <i>(1986,Heroes and Sparrows,70</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> ?over long periods. .in 4;`He won't go out and get a bit of wood from the bush like the rest of us do, he'd rather bludge some from his old mother's shed.' Thus also, to make an occasional request, in good humour. .in 4;`We won't unpack till we get there, we'll call at Don's on the way and bludge a snack. He'll be real pleased to see us.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],77</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> He's got no time for any man bludging./ I owe him [the publican] for a grunter and a bottle/ And he'll think I'm dodging. <i>(1979,Collected Poems,219,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (1960) 1979 Baxter Collected Poems 219]

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i> bludger:</i> a bludger is a SPONGER - someone who is always borrowing and rarely repaying. To bludge is to borrow. It's socially acceptable to say `may I bludge a cigarette (or a cup of sugar)' but calling someone a bludger can be a fighting insult <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,15</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Trying to bludge in on their party he was. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,47</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to enjoy a job at base; live at ease or apparently safely at the front line's expense, (a back-formation from bludger) (See [cf. Bludgers' Hill](../B#bludgers'-hill))

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> He'll immediately assume that you were one of the 95% who bludged at base in Enzed or England or Yankee Land. <i>(1944,Troop Target,186</i>)

### bludger
 
 <i>n.</i> a useless person; one who avoids responsibility or lives on the back of others; a loafer, an idler [cf. dole bludger 1984 14 F E131Wgton Girls C. 10 BLUDGER [useless person]] (See [cf. dole bludger](../D#dole-bludger))

>  What type of skunk Costello is can be gathered from the fact that he is an individual of the bludger class, who will never attempt anything in in the form of honest hard work, and against whom the detec [check] engaged in war. <i>(Sep 8, 1906,NZ Truth,64/4/4</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> See that bludger there? He's an auctioneer's stooge. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,194</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> As far as he was concerned, he said, bludgers [here, members of a search-and-rescue party] could stay lost if they didn't have the sense to follow a creek. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,60</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> This term is used in contempt. .in 4;`Here comes that Z now we've done it on our own. He's always got an excuse to be somewhere else when there might be a bit of work to be done. The greatest bloody bludger of all time'. <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],78</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a thick-edged axe, used by bush workers.

>  <b>DAHM</b> The bushman always carried two axes as he worked. One he called the bludger. It had a thick edge and he used it where the going was tough. The bludger was almost impossible to gap. He would lend the bludger to almost anyone who asked for it. <i>(2000,People of the Axe, The,38</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a criminal who uses a bludgeon

>  Was Griffiths Brutally Butchered by Bludgers? <i>(Jan 10, 1906,NZ Truth,34/7/8</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep or cattle dog that does not perform well. (See [follower](../F#follower))

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> As Wonk did all my heading much better than the other two bludgers, the mustering went on a lot easier for the next couple of days. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,57</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a man who lives with or on the earnings of a harlot.

>  ... What, in slang phrase, we call a `bludger,' or a species of un-human being that lives on the proceeds of an `unfortunate's' prostitution, ... <i>(Aug 25, 1906,NZ Truth,62/5/1</i>)

>  Bludger and Bawd and Their Brats: ... He allowed his wife to go out battling ... of nights while he stayed home. <i>(Aug 11, 1906,NZ Truth,60/5/5</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> In the noon heat everything in Cairo slept except the male defenders of the prostitutes' quarters, known to the dictionary as <i>souteneurs</i>, and to the troops as bludgers. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,96</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> one who has a safe job at base; who shirks active service; a Base-wallah.

>  <b>DAVIN</b> How the hell are you, you big staff-wallahs? ... What are you doing out of bed at this hour? If I had a fine bludger's job like this I'd take it seriously. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,103</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Bloody doctors and dentists and base pay bludgers, even nurses and V.A.D.'s know more about the war than we do who fight it!' <i>(1951,Brave Company,100</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Nobody put up stripes in our outfit. That was for base bludgers. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,92</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> ... You should be jake sitting out the rest of the war in some bludger's job in Trentham [Military Camp]. <i>(1975,Breathing Spaces,124</i>)

>  <b>WAGENER</b> `The Bludger' typifies the Div. man's contempt for a certain type who were numerous enough at one time in a certain part of Maadi Camp to earn for it the title `Bludgers' Hill' [different from<i> base-wallah </i>who is often there through circumstances he cannot control]. <i>(1943,Kiwi's Soliloquy,7</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> inveterate cadger; sponger; occas. one who `borrows' or `lifts' objects

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> The Bludger. He was known among the boobheads as that. When they called him by his name you felt they were being intentionally respectful. ... But connected with him it didn't mean what it was supposed to mean. A bludger is a pimp, a man who lives on women, but I doubt if anyone thought specifically that of him. Bludger is also what it sounds, a fairly indiscriminate term of abuse. When the boobheads called him that they meant a lot of things. A bully, something sly, a chap who'd put a dirty one on to you, not necessarily for any purpose other than it gave him pleasure to do so. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,89</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> A dairy-farmer type ... was enthusiastically entertaining an obvious bludger, spending money. <i>(1962,One of Us,11</i>)

>  <b>WALLIS</b> The bloody working man's the backbone of the country. ... Pack of bludgers, that's what they are. <i>(1963,Point of Origin,10</i>)

>  <i>bludger n.</i> hanger on parasite, ` scrounger'. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),611</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> We'll go up North and start a boarding house. No bludgers allowed. <i>(1982,Collected Plays,39</i>) [NOTES: Wide Open Cage]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> We'll go up North and start a boarding house. No bludgers allowed. <i>(1982,Collected Plays,39</i>) [NOTES: Wide Open Cage]

>  <b>LELAND</b> a bludger is a SPONGER - someone who is always borrowing and rarely repaying. To bludge is to borrow. It's socially acceptable to say `may I bludge a cigarette (or a cup of sugar)' but calling someone a bludger can be a fighting insult. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,15</i>)

>  <b>IHIMAERA & LONG (ed.)</b> He supposed she was thinking, 'Another bloody bludger' and was frustrated because she couldn't do anything about it. <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,127</i>)

>  Coming from the sheltered upbringing of New Zealand, where misguided and ill-thought-out benefit policies by well-meaning governments have produced a generation of bludgers - sons and daughters of people on the dole deciding to follow their parents into the family business, rather than actually work for a living. <i>(Dec 11, 2000,Dominion,6</i>)

>  Some bludger's got my tin hat. <i>(Dec 21, 1942,NZEF Times,II/17</i>)

>  "Don't you know that. It's a bludge. I'm just a bludger." <i>(Feb 9, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  Your correspondent.. unfortunately epitomises the still widely-held view that the unemployed are idle, drug-ridden, lazy bludgers who live a life of ease and luxury on fat welfare cheques. <i>(Sep 19, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  Everybody accuses and abuses them like they were the culprits because they're on the DPB and they're bludgers. <i>(1995,Mana,No. 10 : 31</i>)

>  <b>LACEY</b> Unless you were a Rugby Union bludger you got out on the old pavement outside Eden Park. <i>(1976,Loosehead Len's Bumper Thump Book,7</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> a term of abuse for a beneficiary

>  Social Welfare minister Michael Cullen spoke out yesterday against those who criticised beneficiaries as `undeserving' or `idle bludgers'. <i>(Nov 11, 1988,Dominion,2</i>)

### bludger's fish
 
 <i>n.</i> cadger's fish

>  <b>WILSON</b> MEGRIM ... Sometimes called Lantern-fish (because a lantern could be easily shone through it) and known to the Maoris as `mahue' - the fish that is left behind. Known to Christchurch fishermen in early years as the Bludger's Fish. It was the fish they handed out to well-known bludgers who came down to the docks to cadge fish. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,74</i>)

### Bludgers' Hill
 
 <i>n.</i> nickname for the site of 2NZEF headquarters (Maadi near Cairo) [WW2] (See [cf. Daffodil Hill](../D#daffodil-hill))

>  <b>HELM</b> The hill that has since become famous in the 2 NZEF as Bludgers' Hill, or Daffodil Hill (beautiful but yellow) as it is now known. <i>(1944,Fights and Furloughs,8</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> They drove back along the [Maadi] camp road and up on to Bludger's Hill where the Base Staff dwelt, aloof. First the MS, inscrutable but cordial. Then the Pay Office ... `The working class can kiss my arse, I've got a bludger's job at last', sang Smithy to the tune of the Red Flag. ... `All the boys sing it whenever they pass Bludger's Hill. ... Makes the base-wallahs mad.' <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,147</i>)

>  <b>BRUNO</b> ... before Bludgers' Hill was officially condemned as a title in Routine Orders by sensitive base wallahs. <i>(1948,Maleesh George,58</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> This chap finally admitted he had never come down from Bludgers' Hill and then asked what outfit he had been in. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,66</i>)

>  Maadi, once known to thousands of New Zealanders as `Bludger's Hill' the site of Freyberg's headquarters, the training camp and the main camp for those on leave from the front - is unrecognisable. <i>(Oct 4, 1982,Evening Post,41</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Bludger's Hill</i> nickname for admin. quarters on rise of Maadi base camp near Cairo, WWII. <i>[Jim Henderson] </i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,16</i>)

>  <b>KIPPENBERGER</b> The Fourth Brigade Headquarters was established on the Hill, so on [1940-1941] to be derisively and unfairly known as `Bludgers' Hill.' <i>(1949,Infantry Brigadier,10</i>)

>  They sent ole Bill to Bludgers' Hill, and jacked him up a stripe. <i>(Dec 21, 1942,NZEF Times,II/78 : 17</i>)

>  "What do they all do up there on 'Bludgers' Hill'?" (the name given to that hill in Maadi Camp where Headquarters 2 N.Z.E.F. and other odds and sods resided. <i>(1958,RSA Review,35(1) : 1</i>) [NOTES: Check year]

>  Hot for the foe, Noel "Wig" Gardiner has a year at Bludgers Hill near the Nile, while the bloody defeats of Greece, Crete, Sidi Rezegh and Ruweizat scar into our island history. <i>(Mar 13, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),73</i>)

### bludging
 
 <i>n.</i> (1)(WWII period) appropriating, "borrowing", helping oneself, "liberating" (2)(post 1970) living off state benefits

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Well, how do you like the idea of a bit of bludging at Div HQ, Curly? <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,304</i>)

>  Back at base, they set the pace, when bludgin' was an art <i>(Dec 21, 1942,NZEF Times,II/78 : 17</i>)

>  What is undeniable, however, is that a large number of New Zealanders have come to believe that universal welfare does breed state dependency, that bludging is a national way of life for too many and that a "selfish" older generation would see their descendants reduced to penury so long as their divine right to the pension survives as long as they do. <i>(Jan 23, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> cadging

>  <b>CASEY</b> We came on a bludging trip. Can you let John have Padre to ride for a day or two? <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,194</i>)

### blue
 
 <i>n.</i> nickname for a ginger or red-headed person

>  Andrew James (Blue) Kennedy (1925 - ......) ... Never one to dodge a `blue' with employers ..., he was in fact nicknamed for his wiry crop of ginger hair. <i>(Jan 11, 1987,Sunday Star (Auckland),A7</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to scold; to go off. [[d?] June 20 The Evening Post I don't know where she was when we got there. She started blueing at me about the beer.]

>  Apparently, I had made a meet at the Johnny Horner for that afternoon and when I didn't front she blued. to blue....to abuse someone <i>(May 21, 1963,NZ Truth,19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a bluey, a summons; also a bit of blue a criminal charge. (See [cf. blue paper](../B#blue-paper))

>  He is still a conductor in the tram service, and was shown the blister when Bailey was served with a bit-o'-blue. <i>(Mar 13, 1909,NZ Truth,195/5/1</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  For a man on the Collar, every blue he gets in means maybe another two years. <i>(Nov 1950,Here & Now,15</i>)

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> Bluey had lost his seaman's ticket over his jail blues, and I don't suppose he'll ever get it back. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,127</i>)

>  <i>blue, n</i>. a bill, a summons. Also a fight or row. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),611</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> BLUE (n) Criminal charge <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,245</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the back country (See [i.e. the far blue yonder](../I#i.e.-the-far-blue-yonder))

>  A soggy packing up followed next morning and then it was once more `into the blue.' <i>(1948,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,4/18/300</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> as a distinguishing epithet in names of plants, see: bindweed, borage, convolvulus, grass; grass, couch; grass, tussock; grass, wheat; lupin, morning glory, pimpernel, pine, sedge spaniard, toadstool, twitch, vervain.



 
 <i>n.</i> error, blunder, bloomer (unfortunate, unconscious) (esp. make a blue) (the infrequent verb blue (something), `to break, ruin (sthg)' seems formed on this noun, although it may be related to blue v1.), CCR suggests blow (sthg) with past tense blew it. [1953 16 M A32 Thames DHS 19 BLUE (BLOO) [make a mess]]

>  He rang Mr McGavin, Cromwell's solicitor, asking if there could be any `blues' or defects in title. <i>(Jul 14, 1954,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  <b>KEINZLY</b> `I'm not fighting, Kathy. Not with you. I'm just wanting you back in Auckland, you and the kids with your buses and milk bottles and squint-eyed postman. ... Kathy, I think I've made a blue.' <i>(1960,Tangahano,33</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> As it was not to be a hunting trip, in order to keep the weights of their packs down, they made the blue of not even taking one rifle. [1934] <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,17</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Trouble with your blokes is you won't admit when you've made a blue. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,17</i>)

>  <b>PEARSON</b> She heard him say to one of the men: `By God, I made a blue this morning, Ted', and he sniggered heartily. <i>(1963,Coal Flat,251</i>)

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> The men would ... tease me into admitting I'd had a blue. <i>(1965,Skippers Road,46</i>)

>  <i>blue n. (Coll.)</i> bill, summons; fight, row; mistake (N.Z.). <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> On one ... occasion I made a `blue' which I have not lived down yet. [here a mistaken or bad batch of bread] <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,63</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>blue:</i> a GOOF or mistake. `I made a blue in trying to get on with her on the first date'. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,15</i>)

>  Deans planted the ball down an estimated six inches over the line, then made his second `blue' - he got up. <i>(Sep 18, 1980,Dominion,4/8</i>)

>  <i>blue</i>: mistake <i>(1981,Auckland Secondary Teachers College</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <i>blue ... 8. Austral. sl. </i>an argument or fight:<i> he had a blue with a taxi driver. 9.</i> Also: <i>bluey. Austral. & N.Z. inf.</i> a summons. <i>10. Austral. & N.Z. inf.</i> a mistake; error. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,116</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 76; see 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 53 for sense 9 & 10 below]]

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> Oh Rosie, forgive us, what have we done? Have we made a blue? <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay,42</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a convalescent soldier (See [cf. blue boy](../B#blue-boy),[blue pants](../B#blue-pants))

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> [sic] <i>(1945,Away from Home,77</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a policeman.

>  <b>THATCHER</b> All honor [sic] to the watchful trap,/ .../ He isn't an invisible blue,/ Long may this town by him be haunted,/ <i>'Tis the only case I ever knew/ Of a bobby all there when he was wanted./ </i> <i>(1864,Invercargill Minstrel,11</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the unknown

>  <b>JACKSON</b> Early in the month of September, 1941, the whole battalion moved `up the blue' (a term used to indicate any portion of the sterile Western Desert) to a place called Baguish. <i>(1943,Passage to Tobruk,104</i>)

>  We were striking out into the blue... In the desert the unknown is always "the blue", whether it be Egypt, Cyrenaica, Tripolitania or Tunisia. <i>(Apr 19, 1943,NZEF Times,II/95 : 6</i>)

>  When we had settled down and knew that the Western Desert was ALWAYS "up the blue", I inquired.. where could I learn the language? <i>(Aug 9, 1943,NZEF Times,III/111 : 2</i>)

>  You'd be talking, you'd take somebody shopping or go out for lunch or dinner today. Tomorrow they'd be up the blue and walk on a landmine and you'd never see them again. <i>(Feb 2, 2002,Dominion,13</i>)

>  <b>BARBER</b> ... the whole brigade [the Camel Corps] could, on an hour's notice, go off into the `blue' for five days without any communication or assistance from base. <i>(1984,Red Coat to Jungle Green,93</i>) [NOTES: 1938 John Robertson The Cameliers in Palestine 27 in 1984 Barber Red Coat to Jungle Green 93]

>  Out in the blue! <i>(Sep 2, 1941,2NZEF Times,15/6</i>)

>  In the desert the unknown is always `the blue', whether it be Egypt, Cyrenaica or ... [etc] <i>(Apr 12, 1943,2NZEF Times,4</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Had a bit of a run-in with a pongo MP down the Berka last night. ... Those bastards are too bloody fond of poking the borax at a bloke just back from the blue. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,209</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> [IN THE BLUE : ?away from base : ?in the wrong] In the dense smoke and dust the Otago left had lost touch with the sorely reduced companies on the right [in the advance by Eaucourt, September 1916] ... the companies ... reported their location ... by runner to Battalion Headquarters. ... It was with some consternation that General Braithwaite ... found his troops in a position so much `in the blue' as to invite disaster. <i>(1921,NZ Division,114</i>) [NOTES: ?In trouble; ? too far away from base in the (unknown) desert.]

>  <b>ROBERTSON</b> <i>(1938,Cameliers, The,27</i>) [NOTES: [WW1, the Middle eastern desert]]

>  After they were far beyond their guns they led their men on "into the blue". <i>(May 24, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/44 : 175</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to spend recklessly, to blow (a cheque etc.)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> If I blue twenty cheques they'll never come back. <i>(1905,Sons o' Men,289</i>)

>  It appears that Humby's pa obligingly wheeled his barrow over the border last January and Humby started to blew the sum of 260[pounds] the old man had left behind him. <i>(Oct 20, 1906,NZ Truth,70/3/4</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> You must give a man one chance in a year to blue his cheque. <i>(1907,Tracks  We Tread,70</i>)

>  <b>'ANZAC'</b> The boys had fixed to give us a boncer welcome, but ... in the words of our informant `they blued their cheques, got shikkared, and the show bust up.' <i>(1916,On the Anzac Trail,27</i>)

>  ... those who blued a tanner. <i>(Oct 28, 1916,Hutt Valley Independent,4</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> BLUED THEIR CHEQUES: Spent their money mostly on liquor. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,110</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> blue mussel

>  Blues are farmed prolifically along the coast of Galicia, Spain's Atlantic seaboard ...That region produces 240,000 tonnes ... worth $150 million, les than half the value of greenlips. <i>(May 10, 1989,Marlborough Express,20</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  His airmanship and leadership saw him return home with the ribbons of the DSO, DFC and bar on the left breast of his uniform. He exchanged the blue for working clothes again. <i>(Aug 25, 1998,Sunday Star Times,A9</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> as a distinguishing epithet in names of birds, see: crane, duck n1., duck, mountain; penguin, petrel, shag.



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> an argument, tiff; fight [ca1945 He had a blue with his wife and cleared out. 1984 14 F E138Wgton Girls C. 30B BLUE [fighting]]

>  Some brawlers left before the police arrived. `The bloke who really started the blue isn't here - he shot through,' said Purda. <i>(Aug 11, 1956,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  [Shortland said] `I honestly thought I got the injuries when I had a blue with a constable in the cells'. <i>(Apr 21, 1962,Evening Post</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> ... Abungus came home one night and said he'd had a blue with six Irish seamen, he'd beaten up one of them and all the others had run away. <i>(1965,Stand in the Rain,110</i>)

>  <i>blue n</i>. a bill, a summons. Also a fight or row. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),611</i>)

>  ... that bit of a blue <i>(May 14, 1979,Radio NZ / National Radio,Morning Report</i>)

>  Mr McCutcheon says on numerous occasions Mr Coleman has been told about the committee's dissatisfaction ... Mr Coleman said: `About six months ago I had a blue with the council about this but I've since straightened it out.' <i>(Jul 25, 1980,Evening Post,1/2-3</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Sounds like there's going to be a blue down there tonight ... so I'm not taking any chances.' <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,80</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>blue</i> red-haired man, such as `Blue' Kennedy, secretary of the Meatworkers' Union earlier in the 1980s. - a fight. - a blunder. - being drunk (as in the `blue devils'). All NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,16</i>)

>  <i>Pub notice stops blues</i> [headline] The `Bluey,' - a legal notice aimed at keeping troublemakers out of pubs - has been an outstanding success ... <i>(Sep 22, 1988,Evening Post,11</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>adj.</i> as a distinguishing epithet in names of fish, see: bellowsfish, bream, cod, grenadier, hake, mackerel, maomao, marlin, mussel, shark; shark, pointer; shark, whaler; warehou; see also bluefish, bluenose.



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a hotelkeeper's notice banning an offending customer from admission to or service on the premises.

>  The group was issued with `blues', banning them from the hotel for two years after they began fighting among themselves about 9pm. <i>(Jun 4, 1988,Dominion,3</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>adj.</i> as an epithet in mining and quarrying, see: blue bar, blue bottom, blue reef, blue-stone.

### blue bar
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. blue bottom](../B#blue-bottom),[blue reef](../B#blue-reef))

>  The andesites occur in bars, varying from 20ft. to 300 ft. in width, and are termed by the miners "hard country" or "blue bars". <i>(1896,AJHR,C.-3/50</i>)

### blue billy
 
 <i>n.</i> broad-billed prion, whalebird.

>  Amongst birds he has obtained [at the Chathams] are ...a Dotterel, differing from the common Dotterel of this country ...; a sea-bird, called by the whalers the `Blue Billy', the beak of which is singularly shaped, and of a blue colour, hence its trivial name; the Nelly <i>Ossifraga gigantea</i>) ... <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/64</i>) [NOTES: (Travers Notes on the Chatham Isds)]

>  The Mollymawks we had till we reached the South Tropic. ... In the Pacific I saw ... large flocks of `whale-birds' as the sailors called them - which were the Blue Billy (<i>Prion turtur</i>); but, in the South Atlantic, we met flocks of another but larger-sized grey bird, which they also called `whale-birds.' These were evidently <i>Procellaria glacialoides. </i> <i>(1876,TrNZI,8/199</i>) [NOTES: (Hector Birds Voyage to England)]

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BROAD-BILLED PRION <i>Pachyptila vittata Other names</i>: Parara, Blue Billy (Chathams). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,38</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Prion ... vittatus</i> ... These tiny birds are light grey above; ... The blue bill is very wide and edges of the upper mandible turn upwards. ... They breed on the Chathams. There are three sorts of blue billies as they are locally called. ... Whalebirds, Blue Billies. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,85</i>)

>  <i>Broad-billed Prion Pachyptila vittata vittata</i> (FORSTER, 1777) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Whalebird, scooper, blue-billy, parara. ... Body blue-grey with conspicuous black open-M pattern across upper wings. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,85</i>)

>  ... the most important [Chatham Island] edible sea-birds: AS WRITTEN AS PRONOUNCED ENGLISH hopo ho[macron]p' albatross hakoakoa ha[macron]ko[macron]k' mutton bird taiko tchai[macron]k' pied shear water tupoupou tchu[macron]poup' pied blue billie reoreo ro[macron]ro[macron] Mother Carys chicken turiwati tchu[macron]riwat' dottrel [sic] kuaka ku[macron]k' snipe <i>(1928,Bishop Museum Memoirs,IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 358</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 358]

>  He also mentions.. a sea-bird, called by the whalers the "Blue Billy," the beak of which is singularly shaped, and of a blue colour, whence its trivial name. <i>(1872,TrNZI,IV/65</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Grey Ternlet Procelsterna cerulea</i> (BENNETT, 1840) ... The grey ternlet breeds in loose colonies on the coasts of Pacific islands. ... OTHER NAMES:<i> Grey noddy, blue-grey noddy, little blue petrel, blue billy. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,235</i>)

### blue bird
 
 <i>n.</i> pukeko; cf. blue jimmy [[? is this mere aj + noun]]

>  <b>BRERETON</b> The blue bird, swamp hen or pukeko was very plentiful [at Ngatimoti, Nelson c1860s-1870s] <i>(1947,No Roll of Drums,19</i>)

### blue blind
 
 <i>phr.</i> very drunk

>  <b>LORD</b> We say a man is .../ I nearly forgot to mention `blue blind', `bunkered', and `brimful' <i>(1921,Ballads of Bung 'Stunology'</i>)

### blue blister
 
 <i>n.</i> an early form of local currency (Otago) (See [bluey](../B#bluey),[blister](../B#blister))

>  <b>WILSON</b> We got no ready money as pay [for shearing] but a `blue blister', i.e., a cheque on Johnny Jones. <i>(1912,Reminiscences of Early Settlement Dunedin,68</i>) [NOTES: [early 1860's]]

### blue borage
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Echium vulgare</i> viper's bugloss [f. its blue flower resembling the borage (<i>Borago officinalis</i>) in colour (mainly SI, esp. Marlborough and n. Cant.); also blue borage; a local (esp. Marlborough) name for the blue-flowered Eng. weed viper's bugloss. (See [viper's bugloss](../V#viper's-bugloss),[borage](../B#borage))

>  But now we can smell it. No, not the cow dung, kids, but the clear mountain air and the faint scent of blue borage wafting down the valley. <i>(May 25, 2004,Dominion Post,A10</i>)

>  <i>blue borage [=] viper's bugloss </i>[s] <i>Ibid.</i> 7 <i>borage blue [=] viper's bugloss </i>[s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,6</i>)

>  The main source of nectar in this wild and remote area, flanked by the high mountains of the Seaward and Inland Kaikouras, is viper's bugloss (<i>echium vulgare</i>). Mountain-honey was the first company in New Zealand to market this kind of honey as blue borage, which is the local common name for the plant. <i>(May 2002,Cuisine,44</i>)

>  The Karori restaurant, which offered a Born to Be Wild Salad (blue borage honey-glazed smoked eel and watercress, lime pepper salad with karengo crostini) matched with Monteith's Black, and Pheasantly Phruity Purple Haze (pheasant on crushed urenika potatoes with port and blackcurrent sauce, wild mushrooms and parsnip crisps) matched with Monteith's Celtic Red. <i>(Jul 21, 2004,Capital Times,13</i>)

### blue bottom
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. blue bar; blue reef; bottom](../B#blue-bar;-blue-reef;-bottom))

>  The clays and sandstones are called the `Blue Bottom' ... [<i>Ibid. </i>86] The Blue Bottom is so called because it forms the basement formation of the extensive unconsolidated later Tertiary and more recent deposits of the coastal plain, and consists chiefly of blue clay. <i>(1906,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.1 (NS) 20</i>) [NOTES: (Bell and Fraser, Hokitika)]

>  Like the `blue bottom' of North Westland, the clays [at Parapara, Nelson] frequently contain calcareous concretions, spheroidal or lensoid in shape. <i>(1907,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.3 (NS) 59</i>) [NOTES: (Bell)]

>  ... and it is here assumed to be the marine sandstone of the Blue Bottom. <i>(1938,NZ Geol. Memoir,No. 455</i>) [NOTES: (Modriniak and Marsden, Hokitika)]

>  <i>Blue Bottom Formation</i> ... It is locally called Brighton Bottom at Rimu. <i>(1906,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.1 (NS) 86</i>) [NOTES: (Bell and Fraser, Hokitika)]

### blue boy
 
 <i>n.</i> a wounded or convalescent soldier (See [cf. blue pants](../B#blue-pants))

>  An Educational scheme is under way for the benefit of :- (1) the Blue Boys. <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,65</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> I learned that my number of charges had increased to seven pairs of 'blues". <i>(1945,Away from Home,77</i>)

>  (Gloss) blue boys: wounded, convalescents. [check] <i>(Aug 30, 1916,NZEF Chronicles</i>)

>  An Educational scheme is under way for the benefit of :- (1) The Blue Boys. (2) Lead Swingers. <i>(1918,New Zealand At the Front,65</i>)

>  <b>INGRAM</b> Our host was one of the many kindly Londoners who call at Hospitals on Sundays and take out `Blue-boys' for an airing. <i>(Oct 30, 1917,Anzac Diary,73,1987 ed.</i>)

>  The patients you meet at every corner, landmarks of blue - those well-fitting faultlessly tailored blues! How the tailors of England escaped "a fit of the blues" when they came face to face with them for the first time I do not know.] <i>(Aug 30, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/1 : 12</i>)

>  A Blue Boy appeared, then another, still a third, all ready for some fun. <i>(Sep 15, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/2 : 35</i>)

>  It was infinitely touching to see some of the Blue Boys making the beds and helping to prepare things for their wounded comrades. <i>(Sep 29, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/3 : 59</i>)

>  The New Contingent Association is opening shortly a fine big recreation room in the hospital building for the Blue Boys... It is to be known as the Kapai Club. <i>(Mar 13, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/39 : 62</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> an early Health Stamp

>  Roughly 75,000 of the "red boy" and 112,000 of the "blue boy" were sold. <i>(Jan 29, 1947,NZ Observer,8</i>)

### blue cod
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Parapercis colias</i>, fam Mugiloididae (occas. listed as Parapercidae or Pinguipedidae): a popular coastal food fish, bluish, greenish or brownish-grey, restricted to NZ.; coal fish, pakirikiri, rawaru, rock cod

>  <b>Owen Marshall</b> 'We should try for a fishing trip soon. Blue cod in the Sounds - what do you reckon?' <i>(2007,Drybread,83</i>)

>  Blue cod are pierced with rods and spitted <i>(Sep 28, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,19</i>)

>  (Black Stewart Island) It was no unusual event ...to land ... Moki, <i>Latris ciliaris;</i> Trumpeter, <i>Latris hecateia</i>; Blue cod or Rock cod, <i>Percis colias</i>; and Red cod, <i>Lotella bacchus. </i> <i>(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39</i>)

>  There are restrictions on taking the tasty blue cod here but we bring up a terakihi that is perfect for the pot. <i>(Dec 17, 2010,Waikato Times,14</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> 38. ROCK COD. ... This (<i>Percis colias</i>) is the Coal Fish of Captain Cook and Blue Cod of the settlers in the South, and the Pakirikiri of the Maoris ... <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,113</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> ROCK COD (<i>Percis Colias</i>). ... This is the coal-fish of Captain Cook, and blue-cod of the settlers in the South, and the pakirikiri of the Maoris, and is the most commonly-caught fish among rocks on the coast. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,15</i>)

>  Blue cod (<i>Percis colias</i>) <i>(1890,TrNZI,22/375</i>) [NOTES: (G.M. Thomson)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Blue cod</i>... name given to a New Zealand fish <i>Percis colias</i>... Called also in New Zealand <i>Rock cod </i>(q.v.). <i>(1895,Austral English,38</i>)

>  <i>Blue-cod</i> (Parapercis colias, <i>Forster</i>). <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/544</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson, Otago)]

>  PARAPERCIS COLIAS (Forster) <i>Blue Cod; Rawaru</i>. A highly esteemed food fish; secured in water up to 100 fathoms. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/123</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  42. <i>Parapercis colias</i> Forster. Blue Cod. Maori: Pakirikiri <i>(1929,TrNZI,61/147</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  <i>Parapercis colias</i> (Forster). Blue cod. <i>Rawaru. </i> <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/413</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>POWELL</b> BLUE COD (<i>Parapercis colias</i> ) Rawaru of the Maoris, is our most esteemed food fish. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,69</i>)

>  Blue Cod <i>Parapercichthys colias</i> (Bloch and Schn.) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,409</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 409]

>  <b>MORELAND</b> [=MARBLEFISH] BLUE COD [<i>Parapercis colias</i> ] ... A very old name from Cook's time, coalfish, is still in use. A variety of Maori names are now little used. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,28</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> I hauled in a blue cod ... large enough for the whole family. <i>(1970,Life's Vagaries,46</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BLUE COD</i> (Blue weever, rawaru) <i>Parapercis colias</i> <i>(1982,Collins Guide,267,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Blue cod Parapercis colias</i> ... Found only in New Zealand. Known almost exclusively by this common name, the occasionally listed coal-fish being a name used only by Captain Cook and some early writers, and derived from an unrelated European fish. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,114</i>)

### blue code of silence
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Police officers who forced prisoners to pose for degrading photographs and dealt out excessive punishment were not reported because of a so-called "blue code of silence" that exists within the police. <i>(Oct 11, 2005,NZ Herald,A1</i>)

### blue country
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>"H"</b> He told of the hard long weeks of "driving" through "tight blue country" to pick up a reef. <i>(1898,Grain of Gold, The,13</i>)

### blue crane
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYSON</b> Their larder was also enlarged by the eggs of cranes, shags and gulls which nested in thousands on trees overhanging the river below the swamp paddock. <i>(1937,Thomas,137</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> reef heron

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The Matukutuku, or Crane, of this there are several kinds in New Zealand; the blue crane or that which inhabits the seashore is common to every one ... <i>(1844,NZ Journal,114,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> ('Twas that <i>blue crane</i> with bristly crown -/ You recollect?) ... <i>Ibid.</i> 2/338 Blue crane .... Ardeidae; Herodias Matuku? <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,2/153</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> THE BLUE HERON [title] In the neighbourhood of Takaka ... we watched the nesting customs of the Blue Crane. <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,77</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BLUE REEF HERON <i>Egretta sacra Other names</i>: Blue Crane, Matuku-moana. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,78</i>)

>  <i>White-faced Heron Ardea novaehollandiae novaehollandiae </i>LATHAM, 1790 ... OTHER NAME: <i>Blue crane. ... Throughout New Zealand in wetland habitats; ... Still expanding north an<i>Reef Heron Egretta sacra sacra</i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Blue reef heron, stern reef heron, blue crane, blue heron, matuku-moana</i>. ... Reef herons in New Zealand are at the southeastern limit of their mostly tropical range, and for that reason alone may never be common. d east from Australia. </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 132 <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,129</i>)

### blue duck
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Hymenolaimus malacorhyncus</i>, a [defin. goes here] Also called (blue) mountain duck, (blue) whistling duck, whio. cf also blue duck, a rumour.



 
 <i>n.</i> an uncommon indigenous duck with blue-grey plumage, Hymenolaimus malacorhynchos, living in swift-flowing mountain and bush rivers, and also known as rapid duck on account of its fast-moving river habitat. [aka whio, whistling duck, rapid duck]

>  Irene and I scramble down to the Lambert and immediately spook a pair of blue ducks. <i>(Jul 5, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

>  My first sight, in 1993, of your spectaular and very rare blue duck swimming with young against rushing white waters felt as though I had been given one of life's privileged moments. <i>(Jun 1999,North & South,22</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> We could talk learnedly of wash, blue ducks, seepage races, benching, californian riffles, and all the terms that go with prospecting. <i>(1974,Open Country Muster,62</i>)

>  Easter, 1933, was disastrous with evil storms. Len Boot ... made a blue duck's trip down the Whitcombe. <i>(Aug 1934,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,4</i>)

>  THE endangered blue duck may not know it, but the chances of its survival have received a boost to the tune of $1.5 million over 15 years. The blue duck can only live in its habitat of fast-flowing mountain streams. <i>(Jun 12, 2003,Evening Standard,B1</i>)

>  The wildlife pond was created by building a dam in 1976, doing extensive planting and encouraging scaup, grey teal, shoveler, black and white swans, mallards, Carolina wood ducks and a breeding pair of the endangered Whio (blue duck). <i>(Jun 2002,Elements,17/8</i>)

>  Further down the path I flush a pair of endangered blue duck from their trackside cover. <i>(Jan 1992,NZ Geographic,91</i>)

>  The Taieri Gorge was full of blue duck. <i>(Nov 2, 1920,Otago Witness,54</i>)

>  [Dusky Sound] We shot several curious Birds among which was a Duck of Blue grey Plumage with the end of its Bill as soft as the lips of any other animal, as it is altogether unknown I shall endeavour to preserve the Whole in spirits. [fn] the Blue Duck or whio, <i>Hymenolaimus malacorhyncus</i> (Gm.).] <i>(Apr 1, 1773,Cook's Journals,2/114,(1961) ed.</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> Great quantities of blue ducks, apparently a species of shoveller, are to be met with on this stream. <i>(1846,Account of an Exploring Expedition</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Heaphy Exped South-West Nelson in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 198]

>  Shot a wihu, or blue duck ... <i>(1847,Great Journey,35,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Our provisions here began to fail, but catching some eels and whios (blue ducks), they made a good addition to our scanty commissariat. <i>Ibid.</i> 136 The most abundant member of the family Anatidae... is the whio, the blue duck of the settlers. (Anas malacorhyncus). <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,11</i>)

>  I shot four blue duck, 4 pigeons and a caw-caw and a wecka. <i>(Dec 13, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,70,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> I don't think a minute passed without a brace of wild ducks flying past, - grey, blue, and Paradise. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,181,1870 ed.</i>)

>  ... the light-eyed Teal slunk silently from view; or ... the little Grebe with rosy breast, dived and sported with restless activity; close by a group of sober Grey Ducks; whilst the watchful Paradise Drake basked on the sunny bank above ... <i>Ibid.</i> 48 ... from the swift stream below ... arose the plaintive whistle of the Blue Duck ... <i>Ibid.</i> 72 ANAS SUPERCILIOSA, Gml. Parera. Grey Duck. <i>Ibid.</i> 73 HYMENOLAIMUS MALACORHYNCHUS, Gml. Whio. Blue Duck, Mountain Duck. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/47</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  101. HYMENOLAIMUS MALACORHYNCHUS. Gml. Blue, or Mountain Duck. Whio. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,37</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> HYMENOLAEMUS MALACORHYNCUS. (BLUE DUCK.) ... <i>Native name</i>.- Wio <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,262,{1888 2nd edn 2/276} ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> [A] few Paradise and blue ducks ... uttered their well-known notes. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,6</i>)

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> At the mouth of the river you might fill your boat with ... the blue, grey and paradise ducks, teal, ... and other birds ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,25</i>)

>  <i>Hymenolaimus malacorhynchus</i>. - Whio, Blue Duck. Both Islands. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/113</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <b>HARPER</b> Blue or mountain ducks (<i>Nestor rotabilis</i> [sic - Harper is shaky on scientific names], are not now found in any numbers except in the upper parts of hitherto unvisited rivers, ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,42</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> The Blue Duck [<i>H. malacorhynchus</i>]. Generally called the mountain duck, sa it is mostly found in the rocky torrents up to the grass line. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,237</i>) [NOTES: ca1899 Douglas Birds South Westland in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 237]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Whio ... 2. n. Hymenolaemus malacorhynchus, blue duck</i>; sometimes called <i>whistling duck</i>. <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,583</i>)

>  <i>Hymenolaimus malacorhynchus</i> Gm. Blue Duck; Mountain Duck. This beautiful and characteristic species maintains itself wherever the mountain-creeks which form its home are undisturbed by the too near approach of settlement. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/83</i>) [NOTES: Myers]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> ... the same peculiarity may be seen in the neck of the Blue Duck (<i>Hymenolaemus malacorynchus</i>). <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,90</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Another expression for a hopeless day when rain or fog prevents any chance of mustering is `a blue duck,' and I don't know the origin of this either. <i>(1951,High Country,43</i>)

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> With pork, young rabbits and an occasional blue duck we were never short of meat. Tamer than other wild duck, the blue mountain variety have now been killed out in this district, ... <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,17</i>) [NOTES: [f. c1869]]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> [so 1957 <i>Hymenolaimus</i>] <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,496</i>)

>  blue duck n : a New Zealand duck (<i>Hymenolaimus malacorhynchus</i>) with mostly lead-blue plumage <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,241</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Blue duck .. .. whio .. .. <i>Hymenolaimus malacorhynchus</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>blue duck NZ = WHIO </i> <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,76</i>) [NOTES: [nf. CCON; CCOM 53]]

>  <b>2/244</b> We shot several new birds, viz: a new ashcoloured <i> Whistling-Duck </i> [245] with a white arched broad bill, tipped with black ... [ed. fn] Now the Blue Duck <i> Hymenolaimus malacorhynchos </i> (Gmelin, 1789). <i>(Apr 2, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The</i>)

>  ... the third sort is the blue grey duck before mentioned or the whistling duck as some called them from the whistling noise they made. [ed. fn] The Blue Duck, Hymenolaimus malacorhynchus. It is sometimes called the Whistling Duck, and the Maori name, Whio, is also derived from its call. <i>(May 11, 1773,Cook's Journals,2/136,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> The third sort was a bluish grey whistling duck, about the size of a wigeon; its bill had a remarkable membranaceous substance at the extremity on both sides ... Its breast was sprinkled with ferruginous feathers, and on the wings it had a large white spot. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/157</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> There was a species of grey duck [near the Grey river] with white bills, often described as the whistling duck. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,32</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Whio </i> ... 2. n. <i> Hymenolaemus malacorhynchus, blue duck </i>; sometimes called whistling duck. </i> [so 1957/496 <i> Hymenolaimus </i>] <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,583</i>)

>  <i> whio </i>, a water-bird (Hymenolaimus malacorhynchus), blue-duck, mountain-duck, whistling duck; name echoic, like English whew-duck having a similar name for the same reason. The place-name Whiorau, means blue-ducks a-plenty. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/160</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i> Whio </i> 1. v.i. Whistle ...2. n. <i> Hymenolaimus malacorhynchus, blue duck </i>; sometimes called <i> whistling duck </i>. <i>(1971,Dictionary of the Maori Language,496</i>)

>  A few kiwis, blue ducks and hens were got on the flats.. and caw-caws were fairly plentiful. <i>(1936,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,V/37</i>)

>  Arrived Wataroa safely. Plenty of blue ducks. <i>(1935,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,IV/40</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> Blue Duck Pool.. was so named because for several seasons my father and I saw a blue duck swimming about with her brood of ducklings. <i>(1982,Blue Cliffs,124</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Hymenolaimus malacorhynchus</i>, blue duck, whio.



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) fog or misty conditions that prevent mustering.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> Another expression for a hopeless day when rain or fog prevent any chance of mustering is a 'blue duck' ... <i>(1951,NZ High Country,43</i>)

>  blue duck - fog-in or wet weather. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> something worthless, a dead duck [1895 Crowe.] Something unprofitable; a failure, a lost cause (esp. she's/it's a blue duck). ca1940-1950 Len Climo: 2NZEF and later Very frequent in Army overseas.]

>  She's away for a blue duck. <i>(Sep 25, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/170 : 4</i>)

>  [Answers to correspondents] `Blue Duck': your mss. is synonymous with your non de plume <i>(May 15, 1918,Kia-ora Coo-ee,3/15,1981 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `Having a good time [on leave]?' `She's a blue duck. ... Had a bit of a run-in with a pongo MP down the Berka.' <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,209</i>)

>  <b>MUNDY</b> I said to Mack `she looks like a blue duck.' [i.e., an unprofitable (gold-mining) venture or claim] <i>(1948,There's Gold in Them Hills,112</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Would Mabel last out, and with 30 love and Mabel serving [her too often repeated `double fault'], the result looked like a `blue duck'. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,173</i>)

>  A petition to force a by-election for two new trustees for the Terawhiti Licensing Trust never got off the ground. Organiser Norm Thomas says the petition is a `blue duck'. <i>(Oct 11, 1988,Karori and Western Suburbs News,1</i>)

>  It's a case of "blue duck" with my Deception money. <i>(Feb 27, 1890,Otago Witness,25</i>)

>  <b>OWENS & PERKINS</b> We finished the drain, and as they used to say on the creek, she was a blue duck - there was nothing there [sc. no gold]. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,21</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a (baseless) rumour

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> [The] following [was] employed by those who served in the [Great] War ... <i>blue duck</i>, a rumour, esp. a baseless one; <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Blue duck</i>. A rumour, esp. if baseless: New Zealand soldiers': in G.W. Semantics: <i>quack! quack! </i> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,70</i>)

>  [Slang of the NZEF] A `blue duck' was a rumour; but a `bird' was a prisoner in a cage. <i>(Apr 2, 1938,Press, The,18</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>BAKER</b> Among numerous terms originated in the first Great War we may note ... <i>blue duck</i>, a rumour. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,50</i>)

>  <i>blue duck n. (Sl.)</i> rumour (World War 1 slang). <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>blue duck</i> rumour, especially a baseless one or a dud; chiefly Kiwi army WWI, developed from Australian for anything not coming up to expectations. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,16</i>)

### blue fit
 
 <i>n.</i> orig. a fit of apoplexy; have a blue fit to become very angry or shocked.

>  <b>SARGESON</b> blue-fit: <i>have a blue-fit</i>, be very angry. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,298</i>)

### blue flu
 
 <i>n.</i> mass sickleave (deliberate) by policemen in lieu of striking

>  CHRISTCHURCH police say they are threatening to take `blue flu' sick leave to get around illegal strike action out of bitterness toward planned cuts to their superannuation scheme. <i>(Feb 16, 1990,Dominion,3</i>)

### blue gills
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  40. <i>Glaucopis Wilsoni</i>, Bp. Wattle-bird, Blue Gills, Kokako. <i>(Mar 1885,NZ Journal of Science,377</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  The writer found that in the Wairarapa the <i>Glaucopis wilsoni</i> is sometimes familiarly known as `the blue-gills.' <i>(1874,TrNZI,6/147</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts Birds of NZ)]

### blue grass
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp. use] a palatable native tussock grass, Agropyron scabrum.

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFIELD</b> Blue wheat grass (<i>Agropyron scabrum</i>) grows up to 1 ft. 6 in. chiefly between silver tussock. The only palatable tussocks are (1) the blue tussocks which are easily killed by continuous burning and by continuous close grazing and (2) the blue wheat grass which will stand hard grazing. <i>(1961,Agriculture,272</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> bluegrass: a native grass (<i>Agropyron scabrum</i>) very palatable to stock and most nutritious. Now pretty rare. [blue wheat grass] <i>(1980,Loughnan Glossary,8</i>)

### Blue Greens
 
 <i>n.</i> the environmentalist wing of the National Party.

>  Salmon's exposition of this process to the Blue Greens at National's conference on Saturday elicited many murmurs of approval, including from Smith. ... The Blue Greens' session was closed, too, but Smith kindly asked me in. <i>(Jul 25, 2006,Dominion Post,A11</i>)

### blue ground
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. blue bottom](../B#blue-bottom))

>  The cementing medium of the deposit is finely comminuted fresh mica-schist, resembling coarsely ground cement, hence the term `cement' applied by the miners to this and similar deposits. In the zone of weathering the `cement' is a rusty-brown colour ... Below the zone of weathering lies the unaltered `cement' which forms the `blue ground' of the miners. <i>(1932,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,13/263</i>)

### blue gum
 
 <i>n.</i> a species of Australian eucalypt and its wood

>  TO BE SOLD in Lots, ... good dry Blue Gum at 15s. 6d. <i>(Nov 15, 1851,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> I hope to plant some 40 Blue Gum trees. <i>(Aug 13, 1853,Auckland Journals,143,1971 ed.</i>)

>  Henry hopes in some degree to remedy this [exposure] by a plantation of blue-gums, an Australian production. <i>(Sep 4, 1859,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/489,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1859 Sep 4 Richmond-Atkinson Papers (1960) 1/489 (Maria Richmond)]

>  <i>gum a.</i>-blue' gum n. a species of eucalyptus, so called from the bluish colour of the leaves. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

### blue heeler
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  blue heeler n. Austral. a type of dog with dark, speckled markings: used for herding cattle. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,117</i>) [NOTES: [NZPOD 76; CCOM 53]]

>  <b>RENNIE</b> The other distinctive breed of Australian working dog is the cattle dog, known variously as the Australian heeler, blue heeler, or Queensland heeler. It is also sometimes incorrectly called the Blue Merle, but this is simply the description of a colour type, not the name of a breed, and in any case the phrase should be `blue merled'. <i>(1985,Working Dogs,14</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> any of a breed of medium-sized dogs with a dappled coat, bred in Australia and New Zealand as cattle dogs, the Australian blue cattle dog, having a blue or red-flecked coat

>  <b>CRUMP</b> We ... went out to the dog-breeder's place. The Waiau blokes bought four bully-looking mongrels ... and Vince got himself another blue-heeler. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,163</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> I've lost my dog - my best blue heeler! <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,34</i>)

>  He starts boiling about three hundred yards away ... Jed, the blue-heeler and Puke race after him. [a pig dog] <i>(Dec 1976,Spleen,No.6 19/2</i>)

### blue hen
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Apteryx oweni</i> Gould, grey kiwi.

>  Straight-billed kiwi, Grey Kiwi, Blue-hen of the [West Coast] diggers. <i>(1872,TrNZI,5/188</i>) [NOTES: (Potts)]

>  58. <i>Apteryx oweni</i>, Gould. Kiwi, Straight-billed Kiwi, Grey Kiwi, Blue hen of the diggers. - The breeding season extends through several months, eggs having been obtained on the West Coast during a considerable portion of the [506] year. <i>(Sep 1885,NZ Journal of Science,505</i>)

### blue heron
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) [See also (blue) crane. (There is occas.confusion of names between the reef heron and white-faced heron, from blue heron being popularly applied to the latter as well as to the former species.)] (See [reef heron; white-faced heron](../R#reef-heron;-white-faced-heron))

>  (Queen Charlotte Sound) There is also about the shore a few sea gulls, some blue herons. <i>(Feb 1777,Cook's Journals,3/807,1967 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/807]

>  <b>POLACK</b> <i> Blue herons, auks, sand larks , sand plovers, terns. </i> <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/306</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> <i> Blue herons, auks, sand larks , sand plovers, terns. </i> <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/306</i>)

>  The noble white crane and blue heron are occasionally found ... <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,31</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 72. ARDEA SACRA. Gml. Blue Heron. Matuku <i> A. Matook </i>. GRAY <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,27</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ARDEA SACRA. (BLUE HERON.) ... {<i> Native names </i>. Matuku-tai, Matuku-nuia, and Matukutuku.} [229] The Blue Heron is not confined to New Zealand, but is found along the whole of the Australian coasts and throughout the Polynesian archipelago; ... <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,228, 229, 2/129</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> There is a smaller species of bittern, a blue heron, and possibly others of the family, all known under the common name of matuku. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/221</i>)

>  71. <i> Ardea sacra </i>, Gml. Blue, Purple, or Reef heron, Matuku. - May be observed alighting on reefs immediately on the ebb of the tide so that the birds stand in the water. <i>(Nov 1885,NZ Journal of Science,557</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  ARDEA SACRA, <i> Gml.</i> - Blue Heron. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/126</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  <b>PASCOE</b> The Blue Crane or Heron. <i>(1957,TrNZI,247</i>) [NOTES: ca1899 Douglas Birds South Westland in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 247]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i> Matuku, ... 2. Demiegretta sacra, blue heron </i>; a shore bird. <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,227</i>)

>  Along the western shore of Wellington harbour there lives ... a colony of blue herons. <i>(Aug 21, 1921,Quick March,25</i>)

>  <i>Demigretta matook </i> Vieill. New Zealand Blue Heron. On rocky coasts the blue heron is generally distributed in both Islands. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/84</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <b>HOWARD</b> [Stewart Island birds include] the fern-bird (<i> Bowdleria fulva </i>), the bell-bird (<i> Anthornis melanura </i>), South Island Robin (<i> Miro Albifrons </i>), Tui (<i> Prosthemadera novae zealandiae </i>), Kingfisher (<i> Halcyon vagans </i>), Yellow-fronted parakeet (<i> Cyanorhampus auriceps </i>), Red-headed parrakeet (<i> Cyanorhampus novae zealandiae </i>), Kaka (<i> Nestor meridionalis </i>); and in the second class:Orange-wattled crow (<i> Glaucopis cinerea </i>), Blue Heron (<i> Demiegretta sacra </i>), Weka (<i> Ocydromus </i>) and Kiwi (<i> Apteryx </i>). <i>(1940,Rakiura,323</i>)

>  <i> matuku, two birds, 1, Botaurus poeciloptilus, bittern, and 2, Demigretta sacra, blue-heron; the Maori distinguished these two according to their habitat: 1, matuku-hurepo (swamp matuku) and 2, matuku-moana (sea-matuku). When the Pakeha mentions the matuku he always refers to the bittern; the other he always calls blue-heron. </i> <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/153</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The reef heron or blue heron <i>(E[gretta]. sacra sacra, ...) </i> <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,270</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i> Matuku, n. ... 2. Demigretta matook, blue heron; a shore bird. = matuku-moana ... m&amacr.tukutuku. ... 1. Reef heron (blue heron). = matuku, 2.: a wading bird with long legs, neck and beak. </i> <i>(1971,Dictionary of the Maori Language,195</i>)

>  <i> Reef Heron Egretta sacra sacra </i>(GMELIN, 1789) ... OTHER NAMES: <i> Blue reef heron, eastern reef heron, blue crane, blue heron, matuku-moana. </i>... Reef herons in New Zealand are at the southeastern limit of their mostly tropical range, and for that reason alone may never be common. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,132</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BLUE REEF HERON <i>Egretta sacra </i> Other names: Blue Crane, Matuku-moana. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,78</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

### Blue Magic
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also Blue Stuff](../A#also-blue-stuff))

>  [His lawyer] opened proceedings by dispelling media speculations his client had used blue magic. <i>(Aug 20, 2004,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,15</i>)

>  [He] said HRNZ was committed to a review of the events of the Blue Magic scandal... Incredibly, the saga might have been averted had an early opportunity to crack down on Blue Magic not been lost. <i>(Nov 21, 2004,Sunday Times (NZ),A10</i>)

>  "There is no evidence this trainer has used blue magic. And there are other ways horses can get propantheline - it's common in stomach ulcer drugs." ... >  [Hdg] More positive blue magic tests. Southland pacer Whodunnit has returned two positive swabs for xanthone-9-carboxylic acid in a new twist to the blue magic case... The blue magic drug is a blue liquid believed to contain propantheline bromide. <i>(Jun 7, 2004,Dominion,A11</i>)

### blue moki
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Among the 12 better-managed species at the top of the amber category there are only two - blue moki and blue cod - that I often buy... In the case of blue moki, although the flavour and texture is the equal of tarakihi or snapper, the flesh is threaded with fine dark veins, which makes it less appealing on the plate. <i>(Jun 26, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  [species name] moki .. [scientific name] <i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i> .. [other name(s)] blue moki <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> [Trumpeters are] a family (Latrididae) which contains several species of Australasian fishes closely related and very similar to the morwongs. The Maori name `moki' is given to members of both families. <i>Blue moki Latridopsis ciliaris </i>... Also reported from southern Australia, although this may be a confusion with `bastard trumpeters'. Often simply called moki in New Zealand. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,105</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BLUE MOKI</i> (Moki) <i>Latridopsis ciliaris </i>... The blue moki is a large bottom-living fish, common in southern New Zealand <i>(1982,Collins Guide,246,1984 ed.</i>)

### blue moon
 
 <i>n.</i> a butterfly

>  <b>GIBBS</b> Incidentally, it was Johannes Anderson (1924) writing in the <i>New Zealand School Journal</i> about a specimen of this butterfly which had been caught in Westland, who gave it our common name, saying `I have called it the `blue moon butterfly' because it is so rare; it is found only `once in a blue moon'. Besides it carries the blue moon on its wings, and it is like other blue moons - it is there all the time, but it is only when you are in certain positions that you see it.' The name has stuck and is certainly appropriate. The butterfly is known as the `common eggfly' in Australia. <i>(1980,NZ Butterflies</i>)

### blue mountain duck
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Blue mountain ducks are as tame almost as domestic ducks. <i>(Jun 8, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  <b>MACLEAN</b> Pigeon, weka, duck, and whio (blue mountain duck) are included in the <i>papa ... </i> <i>(Jul 16, 1849,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1849 July 16 Maclean Diary in 1940 Cowan Sir Donald Maclean 48]

>  I went five miles up the creek to get some blue mountain duck eggs and looking for more eggs and stray sheep. <i>(Oct 6, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,146,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Grey ducks, Paradise ducks, (which are rather a species of geese) teal, and widgeon frequent the streams. ... Blue mountain-duck is found in the more elevated regions. <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,31</i>)

>  The blue mountain duck, the whio of the Maoris, was more plentiful in the mountain streams. <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/355</i>) [NOTES: (Park)]

>  The <i>whio</i>, or `whistler'. generally called the blue mountain duck, is much more abundant in the South Island than the North ... <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/341</i>)

>  We saw little squadrons of `whio' the blue mountain duck ...; they called to each other in the whistling cry that has given them their Maori name ... <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,24</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Wood-pigeons (<i>kukupa</i>) and kaka parrots are found in the forests ..., and the handsome paradise-duck (<i>putangitangi</i>) and blue mountain duck (<i>whio</i>) are numerous in the streams and lakes [of Western Southern Alps]. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 64</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> On and around the [Central Otago] streams there were teal, grey duck, and the blue mountain duck, but the last-named was scarce. `I saw stilts, the black-fellows ... wading about ...' <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,120</i>)

>  <b>SEVERINSEN</b> A pair of blue mountain ducks squatted on the farther shore. <i>(1970,Hunt the Far Mountain,123</i>)

>  One bird that has not been noticed much by writers is the blue mountain duck... They are sometimes called the whistler. <i>(Apr 1, 1924,Otago Witness,71</i>)

### Blue Mountains
 
 <i>n.</i> in various uses: prob. orig. f. the Blue Mountains once the western boundary of NSW, but also in use in Otago

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> The blue mountains steep as plummets/ Rise abruptly from the plain,/ <i>(1883,Castle Gay,107</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> [At Port Stevens, NSW] high grounds, known by the name of the Blue Mountains. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/232 ... with the exception of the Blue Mountains, which extending from sea to sea, in the shape of a semi-circle, have, until a very late period, formed the boundary of the colony [of NSW]. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/221</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> There was. indeed, a small rush to what were termed [in Otago] the Blue Mountains in the early part of 1862.] <i>(1863,Report</i>) [NOTES: 1863 V. Pyke's Report in 1887 Handbook NZ Mines 9]

>  <i>Beyond the Blue Mountains </i>Mr Lange is right when he says that New Zealand could not regard its interest in Australia's security as limited by the Blue Mountains [i.e. it embraces West Australia to NZ]. <i>(Aug 23, 1988,Press, The,20</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> Pat had a wonderfully young heart. He entered into our pleasures with as much zest as we did ourselves. I played a game which had no ending or beginning, but was called `Beyond the Blue Mountains'. The scene was generally placed near the rhubarb beds, and Pat officiated as the villain, the hero, and even the villainess, with unfailing charm. ] <i>(1899,Stories,7,1984 ed.</i>)

### blue pearl
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) a cultured pearl derived from paua

>  Blue Pearls are unique to New Zealand... Eyris established the first ocean based blue pearl farm in the Chatham Islands, followed by farms at Akaroa. <i>(Oct 3, 2001,Capital Times,7</i>)

>  It's a story of the vision of New Zealander Roger Beattie who, in 1989, established the first ocean based blue pearl farm at Whangamoe Inlet on New Zealand's Chatham Islands... Although most of the nucleation process is kept a secret, the Eyris Blue Pearl Company are continuing to refine the art of attaching a seed to the inside of the shell, beneath the mantle, so that a blue pearl mabe can develop... Once the seed is inserted beneath the mantle, the paua begins to overlay this nucleus with its brilliant mother-of-pearl, made up of layer upon layer of conchiolin and nacre. <i>(Oct 4, 2002,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: from www.bluepearls.com]

>  Nicki's husband, Roger, farms paua and cultures blue pearls at marine farms in various parts of New Zealand, including Akaroa Harbour, <i>(Sep 2002,Cuisine,No. 94: 52</i>)

### blue pointer shark
 
 <i>n.</i> [term not specific to NZ]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> TIGER SHARK] TIGER SHARK (<i>Lamna glauca</i>). ... In New South Wales it is called the blue pointer, and, as Mr. Hill tells us, is `not only a very powerful, but perhaps the most bold of any of the large sharks visiting our shores. ... [118] Although there is some doubt on the subject, <i>Lamna glauca</i> is considered to be the mako of the Maoris, which is mainly found in the neighbourhood of Mayor Island. ... Mango, it should be remembered, seems [119] almost a generic name for shark in the North, as there is the mango-taniwha, a large variety; the mango-pare, the hammerheaded shark; the mango-reremai, the common dog-fish; the mango-pekepeke, and the mango-aruroa. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,117</i>)

>  [caption] Blue pointer, or mako, <i>Isurus glaucus.</i> <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/269</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Isurus glaucus</i> (Mueller and Henle). BLUE-POINTER; MAKO. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,8,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Isurus glaucus</i>: Blue pointer, mako. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,71</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> GREAT BLUE SHARK ... <i>Other names: Prionace glauca</i>; blue shark, blue whaler shark, blue pointer; mango-pounamu (Maori). <i>Ibid. </i> 184 MAKO SHARK ... <i>Other names: Isurus oxyrhinchus</i>; sharp-nosed mackerel shark, blue pointer. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,178</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> [=MAKO] MAKO SHARK [<i>Isurus oxyrhinchus</i>] ... Other names are sharp-nosed mackerel shark and blue pointer. Widespread in all tropical to cool temperate oceans. .. GREAT BLUE SHARK [<i>Prionace glauca</i>] ... Other names include blue whaler shark, blue-pointer, and mango-pounamu (Maori) <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,10</i>)

>  <i>blue pointer n.</i> voracious shark with a bluish back. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> BLUE-POINTER: <i>see Mako Shark</i> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,225</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>MAKO SHARK Isurus oxyrinchus</i> (Blue pointer, mackerel shark, bonito shark) <i>Ibid.</i> 59 <i>BLUE SHARK Prionace glauca</i> (Oceanic blue shark, blue pointer, blue whaler) <i>(1982,Collins Guide,51,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> <i>blue pointer n.</i> a large shark of Australian coastal waters, having a blue back and pointed snout. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,117</i>) [NOTES: [NZPOD 76]]

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Blue shark Prionace glauca Alternatively blue pointer, blue whaler. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,26</i>)

Memoir,No 4: 25/6</i>) [NOTES: (Modriniak and Marsden)]

### blue shag
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a Stewart Island variety similar to the spotted shag

>  <b>ADAMS</b> Another species, locally called the `blue shag', easily shot, and pretty highly esteemed by the sailors as an article of food. <i>(1874,25 Years of Emigrant Life,130</i>)

>  The following is a list of the birds shot ... <i>Querquedula gibberifrons</i>, little teal; <i>Anas superciliosa,</i> grey duck; ... <i>Graculus carbo</i>, cormorant or black shag; <i>Graculus varius,</i> pied shag; <i>Graculus brevirostris,</i> white throated shag; also another species of shag, locally called the `blue shag' ... pretty highly esteemed by the sailors as an article of food; <i>(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39</i>) [NOTES: (Black Stewart Island)]

>  SPOTTED SHAG (<i>Stictocarbo punctatus</i>). ... This is the `blue shag' of the inhabitants [of Stewart Island]. <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,8/326</i>) [NOTES: (Oliver)]

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BLUE SHAG <i>Stictocarbo steadi</i> Probably best treated as a subspecies of the Spotted Shag, the Blue Shag is nevertheless distinguishable by being slightly dark in tone of plumage, ... <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,73</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> [The spotted shag's] close relative the blue shag (<i>P[halacrocorax] p[unctatus] steadi</i> . . .) is limited to Stewart Island and thereabouts. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,260</i>)

>  <i>86.b. Stictocarbo punctatus steadi</i> (Oliver, 1930) <i>Blue Shag </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,33,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> These cormorants were almost certainly the <i>blue shags,</i> which have rare northern breeding colonies both here and at Twelve Mile Bluff. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,180</i>)

>  <i>Spotted Shag Stictocarbo punctatus </i>(SPARRMAN, 1786) ... <i>Spotted Shag S. p. punctatus</i> (SPARRMAN, 1786). <i>Blue Shag S.p. steadi</i> (OLIVER, 1930). ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Parekareka, ocean shag, crested shag, flip-flap, blue shag. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,125</i>)

### blue shark
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a common shark, vivid blue above, white below; also called blue pointer, blue whaler

 <i>n.</i> the stationery stores of the retail chain 'the Warehouse' [cf. Red Shed]

### blue shirt
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) as part of the early bush costume; cf; Morris

>  <b>BAINES</b> <i>Bush fashion</i>. [page heading] ... He [the Northland sawmiller] wore what is called a `blue shirt', that is, a sailor's shirt of serge, fitting very closely, and a pair of moleskin trousers, very much torn ... from walking through harsh fern and `tea-tree' scrub, ... and a wideawake hat ... <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,39</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> `Let the `blue shirts' and `cabbage-trees' have fair play', [the diggers] demanded. They saw themselves as poor men in their blue shirts, for some years a symbol of the radical working man in early Canterbury as well as Australia, and cabbage-tree hats <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,153</i>) [NOTES: [?What is GWT'S contemporary authority for the following; is he quoting or commenting?]]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Bluey</i> ... (1) A blue blanket ... -->(2) In the wet wildernesses of Western Tasmania a rough shirt or blouse is made of this material, and is worn over the coat like an English smock-frock. Sailors and fishermen in England call it a `Baltic shirt.' <i>(1898,Austral English,39</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) title of a popular post-war song by Ruru Karaitiana, sung by Pixie Williams

 <i>n.</i>
