### Blue Stuff
 
 <i>n.</i> a drug reputedly used in harness racing (See [also Blue Magic](../A#also-blue-magic))

>  A sample of Blue Stufff analysed at harness racing's drug laboratory in Auckland had been found to contain a broncho-dilator not previously detected here.... Rennell said HRNZ was already conducting its own probe into Blue Stuff, when it became apparent their investigations were crossing the paths of local police. <i>(May 9, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B16</i>)

>  While Harness Racing NZ chases round the country looking for the ridiculously named Blue Stuff, four trainers named have been tarnished with the unsaid "druggie" label... The substance is not illegal under New Zealand law but is believed to be performance-enhancing in horses and therefore banned in racing.... So armed with all this, some bright spark in Australia has decided to call it Blue Stuff - not HorsePower or Super Horse, or even methoxyxchlororidepanoprophren.You can imagine the confusion when there's a pre-dawn bang on the door and the coppers are yelling through the letterbox: "We're looking for illegal Blue Stuff." <i>(May 12, 2004,Capital Times,14</i>)

### blue suit/blues
 
 <i>n.</i> hospital clothing

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> The neat blue bundle was placed beside each patient and contained pyjamas, towel, face-cloth and the famous white shirt, blue pants and scarlet tie which were to be worn when the patient was officially allowed up. How they longed to be well enough to wear those "blues". <i>(1945,Away from Home,10</i>)

### blue top
 
 <i>n.</i> vervain

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> VERVAIN (<i>Verbena officinalis</i>), also called blue top, griffin weed, and missionary weed, is a slender tough-stemmed perenial 1 to 2 ft high, with a few opposite rough, cleft leaves, and small lilac flowers, ... It is becoming very common in the country from Wairoa to Gisborne [and is also called recorded from Christchurch]. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,146</i>)

### blue tussock
 
 <i>n.</i> a native fodder grass of high quality, now rare.

>  There was a fair area of yellow tussock on the northern end of the run ... Blue tussock was not plentiful except on some of the spurs running down to the Waihao ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,246,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> ... a flock of Merinos, well in the thousands, grazed on the blue tussock danthonia and other indigenous grasses, rising to ... the Southern Alps ... <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,8</i>)

>  <b>WALLACE</b> The BLUE-TUSSOCK [bold] (<i>Triticum scabrum</i> R. Br.), a grass of superior quality, has been mostly eaten out of the South Island of New Zealand. ... <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,296</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Poa colensoi (See [tussock](../T#tussock))

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Poa Colensoi</i> blue-tussock <i>(1908,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Botanical Survey Tongariro National Park]

>  <i>Poa Colensoi</i> blue-tussock <i>(1909,AJHR,C-11</i>) [NOTES: (And) Turner Botanical Examination Higher Waimarino]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> The blue-tussock (<i>Poa Colensoi</i>) forms much smaller tussocks than either of the above ... <i>(1910,NZ Plants,87</i>)

>  <i>Poa Colensoi</i> blue-tussock <i>(1915,AJHR,C-6</i>) [NOTES: (And). Cockayne Plant Covering Kennedy's Bush (Canterbury)]

>  <i>Poa Colensoi</i> blue-tussock <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>LEVY</b> [Table 1] Blue tussock <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,88,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>242</b> blue tussock n : a valuable grazing grass (<i>Poa colensoi</i>) native to New Zealand <i>(1961,Webster's New International Dictionary</i>)

>  <i>blue tussock [s] Poa colensoi </i>Hook. f. <i>Ibid.</i> 81 <i>tussock, blue [s] Poa colensoi </i>Hook.f. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,6</i>)

>  <b>MARK & ADAMS</b> <i>Poa colensoi</i>(Hook. f.) ... BLUE TUSSOCK ... One of the most common grasses throughout the alpine zone ... . <i>(1973,NZ Alpine Plants,258</i>)

>  <b>WHEELER</b> Unless you walk over this country [Central Otago] you'll miss the native grasses, like danthonia and the blue tussock. To a Merino, one mouthful of this hard feed [13] is worth 10 of soft greenfeed. <i>(1982,Historic Sheep Stations of NZ,12</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Short tussock grassland ... is characterised by the light brown tufts ... of hard tussock (<i>Festuca novae-zelandiae</i>) together with silver ussock (<i>Poa cita = P. caespitosa</i>), blue tussock (<i>Poa colensoi</i>) and blue wheat grass (<i>Elymus rectisetus</i>). <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,149</i>)

>  Blue tussock (<i>P. colensoi</i>) is a favoured food plant for many insects but has been much reduced in abundance by grazing animals, which also seek it out. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/43</i>)

>  One of the effects of the disappearance of the rabbit in the far back is the increase spread of the blue tussock. <i>(Dec 18, 1912,Otago Witness,33</i>)

>  Less grand and altogether less fortunate are the short tussocks. Growing up to half a metre in height, they include such hardy species as blue tussock (<i>Poa colensoi</i>), silver tussock (<i>P. cita</i>) and hard, or fescue, tussock (<i>Festuca novaezelandiae.</i>). <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/38</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a native tussock grass, Poa colensoi or poa intermedia.

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFIELD</b> Blue tussock (<i>Poa colensoi</i> and <i>Poa intermedia</i>) grow about 8 inches high with bluish narrow leaves. The only palatable tussocks are, (1) the blue tussocks which are easily killed by continuous burning and by continuous close grazing.. <i>(1961,Agriculture,272</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> blue tussock: small, now also rather rare, native tussock (poa colensoi) which is sometimes incorrectly called silver tussock. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,8</i>)

>  I think that taxation should occur in proportion to the amount of unimproved land a man possesses - thus 5s per acre is not too great a tax on the property which carries blue tussock and wild Irishman, while 6d per acre is sufficient for the paddock carrying four sheep to the acre per annum, laid down with Poverty Bay rye grass. <i>(Nov 25, 1873,Bruce Herald,6</i>)

>  The configuration of the land is undulating, and all ploughable, save two or three hundred acres of nicely grassed spurs; in fact, the property is said to be one of good blue tussock in its maiden state. <i>(Oct 14, 1893,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  From the Gorge Creek to Gow's Creek, right back to the main range, the alpine country will in summer time carry a sheep to the acre, and this upon the best fattening native grasses and herbs, blue tussock, soft speargrass, aniseed, and cotton plant. <i>(Oct 21, 1882,Otago Witness,13</i>)

### blue vein
 
 <i>n.</i> a cheese

>  Ferndale Dairies, of Eltham, have been producing blue cheese since 1951. That first classic, simply labelled Blue Vein, is still a big seller for the company. <i>(Oct 1991,Cuisine,29/1</i>)

>  Of established blues, the best in the class was Mainland's Galaxy Blue Vein, which is the archetypical New Zealand cow milk blue, with a powerful salty taste and moist body. <i>(Mar 30, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  Most astounding of all is the belated recognition of Galaxy Blue Vein., which has been made to the same recipe since 1951, and is one of the strongest-tasting blue cheeses in the world. <i>(May 15, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Blue vein belongs to that family of cheeses which in England is called stilton, in France roquefort, in Italy gorgonzola, and in Denmark Danish blue. New Zealand blue vein is recognised as a blue cheese in its own right and can be distinguished from its Continental counterparts by the deeper yellow colour of its curd. This arises from a difference in milk colour. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,51</i>)

### blue-lips
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori (See [cf. blueskin](../B#blueskin),[blue-tongue](../B#blue-tongue))

Where the tattooing is confined to the slender lyre-shaped line on the fair one's chin, the effect is really not unpleasing; but the tattooed lip is an abomination. <i>(Dec 27, 1864,Ride Through Disturbed Districts</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Dec 27 Meade Ride through Disturbed Districts in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 441]

>  <b>LYSNAR</b> For the young women this ornamentation [moko] was limited to the lips, and the name `blue-lips' was given to them by the English. Red lips were looked upon as a reproach or disfigurement. <i>(1924,New Zealand (various authors/titles),72</i>)

 <i>v.</i>

Burrows had a little quiet conversation with Grant as a result of what that individual was blue-papered to appear in the S.M. court on a charge ... <i>(Jul 24, 1909,NZ Truth,213/6/2</i>)

### blue-tongue
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a Maori [ca1960's-70s Herb Wilford heard this in Wakatu Freezing Works, Hastings, as an offensive term for a Maori.] (See [cf. blue-lips](../B#blue-lips),[blueskin](../B#blueskin))



 
 <i>n.</i> a rouseabout

>  A rouseabout - also variously known as a rousie, woolhandler, shedhand, fleeco, the person `picking up' or a blue tongue - is responsible for the passage of wool after being shorn from the sheep's back to the wool press where it is baled. <i>(Mar 1988,More Magazine,31</i>)

### blue-water title
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Blue water title may be another term that is not used, or little used, outside New Zealand. NSOD does not record it. nor does DNZE. Perhaps a popular rather than a lawyers' term, it refers to a piece of privately owned land that extends below high water mark, an unusual but not unknown occurrence. <i>(Jan 1999,NZWords,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> title to land that extends below high-water mark, the usual limit of privately-owned land. [Jim Cameron writes: This is perhaps a popular rather than a lawyer's term, analogous to <i>paper road</i> and <i>Queen's chain</i>. Finding a reference has been elusive. I saw it in writing several times a few years ago, but cannot recall where.]

### blue-wattled crow
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The kokako, or blue wattle crow, is a threatened species and is bred in captivity at Mt Bruce. <i>(Mar 31, 2001,Dominion,8</i>)

>  Sir James Hector, in drawing attention.. to a very fine specimen of the New Zealand crow or jay. as it should more properly be called, made interesting references to the complete isolation of the orange wattle crow, in the South Island, and the blue wattle crow in the North Island. <i>(Dec 21, 1897,Otago Witness,10</i>)

>  The blue-wattled crow is of a dark bluish-grey colour, with the lower part of the back and abdomen tinged with rufous-brown. <i>(Apr 17, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Environment) The two spp. also attract the occas. names <i>C. wilsoni</i>, blue (-wattled), North Island crow; <i>C. cinerea, </i>orange-wattled, South Island, (occas. early) yellow-wattled crow. (See [see also kokako](../S#see-also-kokako))

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 42. GLAUCOPIS WILSONI. Bp. Blue-wattled Crow. Kokako. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,16</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> GLAUCOPIS WILSONI. (BLUE-WATTLED CROW.) ... <i>Native name</i>.- Kokako <i>(1883,Birds of New Zealand,153,{1888 2nd edn I/1} ed.</i>)

>  <i>Glaucopis wilsoni</i>. - Kokako, Blue-wattled Crow. North Island. Very irregular in distribution. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/112</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  I have paid special attention to the nests of three birds- the Native Canary (<i>Orthonyx ochrocephala</i>), the Robin (<i>Myiomoira albifrons</i>), and the Tomtit (<i>Myiomoira macropcephala</i>). Sir Walter Buller says ... that the Blue Crow (<i>Glaucopis cinerea</i>) lays a single egg, and ... leaves the care of the chick to the to the Popokatea, or Canary (<i>Orthonyx albicilla</i>). <i>(1904,TrNZI,36/119</i>)

>  <b>MYERS</b> <i>Callaeas wilsoni</i> Bonaparte. Blue-wattled Crow. This beautiful species supplies one of the greatest surprises of the investigation. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/97</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Thus I learnt that the Blue Wattled Crow (<i>Glaucopis Wilsoni</i>), ... was at one time common also on Tutira. <i>(1926,Tutira,113</i>)

>  <i>ko[o]kako</i>, two birds (Callaeas wilsoni - North Island, and C. cinereous[sic] - South Island), blue-wattled crow and orange-wattled crow, the colour of the wattles being the only difference; nearly if not quite extinct: this is the bird to which the echoic name bell-bird was first applied. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/144</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>POWELL</b> BLUE-WATTLED CROW (<i>Callaeas wilsoni</i>). A moderately large dark bluish grey bird with a stout black bill, ... and conspicuous wattles of bright blue. The South Island species, <i>C. cinerea</i>, is almost identical except for the wattles, which are orange ... The Orange-wattled crow is still plentiful ... The Maori name for both <i>species </i>is Kokako. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,87</i>)

>  <i>blue-wattled crow n</i> : a passerine bird (<i>Callaeas wilsoni</i>) of New Zealand that resembles a starling <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,242</i>)

>  <i>Kokako Callaeas cinerea</i> (GMELIN, 1788) ... <i>North Island Kokako C.c. wilsoni</i> (BONAPARTE, 1851).<i> South Island Kokako C.c. cinerea </i>(GMELIN, 1788). OTHER NAMES: <i>Blue wattled crow, blue gill, orange wattled crow, organ bird. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,303</i>)

(c) Other common names: (i) Skipjack; Striped Bonito (ii) Bluefin Tuna; Southern Bluefin; Southern Tuna (iii) Yellowfin All sometimes referred to as Tunny. (d) Principal localities where taken: (i) A summewr visitor to New Zealand ... (ii) Southern Bluefin Tuna is a large species of temperate Australasian seas and migrates south to Foveaux Strait in summer months. <b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>GROPER</i> (two species) (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Polyprion oxygeneios</i> (ii) <i>Polyprion moeone</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) New Zealand Groper; Hapuku (ii) Bass. (c) Other common names: (i) Hapuka, Stone-eye, Blue-nose, Bonita (ii) Deepsea Groper. (d) Principal localities where taken: [Mainly easten coasts, Foveaux, and Chathams] ... [27 Groper and Bass] should not be confused with the Australian and tropical groupers which they resemble superficially. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,26</i>)

### Blues
 
 <i>n.</i> a Super rugby franchise based in Auckland (also in sing. a member of the Blues team)

>  Both the Blues and the Hurricanes are heavily dominated by Polynesian players and their performances tend to reflect that. <i>(May 7, 2004,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,2</i>)

### blues
 
 <i>n.</i> (the blues) spec. alcoholic depression; DTs

>  <b>THOMSON</b> There is no keeping him from [drink] as long as there is a <i>shot in the locker</i> ... I have seen him, when he is in the blues run up the gully as if he had a thousand devils after him. <i>Ibid.</i> 51 He said he had been [on the spree] for three weeks, and did not expect <i>the blues</i> for a fortnight yet. <i>(1867,Rambles with a Philosopher,50</i>)

### blueskin
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] blueskin 2053 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> early whalers' nickname for a tattooed Maori (See [cf. blue-lips](../B#blue-lips),[blue-tongue](../B#blue-tongue))

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> I do not know Tommy Roundhead's Maori name, but there was a heavily tattooed Maori known to the whalers as Blueskin, and Blueskin Bay at Waitati was named after him, although Blueskin Beach at Goodwood was named because of a plant with berries with a blue skin. They were small and good to eat and were numerous there. The old chief at Blueskin Bay was very blue in his tattoo; ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,22</i>)

### bluestone
 
 <i>n.</i> a volcanic rock used in buildings, particularly in Otago; a basaltic rock used for road metal and (esp. rubble) building material (cf greenstone).

>  Perhaps most truly characteristic of the city [sc. Dunedin] is the "bluestone". a class of volcanic rock used for the basements of main buildings (Knox, Bishopscourt), coarsely hammer-dressed because it was too hard for fine dressing. <i>(Dec 10, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>) [NOTES: See also Knight & Wales : <i>Buildings of Dunedin</i> 1988]

>  <b>VOGEL</b> dolorites (bluestones) <i>(1875,Official Handbook of NZ,133</i>)

>  <i>Bluestone</i>, which is so largely used for road metal ... is to be found in almost all districts that have been disturbed by volcanic agencies. ... The basements of nine-tenths of the buildings in Dunedin are built of bluestone rubble ... <i>(1876,TrNZI,8/134</i>) [NOTES: (Blair Building Materials Otago)]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> Basalts, locally called `bluestone', occur of a quality useful for road metal etc. <i>(1879,Handbook of New Zealand,46</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> Basalts, locally called `blue-stones', occur of a quality useful for road-metal, house-blocks, and ordinary rubble masonry. <i>(1886,Handbook of New Zealand,58,(4th edn) ed.</i>)

>  Basalts, locally called `bluestones', occur of a quality useful for road-metal, house-blocks, and ordinary rubble masonry. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,195</i>)

>  There is abundance of good building stone [in South Canterbury], chiefly bluestone (dolomite), and white stone something like the famous Oamaru stone, ... <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,129</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> [At Macraes Flat, Central Otago] the old cock [carved in stone] is still there, stretching its neck to breaking point against the bluestone wall of Stanley's Hotel. <i>(1944,Pure Gold,16</i>)

>  <i>blue</i> ... blue'stone n. a durable basaltic rock used for buildings, road-making, etc. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

>  <i>bluestone n.</i> dark building stone a basalt, common in Victoria and New Zealand. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),611</i>)

>  <i>bluestone n.</i> dark building stone, a basalt, common in Victoria and N.Z. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> The installation of plumbing in the bluestone fortress [Mt Eden prison] would cost at fortune. <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,14</i>)

>  <i>bluestone n. NZ & Austral</i>. basalt used for building, roads, etc. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,76</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 117]]

>  BLUE STONE: A term correctly applied to basaltic rock and loosely applied to any similar volcanic stone. <i>(Apr 27, 1951,Draft for Code of Building Terminology D,8/</i>)

>  Who would have thought Richard Turnbull's bluestone store and hall would become a first class theatre. <i>Ibid.</i> 20 In 1986 within thirteen kilometres of Timaru there were fifteen stone arch bridges and over fifty culverts built of local bluestone basalt rock. <i>(May 1994,NZ Historic Places,47/4</i>)

>  The magnificent bluestone Bank of New Zealand has been replaced with a 1960s building... <i>(May 1996,NZ Historic Places,58/3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to treat sheep footrot in a bluestone footbath.

>  Got in 32 sheep from reserve. One dead one. Plucked him, footrotted and bluestoned 8. <i>(Jul 1, 1893,Brabazon Diaries</i>)

### bluestone whisky
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The unscrupulous brewer may sell his decoction of glucose dignified by the name of beer, and poison people with bluestone whisky. <i>(Apr 16, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,21</i>)

### bluestoning
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the treatment of wheat seed with bluestone.

>  Bluestone is the most commonly used dressing [for wheat seed]. During the last two years some farmers have given up bluestoning under the impression that it was not necessary. <i>(May 1909,NZ Farmer,353</i>)

### bluestriped red mullet
 
 <i>n.</i> -

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] bluestriped red mullet 1476 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### Bluesville
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) Auckland

>  Troy Flavell's back in the frame for this weekend. Some serious head-scratching lies ahead in Bluesville. <i>(Feb 20, 2006,Dominion Post,C3</i>)

### bluey
 
 <i>n.</i> a swagger's pack; 1. See swag (from colour of blanket wrapping a rolled swag). 2. (informal) a summons to court, especially a traffic offence notice (from former colour of paper). 3. (plural) workmen's blue overalls. 4. (nickname for) a red-headed person. =swag.

>  <b>[WICKHAM]</b> In other countries tramps have a language of their own. Our 'blueys," however, are only in the rudimentary sign stage. <i>(1897,Casual Ramblings,87</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> Nickname for a swag common in the `Nineties'. In those days most station blankets were blue (earlier still, I believe, they were red.) <i>(1933,Sheep Station,Glossary</i>)

>  <b>O'REGAN</b> You can fancy, old mate, now I'm longing .../ To flee with `billy' and `bluey.' <i>(1896,Poems (various authors),29</i>)

>  [Quoting Taihape Post] He was absolutely penniless, and did not possess even the rudest `bluey.' <i>(Sep 9, 1905,NZ Truth,12/6/4</i>)

>  [Referring to <i>Maoriland Memories</i>] We are told ... that each [West Coast prospector in 1860] carried his swag of provisions, with the necessary `bluey' and `matilda.' <i>(May 26, 1906,NZ Truth,49/7/4</i>)

>  Therefore, Woollcombe, get thee hence; go home thou wowser on the wallaby; hump bluey, carry Matilda, pad the hoof, thou parsonical howler, thou Wowseristic whaler Woollcombe. <i>(Nov 6, 1909,NZ Truth,228/1/3</i>)

>  Myself with a `bluey' hung on a broom. <i>(1916,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1916 Letter in 1939 Winter King Country 14]

>  He could roll his "bluey" and reach the spot where the work was thickest. <i>(Oct 4, 1913,NZ Observer,3</i>)

>  <i>Bluey</i>.- Swag. A nickname for a swag common in the 'nineties. In those days most station blankets were blue (earlier still, I believe, they were red). Hence <i>bluey up</i>-- carrying a swag. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>VAILE</b> (Glossary) <i>Bluey</i>: Colloquial name for a pack; a swag. <i>(1939,Pioneering the Pumice,229</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>Bluey</i>: bedroll, or swag. <i>(1947,Wandering on the Islands of Wonders,Gloss</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> BLUEY: A roll of blankets, so called from their colour. Carried by the man on the road looking for work. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,110</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> They greased their swag-traps, packed their `blueys' and took the road again. <i>(1956,Green Kiwi,210,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <i>blue </i>... -blu'ey n. a blue blanket in which the bushman wraps his belongings; a smock-shirt made of coarse material. -to hump one's bluey v.i. to go on the tramp carrying a pack on one's back. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

>  <i>bluey ...n</i>. orig. a blue blanket commonly used by swagmen for wrapping their possessions, now the swag (q.v) itself; a summons (N.Z.) <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  <i>~ n</i>. originally a blue blanket commonly used by swagmen for wrapping their possessions, now the swag (q.v) itself. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),611</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> If folks were reduced to walking and humping the bluey the least one could do was to give a lift, was his unvoiced attitude. <i>(1970,Life's Vagaries,73</i>)

>  <i>bluey n. Austral. inf. 1.</i> a blanket. <i>2</i>. a swagman's bundle.<i> 3. hump one's bluey</i>. to carry one's bundle; tramp. <i>4.</i> a variant of<i> blue</i> (sense 9). 5. a cattle dog. [(for senses 1, 2, 4) C19: < BLUE (on account of their colour) + -Y] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,117</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 76]]

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> He [the swagger] said to me, `Well, I'm going through to the coast, and if you like you can roll up your bluey and come along with me.' ... I had a light bedroll, and it was good weather at the time, so I was down to what swaggies call a `heatwave knot' - down to a very light bedroll. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,70</i>) [NOTES: (Bob Edwards, bushman)]

>  <b>FOSTON</b> ... his bluey on his back. <i>(1911,In the Bell Bird's Lair,43</i>)

>  He said that : `He did not care ... for anyone; he was there to govern, and govern he would, and that for two pins he would pick up his `bluie' and clear out [new Resident of Raratonga]. <i>(1911,Poo-Bah of the Pacific, The,2</i>)

>  With them have vanished the old pioneer types, the swagger with his "bluey". <i>(1914,History of North Otago,117</i>)

>  I packed up my "bluey" and made out to Whaterangi. <i>(1911,Kiwi on the Swag,14</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a parking-offence notice or ticket, =sticker (originally blue, but now other colours).

>  [In local criminal slang] [a] `bluey' is a summons, ... <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  The speed cop who gave me my last bluey. <i>(Mar 16, 1942,2NZEF Times,6</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> blue ?2, a mistake

>  <b>SUMMERS</b> `Oh, Dinah, did you see that frightful bluey in our ad?' ... `They got the captions under the blocks mixed. Look'. <i>(1964,Smoke and the Fire,88</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a sea-fish

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> <i>Hemerocoetes Waitei</i> (Regan) ...The above mentioned anglers and fishermen [of Port Chalmers] had various names to distinguish this fish from Opal-fish - which are as follows: Bluey, Blue-cap, Blue-Bonnet, which I have used. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,317</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a nickname for a red-headed man

>  <b>PICARD</b> ... put my bag of tricks in the hall - haden't [sic] formed acquaintance with dear Matilda or Miss Bluey in them days - ... <i>(1906,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,7</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> `Bluey' Jameson next morning told him to fall in line with Dave Lester's squad ... <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,59</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bluey.</i> 5. A nickname for a red-headed man: from ca1890, esp. in Australia and New Zealand. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,71</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> Following are examples of colloquialisms common to New Zealand and Australian English, with their English or American equivalent ... <i>bluey</i>: redhead; also, a court summons <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),115</i>)

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> He ... had red hair and of course, he was called Bluey. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,123</i>)

>  <b>TUCKER</b> In our ranks at the time [c1940, Army Camp] was a very tough citizen by the name of Bluey Drysdale ... In addition he had a shock of curly red hair and an almost complete absence of front teeth, ... <i>(1978,Thoroughbreds Are My Life,16</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Fyffe was a typical redhead ... The Maoris called him Puni-whero (redhead). ... He was later nick-named Bluey. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,35</i>)

>  <i>bluey </i>... red-haired person. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,76</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
 <i>n.</i> a blue cod

>  For an hour we cruised south to Long Point, where we lifted crayfish pots and caught a few 'bluies' [blue cod]. <i>(Jul 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 64/39</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a notice of summons (to court: orig. printed on blue (laid) paper) (See [cf. blue; blue paper](../B#blue;-blue-paper))

>  The hundreds of "blueys" which summoned guests to the Law Ball. resulted in.. a nice variety of 'finely-worded replies. <i>(Dec 13, 1947,Sports Post,9</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> I'll show you walls papered with blueys, and cracks in them closed up with scrip. <i>Ibid.</i> 48 HOW THEY SERVED THE `BLUEYS'. [title] <i>Ibid</i> 49 How they served the `blueys.' <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,13</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bluey</i>. 2. In New Zealand 20C., a summons. Ex the blue paper on which it comes. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,71</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> Following are examples of colloquialisms common to New Zealand and Australian English, with their English or American equivalent ... <i>bluey</i>: redhead; also, a court summons <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),115</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> A summons by the police: New Zealand and Australian: since ca1910: 1932, Nelson Baylis (private letter); Baker, 1942 with variant blue (as also Kylie Tennant, Foveaux, 1939). Ex the blue-paper form with which it is served. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,55,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HUTCHESON, BOWMAN & JOHNSON</b> Blue laid: Paper that is `legal' blue in colour and shows <i>Laid </i>watermarks. Gives its name to the ever-unwelcome `Bluey.' <i>(1938,Handbook (Printers'),Pt 2/5</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> 'Ickey. Oi 'ave 'ere a bluey for you to appear in the Warden's Court at Greymouth on the fourteenth inst.' `Much obliged, constable. Oi'll be there.' <i>(1984,Blackball 08,131</i>)

>  Pat says that in the 30s, a summons from the Magistrate's Court for a minor offence was always blue in colour and invariably called a "bluey". <i>(Jun 10, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> blue working overalls or trousers

>  Rank oil is not in sympathy with Gallienic tears,/ Though lovely on the blueys of our College Engineers. <i>(1923,College Rhymes,15,1901 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> And when Bob Withers came home, with his workbag of coal in one hand, and his dirty blueys in the other ... he kissed his wife ... and his overalls dropped on the floor and unrolled ... <i>(1957,Owls Do Cry,53,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> The girls were shown into Jim's [the hotel's hired hand] room, complete with razor strop ... and his old blueys hanging on the red rail. <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,117</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> ... we'd have a pair of dungarees - that's like the blue jeans they wear today - denims they call them but we used to call them blueys. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,29</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> ... in his work he didn't get covered with oil and coal on his blueys, .. <i>(1982,To the Is-Land,146,1984 ed.</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a warning letter [orig. on blue paper] to farmers to eliminate noxious weeds or face prosecution and fine.

>  <b>SWINBURN</b> Anybody growing noxious weeds would get a bluey written on blue paper which was virtually a summons. <i>(1999,Personal Communication,Letter 1 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> any sort of official summons or information

>  <b>FRAME</b> But all that was before the headmaster sent Mum a bluey saying action would be taken unless ... <i>(1951,Lagoon, The,116</i>) [NOTES: (`Treasure')]

>  After half a century, the era of the `bluey' is over. From now on, thanks to Dataprint, the Police Department's new [dollars]66,000 computerised traffic summons system, offending motorists will receive `whiteys.' The blue summons paper has been replaced by white computer paper. <i>(Dec 5, 1974,Sydney Herald</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a colonial bushman's smock shirt.

>  blue a. ... -blu'ey n. a blue blanket in which the bushman wraps his belongings; a smock-shirt made of coarse material. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Blue&#769;.y</i> (bl&umacr.&ybreve.), <i>n. 1. </i>A bushman's blanket; - so called from its color. <i>2</i>. A bushman's bundle; a swag; - so called because a blamket is sometimes used as the outside covering. <i>To hump bluey,</i> to carry a swag; hence, to travel about in the bush, esp. in search of work. <i>[Colloq.] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2018</i>)

>  <b>GLEN</b> `The hose!' shouted Uncle John. `Wet the blueys!' cried Uncle Stephen [to put out a grass fire on a Canterbury station]. <i>(1917,Six Little New Zealanders,84,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <i>bluey</i> The Australian name for the blue blanket usually carried by tramps. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),43,(8th edn) ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a suitcase; things packed for a journey; portsammy.

>  <b>THOMSON</b> `Come along with me to Southland,' said my old mate then to me,/ You'll be sure to get some yacker ...'/ I agreed. We packed our blueys, and together then we went/ By train to Invercargill .../ <i>(1909,Ballads about Business,91</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a publican's written notice banning a person from a hotel bar.

>  <i>`Bluey' ban for pests in bar-rooms </i>... Hotel patrons who misbehave in Christchurch hotels will soon be served with a `bluey' - a legal notice warning them to stay off the premises. <i>(Sep 11, 1987,Press, The,1</i>)

>  The sort of behaviour that had taken place was not and would not be tolerated, especially with the advent of `Blueys', the recently introduced banning notices for misbehaviour. <i>(Sep 21, 1987,Dominion,3</i>)

>  <i>Pub notice stops blues</i> [headline] The `Bluey,' - a legal notice aimed at keeping troublemakers out of pubs - has been an outstanding success ... The `Bluey' combines provisions of the Trespass and Sale of Liquor Acts. <i>(Sep 22, 1988,Evening Post,11</i>)

### bluey-humper
 
 <i>n.</i> a swagger (See [hump one's/the bluey](../H#hump-one's/the-bluey))

>  That is, if we wear slouchers,/ And boots a bit square-toed -/ It ain't for bluey-humpers/ To travel on his road./ <i>(1903,King Bill's Philosophy,28</i>)

### blueyed
 
 <i>adj.</i> Sc. swagged up

>  <b>FANNING</b> I was tramping with a mate ... to Westland, and it was our whim to be slouch-hatted, and `blueyed', `billied', unshaven, and otherwise orthodox swaggers. <i>(1910,Players and Slayers,81</i>)

### bluff
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... it would either go over a bluff or drop down from exhaustion. <i>(1907,TrNZI,39/74</i>)

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> Walked to the north headland of the bay, called the north bluff, about seven miles; and to the north bluff river, ... <i>(Apr 19, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Apr 19 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 208]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> A shoal ... extends from this towards the eastern Head [of Otakou inlet] - a steep round bluff - leaving a channel ... running close to the bluff, ... <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,9</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> A high bluff on the northern side ..., which cannot be passed by travellers except by climbing over its summit, was next examined. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,162</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> On the left side arose a succession of grassy bluffs surmounted by native `bush'. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,IV/iii/122</i>) [NOTES: (1889,1974)]

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Someone asked me the other day what I meant by a bluff. To us a bluff is any precipitous outcrop of rock or any steep broken face where the rock is bare and the fragments fall away below in screen [? =scree] and shingle slide. <i>(1951,High Country,23</i>)

>  At the first bluff he sidled up the spur but he got off the discs and had to bash through the lawyers and windfalls. <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to drive sheep over a bluff (see also bluffed).

>  <i>Bluff.</i>-Used in some parts as meaning, to drive sheep over a <i>b</i>., in others as meaning, `to get stuck in a <i>b.'</i> <i>(Jan 13, 1934,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss. additions]

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>bluff</i>. Used in some parts as meaning `to drive sheep over a bluff', in others as meaning `to get stuck in a bluff'. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,357</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> But we got through, though one man had bad luck bluffing twenty sheep. They all ran out on to a rocky ledge, and we could see that to go near them meant that they would follow each other - like sheep - over the edge. .h;.us; [cf. OED2a. 1 b. Of a shore or coast-line: `Presenting a bold and almost perpendicular front, rather rounded than cliffy in outline.' 1658.] <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,40</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [high country mustering] a sheer rocky vertical face, upon which sheep, dogs and musterers can be trapped.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> Someone asked me the other day what I meant by a bluff. To us a bluff is any precipitous outcrop of rock or any steep broken face where the rock is bare and the fragments fall away below in scree and shingle slide. <i>(1951,NZ High Country,23</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> The same dog on a hot shingle face in mid-summer is likely to go and lie under a bush instead of climbing a thousand feet to hunt a dozen woollies through a bluff. <i>(1951,NZ High Country,45</i>)

>  <b>JARDINE</b> She pulled too hard and nearly put them [the sheep] over a bluff. <i>(1978,Shadows on the Hill,7</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> bluff: as distinct from the Oxford English Dictionary's 'perpendicular headland', a musterer refers to a bluff as a similar sheer face in the back country. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,8</i>)

### bluff gully
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheer-sided valley dangerous to grazing stock.

>  <b>ACLAND</b> .. what looks from above a perfectly good spur, but really ends in two bluff gullies. <i>(1946,Early Canterbury Runs,144</i>)

### bluff off
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [high country] to slip over or end in a bluff.

>  <b>DOMINY</b> On the top the language is lateral again .. here 'the fences bluff off' <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,157</i>)

### Bluff oyster
 
 <i>n.</i> the dredge oyster of the Foveaux Strait, New Zealand's chief commercial oyster [from Bluff, the mainland home port of Foveaux oysterboats]; also called Foveaux Strait oyster, Stewart Island oyster

>  I can only dream of the exquisite taste of the champagne bluff oysters. <i>(Nov 1998,Cuisine,14</i>)

>  By the time we'd reached our destination, Ohawini, bluff oyster of the north, it was my considered opinion that there was and is a desperate need in our stressful society for the hiring of billboard space to accommodate the names of offending wimps and nerds. <i>(Feb 1989,North & South,20</i>)

>  The Bluff oyster, we believe, is our most noble heritage. It is the big band of oysters, insistently pounding its message to the senses. It is closely rivalled by the Nelson oyster which, you will understand if you know Nelson, is an oyster of a rather more culturally-conscious sort, more your chamber music ensemble oyster, seducing the senses with subtlety. <i>(Oct 20, 1999,Evening Post,26</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> BLUFF-OYSTER: The Bluff or Stewart Island Oyster is the gourmet's delight and its annual harvest in awaited throughout the country with much interest. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,171</i>)

>  [species name] oysters bluff .. [scientific name] <i>Tiostrea lutaria</i> .. [other name(s)] -- <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  The Bluff oyster has long been recognised as a delicacy in New Zealand. As early as the 1860s commercial harvesting began ... <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,60</i>)

>  The dredge oyster, Bluff oyster or Foveaux Strait oyster is by far the most popular in New Zealand. <i>(May 1985,Metro,47/28</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> Dredge oyster Tiostrea lutaria ... Alternatively Bluff, Foveaux Strait, or Stewart Island oyster. Native to New Zealand, with a similar species in Chile. <i>(May 13, 1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,166</i>)

>  No other oyster in the world comes within cooey of the Bluff oyster. <i>(May 13, 1988,Contact (Wgtn),2</i>)

>  Bluff oysters are the best in the world by a Southland mile. <i>(May 26, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  So when the Bluff oysters come in I've been hanging out for nine months, and the whitebait season has just kicked off so I'm thinking about that. <i>(Sep 3, 2006,Sunday Star Times,ESCAPE 3</i>)

>  Bluff oysters are identified as <i>ostrea angasi</i>, first cousin to the highly prized French belon oyster from Brittany. <i>Ostrea angasi</i> are also found in Tasmania and around the NSW coast, where they are known as Jervis Bay oysters, native oysters, mud oysters or flat oysters. <i>(Nov 2001,Cuisine,127</i>)

>  Bluff Oysters! Guaranteed Fresh, 3d per dozen. <i>(Jul 7, 1896,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Bluff Oysters .. Sold by all Storekeepers. <i>(Nov 25, 1903,West Coast Times,1</i>)

### bluff road
 
 <i>n.</i> a road cut out on the side of a bluff

>  <b>EDGE-PARTINGTON</b> The word `gorge' hardly defines it. It is a valley with most precipitous sides ... The road is what is called a bluff road, that is, it is cut out of the side of the precipice, no wider than was absolutely necessary for the coach. <i>(1883,Random Rot,325</i>)

### bluff weta
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Kaikoura ranges are the only place in New Zealand where 3 of the 11 recorded species of giant weta occur; the bluff weta, the scree weta,and the Kaikoura Giant weta. <i>(May 2008,Internet - see Notes,3</i>) [NOTES: www.kaikoura.govt.nz/docs/our%20services/Sustainable%20Development/May_09_biodiversity]

>  Three species of giant weta and an undescribed species of ground weta live in the Kaikoura ranges: the Kaikoura giant weta is restricted to South Marlborough; the bluff weta is found only in South Marlborough and at Mt Somers in mid-Canterbury; and the scree weta reaches its northeastern limit in the area. <i>(Aug 2002,Forest & Bird,305/34</i>)

### bluffed
 
 <i>adj.</i> lost, forced to retrace one's steps, be, get bluffed; to be or get held up by a bluff; be stopped or hindered by a bluff.

>  We traversed beyond the correct spur, were `bluffed', and retreated. <i>(1993,NZ Alpine Journal,V/20/177</i>)

>  <i>Bluff.</i>-Used in some parts as meaning, to drive sheep over a <i>b</i>., in others as meaning, `to get stuck in a <i>b.'</i> <i>(Jan 13, 1934,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss. additions]

>  <b>STRONACH</b> [musterers'] Sheep often get stuck in the cliffs - `bluffed' - and so do the dogs; and so, sometimes, do the men. <i>Ibid.</i> 38 It was alpine climbing and I found it difficult to keep the dogs from continually getting bluffed. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,24</i>)

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> Sheep drifted down ... seeking young green food about the terraces and often got `bluffed' on the almost sheer rock faces. <i>Ibid.</i> It wasn't until his sons grew old enough to be interested in `bluffed' sheep that be decided a bullet was the best ... answer to this problem. <i>(1965,Skippers Road,37</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> flummoxed; ?lost; ?beaten by circumstances [ca1940 Havelock That question's got me bluffed.]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> [?=lost] This was at the very time we were properly bluffed, out in that blizzard that afternoon. <i>(1978,Soldier Country,125</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of stock and musterers] stranded on a steep cliff shelf.

>  <b>DOMINY</b> - you can, for example, go uphill and still be bluffed at a gut as I was ... <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,172</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Sheep often get stuck in the cliffs - bluffed and so do the dogs; and so, sometimes, do the men. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,24</i>)

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> Sheep drifted down from their winter blocks seeking young green food about the terraces and often got 'bluffed' on the almost sheer rock faces .. It wasn't until his sons grew old enough to be interested in 'bluffed' sheep that he decided a bullet was the best and safest answer to this problem. <i>(1965,Beyond the Skippers Road,38</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR & TROTTER</b> I've got half a dozen sheep bluffed up there, just under the Remarkable Gap. <i>(1994,From Caithness to Southland,25</i>)

>  <b>DOMINY</b> Rather than risk being bluffed, most musterers will climb a thousand feet ... <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,171</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>adj.</i> characterised by bluffs; precipitous, steep

<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [high-country mustering] [of steep faces and shingle guts] sheer and dangerous.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The Esk at that particular spot carves its way through a deep bluffy gorge. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,82</i>)

### bo

### Boag
 
 <i>v.</i> (ref. is to Michelle Boag, sometime President of the National Party)

>  The list of those who have felt the cold hand of the president - or been "Boaged" as New Zealand First leader Winston Peters puts it - is growing. <i>(May 19, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C9</i>)

>  There's a new verb doing the rounds in Parliament: to boag. It means to chop the legs out from underneath, as in the deadwood Nats who were denied the chance to exit with dignity when National Party president Michelle Boag decided to clear space for new blood. The word most recently appeared in an e-mail suggesting Clark apologise to deadwood MPs for "the injustice and mental abuse served up to us by the National Party". If she does, the missive says, "then we can boag Boag." <i>(Jun 14, 2002,Dominion,8</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a Crown-and-Anchor board.

>  <b>WILSON</b> I squatted beside the piece of baize. The Maori did not twitch a muscle of his face. ... I said, `This is a raid. Fold up your board, Kiwi, for half an hour.' [WW2 troopship] [139] `All?' `All those running boards.' `Have you ever played crown and anchor?' <i>(1956,Sweet White Wine,138</i>)

>  He turned to the Maori. `You remember me, Joe? I've lost a few quid up at your place. On the board. On Saturday. Remember, eh?' <i>Ibid </i> 30 Joe's done me some good turns. He runs a crown-and-anchor game down in McLagan Street. <i>(1985,Horse,26</i>) [NOTES: [Dunedin pub] c1962 Baxter Horse (1985) (Name and the Game) 26]

>  He turned to the Maori. `You remember me, Joe? I've lost a few quid up at your place. On the board. On Saturday. Remember, eh?' <i>Ibid </i> 30 Joe's done me some good turns. He runs a crown-and-anchor game down in McLagan Street. <i>(1985,Horse,26</i>) [NOTES: [Dunedin pub] c1962 Baxter Horse (1985) (Name and the Game) 26]



 
 <i>n.</i> a shearing floor, a killing floor of a freezing works; the area in a woolshed used for shearing on; or the working floor of a freezing works; occas. the complement of shearers at work, cf all aboard, clear the board, shearing board (usu. constr. with the, and sometimes pl the boards).

>  This doyen of shearers.. has put in forty years on the shearing board and is still going strong. <i>(Jan 20, 1912,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

>  Some men are good pen-men. Those who have watches are working hard to get the sheep they have on the board off, and catch another, before the manager calls out: "Clear the board." <i>(Sep 28, 1878,Otago Witness,3</i>)

>  [1986 NZPOD 77; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 53] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,118</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Fred, being the ganger, was on No. 1 stand. Mick was No. 2, Thornton No. 3, and I, being a learner, was on the traditional `drummer's' stand, at the far end of the board. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,73</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Board</i>. The wooden stretch of floor in a woolshed on which the sheep are shorn. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94</i>)

>  <i>board</i>: The wooden floor of the shed, about ten feet wide, on which the sheep are shorn. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/21</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

>  He shore till he was 52 and had a stroke on the board. <i>(Apr 22, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> William Steuart, with the solemnity of one who views things going wrong, walked the board, with a quiet mien, but with an ever rising hostility in his Celtic nature. <i>Ibid.</i> 110 The only European [in the shearing gang] was the classer, but, of course, some of the team were half-castes, and what a mighty job this team did, ... and Joe Gillies walked the board as boss. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,49</i>)

>  <i>(Jan 24, 1981,Evening Post,7/2. 7/4</i>) [NOTES: Clem Goddard on the Gear Meat Co. see ringer]

>  <i>board ... NZ </i>part of meat-processing plant where animals are killed. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,77</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 53]]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Anybody.. would.. be given the opportunity of demonstrating once again his old skill on hillride or shearing-board. <i>(1967,Return to Open Country,247</i>)

>  He was for some time.. tabooed by all on board. <i>(Feb 19, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,20</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> You should have fined them [sc. the shearers] GBP2 each for leaving the board. <i>(1969,Molesworth,86</i>) [NOTES: Quote from Acton-Adams, 1901]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> That made nine shearers on the board. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,19</i>)

>  <i>Board</i> (b&omacr.rd), <i>n. [Add.] 9.</i> The floor of a woolshed; hence, the whole number of shearers employed in a single woolshed. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2018</i>)

>  <b>ACTON-ADAMS</b> <i>Shearing</i>. I have arranged with the Smoko Shearing Company of Wellington, to erect 10 machine shears on the western board of the shed. <i>(May 22, 1900,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1900 May 22 Acton-Adams Letter in 1969 McCaskill Molesworth 83]

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> Twenty-four held place on the boards. <i>(1904,Sons o' Men,78,1905 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Others have to walk from their stand to cadge a sharpening from stones, a pen or so down the board. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,230</i>)

>  <i>To Shear and Expert </i>`That Remit No. 7 be struck out, i.e. - ... `That no shearer be allowed to shear and expert on the same board.' ... `That no shearer expert and shear on a board of more than five shearers.' <i>(1912,NZ Shearers (Report Third Annual Conference),11</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> ... half the board (i.e., half the total shearers) are Maori, and the other half Pakeha (white)... On large boards a sweeper is employed who brooms away loose locks and dirt. <i>(1913,Country Work,14</i>)

>  <b>REES</b> The shed hands with brooms [swept] the `board' clean. <i>(1925,Lake of Enchantment,113</i>)

>  <i> ~ n.</i> the floor on which sheep are shorn.- with a full board, with the full number of shearers. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

>  <i>Board</i>.-The plank floor in a woolshed on which the sheep are actually shorn. <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BURDON</b> Shearing was done on a tarpaulin or on boards put down for the purpose. <i>(1938,High Country,84</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> `That second is Maori ringer this year,' said Darien, looking down the dim broad board flecked with light from the little windows, with the flash of clicking blades, ... <i>(1938,Promenade,441</i>)

>  `Press' and `sweep' are technical terms for necessary operations in the woolshed at shearing time. The former refers to pressing the fleeces into tight-packed bales ... to facilitate the transport of an otherwise bulky commodity. The latter is the process of keeping the floor (the `boards'), on which the sheep are shorn, clear of loose ends of wool, dirt and excrement. <i>(1945,NZ Geographer,1/39</i>) [NOTES: (Pascoe `Canterbury High Country') [is ths a true pl: or an error on writer's part]]

>  <b>PAUL</b> One of these huts must serve you for your first year's wool-shed, with the help of a few hurdles in front, and a tarpaulin or a few boards to shear on.] <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,90</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ... Wally took the broom to sweep up stray bits of wool from the board; ... <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,113,1974 ed Rhodes ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Board</i>. 4. `The use of <i>board</i> for the floor of a shearing shed is also slang, whence comes<i> a full board</i> and <i>boss-over-the-board,</i>' B. 1941: New Zealand and Australian rural: since ca1880; by 1910, coll <i>over the board</i> occurs in Archer Russell, <i>Gone Nomad</i>, 1936. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,994</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> Along the front [of the woolshed] is the shearing board where the actual shearing is done, and the wool room usually forms a large projection on the front of this again. <i>(1951,High Country,10</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> BOARD: That area of the floor of the shearing-shed on which the sheep are shorn. When there is the full number of shearers required, a shed is said to have a `full-board'. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,110</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> When we had washed the dung and oil and sweat from the shearing board ... we ... sat on a bale near the press. <i>(1959,Stone, The,49</i>) [NOTES: [?to shearing board]]

>  <i>Board</i>: The area in a woolshed used for shearing. In some 16-stand sheds there are two boards, each with its ringer. At Masterton there is a six-stand board with catching and counting-out pens behind, extending for over 50 feet across the front of the stadium. <i>(Feb 26, 1965,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>) [NOTES: (H.A.L. Laing)]

>  <i>~ n</i>. floor of shearing shed; the whole number of shearers employed in a single woolshed: boss-over-the-board, the overseer; to clear the board, to complete shearing. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),611</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ~: Shearing board. The area in shearing shed where the actual shearing is done. The shearer catches each sheep in the catching-pen and carries it out onto the board to shear it. -- <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,185</i>)

>  <i>BOARD:</i> The area in a shed used for shearing. In some 16-stand sheds there were two `boards' each with its `ringer' <i>(Feb 21, 1968,Straight Furrow,20</i>)

>  <i>board n</i>. floor of shearing shed; collectively, the shearers at work; <i>boss-over-the-board</i>, the overseer;<i> to clear the board</i>, complete shearing. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  The area in a shed used for shearing. ... In some 16-stand sheds there were two `boards' each with its `ringer'. <i>(Feb 24, 1977,Dominion,11</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> of various statutory administrative boards

>  <b>BACON</b> <i>Board, Education</i>-New Zealand State Schools are administered by one or other of ten Education Boards, who make teaching appointments for all schools in their respective areas. Teachers are graded following inspection visits, and according to the Inspector's assessment, are given `grading marks'. In applying for a position, a teacher quotes his mark, the vacancy being filled by the highest graded applicant. Local parents elect School Committees bi-annually. Committee has no say in curriculum, etc., but is, in effect, a caretaking body. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,180</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a complement of shearers in a woolshed.

>  <b>PINNEY</b> Middleton got a full board [in 1885], though fewer Australians had come than usual, and shearers were rather scarce. He commented:`Knockabouts [37] are plentiful enough but three-fourths of them are not worth their salt in a woolshed.' <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,36</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) area in a shearing shed where the shearing is done.
