### board shed
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a design of woolshed with a very long board, incorporating several stands.

>  <b>BOWEN</b> A board shed with a long board involves bringing the wool much further than the wool table than in a chute shed. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,111</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> I have shorn mostly on board sheds, and it is for this reason I would prefer not to be dogmatic in my views on this matter. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,112</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> These two plans illustrate the two main types of [woolshed - the board shed and the chute shed. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,110</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> These two plans illustrate the two main types of woolshed - the board shed and the chute shed. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,110</i>)

### board walker
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shearing gang boss or a chargehand in a commercial slaughterhouse.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> boardwalker (i): the boss of a shearing gang. (ii): a certain chargehand in a commercial slaughterhouse. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,8</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Boardwalker. The boss, manager or his agent who walks up and down the board keeping a check on the standard of the shearers' work. Boardwalkers have become unnecessary as the general standard of shearing among blade shearers has improved. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Board walker: .. His main job is to patrol the board to see that the shearers are making a satisfactory job. <i>(1949,High Country Days,193</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the boss of the shearing board (See [board](../B#board))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> BOARD WALKER: In some of e larger Australian sheds a man is employed just to keep his eye on the work in the shed. His main job is to patrol the board to see that the shearers are making a satisfactory job. The board is that portion of the shearing shed on which the actual shearing is done. <i>(1949,High Country Days,193,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Boardwalker.</i> The boss, manager or his agent who walks up and down the board keeping a check on the standard of the shearers' work. Boardwalkers have become unnecessary as the general standard of shearing among blade shearers has improved. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Boardwalker.</i> The boss, manager or his agent who walks up and down the board keeping a check on the standard of the shearers' work. Boardwalkers have become unnecessary as the general standard of shearing among blade shearers has improved. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,94</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (freezing works) (See [cit](../C#cit))

>  <b>GORDON</b> The <i>department-boss</i> is in charge of the <i>board-walkers </i>often promoted from the ranks, who are in charge of the chain etc.; they vary the speed the speed of the drive on the chain, which is done by a chain hanging down, giving rise to cries `Give it a pull' (i.e., speed it up) or `Pull it back' (slow down). If some are tired and he speeds it up, there are cries of `Get out you whip!' or `Bring your whip tomorrow!'. If the men are very tired to the point of <i>mutton-staggers</i> there will be cries of `Give him a drink of water.', and responses `But he had water yesterday'. <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I slipped on the bloodied floor and the carcase should have fallen on me, but the wheel jammed somehow on the rail and I crouched under swinging death, sick in the guts. The board-walker had to climb up on the wall and saw through the hind leg. <i>IBid.</i> 67 The boardwalker came in for men to help with the cleaning up and I said you can stuff that, sport. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,13</i>)

### boarder
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm animal, particularly a cow, that eats well but produces little.

>  The veriest novice in dairying keeps the heifer calves from his best butter-fat cows to fill the places of the 'boarders.' <i>(Aug 22, 1912,Hawera Star,3</i>)

>  1037 Their efforts, unfortunately, have had little result, and the unproftitable 'hit or miss' methods of estimating whether a cow is a profit maker or a boarder still obtain. [boarder cow] <i>(Jul 2, 1923,NZ Farmer</i>)

>  It is pretty safe to say that any cow yielding less than 400 gallons is a 'boarder' that yields no profit ... <i>(Jun 10, 1909,Weekly News,45</i>)

### boardies
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) short for boardshorts [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  <b>Tracey Slaughter</b> He only had boardies on, crusted with sand, knotted with a shoelace where the hair spiralled down on his belly. <i>(2007,Six Pack 2, The,154</i>)

### boardman
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural, informal) a shearer or other shearing-board worker.

>  One by one the other boardmen dropped their sheep and shears in a heap and stood staring ... <i>(May 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,85</i>)

### boardose
 
 <i>v.</i> [joc. play on 'bulldoze' presumably]

>  <b>MASTERS</b> As soon as he saw me, that boar seemed to lose all interest in the dogs. He boardosed his way through a stunted bush, forcing the dogs to lose their hold in the process and charged straight at me. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,105</i>)

### boarfish
 
 <i>n.</i> applied to various dissimilar fishes which have projecting snouts

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Boarfish <i>Paristiopterus labiosus</i>(Gunther) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,407</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 407]

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family HISTIOPTERIDAE. The histiopterids ... supposed to be a delicacy, are rare, In the common boarfish, <i>Paristiopterus</i>(not really common, but perhaps less rare than other kinds), the sexes are markedly different. The long-finned boarfish (<i>Zanclisteus</i>) rather resembles the tropical angel fish of aquariums; very rare indeed is the Nelson boarfish, <i>Griffinetta nelsonensis</i>, collected once from its nameplace, and once from the Chathams. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,222</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>SOWFISH - Paristiopterus labiosus</i>, also known as Boarfish. Taken occasionally in the north and as far south as Cook Strait. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,46</i>)

>  [species name] sowfish .. [scientific name] <i>Paristiopterus labiosus</i>.. [other name(s)] giant boarfish, boarfish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Boarfish [species name] sowfish .. [scientific name] <i>Paristiopterus labiosus</i>.. [other name(s)] giant boarfish, boarfish Whitley and Phillipps <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,407</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 407]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> BOAR FISH [species name] sowfish .. [scientific name] <i>Paristiopterus labiosus</i>.. [other name(s)] giant boarfish, boarfish Whitley and Phillipps <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,247</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Boarfish <i>Zanclistius elevatus</i> (Ramsay and Ogilby) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,407</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 407]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] boarfish 1029, 1526-42 BOARFISHES 1526-42 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  Three species of boarfish are found in coastal New Zealand waters. The giant boarfish has the greatest range, from the Three Kings Islands...to Foveaux Strait in the south. <i>(Aug 2009,Forest & Bird,333/48-49</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> BOARFISH <i>Histiopteridae</i>: Spiny-finned, deep-bodied family of fish characterised by small, rough scales and a protruding snout. There are three species found in New Zealand waters. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,35</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [Richardson is Sir John Richardson, 1787-1865, Arctic Explorer and Naturalist, friend of Robert Burns and colleague of Joseph Banks, worked up fish from Erebus & Terror Expedition.]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> The term Boar Fish (<i>Cyttus australis</i> ) has been applied to the fish of which several specimens were cast up on the shore of Cook Strait during the south-east gale of November last. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,112</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> BOAR FISH (<i>Cyttus Australis</i> ). ... The term boar fish has been applied to fish of which several specimens were cast up on the shores of Cook Strait. They resemble the Dory somewhat in form and size, but are easily distinguished by the rough skin and the absence of the round black spot on the side. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,13</i>)

>  <i>Boar&#769;.fish&#769;</i>. (b&omacr.r&#769;.f&ibreve.sh&#769;.),<i> n. [Add.] (c) </i>A fish (<i>Cyttus australis</i> ) of the family <i>Cyttidae. [New Zealand]</i> See <i>Bastard dory</i>, under DORY. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2018</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Boar-fish</i> ... a name applied in England to various dissimilar fishes which have projecting snouts. (`Century.') In New Zealand it is given to <i>Cyttus australis</i> , family <i>Cyttidae</i>, which is related to the <i>John Dory</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,40</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Cyttus australis</i> Richardson. BOARFISH. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,25,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Cyttus australis</i> Richardson. Boarfish. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/406</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Boarfish <i>Cyttus australis</i> (Richardson) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,404</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 404]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 404 <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,181</i>)

### boarslayer
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hunter of wild pigs [principally boars].

>  <b>NGAWHIKA</b> I have hunted with a few 'boarslayers' who gave the impression that a wild boar is a man-eating, dog-killing time bomb. <i>(1996,Tell Them I've Gone Pighunting,83</i>)

### boarslaying
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) hunting of wild pigs.

>  <b>NGAWHIKA</b> Just when you think things are going well and it looks time to settle in for a long period of boarslaying ... <i>(1996,Tell Them I've Gone Pighunting,98</i>)

### boarweed
 
 <i>n.</i> rank, bad tobacco [ca1949 Havelock A store customer writes about local tobacco: `Don't send me any more boarweed.] (See [raurau](../R#raurau))

### boat knife
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. blubber knife](../B#blubber-knife))

>  38 Irons - .. 8/- ea. ... 14 Lances - .. 8/- ea. ... 1 Cutting Spade - .. 8/- ... 2 Boat Spades - .. .16. 4 Boat axes - .. 4/- ea. ... 5 Boat Knives - .. 2/- ea. ... 3 Pughs - .. .9. ... 2 Blubber Knives - .. . 8. ... 1 [Boat] Traveller - .. .4. <i>(1837,Piraki Log,49,ed. Anson 1911 ed.</i>)

### boat nigger
 
 <i>n.</i> (offensive) a person hired to do domestic interior cleaning on pleasure boats, yachts, etc. at marinas.

>  Boat Nigger [heading] Most weekdays on P Marina at Westhaven you'll see a pretty young woman with a vacuum hose round her neck, ... She's a `boat nigger'. At least that's what her job is called by the boating fraternity. Aquatic charlady might be more appropriate in these times. Jules cleans boats. <i>(Jul 1986,Metro,24</i>)

### boat spade
 
 <i>n.</i> (offensive)

>  38 Irons - .. 8/- ea. ... 14 Lances - .. 8/- ea. ... 1 Cutting Spade - .. 8/- ... 2 Boat Spades - .. .16. 4 Boat axes - .. 4/- ea. ... 5 Boat Knives - .. 2/- ea. ... 3 Pughs - .. .9. ... 2 Blubber Knives - .. . 8. ... 1 [Boat] Traveller - .. .4. <i>(1837,Piraki Log,49,1911 ed.</i>)

### boat-axe
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  38 Irons - .. 8/- ea. ... 14 Lances - .. 8/- ea. ... 1 Cutting Spade - .. 8/- ... 2 Boat Spades - .. .16. 4 Boat axes - .. 4/- ea. ... 5 Boat Knives - .. 2/- ea. ... 3 Pughs - .. .9. ... 2 Blubber Knives - .. . 8. ... 1 [Boat] Traveller - .. .4. <i>(1837,Piraki Log,49,1911 ed.</i>) [NOTES: List of Gear Lost]

### boat-steerer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Our Captain advised the Boatsteerer to take 6 muskets, but he refused, ... <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 137]

>  Richards, a boat-steerer, ... behaved in a most disrespectful ... manner to the Captain. <i>(1836,Whaling Journal,5</i>) [NOTES: 1836 (1954) ed Straubel Rhodes Whaling Journal 5]

>  <b>CAPT JOY</b> he can do nothing in the boat and has been Sick and dozey 1/2 the time we have been here this last 7 days I sent out 2 boat steerers in the Starbd boat <i>(Jul 25, 1836,Log of Mary Mitchell</i>) [NOTES: 1836 July 25 Capt. Joy Mary Mitchell log in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 451]

>  <b>McNAB</b> [James Davidson's evidence] I know a fish called mutton fish, which is much eaten by the men, but I never heard of its being unwholesome or producing worms; ... there was a man name [sic] Graham, Palmer's boat steerer who was ill, and Palmer attended him; ... <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,209</i>) [NOTES: 1838 May 16 Palmer Trial Evidence in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 209]

>  <b>POLACK</b> A whale ship carrying four boats will be manned by a complement of thirty men, including a master, three mates, four boat steerers, or harpooners, a carpenter and a cooper. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/419</i>)

>  Boat-steerers employ'd fitting Boats. <i>(Apr 11, 1839,Piraki Log,82,1911 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Each [whaleboat] has either five or six oars, and a crew accordingly. The boat-steerer and headsmen are the principal men in the boat, and are generally Europeans; the rest are natives. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/49</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The <i>boat-steerer </i>pulls the oar nearest the bow of the boat, fastens the whale with the harpoon, and takes his name from having to steer the boat under the headsman's directions while the latter kills the whale. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/317</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> An old-time bay whaling station consisted ... of at least two boats, with their crew of six men each. The headsman, or mate, four ordinary oarsmen, and the harpooner, or `boat-steerer,' who pulled the bow-oar, and drove in the harpoon, and who, when the whale had been struck and `fastened on,' went aft and steered the boat, while the mate took his place, and by repeated lance-thrusts killed it. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,75</i>)

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> The `Port Waikato' wanted a boat steerer for the surfboat to land stores there. <i>(1985,Last Entail Male,128</i>) [NOTES: [Chatham Islands]]

### boatel
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [He] reckons a boatel [27] development would be the thing. <i>(Feb 21, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

### boatgirl
 
 <i>n.</i> a prostitute (often amateur) who works the ports; a shipgirl.

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> And a boatgirl with tired eyes said to him, You got a moneys eh I saw you give that kid a moneys. <i>(1978,Glory and the Dream, The,210</i>)

### boatie
 
 <i>n.</i> someone who runs a small boat, especially one which is power-driven, one who runs a small (power-)boat, cf yachtie.

>  More than 30 years ago Parkercraft introduced the first aluminium dinghy to New Zealand boaties. The ubiquitous "tinnies" have since come of age. <i>(Sep 4, 1993,Evening Post,38</i>)

>  Boaties are behaving themselves this summer. <i>(Feb 18, 1993,Contact (Wgtn),1</i>)

>  Department of Conservation pest plant spokeswoman Sarah Crump said boaties needed to clean up boats and equipment when leaving a lake. <i>(Oct 4, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,6</i>)

>  `Skippers and boaties have to be very careful when using the channel ... Some boaties take little notice of the markers and navigation aids,' he added. <i>(Sep 29, 1972,Marlborough Express,6</i>)

>  <i>boatie n N.Z. inf.</i> a boating enthusiast. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,118</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 77; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 53]]

>  <b>BURCHFIELD</b> boatie /|beuti/. N.Z. (and in restricted use in <i>Austral.</i>) One who runs a small (power-driven) boat or is a member of the crew. <i>(1988,Lexicographical & Linguistic Studies,193</i>)

>  Among them the `elite' cutter group put themselves voluntarily `on call' 2 hours a day for rescue work. Such dedication kept well before the boating public would-be boaties. <i>(Oct 25, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7/3</i>)

>  Mar. 17 (Hdg) Tenders for a boaties' haven. <i>(Mar 17, 1981,Sydney Herald,18</i>)

>  This Saturday the flotilla of small craft crewed by eager boaties will be out in force. <i>(Feb 15, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24/2</i>)

>  Where else are they so big on hypocorisms? ... They're those dinky little words we make up, like these: kiddies (children), cardies (cardigans), boatie (person who sails boats), yachtie (person who sails yachts), drinkies (drinks), bikies (people who get together to ride motorbikes), wrinklies (old people), rellies (relations), wellies (Wellingtons, as in gumboots), wallies (variation on wankers), littlies (children). <i>(Sep 1988,North & South,41</i>)

>  <i>(Apr 2, 1975,Turangi (NZ) Chron,1/1</i>)

>  The seagrass beds will be protected from the ravages of thoughtless boaties. <i>(Jul 29, 1984,Canberra Times,2/7</i>)

>  Yet despite this shocking display of bad boatie behaviour at its worst, we find a Coastguard spokesman stating they would be reluctant to charge the boaties because "it would set a dangerous precedent." <i>(May 5, 2000,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  [heading] <b>Boaties ready to fight proposed coastal occupation charge</b>. Boaties are prepared to fight a proposed charge for using public space in Auckland's coastal marine area, which includes berths, moorings, jetties and boatsheds. <i>(Jun 28, 2005,NZ Herald,A8</i>)

>  Those about the scuttlebutt want to know precisely where, en route from Auckland to Great Barrier Island, a boatie might stop to pick up a paper. <i>(Dec 1, 1999,Independent (Auckland),16</i>)

### boatshed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FORDYCE & MACLEHN</b> The public dressing sheds were renovated in 1950 and many boatsheds were rebuilt between 1950-1953. Since then the boatsheds at both ends of the beach have become more than background buildings; they are now icons of Titahi Bay beach. <i>(2000,Bay, The : A History of Community at Titahi Bay,99</i>)

### bob
 
 <i>n.</i> orig. worms threaded and coiled (see cit. 1967), but also meat, bacon, etc. held in a tangle of twine or shredded flax-blade, attached to a line, for catching eels. [ca1940 a bob of tangled bindertwine, or flax, holding a bait for eels whose backward-sloping teeth would become entangled allowing them to be dragged from the water]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The white man always tries to catch eels with a bob or hook, but when I was a boy I saw the Maoris get them in eel baskets ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,16</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> With a darning needle and thread, ... they soon had a [104] five foot string of worms which they coiled into a bob and tied in several places. This was fastened with flax to a strong stick about six feet long. [early 20C.] We went down to the river about dusk, each of us with a rod, ... our bobs suspended in a deep pool. <i>(1967,Tang of The Bush,103</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> fiddle @v (1952-53)



 
 <i>v.</i>



 
 <i>v.</i> to fish for eels

>  <b>LORETZ</b> A 'bob' is simply a bunch of worms strung on flax fibre and tied to a light pole. <i>(1976,Moments of Life,6</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> The manner most frequently adopted for catching eels, called 'bobbing".., is primitive enough, and is by means of a line formed of narrow strips of flax leaves, to the end of which is tied a bundle of earth worms. The eels bolt the "bob" and are readily pulled out of the water. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,243</i>)

>  In some parts the eels were "bobbed" or "gaffed" out by the hundredweight. <i>(Sep 11, 1890,Otago Witness,28</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> shilling; a shilling (-piece), recorded in NZ f. 1841 to decimal coinage: see bob-day, bob-in also half-a-bob, `sixpence', two-bob-bit, `florin'; (See [cf. colonial Robert](../C#colonial-robert))

>  <b>ELKINGTON</b> You can see the huku danced for a couple of shillings, and even this is not bad, for sometimes the dancers get worked up and forget the two bob. <i>(1906,Adrift in NZ,178</i>)

>  <b>[WICKHAM]</b> People do not mind paying the odd "bob". <i>(1897,Casual Ramblings,20</i>)

>  He had.. earned six "bob" since he had been in Masterton.. <i>(Mar 10, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  <b>POSPOSIL120</b> Together we made something in the vicinity of six shillings, or two bob per person. <i>(1935,Wandering on the Islands of Wonders</i>)

>  The seven or eight "bob" he had on him when arrested was the remnant of the money he had received from his ship. <i>(Apr 2, 1910,NZ Truth,7</i>)

>  The old hippopotamus head.. had been purchased at a recent sale in Ngaere for "five bob." <i>(Nov 9, 1918,Stratford Evening Post,7</i>)

>  The humble "thrummer" is a big item on a shilling and two bob a day stunt. <i>(May 24, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/44 : 183</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (where [x] is a gradient from 1 to 19) transf. uses and fig. senses (often graded between 1 and 19) to express various conditions of alleged intellectual handicap, mental bankruptcy, on the part of the addressee, implying that the person is not all there: see also shilling, quid [brief entry only at pound].

>  <b>DAVIN</b> He wasn't a bad bloke even if he was only fifteen bob in the pound. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,118</i>)

### bob each way
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  "He had a bob each way: We [sic] are not at the top of the cycle - unless we have to go one more." <i>(Jan 27, 2006,NZ Herald,C1</i>)

>  <b>McCAlLUM</b> I tell my pakeha friend that I will put ten bob each way on this prad. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,22</i>)

>  Given the problems Aussie cyclists have had recently with growth hormones, [his] bob each way is refreshing if somewhat disappointing. <i>(Jul 14, 2004,Capital Times,14</i>)

### bob hop
 
 <i>n.</i> a dance with low admission price

>  A bob hop is held there every Saturday night. <i>(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(23) : 8</i>)

>  On Friday night there was a bob hop. I wore my sheer and it is a sweet frock but I find that after all my deliberating it <i>is</i> a bit long. <i>(1989,Hot October,74</i>)

### Bob Munro
 
 <i>n.</i> Rhyming <i>In (occas. out) you go says Bob Munro</i>, used as a catchphrase of encouragement for entry into an enterprise, a toast, etc, (See [cf. be in](../B#be-in),[Mrs Cutts](../M#mrs-cutts))

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `Well, in ya go says Bob Munro,' he toasts and we drink some slowly and put the glasses back on the bar. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,45</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>in you go, says Bob Munro</i> an encouraging c/p. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,61</i>)

### Bob Semple
 
 <i>n.</i> a train

>  <b>CHURCHMAN & HURST</b> The mixed train which arrived at Rewanui about 11.30 p, Sunday to Thursday was known as the 'Paddy Webb' or 'Bob Semple' after the miners who became Cabinet Ministers in the first Labour Government, and who first arranged this train to make it easier for the miners changing shift at midnight. <i>(1990,Railways of NZ,196</i>)

### Bob Semple tank
 
 <i>n.</i> a makeshift tank for home defence, comprising a protective iron shell on a bulldozer body, the enterprise for which attributed to Semple, the then Minister of Works (See [Semple tank](../S#semple-tank))

>  <b>PORTER</b> I had to wear [the armband] that way the next day in a big parade of the different services, following a huge Bob Semple tank, carrying a banner emblazoned with `Law and Order',.... <i>(1970,Nor'west Arch,23</i>) [NOTES: [ca1940]]

### Bob Semple's art union
 
 <i>phr.</i> a ballot for military service [ca1940-1941 (R. Mason)]

### bob up
 
 <i>v.</i> How are you bobbing up? `How are you getting on?, ? as a greeting or polite enquiry.

>  <b>GRACE</b> I just looked round to see how you were `bobbing up.' <i>(1914,Tale of Timbertown,75</i>)

### bob's-a-dying
 
 <i>n.</i> see bobsy-die

>  <b>MARSH</b> `If she's right,' said Fox `it plays Bobs-a-dying with the whole blooming case. <i>(1941,Surfeit of Lampreys,xvi/251,1951 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Kick up Bob's-a-dying.</i> To make an unneccessary fuss or noise or commotion: New Zealand: since ca, 1890. (Prof. Arnold Wall., letter of Aug. 1939.) <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1092</i>)

### bob-day
 
 <i>adj.</i> `a-bob-a-day'. -

>  <b>REVANS</b> I told him to work as a bob-day labourer, rather than be idle and starving. <i>(1841,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Revans to Chapman 96]

### bob-in
 
 <i>n.</i> (perhaps similar to Hokitika swindle)

>  <b>DAVIDSON</b> From tricks at cards, the fun changed to `a bob in' the winner shouting. <i>(1889,Stories of NZ Life,5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Often `have a bob-in', a bar-room drinking system whereby one pays a shilling (more or less) into a round of drinks, so that one can withdraw from the school when the money is cut out.

>  <b>HICKEY</b> [A] goodly number were non-drinkers, and those who did take a `spot' confined their imbibing to `bobs in' or `West Coasters,' as the delegates [to the 1910 conference of the Federation of Labour] from other parts of NZ termed them. <i>(1925,Red Fed. Memoirs,31</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Drinking on the West Coast has an etiquette of its own. It condones none of the `bob-in' touch of a city bar when the man will have the two drinks he wants and then leave. `Be in or be out', you will be told, is the motto on the Coast. <i>(1948,NZ Geographer,166</i>) [NOTES: (Pascoe `Coal from Buller')]

### bob-robin
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  One of our party proceeded to chop fork- sticks.. and immediately around came a family of bob-robins within measurable reach of his tomahawk. <i>(Sep 14, 1927,NZ Tablet,37</i>)

### bobber
 
 <i>n.</i> a charge hand in a freezer gang

>  A "bobber," a charge hand in a freezer gang, was so named because originally he was paid an extra "bob" or shilling a day. <i>(Feb 11, 1977,Press, The,19</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a chargehand in a freezing gang.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> bobber: a chargehand in a freezer gang, so named because he was originally paid a 'bob-a-day' extra. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,8</i>)

### bobbing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> The manner most frequently adopted for catching eels, called 'bobbing".., is primitive enough, and is by means of a line formed of narrow strips of flax leaves, to the end of which is tied a bundle of earth worms. The eels bolt the "bob" and are readily pulled out of the water. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,243</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> `I used to go bobbing for eels ... when I was a little girl,' said Miss Ambrose. <i>(1978,Elver, The,64</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> I just looked round to see how you were `bobbing up.' <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,44,1935 ed.</i>)

### bobbing on the skids
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) the sawing of logs into appropriate lengths to convert into timber.

>  <b>MARRIOTT</b> Darcy had been offered a job up at Denny's sawmill, bobbing on the skids and acting as a general hand. For those who don't know, bobbing on the skids means sawing logs up into the best lengths to convert into timber. <i>(1981,Life in the Gorge,135</i>)

### bobby
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to kill a young calf for veal.

>  If more calves are bobbied this year, a shortage looms in 1997. 818,000 dairy calves were bobbied last year ... <i>(Jul 6, 1995,NZ Farmer,3</i>)

>  Probably killing a calf at four days of age, or those too small to be bobbied, upsets them most. <i>(Mar 23, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> - [cf. catamaran, jinker (1892)] (See [cf. catamaran](../C#catamaran),[jinker](../J#jinker))

>  <b>REED</b> Curves were laid with a camber ... to prevent the cat from `pulling off', and these bends were laid with much longer skids, to widen that part of the road. At such points too, a `bobby' - a short skid extending from the bank to the road - was placed to prevent the cat from running into the bank. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,181</i>) [NOTES: [? ca1900]]

>  On the bank side, that is, the uphill side of the road, there would be short pieces let into the ground and standing up against the bank and placed just in front of the skid. These were called bobbies, and were to prevent the cat from jamming against the bank of the road. <i>(1951,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: [ca1914] 1951 H.S. McCarroll The Days of the Kauri Bushmen [Radio Talk 4: TS]]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bobby calf or milk-calf.

>  You should sell your bobbies to those exporters who kill at Horotiu, Southdown or Moerewa works. <i>(Aug 1, 1936,NZ Dairy Exporter,18</i>)

>  .. the return from the whey was greater when fed to convert bobbies to vealers than when fed to pigs. Early on the morning of each killing day for bobbies, he went through the mob ... <i>(Sep 29, 1937,Weekly News,81</i>)

>  It has been suggested that the word 'bobby' arose from the fact that in the early days of the trade, the calves sold for a shilling per head at the farm gate ... <i>(Oct 2, 1950,NZ Dairy Exporter,46</i>)

>  <b>ELLISON</b> There were so many potentially good heifer calves that had to be sent out as bobbies every year ... <i>(1956,Whirinaki Valley, The,160</i>)

>  The bobbies are left on the mother for the first four days or until they are ready for the works. <i>(Nov 11, 1963,NZ Dairy Exporter,5</i>)

>  Let's not forget bobbies. Southlanders have brought them overland from as far as Nelson, a distance over 600 miles. <i>(Jul 22, 1971,NZ Farmer,35</i>)

>  They are too young for traditional beef markets and will not attract the premium over bobby prices. <i>(Nov 13, 1987,NZ Dairy Exporter</i>)

>  He expects the number of bobbies coming forward this season will be similar to last year. <i>(Apr 2001,Fencepost.com,Dairy Meats</i>)

>  ... a good selection of sheds for calf rearing or bobbies. <i>(May 6, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,2</i>)

>  [Hdg] Bobbies set to bounce back... His "best guess" at the price of calves is $55, based on a bobby price for heavyweight calves of $1.40-$1.50 a kilogram... <i>(Jul 29, 2004,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

### bobby calf
 
 <i>n.</i> an unweaned, usually male, calf for slaughter; a calf to be slaughtered when over 4 days old fed solely on whole milk, defined as under 45.5 kg live weight with carcasses of not less than 9.98 kg. [ca1910 W.H.B. Orsman]

>  Only small quantities of "bobby" veal calves came forward at Mataura last week. <i>(Aug 27, 1928,Clutha Leader,4</i>) [NOTES: From George Griffiths]

>  It is most difficult to find the cost of production of a bobby calf as compared with that of a pound of butter. <i>(1948,NZ Parliamentary Debates,281/1125</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Mati was somewhat out of her depth in explaining the workings of the bobby-calf trade. <i>(1955,Land of My Children,201</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> ... it was not in his nature to kill anything, let alone the heifer's calves that were even too small to be sent away on the bobby calf truck. the field to her pet bobby calf, ... and tried to make him come to her. <i>(1986,Address to a King,59</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> ... the sight and sound of pens of bobby calves waiting at the gateway for the butcher's lorry; ... <i>(1941,New Zealand Now,89</i>)

>  Dairy farmers who sent undernourished and bruised bobby calves to freezing works were losing thousands of pounds a year. <i>(Sep 22, 1954,NZ Truth,16</i>)

>  Latest bobby calf slaughter figures show kill rates are similar to last year. <i>(Sep 9, 2004,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

>  However, since bobby calf prices look to be much the same as last year, the only price driver is likely to be competition among rearers. <i>(Jul 29, 2004,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

>  Fifty thousand "bobby" calves is the estimate of the season's kill at the Woodville and Napier works. <i>(Sep 5, 1928,NZ Free Lance,42</i>)

>  The New Zealand Dairy Board to be empowered to organise a national co-operative system of local pools for the collection and disposal of bobby calves and all bobby calf products. <i>(May 4, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,82</i>)

>  <b>BROWN</b> The passing of the Bobby Calf lorry, with its sadly vocal load, struck such a dissonant chord that the lute of my delight lay broken. <i>(1939,Farmer's Wife, The,261</i>)

>  <b>HAWES</b> ...his eyes were that sort of mad-wide that bobby calves' go when they know what's up... <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,123</i>)

>  The North Island meat giant has just announced that it has made an offer for the business of Dairy Meats NZ, the biggest processor and marketer of bobby calves, handling about half of the 1.5 million bobby calves killed each year. <i>(Nov 29, 2004,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

>  Over 59,000 bobby calves were carried by rail last week in the North island. <i>(Sep 4, 1952,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  The necessity for some suitable shelter being provided by farmers for bobby calves, while they are waiting at the farmers' gates to be picked up by the motor lorries was stressed.. at the annual meeting of the Okato branch of the Farmers' Union. <i>(May 24, 1938,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  While driving through the central North Island recently I was pleased to see that on one farm shelters had been provided for bobby calves. <i>(Oct 30, 1992,Dominion,6</i>)

>  He believed that the carting of bobby calves was now back on the old bad basis. <i>(Oct 12, 1937,Morrinsville Star,6</i>)

>  The bobby calf trade established several years ago, was soon recognised by farmers as a means of securing additional revenue... There are 300 men employed at the works and practically all of them are engaged in the bobby calf industry during the season when these are handled. <i>(Sep 7, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,83</i>)

>  Bobby calves are finding themselves victims of the low dollar and poor American beef market... <i>(Jul 18, 2003,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

>  The once-despised bobby calf is in the big money field today and.. the total export values of bobby calf products this year would be between GBP3 million and GBP4 million. <i>(Jul 1959,NZ Dairy Exporter,11</i>)

>  Most of the calves go off as "bobbies". <i>(Sep 1971,NZ Dairy Exporter,130</i>)

>  It is indeed a far cry back to the days of the inception of the bobby calf pools when the farmer was getting next to nothing for his surplus calves. <i>(Jul 1959,NZ Dairy Exporter,11</i>)

>  The bobby calf payout is high in this area. <i>(Sep 1971,NZ Dairy Exporter,21</i>)

>  The procurement, toll processing and marketing of bobby calves continues for the third year in succession. <i>(2002,Ann. Rep. Allied Farmers Ltd,10</i>)

>  Small male calves, "bobbies", cute and appealing with their soulful eyes and long, sweeping eyelashes, are left at the farm gate to be collected and killed as vealers. <i>(Nov 1992,North & South,84</i>)

>  <b>GRIEVE</b> It should ... be explained that `bobby calves' are the unwanted bull-calves which the farmers part with for a few pence. Buyers canvas the districts and collect the animals, so that it is quite a common sight to see a boarded-up motor-lorry speeding along the road with a cargo of `bobby calves'. <i>(1939,Sketches from Maoriland,133fn,1961 ed.</i>)

>  [OED2] 30,000 bobby calves, mostly from the Waikato, were exported ... last season as boneless veal. <i>(Jul 26, 1928,Auckland Weekly News,58/3</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Calves not kept for the herd become <i>bobby calves</i>, a truly standard word since it was defined in the <i>Bobby Calf Marketing Regulations</i> in the New Zealand Statutory Regulations, 1938: `Bobby calf' means a calf which is intended for slaughter for human consumption as boneless veal.' The origin and dating of the word are doubtful. There is a similar word poddy calf for a hand-fed calf, recorded in Australia from last century. The present New Zealand meaning seems to have arisen about 1929. The Christchurch Times, 17 August 1929, reported: `During the last year or so quite a trade has grown up in young, or `bobby' calves, which are taken in by the freezing works, killed and exported.' The term <i>vealer</i> is also used. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,150</i>)

>  ~ Calf to be slaughtered when over 4 days old that has been fed solely on whole milk. The stomach (vel) provides rennet that is used as a coagulant in cheese making. They are calves defined as under 45.5 kg live weight with carcasses of not less than 9.98 kg. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,19</i>)

>  [OED2] `Bobby calf' means a calfwhich is intended for slaughter for human consumption as boneless veal. <i>(1938,NZ Statutory Regulations,334</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> <i>Bobby-calves</i> are the young calves which become veal. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,86</i>)

>  ~ The trade term used in Australia and New Zealand for a young, unweaned calf. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),44</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> And the bobby calves, poor little beggars. Hanging from the chain in horrible bunches or ten or a dozen at a time. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,13</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> [Mabel Howard M.P. is] the minister for the bobby-calf. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,10</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The<i> bobby </i>calf is a term of the stock trade. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/680</i>)

>  <i> ~ n</i>. calf slaughtered soon after birth. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),611</i>)

>  <i>bobby calf</i> also <i>bobby n </i>-es [E dial. <i>bob </i>young calf (prob. fr. the name <i>Bob</i>) + -y] <i>Austral, NewZeal, & Africa </i>: a young calf : specif : one of less than 100 pounds live weight <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,245</i>)

>  </i>bobby calf ...n.</i> calf slaughtered soon after birth. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  <i>bobby calf n. N.Z</i>. an unweaned calf culled for slaughter. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,119</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 77; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 54]]

>  <b>KNOX</b> Shortly after she found a rope and walked out over <i>(1987,After Z-Hour,13</i>)

>  <b>470</b> Prior to the season 1927-28, it was customary to kill all surplus calves on the farm, and dispose of their skins only. The trade, which is known locally as `The Bobby Calf' trade, developed during that season, and has extended rapidly each year since. Under this system of disposal, calves are collected by motor lorry and delivered at freezing-works, where they are slaughtered and packed as boneless veal. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a male or excess female calf at least four days old that has been fed only on whole milk and is sent to slaughter for consumption as veal.

>  A leading farmer referred some time ago to the possibilities of extension of the 'bobby' calf business. <i>(Dec 8, 1928,Hawera Star,14</i>)

>  I think that they [sheep] are of much more consequence than the 'bobby' calf. <i>(Jan 1, 1929,NZ Farmer,6</i>)

>  Eltham District Bobby Calf Pool collections throughout the area on Thursday 17th July and Thursday 24th July. <i>(Jul 16, 1947,Hawera Star,6</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> bobbycalf: milk-fed calf slaughtered very young for its tender flesh, aka vealer; defined by the 1938 Bobby Calf Marketing Regulations as a calf intended for human consumption as boneless veal. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,15</i>)

>  There are a number of reasons why we have got into bobby calves. <i>(Jul 8, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,16</i>)

>  Calf rearers are paying up to $200/head for beef cross bobby calves. <i>(Apr 6, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,19</i>)

>  Dairy Meats tops bobby calf payout stakes. <i>(May 17, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

### bobby calf pen
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small crate or pen holding bobby calves for collection [usually situated at a farm gate.]

>  <b>ELLISON</b> Better put her out in the bobby-calf pen again. <i>(1956,Whirinaki Valley, The,134</i>)

>  For sale: bobby calf pen ... <i>(May 24, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,19</i>)

>  Clearing sale: 2 x bobby calf pens (1 on wheels) <i>(Apr 13, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,46</i>)

>  Clearing sale: ... loading ramp, bobby calf pen, deer netting ... <i>(May 11, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,47</i>)

>  For sale: bobby calf pen, portable, excellent order. [bobby-calf pen] <i>(Aug 3, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,24</i>)

### bobby pin
 
 <i>n.</i> a type of hair fastening; a kind of sprung hair-pin or small clip, orig. for use with bobbed hair. 1936. [Jim writes: The British equivalent seems to be <i>kirby grip</i>. In NZ Oxford Paperback. OED tags it as US with a 1936 first citation. This needs investigating: see HNZD2: OED2 US [etym. uncert. but cf v5 and bobby(-sox)]. [HWO]]

>  <i>bobby pin n. U.S. & N.Z.</i> a metal hair pin bent in order to to hold the hair in place. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,119</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 54]]

### bobby truck
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a carrier that collects bobby calves from farms for slaughter.

>  <b>ELLISON</b> At about mid-day that day I had heard and then seen the bobby-calf lorry go by ... <i>(1956,Whirinaki Valley, The,132</i>)

>  There is one exception of course - the sound of the bobby truck approaching - speeding up the road and going past our gate before we've had time to eartag a mob of 13 (definitely an unlucky number that morning.) <i>(1999,NZ Dairy Exporter,96</i>)

>  .. his calves are going to be taken off Mum and reared indoors or sent on the bobby calf truck anyway. <i>(Sep 2001,Growing Today,61</i>)

>  Middelmost. And then the dairy farmer with the jerseys rang to say he had two beautiful jersey heifers destined for the bobby truck. [bobby calf truck; bobby-calf lorry] <i>(Oct 20, 2001,Lifestyleblock.co.nz</i>)

>  <b>JOCHEMS, Bridgette</b> In the spring this wouldn't have been a problem: we'd just have put them on the bobby truck. <i>(2001,Chewing the Cud,77</i>)

### bobby veal
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Veal is produced from young animals of 3 to 10 months, and from calves of up to 3 months old (`bobby' veal). <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,185</i>)

### bobby-dazzler
 
 <i>n.</i> a good or amazing person or thing (mainly children's). 1. Anything fine, superior, or outstanding of its kind; ironically, an `eccentric, curious (person)'; corker [cf. ruby-dazzler 1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 8 BOBBY(-)DAZZLER [something good] -->1951 14 M 10 Wellington H.S. 8 BOBBY DAZZLER [M2] -->1953 14-16 M A17 Thames DHS 8 BOBBY DAZZLER [M4] -->1960 18C F B12 Horowhenua C. 8 BOBBYDAZZLER [NB in ODE as Brit. dated; not in DNZE, correctly]] (See [cf. ruby-dazzler](../R#ruby-dazzler))

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Those mosquitoes were getting busy. They were "bobby-dazzler", the biggest I've ever seen. <i>(1967,Return to Open Country,84</i>)

>  A bobby-dazzler - the pretty cook. <i>(1909,NZ Truth,194/1/5</i>)

>  The Hawera owner does not often fluke a champion, but this time his friends claim he has unearthed a bobby dazzler. <i>(Feb 26, 1910,NZ Truth,244/2/7</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> In constant use by our youngsters ... bobby dazzler, ruby dazzler, [etc.] ... <i>(1941,NZ Slang,51</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> He looked a real bobby-dazzler. <i>(1945,When the Wind Blows,9</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> [And he had on] the new shirt ..., and the cap ... and the new school-bag made of real leather ... He looked a real bobby-dazzler, was what father said - <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,5,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> `Any good, do you think?' `Harry says it's a bobbydazzler, but then he always does.' <i>(1960,Word for Word,198</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> GILHOOLY, <i>joyfully</i>: The man's a winner! A bobby-dazzler! <i>(1969,Awatea,34,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Poor guy,' I said. `She's a real bobbydazzler.' <i>(1976,Pacific Star,152</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> <i>(1981,Hot & Copper Sky,153</i>)

>  <i>Little bobby dazzlers</i> [headline] Pictured here are the prizewinners in the New Zealand Police/New Zealand Times Design a Poster Competition <i>(Dec 29, 1985,NZ Times,14</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a carbide bicycle lamp (cf. bobby-dodger) [ca1920 Invercargill boys' Les Souness; 1966 cf reference to `heyday of Cycling Clubs']

### bobby-dodger
 
 <i>n.</i> a kerosene bicycle lamp (old-fashioned). [ca1930 Palmerston North children's N.C.C. Small]

### bobsidie
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [bobsy-die](../B#bobsy-die))

### bobsy-die
 
 <i>n.</i> kick up bobsy-die - to make a fuss; a great fuss; pandemonium [-->ca1900-1905 W.H.B. Orsman recalled (but vaguely: p.c. 1972) a children's game involving clapping of hands, called <i>Bob's-a-die</i> or <i>Bob's-a-daisy</i> -->1953 17 M A35 Thames DHS (BOB SA DY) [expressing annoyance] -->1953 17 M A39 Thames DHS (BOBBSY DY) -->1953 16 M A34 Thames DHS (BOBBSY-DYE)]

>  Or Dido kicked up quite such bobsy-die/ If she had a squint in her left eye. <i>(1952,Here & Now,2/iv/25</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> `Been running round kicking up bobsy-die all morning. Must have a hangover, I reckon.' <i>(1960,Word for Word,181</i>)

>  Local publican Bruce Ford said the two youngsters had `kicked up bobsie die' on the boat during the journey from Bluff to Stewart Island. <i>(Jan 12, 1986,NZ Times,1</i>)

>  <i>sy-die n. N.Z. inf</i>ss; confusion (esp. in<i> kick up bobsy-die</i>. [< C19<i> Bob's a-dying</i>] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,119</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 77; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 54]]

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> kick up bobsy-die make a fuss <i>(1984,Blackball 08,244</i>) [NOTES: (Glossary)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> Another time she went to the outhouse and sat on a hedgehog and kicked up bobsy-die, ... <i>(1985,G'day Country,61</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bobsy-die</i> fuss or fun; common phr. `kicking up bobsy-die'; ironic C18 English nautical almost rhyming `bob's-a-dying/idling' underwent sea change to NZ C19, possibly picking up `bobbery' or shindy, from Hindi `bapre'. Ngaio Marsh used original in <i> Surfeit of Lampreys</i>: `If she's right ... it plays Bobs-a-dying with the whole blooming case.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,17</i>)

>  <b>McLEAN</b> By generally kicking up bobsy-die. <i>(1960,Kings of Rugby,vii/50</i>) [NOTES: (OEDS)]

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> They had paper down - but me mother kicked up bobsydie about that place. <i>(1974,Sugarbag Years,72</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> If he kicks up bobsy-die, if he tries to send anyone down the road, then it's a strike all right. <i>(1984,Blackball 08,109</i>)

>  The brown torrent kicked up "Bobsie Die" in deafening manner. <i>(1935,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,IV/36</i>)

>  [He] kept watch for villains, "not only burglars but the ratbags coming and kicking up bobsy-di." <i>(Feb 13, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),25</i>)

### bobtail man
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rabbiter.

>  Heard of poor Daniels (bob-tail man) having been drowned. <i>(May 2, 1864,Haldon Station Diary,251</i>) [NOTES: in Vance (1965) High Endeavour 251]

### bocacker
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pukeko](../P#pukeko))

### BOD
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Beauty-on-Demand [proprietary name]

>  Beauty-on-demand (nicknamed "bod") is aimed at busy business women who, like Kenny, juggle children, a business and a life, "but still want to look good." <i>(Nov 14, 2004,Sunday Star Times,D4</i>)

>  Kenny said she has 25 experienced beauty therapists on her books who want to be "bod girls"... <i>(Nov 14, 2004,Sunday Star Times,D4</i>)

### boddler
 
 <i>n.</i> a smallish person who is not really a baby but not quite a full-blown toddler

>  By definition a boddler is a smallish person who is not really a baby but not quite a full-blown toddler. The expression, boddler boy, was copied after a mother from Waimahaka, in Southland, was savagely attacked by her 14-month-old son with a sawn-off rattle. <i>(Oct 15, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),103</i>)

### bodgie
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal)

>  <b>GEE</b> This was.. because the badminton association headquarters opposite her home was staffed by "bodgies and widgies." "So bad is their behaviour that recently I have had to erect a six-foot fence costing sixty pounds along the side of my house to stop them making love under my pine trees". <i>(1959,Our Mabel,187</i>)

>  This is partly his nature: the natural bodgie keeps colliding with the responsibilities of office. <i>(1998,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>) [NOTES: Issue of June ? 1998]

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> Gangs of boys roamed about in jeans and leather jackets. They were called bodgies and I thought they might beat me up. <i>(1995,Mum,63</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> I suppose a bloke who becomes a bodgie is somebody who's not interested in what they're doing. <i>(1965,Open Country,89</i>)

>  The bodgies and widgies (who did not drive motorbikes) were here! They drank fearsome mind-altering concoctions like Bodgies Blood - a mixture of raspberry syrup and soda water ... <i>(Nov 27, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> Her mum had probably been shot by some thoughtless local bodgie. <i>(1989,Then Upon the Evil Season,134</i>)

>  Mr Mallard is enjoyed in the House as that rare thing, a superbly well-organised bodgie. <i>(Sep 21, 2000,Dominion,1</i>)

>  <b>MANNING</b> The terms "Bodgie" and "Widgie" are peculiar to the middle 20th Century, but the characteristics are not... In the under-privileged masses the counterparts of Bodgies were variously styled as 'yahoos', 'larrikins', 'hooligans' and 'yobs'. The 'playboy' of upper class society is the "Bodgie" of the poor. <i>(1958,Bodgie, The,7</i>)

>  One of the first things that emerged in Mr Manning's study was that neither bodgies nor widgies lack intelligence but there was widespread resentment at the lack of freedom in school and education. <i>(Aug 8, 1958,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  Young New Zealand servicemen decided last evening to clean Queen Street of the "teddy boy" and "bodgie" element. <i>(Oct 31, 1955,NZ Herald,8</i>)

>  On Labour weekend the island was overrun with hundreds of boozing, cursing, bottle-smashing bodgies and widgies... The bodgies favour blue jeans, the widgies black sweaters and tight black shorts <i>(Nov 2, 1955,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  [Hdg] S.M. Refers to Waiheke Bodgie- Widgie Trouble. <i>(Nov 16, 1955,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  "I refuse to speak to widgies, sir"... "Widgie? Widgie? I am a widgie?"... "Well, what do they call a boy widgie?" "A - a bodgie is what you mean." <i>(Dec 21, 1955,NZ Truth,4</i>)

>  Some of those "Teddy boys" and "bodgies" carry knives and once someone started cutting their hair anything could have happened. <i>(Oct 31, 1955,Evening Post,12</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (informal)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Another thing I don't like about coming to town, and that's these bodgie jokers you see around the place. <i>(1965,Open Country,89</i>)

>  There will be niggles - the arbitrating advantage of high-income individuals now forming themselves into bodgie companies... <i>(Mar 27, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  <b>52</b> The bodgies and widgies.. drank fearsome mind-altering concoctions like Bodgies Blood - a mixture of raspberry syrup and soda water ... <i>(Nov 27, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  Bodgie kid, farm trainee, hairdressing apprentice, horse-riding move extra, then - BANG1 - writer of a truly trashy phenomenon involving teenie virgins, a kinky, fish-netted scientist and his coterie of misfits with raging libidos. <i>(Nov 21, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),60</i>)

>  It did stage too many bodgey and bogan gigs. <i>(Jun 18, 1992,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  [The car] natched my brother's Bodgie haircut and winklepicker shoes. <i>(Nov 2001,AA Directions,Summer 50 : 33</i>)

>  He was more bodgie than Ted. He got around in a flat-top. <i>(Nov 26, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

### bodgie up
 
 <i>v.</i> (informal) falsify

>  Anyway, if Parker is a criminal so is anyone who has bodgied up a receipt for the tax man, ratcheted up an insurance claim, or even contributed to the black economy... <i>(Mar 29, 2006,Porirua City News,17</i>)

### bodgie-esque
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  That he has just spent time in Sydney as an official guest at the Olympics made this [insulting] observation daringly bodgie-esque. <i>(Sep 21, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

### bodgiedom
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal)

>  He and Sharon have settled into the comfortable bodgiedom of late middle-age; he drives a black Holden, she a new four-wheel drive. <i>(Jan 25, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

### bodgy
 
 <i>adj.</i> (informal, New Zealand & Australia) worthless; false

>  The freelance writer insists that the booklet ... is a souvenir to mark Rotorua's first league international. Angry officials claim it is a bodgy programme. <i>(Jul 16, 1989,Sunday Star (Auckland),A1</i>)

### bodifying
 
 <i>adj.</i> (informal) [On shampoo bottle - "bodifying shampoo"]

### Bodmin
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [orig. trade name] a milking system or part of the sytem designed by a Waikato farmer, Syd Bodmin.

>  Build up production with Bodmin. <i>(Jan 28, 1965,NZ Farmer,16</i>)

>  In its ability to milk cows, then, the Bodmin system cannot be seriously faulted. The wrangle between satisfied users of Bodmin milking clusters and the Ministry of Agriculture's Dairy Division is healing up. ..This means that a Bodmin plant is considerably cheaper to install than a conventional plant. One farmer told me that two things suggested to him that his Bodmin clusters were gentler in action than conventional types. <i>(Nov 13, 1975,NZ Farmer,25</i>)

>  This is my second season using the Bodmin units. <i>(Nov 27, 1975,NZ Farmer,33</i>)

>  The Bodmin system. As a Waikato farmer, Syd Bodmin the designer was concerned at the damage inflicted on cows by milking machines ... [Bodmin cluster; Bodmin plant; Bodmin unit ] <i>(May 1981,NZ Dairy Exporter,4</i>)

>  The Bodmin can also save you up to a third in electricity costs. <i>(Dec 23, 1976,NZ Farmer,40</i>)

### body
 
 <i>n.</i> the centre pair of a six-bullock team.

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> In breaking in a young bullock to the team [c1900] the first step was to couple the young beast to a steady near-side bullock used to working in the body (centre) of the team. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,137</i>) [NOTES: (c1900) 1940 Studholme Te Waimate 137]

### body and sleeves
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>MASTERS</b> `Fences near the rivers went body and sleeves' <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,100</i>) [NOTES: [From a letter written by Jock Anderson, 11 May 1897]; 1959 L. Masters Tales of the Mails 100]

### body bullock
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BUTLER</b> In the small river Ashburton ... we had a little misunderstanding [105] with the bullocks; the leaders ... slewed sharply round, and tied themselves into an inextricable knot with the polars [sic], while the body bullocks, by a manoeuvre not unfrequent, shifted, or as it is technically termed, slipped, the yoke under their necks, and the bows over; ... <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,vii 104-105</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a beast yoked in the central part of a bullock team.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> A beast yoked up in the central part of a bullock team. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,8</i>)

### body corporate
 
 <i>n.</i> a legal entity comprising all owners of a unit-titled property

>  Irresponsible body corporates could leave complacent apartment owners facing ruinous repair and maintenance bills, a property management expert warns... A body corporate is made up of all owners of a unit-titled property. There is no opting out. The main player is the body corporate secretary, usually a professional... The body corporate: Comprises all owners of a unit-titled property... <i>(Apr 11, 2004,Sunday Star Times,D22</i>)

>  Body corporate chairman Joe Dowrick said residents found out about the development only a few days before its launch. <i>(Jul 27, 2004,Dominion Post,A7</i>)

### body louse
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a type of sheep louse.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> There are several species of lice found on sheep, but those which occur in New Zealand are: 1) the red louse or body louse, or wool louse (Damalinia ovis) .. The wool or body louse is widely distributed and more difficult to deal with. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,83</i>)

### body-snatcher
 
 <i>n.</i> a casual wharf labourer who works only when labour is short.

>  We have heard a good deal about Seagulls and Body-snatchers, yet there are few people who know the origin of the phrase or the appellation. The words have been coined and used by the disgruntled section of the Watersiders' Union. ... Now it is generally known that a Body-snatcher is a man who rushes in on another man's job when the going is good and slips back to his own work when things get slack. <i>(1945,Body-Snatcher and the Seagull, The</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fat stock buyer.

>  Dry humour runs thick through this collection; for example .. - body-snatcher - a stock buyer. <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

>  Person who selects stock for sale - a body snatcher. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### Boeing seat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MOORE</b> I wanted a `Boeing seat' (an electorate with an airport serviced by Boeing aircraft - a big-city seat) and preferably one with radio and television studios. <i>(1987,Hard Labour,63</i>)

### bog
 
 <i>adj.</i> as a distinguishing epithet rather than in combin., see also anise, celmisia, club-moss. epacris; fern, umbrella; gentian; grass, bent-grass; heath, lily, moss, pine, poa, rush, stitchwort.



 
 <i>n.</i> in NZ occas. used for the usu. swamp



 
 <i>n.</i> fellow [ca1930 W. Cameron's mother e.g. How are you bogs?]



 
 <i>v.</i> to shovel (See [cf. bogging](../B#bogging))

>  <b>McCARTHY</b> The same thing applied after the face had been `shot': before shovelling (or bogging, as it was called) the stone down the pass it had to be thoroughly drenched with water ... <i>(1973,Listen...!,51</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to clog

>  <b>BOWEN</b> There are two grave errors in tension adjustment. One is to start the handpiece with too light a tension, so the when the first blow goes in the gear bogs and chews, and small balls of wool collect under the cutter. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,77</i>)

>  <i>bog</i>: If a shearer attempts to cut too much wool at once the shears get `bogged' in the wool. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/21</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <i>to bog</i>: to fibreglass, to fill <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

 <i>phr.</i>

 <i>n.</i> <i>Angelica trifoliata.</i> [one entry bog anise]

 <i>n.</i> <i>Sphagnum</i>

### bog orange
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a potato, or [mainly high country] a swede.

>  The time is fast approaching when the entries for the potato competition close, and all who are interested in the cultivation of the 'bog orange' should try for the munificent prizes which are offered. <i>(Jul 21, 1888,Egmont Star,7</i>)

>  We observe many fine specimens of gooseberries, tomatoes and that excellent if not melting fruit the 'bog orange' which is at present retailing at twopence per pound. <i>(Dec 1892,NZ Farmer,503</i>)

>  <b>PUGSLEY</b> The diet [on troopship] was : Breakfast - porridge, coffee, bread and butter. Dinner - tough roast beef and potatoes or `bog oranges', beans and cold tea or a mug of soap.[sic] <i>(1984,Gallipoli: the New Zealand Story,63</i>) [NOTES: [c.1914]]

>  Old high country slang for .. swedes, bog oranges. <i>(1984,NZ Journal of Agriculture,Jan/Feb 13</i>)

### bog pine
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a widespread, bushy shrub of subalpine and alpine scrub, found on boggy ground and well-drained stony terraces [<i>Halocarpus bidwilli</i>, previously <i>Dacrydium bidwilli, D. colensoi </i>; aka Bidwill's pine.]

>  The river was running over boulders, some of them as big as a house, and then between walls of rock, the banks were being of the same boulders, covered with the worst scrub I ever saw - a kind of manuka, grass, trees and bog pine all twined together. <i>(Mar 25, 1875,Nelson Evening Mail,2</i>)

>  The river was running over boulders, some of them as big as a house, and then between walls of rock, the banks being of the same boulders, covered with the worst scrub I ever saw - a kind of manuka, grass, trees and bog pine all twined together. <i>(Mar 25, 1875,Nelson Evening Mail,2</i>)

>  A few hundred feet up the bush gave place to a belt of stunted bog pine, above which sparsely scattered tussock tufts obtained a grudging existence, and then came peaks of bare granite. <i>(Apr 28, 1904,Southland Times,1</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> To the west of this valley southern rata is found ... joined on the ridges by yellow silver pine and bog pine (<i>Dacrydium bidwillii</i>) ... <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,163</i>)

>  A remnant bog pine and mountain toatoa community of great scientific interest is part of the 396ha set aside under the Ben Dhu [Station tenure] review. The property is in the Upper Waitaki Basin, west of Omarama. <i>(Dec 5, 2005,Press, The,B4</i>)

>  <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/85</i>) [NOTES: (Buller) [=?]]

>  <b>WILSON</b> Bog pine (<i>Dacrydium bidwillii</i>) has been reported many times on Stewart Island, but plants so called appear to be only forms of pink pine ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,48</i>)

>  Woody scrub, including bog pine and mountain toatoa, grows around the edge of the clearing. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,66</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> <i>Dacrydium Bidwillii</i> bog pine <i>Dacrydium Colensoi</i> bog pine <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>Dacrydium Colensoi</i> bog pine <i>(1896,Dunedin Field Club Plant Catalogues</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  ... the track leads on to low peaty land ... which has recently been covered by a young forest of bog-pine (<i>Dacrydium Bidwillii</i>). <i>(1911,TrNZI,43/248</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> ...a group of conifers formerly included in <i>Dacrydium</i>; silver pine (<i>Lagarostrobos colensoi</i>), yellow silver pine (<i>Lepidothamnus intermedius</i>), bog pine (<i>Halocarpus bidwilli), H.biformis </i>and manoao (<i>H. kirkii</i>). <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,40</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [aka bog-rush]

>  <i>bog-rush [s] Schoenus pauciflorus </i>Hook. <i>Schoenus pauciflorus</i> <i>Ibid.</i> 65 <i>rush, bog- [s] Schoenus pauciflorus</i> Hook.f. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,7</i>)

 <i>n.</i> <i>Gaimardia ciliata</i>

>  shearer's handpiece - bog-iron, boggi or boggle-eye. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### bog-saddle
 
 <i>n.</i> a boggy hill-saddle.

>  <b>HAAST</b> On the highest point between the Maruia and Grey [rivers] lies the most extensive tract of this swamp ground. Five miles from this bog-saddle ... <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,78</i>)

### bog-wallower
 
 <i>n.</i> [?Is this a real term.]

>  <b>JOURNET</b> Your horse always turns out to be tender-hoofed on dry days and a bog-wallower on wet ones. <i>(1954,Take My Tip,75</i>)

### bogan
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  This writer, who has spent her entire adult life breathing the rational, liberal Wellington air, and has traditionally sneered at law and order hardliners, caught herself uttering the phrase "bogan scum" when her aged car turned up stripped and smashed on the banks of the Hutt River. <i>(Jul 17, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  Although Dunedin is perceived as the label's [sc. Flying Nun] spiritual home, the whole shebang started as a cottage industry in the bogan-infested flatlands of Christchurch. <i>(Mar 17, 2002,Sunday Star Times,F3</i>)

>  If you believe the hype, it would seem we will never escape the pioneering, bogan spirit that has seen generations of Kiwis get hammered, fondle the chicks, puke on the lawn, and then drive home. <i>(Aug 5, 1999,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Don't be misled, mate, by the bogan image [The Mad Butcher] puts on during his frequent appearances on the radio. <i>(Jan 2000,North & South,60</i>)

>  The prize was the best bogan party the world has ever seen - a pool table, a spa pool, a hulking great stereo, all the beer and sausages and lamb chops you and your friends could gobble and the Radio Hauraki band, live at your place on a Saturday night, playing all the AC/DC you could handle. <i>(Dec 2, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  Larkin thought he had died and gone to bogan heaven. <i>(Sep 23, 1999,Evening Post,21</i>)

>  The bar gets its fair share of Goths who drink cider (it's cheap) and bogan chicks who stay ever faithful to bourbon and Coke. <i>(Nov 11, 1999,Dominion,13</i>)

>  ...the momentary appeal of bogan retro is part of the film's spirit... <i>(Sep 27, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),63</i>)

>  West Auckland is the legendary "bogan country". <i>(Jul 12, 2002,Dominion Post,A4</i>)

>  Three weeks ago they wrote his retirement tributes. Today Bogan Fella is back on track for another tilt at the New Zealand Cup. <i>(Sep 24, 2001,Sunday Star Times,B17</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "I certainly wouldn't call anyone bogan to their face. If I did, I'd probably get knocked out especially if it was in Upper Hutt on a dark night." <i>(Jun 28, 2003,Dominion Post,F2</i>)

>  Fellow bogans of Upper Hutt, let us arise, especially we fat and sloppily dressed ones. <i>(Jun 27, 2003,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Petrol heads, bogans, and gruffs - these names mean one thing to west Auckland comedian Ewen Gilmour : "westies". Gilmour believes westies live all over the country. No matter where they live they share a love of tight black jeans, the smell of burning rubber and the feel of a leather jacket. Being a westie is about lifestyle, not location. <i>(Jun 15, 1998,Evening Post,TV Week / 15</i>)

>  A stereotypical bogan or "westie" is a male clad in black jeans and black T-shirt embellished with the name of a heavy metal band. Females of this genus are normally found wearing ill-fitting jeans and lace blouses or velvet dresses and an optional speckled leather jacket. The bogan car of choice is Holden and the more grunt the better. Fluffy dice are mandatory and not optional. The bogan drink of choice is Lion Red and preferably from a crate. Bogan pastimes usually involve their car and ranged from drag racing down residential streets to peel-out sessions in vacant lots. <i>(Nov 28, 2000,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Most of us make random forays into the world of bijoux, be it something functional from Pascoes, an anonymous antique, or a bogan's delight from a fleamarket. <i>(Sep 16, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

>  Rugby bogans? I thought bogans hated sport and loved black home-knit jerseys and Escorts. <i>(May 10, 1999,Salient,43</i>)

>  Sometimes a boy and girl who can only describe each other as "sort of all right", " a real dog". "stink" or "a bogan" are obliged to go further than they want just to keep up with the group. <i>(Feb 18, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  No one will ever know just how many bogans - "Wellington westies" - lie on the ocean floor stretching out beyond Paraparaumu beach (having failed in their intoxicated attempts to swim out to Kapiti wearing upward of two pairs of black jeans). <i>(Apr 29, 1996,Dominion,21</i>)

>  So what exactly is a Westie? "I think you call us Bogans down in Wellington. <i>(Apr 24, 1996,City Voice,5</i>)

>  At a time that was already stressed enough, my grandmother became the target of a mindless, violent act of some boguns. <i>(Jul 20, 2000,Evening Post,00</i>) [NOTES: check page]

>  Reformed Otorohanga bogan Rohan Max, now of Hamilton, says "a bogan is working class, talks about loud music, fights, dogs, women they've never met, drinking and cars." <i>(Apr 2, 1997,Waikato Times,00</i>) [NOTES: get page]

>  Certainly he felt like a bogan in a china store at the flash Auckland do where they announced the three finalists last month. <i>(Jul 27, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  Dumb guitar rock made a surprise return in 2002, and the Datsuns were in the right place at precisely the right time, with the kind of music that bogans in the New Zealand backblocks have been banging their heads to for decades. <i>(Dec 21, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),61</i>)

>  It did stage too many bodgey and bogan gigs. <i>(Jun 18, 1992,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  BOGAN An unhip fellow from an unfashionable suburb. Derived, some claim, from "Bougainville", in a back-of-beyond sense. <i>(Aug 21, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  Bogans as fashion icons? It sounds unlikely, but Massey University design student Genevieve Packer's bogan-inspired fashion collection is ultra-cool... "Bogans are one of New Zealand's last bastions of anti-cool - I wanted to take that and re-interpret it in a fashion context as cool... I think bogans are something we all identify with - they are part of our national identity and we should appreciate them."... Packer's fringed leather cuffs anbd collars are inspired by bogan chicks' tasselled boots. Her line of T-shirts feature popular bogan names like Shazza, Mussa, Gazza and Bazza as well as bogan vehicles like Holdens, Cortinas and Toranas. <i>(Nov 11, 2000,Dominion,29</i>)

>  It is unacceptable for a bogan in boatshoes to abuse and insult staff.. if said bogan is unable to keep track of its personal possessions. <i>(Jul 1995,Metro,130</i>)

### boganhood
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "Westie" comedian Ewen Gilmour, whose show, <i>Full Leather Jacket</i> was part of the Laugh Festival, also embrace his boganhood. <i>(May 18, 2000,Dominion,11</i>)

### boganism
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Usually I'd consider a rugby league jersey to be a sure sign of, well, boganism. <i>(Dec 14, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),22</i>)

>  "Bogans are part of our cultural identity - a lot of people will deny it, but so many things are attributed to boganism," Packer says. <i>(Jun 5, 2001,Evening Post,00</i>)

>  Bogans are my favourite people. I return to my essential boganism from time to time. I get in the car and rev it up and down the road a bit. Then - not at the same time, of course, I swig a few beers in quick succession and take myself back to a simpler life. <i>(May 18, 2000,Dominion,11</i>)

### boganity
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  And while they've thrown off the shackles of boganity (notably the three feet of hair that once encircled his head and torso) there's still a bit of the Metallica about Shihad. <i>(May 18, 1997,Sunday Star Times,F3</i>)
