### bogie
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  At the bins, the tubs having been emptied into enormous containers, the coal is screened (sifted) ... and loaded into hoppers ... [which] are fitted into a four-wheeled frame called a bogie. <i>(1948,Our Own Country,103</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  BOGEY: A framework consisting of a carriage and 4 wheels used for carrying logs on bush tramways <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

### bogie lokey
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MAHONEY</b> A new type of lokey appeared that was distinctive to trams: the bogie lokey. This specialist type was first developed in the United States timber industry. A boiler and engine were mounted on a chassis riding on two or more bogies. All bogie wheels were driven by driveshafts and gears. <i>(1998,Era of the Bush Trams in NZ, The,14</i>)

### bogtrotter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FARRELL</b> Mary Agnes's older brother Leo played the jovial Irishman, yarning and cracking with the men in the bar, while in private despising them for the bogtrotters they were, the feckless idle Paddies of the jokes and the skits, content to root a bare living from the sour coastal pakihi, exactly as they might have done at home. <i>(1992,Skinny Louie Book,54</i>)

### Bohea struggle
 
 <i>phr.</i> a tea-fight, bun-fight

>  <b>HODDER</b> `Bohea struggles', or tea parties are common [in Nelson]. <i>(1862,Memories of NZ Life,52</i>)

### Bohemian
 
 <i>n.</i> a Sudeten German settler in the Puhoi district north of Auckland [ca1964 J. Winchester] (See [cf. Dalmatian](../D#dalmatian))

### boil
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Crossed the Whytangi in two streams, they meet 200 yards below in a boil where we nearly lost one dod [sic, dog]. <i>(May 4, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,36,1950 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the act of making tea in a billy (See [boil-up](../B#boil-up),[boil the billy](../B#boil-the-billy))

>  <b>GILKISON</b> But the best part of the tramp on the home hills is the mid-day `boil.' ... True, on other expeditions we boil the billy ... <i>(1940,Peaks, Packs & Mountain Tracks,82</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> We halted at the Saxon for a `boil'. Each filled his small mustering billy and held it over the fire on the end of the mustering stick, ... <i>Ibid </i> 26 We had a boil and then started the ascent. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,8</i>)

>  <i>boil ... 2. ... NZ & Austral. colloq</i>. (also <i>boil-up</i>) ct of making tea. <i>3. ... boil the billy, boil up NZ</i> &<i> Austral. colloq</i>. make tea. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,78</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 105, BILLY]]



 
 <i>v.</i> boil down, to reduce by boiling, formerly, esp. sheep carcases to produce tallow, try out

>  <b>CAPT JOY</b> .. began to boil at 7 our boats returned [445] June 5 cut in our whale and began to boil this is a Monstrous whale 10 feet bone <i>(May 31, 1836,Log of Mary Mitchell</i>) [NOTES: 1836 May 31 Capt. Joy Mary Mitchell log in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 444]



 
 <i>v.</i> boil the billy, boil up, (informal) to have a break from work, esp. to make tea,

>  `She boild! Mugs up. Here's the cow; who's got some grit.' <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering] a boil-up or cup of tea.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> By the time we arrived at Anna it was well past mid-day, and Des and I decided that we would stop for a boil and give the horses half an hour's spell. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,152</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> We had a boil and then started the ascent. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,26</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. we left our horses grazing in the creek and called in for a 'boil.' <i>(1952,High Country Journey,170</i>)

### boil down
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to dispose of unwanted sheep by cooking them and selling the tallow, especially prior to refrigeration of meat for export in 1882.

>  <b>FULLER</b> .. it may be required to boil down other than very old sheep for tallow ... <i>(1859,Five Years' Residence in New Zealand,165</i>)

>  .. they [graziers] would elect to boil down their stock which are now fat rather than to keep them till March or April. <i>(Nov 29, 1872,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  The parts retained will boil down to an average 15 lb to 20 lb of tallow. <i>(May 14, 1883,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  Old done ewes will, I think, be almost unsaleable ... the sooner they are boiled down the better. <i>(Feb 3, 1906,Farmers' Union Advocate,12</i>)

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFIELD</b> The only way surplus sheep could be properly disposed of was to boil them down for tallow. <i>(1961,Agriculture,2</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> Then came the outbreak of scab, and thousands of sheep had to be slaughtered to boil down for tallow. <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,159</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> The first sheep shorn at Tarndale were from the straggle muster of the autumn of 1881 and their wool was taken ... for shipment from Lyttelton. Five thousand sheep were boiled down soon after, ... <i>(1969,Molesworth,53</i>)

>  ... as yet the sheep-owners have not been driven to `boil down' but expect to do so as their stock increases, ... <i>(1850,Speeches & Documents,27</i>) [NOTES: (1850) 1971 Speeches and Documents 27 quoting July 8 Godley letter]

>  <b>RIVES</b> I loafed about the station and had a look at the 12,000 wethers they had got in from which they were to draught 7,000 to boil down. After lunch I walked down with W. to look at the boiling down house. ... The boiling down house is about two miles from the homestead to avoid the stench. It is built of galvanised iron. <i>(Mar 24, 1875,Jottings on the Spot,16</i>)

>  If a squatter ... should proceed to boil down the whole flock ... he would be denounced as a lunatic ... <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/184</i>) [NOTES: (Firth)]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> Practically all the stock [sc sheep] on the runs in New Zealand is fat; and, as there is an over-supply for consumption, squatters are forced to turn their fat stock [96] into money by boiling them down for their tallow. There is a boiling-down works at Featherston, ... where thousands of these large fat sheep ... are boiled down. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,95,1935 ed.</i>)

### boil out
 
 <i>v.</i> (whaling) to try out (See [cf. boil down](../B#boil-down))

>  <b>RHODES</b> ... began to boil out. <i>Ibid </i>20 Two strange sail in sight boiling out. <i>Ibid </i> 22 At 10 began to boil out. <i>Ibid</i> 23 Employed clearing the decks and boiling out <i>(1836,Whaling Journal,17</i>)

### boil over
 
 <i>v.</i> [ca1900 Jane Morison A song or catch sung to tune of `What Shall we do with the drunken sailor.' What shall we do when the billy boils over [repeat] Nelly lie over close to the wall.]

### boil the billy
 
 <i>phr.</i> to make tea in a billy; to stop for a smoko or tea and a snack (See [billy](../B#billy),[boil n](../B#boil-n),[brew up](../B#brew-up))

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> No break for a smoke, no boil the billy, only your cold tea to drink at mid-day. <i>(1974,Sugarbag Years,90</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> We ... boiled the billy and feasted on damper and tea,/ <i>(1897,Station Ballads,48,1945 ed.</i>) [NOTES: `Over the Ranges']

>  <b>HOSKEN</b> One day, a resident of the Mackenzie Country, when travelling in a dray on the Braemar Road, overtook a young swagger. `Throw your swag up, and get in,' he remarked, `and we'll boil the billy soon.' But this young Englishman, a stranger to New Zealand and its ways, not knowing what `boiling the billy' meant and mistrustful of his friend's rather wild and unshaven appearance, refused the kind offer and decided to push on. <i>(1964,Life on a Five Pound Note,34</i>)

>  <b>HEBERLEY</b> [We] boiled the Billy and made some Tea out of tawa bark. <i>(1839,Autobiography,87</i>)

>  <b>GOLDIE</b> No wood - not as much as would boil the billy. <i>(Dec 22, 1861,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Dec 22 John Goldie's Diary in 1947 Beattie Pioneers Explore Otago 87]

>  <b>CAIRD</b> ... boiling the `Billy.' <i>(1874,NZ Sheepfarming,116</i>)

>  boil ... 2. ... NZ & Austral. colloq. (also boil-up) act of making tea. 3. ... boil the billy, boil up NZ & Austral. colloq. make tea. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 105, BILLY]]

>  <b>MOFFAT</b> ... I went up the left-hand branch, boiled the billy, ... <i>(1896,Adventures by Sea and Land,72,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> We stop - boil the billy and have tea and herings - [?sic] <i>(1907,Urewera Notebook,48,1978 Gordon ed.</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> With the usual colonial freedom, we boiled the billy and had a meal in one of the huts before shouldering our loads. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,127</i>)

>  At 2,200 ft the billy is boiled. <i>(1910,TrNZI,42/17</i>) [NOTES: (Aston)]

>  We boiled the billy, and after a sumptuous lunch set out for the waterfall. <i>(Apr 1911,Kai Tiaki,57</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> `Boiling the billy' ... when you boil the `billy', you make tea, voila [a-grave] tout. <i>(1911,In Maoriland Bush,150</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> ... they boiled the billy for supper, which they had on the banks of the stream, ... <i>(1933,Tides of Youth,63</i>)

>  ... stop to boil the billy. <i>(Aug 1934,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,18</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> They had ridden together ..., and she had taken him to a picnic ... in a favourite little river-bed, where they boiled a billy near a rare clump of trees ... <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,320</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> His first task each morning was to boil the billy and prepare breakfast. <i>(1937,Thomas,27</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> The next halt was at Waipatiki stream, where the horses were unloaded again and allowed to fend for feed, while the packman boiled the billy and had lunch. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,9</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> ... we lingered there and boiled the billy for our tea. <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,121</i>)

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> The only thing I had forgotten [in my musterer's equipment] was a walking stick, which was used for crossing creeks and also for boiling the billy. <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,37</i>)

>  <b>NEAVE</b> At the top [of the path at Wanaka], the billy was boiled for the tea break, and ... somebody tapped the billy to induce the tea leaves to sink. <i>(1980,Land of Munros, The,47</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> I was the `new chum' so it was my job to boil the billy [for the bush gang] <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,59</i>)

>  <b>TUI FLOWERS</b> On back-country trips, it's customary to <i>boil the billy</i> (heat water in a small can over a campfire for tea). <i>(Jan 14, 1987,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: 1986 a U.S. food-writer's written remarks: p.c.Tui Flower NZ Woman's Weekly (14 Jan 1987)]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> We boiled the billy ... <i>(1863,West Coast Notes,2</i>)

>  `She boild! Mugs up. Here's the cow; who's got some grit.' <i>(Apr 17, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> `Not as much firewood left as would boil the billy,' Maurice said afterwards, with much exaggeration. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,199</i>)

>  <i>boil the billy,</i> stop for a meal; <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),3</i>)

>  <b>BRUCE</b> A little after mid-day they arrived at a stream where the silent man threw down his swag and boiled his billy. <i>(1977,Life in the Hinterland of the South Island,32</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> This boundary was always debatable ground - it was always imperatively necessary that claims should be `pegged out', and this rule was of necessity `more honoured in the breach than in the observance.' Pegs were not to be had, unless imported, and when introduced stood a great chance of helping to boil the neighbour's `billy'.' <i>(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries  in Otago,80,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> It was midday when he roused himself, opened his tucker-bag, and made a fair meal of what he found there. He did not boil his billy, ... but contented himself with a drink from a small stream that ran in a gully near by. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,51</i>)

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> Before the real Australian guides you out of the bush, he will probably halt at some place where there is water and say, `How about boiling the billy?' In saying that he is not asking you for a cigarette, a pipe of tobacco, a chew of gum. ... He is simply extending to you the original and (I hope) the everlasting hospitality of all Australia; he is suggesting that he make a brew of tea. <i>(1945,If You'd Care to Know,99</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [shepherding, mustering and hunting] [originally] to light a fire and heat water for tea; [recently] to have a cup of tea/coffee.

>  <b>CAIRD</b> .. I rose from my incumbent position, and began to assist in the preparations for lighting the fire, and boiling the 'Billy.' <i>(1874,Notes on Sheepfarming in NZ,16</i>)

>  It was a most uncomfortable situation - not a stick to boil the billy and nothing but big snow tussocks for the horses to eat. <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,74</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> When you boil the billy, you make tea. <i>(1911,Maoriland Bush,150</i>) [NOTES: KOEBEL In Maoriland Bush]

>  <b>DREAVER</b> My favourite memories were of how pleasant it was to boil the billy in a sheltered gully at the top tailing pens at Shingly Creek, and after a day's tailing to sit back and relax for a while. <i>(1994,Catlins River Wisp Run,57</i>)

### boil up
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [mustering, hunting, tramping] to make a billy of tea out-of-doors.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> In some back-country districts in Canterbury, it is the practice for musterers .. to boil up .. when cirumstances permitted during their beat. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,35</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. if ever I saw the practice of boiling up on the hill carried to the extreme, it was that day. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,86</i>)

>  <b>MILLS</b> Apparently satisfied that here at least was one motorist who knew how to go through stock he disappeared from view again. Coming to the end of the mob, the reason became obvious. He was "boiling up" over the bank. <i>(1966,Huntaway,195</i>)

>  <b>JANES & ROWE</b> When we reached a point overlooking the basin where the Trent plunges into the bush we boiled up and ate our lunch ... <i>(1994,Great New Zealand Hunting Stories,30</i>)

>  <b>CURTIS</b> We were lucky enough to find a dry overhang under which we could shelter and boil-up. <i>(1996,Beneath the Mountain Mist,111</i>)

>  <b>CURTIS</b> .. we did not linger there, but hurried away to find a sheltered spot where we could boil up. <i>(1996,Beneath the Mountain Mist,142</i>)

>  <b>WATERSON</b> It must be about kai time. We'd better boil up. [boil-up] <i>(1999,Some Buggers I've Seen,53</i>)

>  <b>56</b> They cooed out, and while he skinned the spiker they found a possie in a bit of a trog and boiled up. <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

### boil-up
 
 <i>n.</i> a meat and vegetable stew [noun& attrib. aka boil up]

>  ...the days of a boil up on the marae are being reinvented by keen chefs and entrepreneurs. <i>(Aug 21, 2004,Dominion Post,E18</i>)

>  Meal. It would definitely be a boil-up. You take a big hunk of meat, some spuds and a couple of other veges, throw them in a pot and you're away. <i>(May 3, 2003,Dominion Post,F8</i>)

>  When Karen, who cooked for 13 people a day on $150 a week, served Rick a boil-up in the first episode, I'm sure middle (course) New Zealand sniffed. <i>(May 18, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),23</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Sean could smell roast meat, watercress and puha boil-ups, smoked fish... <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,217</i>)

>  There is also an "eye of the cat" (karupoti) and an "egg of the seagull" (huakaroro), a hard yellow waxy variety which is a favourite for boil-ups of meat and vegetables. <i>(Jun 27, 2001,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  Neophytes will receive an authentic introduction to pork bones and puha (only the correct term is kai tutura or "boil-up", bros) and who knows, they might even venture to order their first kina. <i>(Aug 10, 2000,Evening Post,21</i>)

>  He was so calm and more concerned about the fish boil-up he missed on the way back from being out fishing after my emergency call. <i>(Dec 6, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,2</i>)

>  The idea was that he'd be a role model for Maori, showing them the good effects of being a "good old Maori boy who likes a boil up". <i>(Aug 9, 2001,Dominion,6</i>)

>  Surviving on their father's labourer's wage, the staple dish was "boil-up" - meat and vegetables boiled together in one cauldron-sized pot. <i>(Jun 21, 2003,Dominion Post,F6</i>)

>  He's cut down the "boil-ups" to once a week. "But I eat more Weet-Bix, for example, for breakfast." <i>(Nov 2, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  The idea of eating anything else green took some getting used to, as did giving up a lifetime of greasy food, which started with boil-ups, brisket and mutton. <i>(May 10, 2006,Dominion Post,A10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> tea (in a billy), often replaced by post-WW2 brew-up.

>  On patrols, he [the NZ Mounted Rifleman] is characterised by a fine capacity for busines [sic] and a passion for innumerable `boil-ups' for the ever welcome mug of tea that helps so much to relieve the tedium of dull, weary hours. <i>(Aug 15, 1918,Kia-ora Coo-ee,2/16,1981 Second Series ed.</i>)

>  We had a welcome boil up. <i>(Aug 1934,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,52</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTSON</b> No opportunity was lost of having a `boil up.' <i>(1938,With the Cameliers in Palestine,113</i>)

>  <b>ORBELL</b> At the midday stop, some people always insist on a boilup. <i>(1952,Comfort & Commonsense in the Bush,66</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> [caption] Shooters halt for a `boil-up' after travelling several hours up a creek bed ... <i>Ibid.</i> 125 ... having had our proverbial `boil-up' ... <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,Plate VI</i>)

>  The billy brings to our mind the inevitable <i>boil-up</i>, the boiling of the billy for a cup of tea. No one seems to have collected this expression. It may be pure New Zealand. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,26</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail']

>  Midday boil-up was at the end of the first long flat, after which our route lay over river boulders to a small flat ... <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,48</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>boil-up</i> tea-break, mostly among thirsty trampers boiling the billy. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,17</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural, informal) [mustering, hunting, tramping] a cup of tea on the hill. [Noun.]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> VI Shooters halt for a 'boil-up' after travelling several hours up a creek bed. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,48</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> Our first comments about a miserable old sod, who would knock off for a boil-up while the hired help ... <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,66</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> While the shepherds had a boil-up, the sheep would string well up the face and were ready for the final lift up to the top, and through the rimrock to the easy country above the station buildings. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,92</i>)

>  <b>WATERS</b> I can still see that little billy clipped on to the saddle, and the saddle bag with the necessary ingredients for a boil-up and a bite at any time. <i>(1976,Mostly a Farmer,124</i>)

>  <b>CURTIS</b> About mid-morning we had our first boil-up and, as usual, we selected an idyllic spot. <i>(1996,Beneath the Mountain Mist,41</i>)

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> A good rest at the water and several boil-ups and they were as ready again as they would ever be. <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,84</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the boiling of clothes (in a copper). [Noun.]

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> ... first `boil up', Rangitoto, 18th October, 1925. <i>(1946,21 Years with Boots and Pack,4</i>)

>  So long as there are babies ..., there is washing to be done daily, ... Once a week or once a fortnight there's a copper boil-up. <i>(Oct 1949,Here & Now,17</i>)

### boiled as an owl
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Finally, he was as boiled as an owl. <i>(Oct 2, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/171 : 10</i>)

### boiled baby
 
 <i>n.</i> a (brown) blancmange-type dessert [ca 1941 slang (also boiled Maori?)]

### boiled dog
 
 <i>n.</i> pretension, affectation

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>boiled dog</i>. `Side'; New Zealanders': from ca. 1910. Perhaps on <i>boiled shirt</i>. [Supp. Also Australians' ca. 1918: <i>put on dog</i>.] <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,75</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Other twentieth century New Zealand expressions ... include ...<i>boiled dog,</i> `side' or affectation. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,56</i>)

### boiled lolly
 
 <i>n.</i> a boiled sweet.

>  Then come the bloody boiled lollies. <i>(Jun 15, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  <b>HOLCROFT</b> As a schoolboy I had to go off early to bed ... Early evening ... I was given one boiled lolly - a blackball - from an old, curiously ornamented tin ... <i>(1984,Way of a Writer, The,6</i>)

>  <b>GIBBONS</b> Pencil cases, pencils, boiled lollies and small bars of chocolate were the normal sort of birthday gifts. <i>(1988,Recollections (various authors/titles),37</i>)

>  It reminds me of an evening around the brazier: dried hedge-wood, tobacco and medicinal herbs predominate, then comes a sweet vanilla layer combined with cassis like yummy boiled lollies, with a final whiff of lamb chops. <i>(Apr 7, 2010,Capital Times,18</i>)

### boiler
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. burst one's boiler](../B#burst-one's-boiler))

>  <b>DUFF</b> A neighbour whose bull I had arranged to hire told me a few days ago that `the old fool had knocked himself out' and was now `finished - a boiler'. <i>(Jan 13, 1951,Shepherd's Calendar,1</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a surplus sheep, boiled down for tallow.

>  At the Rangiora sheep market recently for the first time for many years, 'boilers' were sold, the prices being 1/- to 3/6 each. In addition to the fat sheep coming forward, a few large lines of boilers are waiting their turn. <i>(Mar 1907,NZ Farmer,183</i>)

>  Although large lines have been booked for boiling-down purposes, boilers have not yet been handled in any quantity as yet. <i>(Mar 1907,NZ Farmer,185</i>)

### boiler room
 
 <i>n.</i> (rugby) (See [cf. engine room](../E#engine-room))

>  There was to be no solo tries this time but he went about his work in the boiler room efficiently. <i>(Jul 16, 2000,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

### boilermaker
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DUGGAN</b> [Riley's Handbook] ... the afternoons my own [in the pub] until five ... when the railwaymen come in for their boilermakers, whisky and a beer chaser. <i>(1970,O'Leary's Orchard,150</i>)

### boiling down
 
 <i>n.</i> [(attrib. uses)]

>  The fine grassy land bordered on the overstocked district of Sydney - the `boiling-down' system was then unknown - the soil overstocked, Pat Philip undiscovered. <i>(1852,Saunders' Monthly Magazine,1/</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Saunders' Monthly Magazine Vol I in 1976 Canterbury Colony 10]

>  It is said there are thousands of fat wethers ... ready for the pot, and as no market can be found for them a `boiling down' company has been established. <i>(1867,Timaru Herald</i>) [NOTES: 1867 Timaru Herald in 1938 Burdon High Country 112]

>  <b>CRAWFORD</b> The Boiling Down works ... provides a great service to the runs in freeing them of their surplus stock,... <i>(1981,Station Years,69</i>) [NOTES: 1870 McLean in 1981 Crawford Station Years 69]

>  <b>JACKSON</b> I believe [the kea] acquired his liking for fat in the old `boiling down' days. <i>(1935,Annals of a NZ Family,132</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> After which time, assuming favourable circumstances, ... and surplus stock worth something above boiling down rate, they will give a large return for the capital laid out; <i>(1855,Journal,2/147,1980 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the process of, or tanks for, boiling down sheep carcasses to extract tallow.

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> `Boiling-down' was formerly practised to a considerable extent in Australia; the mere fat or tallow of the animal for export to England <i>being worth far more than the meat for colonial consumption</i>. `Boiling-down establishments' were formed in the pastoral districts, and the process became a regular business. The sheep or cattle were killed and skinned, the carcass thrown into the boiling vat, the fat skimmed off for export tallow, and the meat thrown away. `Boiling down' will probably remain a permanent feature of Australian grazing in the districts remote from large towns. <i>(1861,New Zealand (various authors/titles),374</i>)

>  <b>BENMORE</b> On 25 may, B.D. started on benmore; boiling down, like other unpleasant things, was usually known by its initials. <i>(May 25, 1870,Diary</i>) [NOTES: >[1870 May 25 Benmore diary] in 1981 Pinney Early Northern Otago Runs 24]

>  <b>BURDON</b> Boiling down did not begin in New Zealand until later [than the Australian 1850], as there was no need for it; ... <i>(1938,High Country,112</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> ... some run-holders set up boiling-downs [specific reference to 1880 slump] <i>(1944,Moonshine,7</i>)

>  <i>boiling down n</i>. process of rendering tallow from sheep's carcases. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the slaughtering and boiling of surplus sheep for tallow.

>  Boiling-down appears likely to become an important industry in the province of Wellington. <i>(Feb 20, 1869,Otago Witness,17</i>)

>  .. low-priced meat and boiling down are synonymous terms. <i>(Nov 29, 1872,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>KAY</b> One farming practice long gone is remembered by the name, Boiling Down paddock. The boiling-down of mutton carcasses for tallow was the end use of sheep until refrigeration was an alternative. [boiling-down] <i>(1996,Anthony Wall, Settler of Porirua,124</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep surplus to requirements, destined for the boiling-down or rendering-down works [1880s].

>  I brought in a lot of boiling downs. <i>(May 24, 1886,E.M. Hutchinson Diary</i>) [NOTES: EM HUTCHINSON Diary May 24 (1981)]

### boiling the billy
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> The lapping of the sea mingled with the bell notes of the moko moko and tui and ... that of the burning bush wood in the boiling of the `billy'. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,70</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> So that `Mother' would know he was nearing home, Chaffey habitually hung a piece of white calico on a tree stump, a boiling-the-billy distance away from the whare, ... <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,24</i>)

### boiling-down house
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RIVES</b> I loafed about the station and had a look at the 12,000 wethers they had got in from which they were to draught 7,000 to boil down. After lunch I walked down with W. to look at the boiling down house. ... The boiling down house is about two miles from the homestead to avoid the stench. It is built of galvanised iron. <i>(Mar 24, 1875,Jottings on the Spot,16</i>)

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> ... the latter [sc. offal] are taken to the boiling-down house, on the opposite side of the road, where tallow is made, ... <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,79</i>)

### boiling-down plant
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) farm or district equipment for the rendering of tallow from sheep carcasses [esp. 1860s-1880s.]

>  <b>THORNTON</b> In the huge Molesworth block at the head of the Awatere Valley there was a small boiling-down plant erected at the Tarndale run in 1880... <i>(1986,NZ Heritage of Farm Buildings,221</i>)

>  <b>KAY</b> The boiling-down plant was on the neighbouring Huangarua land. [boiling-down works] <i>(1996,Anthony Wall, Settler of Porirua,124</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> There [at Tarndale station] he built a shearing shed for fifteen men, fenced a holding paddock of 2,000 acres and ordered a boiling-down plant from Andersons in Christchurch. <i>(1969,Molesworth,53</i>)

### boiling-down point
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARSHMAN</b> The ultimate value when the boiling-down point has been reached, will be ... the worth of the wool and tallow. <i>(1864,Canterbury, NZ, in 1862,41</i>)

### boiling-down price
 
 <i>n.</i> the lowest, bedrock price (See [cf. Morris boil down (fig.)](../M#morris-boil-down-(fig.)))

>  <b>WELD</b> in prudence a man should not calculate on a higher than the minimum, or boiling-down price of about 5 <i>s</i>. a-head [sic] for male (wedder) [sic] stock; ... <i>(1851,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,5</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> ... a boiling-down price for wethers would be reached in a few years. <i>(1859,Five Years' Residence in New Zealand,166</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [stock sales] a low price such as that for surplus sheep boiled for tallow.

>  <b>WELD</b> I think that in prudence a man should not calculate on higher than the minimum, or boiling-down price of about 5s a head ... <i>(1860,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,13</i>)

>  Store pigs were hardly saleable which is easily accounted for by the almost entire stoppage of the boiling down establishments in this district - sheep ruling much above boiling-down rates. <i>(May 8, 1880,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  .. every spring it [spring mutton] appears scarce and dearer than the previous one, from 15s to 17s per head being a substantial difference to the boiling-down price of a few years ago. <i>(Oct 1890,NZ Farmer,410</i>)

>  Old ewes that had fallen in condition were parted with at less than boiling-down rates. [boiling-down rate] <i>(Apr 1908,NZ Farmer,323</i>)

### boiling-down works
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We ate our campoven bread and fat chops, and dream 'd of great flocks to be drafted off every year to boiling-down works. <i>(Aug 7, 1890,Otago Witness,36</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> Practically all the stock [sc sheep] on the runs in New Zealand is fat; and, as there is an over-supply for consumption, squatters are forced to turn their fat stock [96] into money by boiling them down for their tallow. There is a boiling-down works at Featherston, ... where thousands of these large fat sheep ... are boiled down. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,95</i>)

>  Drive with W.Beetham to his boiling down works and meat preserving factory [at Masterton] <i>(Aug 31, 1885,Chudleigh Diary,335,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> Boiling down works had also been started at Green Island. Merinos were boiled down for their fat and pelts : and as the hind legs of merinos contained no fat they were given away. Jock Graham did a roaring trade in Dunedin by purchasing carrots in the Taieri and tying three of them to a hind leg of a sheep. This was known as a fiddle and he would hawk them through the streets and dispose of them for sixpence. <i>(1937,Thomas,153</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> Two years later [sc. 1869] boiling down works were started at Washdyke in South Canterbury. <i>(1938,High Country,112</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> ... I do not want to leave station life without mentioning one feature that was beginning to appear on most stations in the 'seventies. These were the boiling-down works. <i>(1950,My First 80 Years,20</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Mr. Nairn had a boiling down works and large wool dump and storage shed at Pourere, and wool and tallow used to be shipped direct from Pourere. [1880's] <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,123</i>)

>  <b>36</b> In the succeeding two years the ex-coke-shoveller, boundary-rider, stockman, boiling-down works labourer, shearer, rouseabout, ... examined a mining boom ... <i>(Feb 10, 1921,Quick March</i>)

>  Another [photograph] shows the boiling-down works, another the Richmond stockyards. <i>(Jan 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 61/68</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> Day after day we saw mobs of dusty sheep cramming the road, kept in order by busybody little dogs. They were en route to the boiling-down works. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,27</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) farm or district equipment for the rendering of tallow from sheep carcasses [esp 1860s-1880s].

>  <b>WILSON & FRASER</b> .. after the boiling-down works opened at Woodlands, large drafts were sent there every year. <i>(1936,Book of the Pioneers, The,30</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> In 1887 the station established its own boiling-down works. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,65</i>)

>  <b>THORNTON</b> .. excess sheep had been a real problem as they either had to be disposed of in boiling-down works or simply destroyed. <i>(1986,NZ Heritage of Farm Buildings,9</i>)

### boilover
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) an upset or unexpected result

>  The students caused a boil-over in Wairarapa senior club limited over cricket on Saturday defeating previously unbeaten Landsdowne. <i>(Dec 7, 2005,Wairarapa News,31</i>)

>  Oliver.. had no hesitation in describing the boilover the gutsiest win he had had in the blue and gold jersey. <i>(Apr 6, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

### bok choy
 
 <i>n.</i> [in ODE as US spelling of PAK CHOI] (See [also Chinese cabbage](../A#also-chinese-cabbage))

>  Bok Choy:... Karanda says the leafy vegetable which tastes a little like cauliflower, is used a lot in everyday meals in China and has found a widespread popularity among Europeans. <i>(Feb 6, 2002,Capital Times,17</i>)

>  Statistically, I'm not going to find a bok choy nibbling, shiraz drinking, male model with a penchant for picking up my dry-cleaning and spontaneous Pacific Island holidays who wants to hang out for no more than 24 hours at a time. <i>(Mar 10, 2002,Sunday Star Times,D3</i>)

>  Bok Choi, which is similar to silver beet, is grown from flowering stems as well as its leaves. <i>(Feb 3, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  Bok choy and other Asian brassicas are also in peak condition at this time of the year. Large bok choy can be rather chewy... <i>(Jul 27, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

### bokaka
 
 <i>n.</i> a pukeko (See [(1) pokaka (hinau); (2) pukeko](../(#(1)-pokaka-(hinau);-(2)-pukeko))

>  While thinking of greatness in C Coy., one will never forget C.S.M. Flackton, more frequently referred to as the "Bokaka." Notwithstanding the elasticity and length of his shapeless limbs, try as he may, he will never succeed in getting Billy Whitlock's mantle to fit him. <i>(1918,Link, The,31</i>)

### bokako
 
 <i>n.</i> . [<i>Elaeocarpus hookerianus</i>, similar to hinau; aka pokaka, bokaka]

>  <b>DEWAR</b> At the season when bokakas dropped their berries the wild pigs made a migration from tree to tree, and a `Captain Cooker' was not despised [in Southland c1900]. [fn] Bokaka : Pokaka. <i>(1953,Chaslands,82</i>)

### boke
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pukeko](../P#pukeko))

### Bokkies
 
 <i>n.</i> the Springbok rugby union team

>  <b>McGEE</b> ... a Pinetree bestriding the '60s with a sheep under each arm, the Bokkies in '73 - <i>(1981,Foreskin's Lament,93</i>)

### boko
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) though gen. Engl. slang for `nose' (OED2 1859), infreq. in NZ use (esp. since the 1920s), and occas. perceived as `head', (See [cf. poke](../P#poke))

>  <b>GRIFFITHS</b> one hears something like this in NZ without knowing how to spell it: `bang him on the boko'; but also something like `bonko <i>(1987,Personal Communication</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> as a form of address for a male [slang]

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> ... but New Zealand was, well, a bit behind the times, a bit slow, eh, boko? <i>Ibid.</i> 149 Amusing beggar too, with his strange expressions [? from US], his okay, his boko, his sure, sure, you bet, ter-rific. <i>Ibid.</i> 242 boko cheeky form of address <i>(1984,Blackball 08,26</i>)

### bold
 
 <i>n.</i> a class of superior kauri gum

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> The scrapings [sc. of gum] were kept and sold as 'dust', the bigger pieces were known as 'nuts' and the biggest as 'bold'. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,110</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> In the earlier days of the industry the `nuts,' ranging in size from about a Brazil nut to a hazel nut, were the smallest pieces of gum then saleable. By the 1920s good quality `bold' gum - the larger, hard pieces - had become increasingly scarce, and buyers, particularly b. Bold East Coast. <i>(1972,Gumdigger, The,82</i>)

>  <b>BEALE</b> The pure translucent [kauri gum], known as `Bold East Coast' ... was [in 1850s -1860s] being used as a domestic fire-kindler. <i>(1937,Seventy Years in ... Auckland,11</i>)

### bold colonial boy
 
 <i>phr.</i> ironic form of wild c. b.

>  <b>CHARL;ES</b> Our bold colonial boy was completely overcome./ He dropped his gun/ And turned to run/ And hide behind his mum./ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,55</i>)

### boldies
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>STEWART</b> Boldies, or bole-bearings, were big silvery ball-bearings, and when you hurled them into the centre of the ring the dakes would fly out in all directions; some of them would even be smashed. <i>(1983,Springtime in Taranaki,100</i>) [NOTES: 1991 edition]

### bollock
 
 <i>adj.</i> esp. <i>stark bollock(y)</i>, naked (also @n<i> in the bollocky</i>). [[Is this merely a by-form of<i> ballocky </i>P8: 1905 f. (stark) ballock-naked.: nf WF; OED2 cf <i>bollock 3</i>.]]

>  <b>WILSON</b> He's stark bollock. <i>(1950,Brave Company,142,1951 ed.</i>)

### bolster
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>REED</b> A small log, eighteen to twenty inches in diameter, and about ten feet long - called the bolster - was then placed across the line of chute near the driving creek and sunk in the clay, leaving about a third above the ground <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,177</i>) [NOTES: [get more context].]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> BOLSTER: Bulky timber fitted above axle to carry load. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,185</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 185]

>  <b>MALFROY</b> Bolster .. The cross-beam which swings on a centre-pin which passes through the centre of the trolly. The log is loaded on and fastened to the bolster only. <i>(1923,Small Sawmills,38</i>)

### bolt
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TURNER</b> [A builder] also told me that a soldering iron is called a <i>bolt</i> in Dunedin. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,178</i>)

### bolt-on
 
 <i>n.</i> additional clause

>  A defining feature of the Ngai Tahu settlement was the "bolt-ons" - a deferred selection process that allowed the tribe 12 months to buy selected Crown assets, and a perpetual right of first refusal on every surplus Crown asset in its rohe, or tribal area. <i>(Feb 10, 2001,Dominion,28</i>)

### bolter
 
 <i>n.</i> (Sport) an unexpected selection [J Boon (jboon@unitec.ac.nz) suggests this comes from the phrase "bolt from the blue". Trish Ryan (dentrish@clear.net.nz) says it comes from "infamous horse betting circles. Back before the days of judicial videos, race results were sometimes 'pre-arranged'. The backed horse would be given the perfect run in behind a nominated pacemaker. Other horses would be ridden into deadend passages or taken wide in the run home. Occasionally an uncooperative pacemaking horse would 'bolt' on its jockey and win the race - and lose large betting plunges that had been made on the intended victor." >Google search showed in used in Australia also and to a lesser degree also UK, the Netherlands.]

>  Royce Willis has yet to start in a national provincial championship game for Waikato but is the big bolter in the All Black squad. <i>(Jul 1, 1998,Dominion,1</i>)

>  [Hdg] Rookie lock bolter in All Black test squad. <i>(Jul 1, 1998,Dominion,48</i>)

>  For a number of reasons, Graham Henry and his fellow selectors made sure that there wouldn't be many bolters into the 2007 line-up .. When looking at possible bolters, there didn't appear to be many options who hadn't already been tried. <i>(Jul 26, 2007,Wellingtonian,34</i>)

>  They still produced one "bolter" in the Chiefs No. 8 Dylan Mika, who moved to that franchise when he was not wanted by the Blues. <i>(May 25, 1999,Dominion,28</i>)

>  Greg Feek: A bolter in the All Black selections. <i>(May 30, 1999,Sunday Star Times,B3</i>)

>  There would be polite appplause when the.. certainties were named, and whistles and shouts when a bolter was chosen. <i>(Jun 5, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  Jesse Ryder has put his troubles in the past and is now being tipped as a Black Caps bolter. <i>(Dec 17, 2005,Dominion Post,D1</i>)

>  What's going on? I'm after Nathan Cayleess, the bolter in the Kiwi league team, but some bloke with a strong Australian accent has sneaked into his room and answered his phone. <i>(Oct 9, 1998,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  Was it Colin Cooper telling the All Blacks bolter that he had been drafted into the Hurricanes? <i>(Oct 29, 2005,Dominion Post,D3</i>)

>  Marian Hobbs : The Broadcasting and Environment Minister is the bolter in the Cabinet. <i>(Dec 20, 1999,Dominion,2</i>)

>  ..represents the last chance for Bracewell to make some considered adjustments to his squad..and it's not beyond all possibility that he could come up with another bolter. <i>(Feb 9, 2006,NZ Herald,C18</i>)

>  Bolters are rare in the modern era . . . <i>(Oct 24, 2005,Dominion Post,D1</i>)

>  All Blacks coach Graham Henry picks two bolters for next month's norhtern hemisphere tour, but his sights are set on the 2007 World Cup. ... When the net was dragged yesterday it contained at least two old-fashioned bolters in Taranaki lock Jason Eaton and Auckland's Isaia Toeva, neither of whom have played in the Super 12. ... Bolters are rare in the modern era but the selection of Taranaki lock Jason Eaton and Auckland utility back Isaia Toeava caught them and just about everyone else by surprise. <i>(Oct 24, 2005,Dominion Post,D3</i>)

>  The teenager, who was Graeme Henry's bolter for the touring team to Britain, gets his first cap as the All Blacks chase only their second Grand Slam since 1978. <i>(Nov 23, 2005,NZ Herald,A1</i>)

>  Every All Black coach has his favourites and many have tried to leave their mark by picking a bolter and hoping he comes off as a star. <i>(Apr 30, 2000,Sunday Star Times,B5</i>)

>  While the squad contains few surprises, with Macdonald perhaps the only "bolter", it does signal the end of Robin Brooke's international career. <i>(May 29, 2000,Dominion,32</i>)

>  Tracie Palmer of Christchurch raises the matter of the word <i>bolter</i> applied to a rugby player.... She was watching the televised announcement of the national team when one commentator turned to the other and said, "Leon McDonald's a <i>bolter</i>. The reply was, "Yes, he's a <i>bolter</i>"... If she [was interested in racing], she would have heard the term <i>bolter</i> applied to any horse that wins unexpectedly. It's really a synonym for <i>outsider</i>. Horses are half-witted, and can <i>bolt</i> at any time. <i>(Jun 3, 2000,Dominion,20</i>)

>  And it's thought Douglas Howlett might hold on to his right winger's spot, although Chiefs flyer Roger Randle - with a dozen Super 12 tries - could be a bolter, depending on Mitchell's thinking. <i>(May 19, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

>  When the net was dragged in yesterday it contained at least two old-fashioned bolters in Taranaki lock Jason Eaton and Auckland's Isaia Toeava, neither of whom have played in the Super 12. <i>(Jan 24, 2005,Dominion Post,D1</i>)

>  Other features are... Several bolters making their way into the New Zealand XV from outside the two summer World Cup squads. <i>(Apr 9, 1995,Sunday Star Times,B1</i>)

>  I repeat, we cannot close off to the bolters. <i>(Apr 9, 1995,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)

>  Nevertheless, he was clearly one of the bolters to make the All Black side. <i>(Jul 22, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  The 26-year-old bolter was rewarded for an outstanding Super 14 campaign with the embattled Highlanders while his inclusion meant there was no room for the Crusaders' Kieran Read. <i>(Jun 5, 2008,Rugby News,12</i>)

### bolter's chance
 
 <i>n.</i> [Miss Betty Perkins (Middle Run, Main Rd, Paekakariki) writes on 19/09/1999 that her father was a (legal) Queensland outback bookie and used the full phrase "not a bolter's chance on a greasy track" which he said came from outback country horse meetings when the race was started by the drop of a rope. If a horse bolted on a greasy track and won, the win would be most unexpected.] (See [bolter's show](../B#bolter's-show))

>  Arguably, she was more genuine in this because, unlike most of the MPs in the House today, there was not a bolter's chance of her making Parliament at the time she signed up for Mana Motuhake. <i>(Jun 12, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

### bolter's show
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [bolter's chance](../B#bolter's-chance))

>  Before the fight for the 2002 election began in earnest, National knew it hadn't a bolter's show, so it selected candidates for 2005. <i>(Jul 23, 2002,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

>  Mr Shewan also believes that if New Zealand stands a "bolter's show" of increasing wages, retaining talent and improving living standards it will come through a material lift in education standards at all levels. <i>(Oct 4, 2006,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

### bolting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Bolting</i> - The explosive grenade inside the head of the bomb-lance that explodes when electrically detonated by the driver of the whale-chaser. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,226</i>)

### bomb
 
 <i>v.</i> to end a relationship; esp. get, be bombed to be blacklisted.

>  To bomb someone out is to tell them you've finished with them. <i>(Sep 19, 1973,Salient,11</i>)

>  John Doran had a go at me for not asking his permission to go overseas. I got bombed and I couldn't get a job as a rigger in the Labourers' Union after that. ... He just told me I'd be getting no f..... work, he told me I'd be bombed - and I'd be yard bombed. <i>(Nov 2, 1986,Sunday News,8</i>)

>  They [Pete and Sam] got up the low scree, sidled across the first face ... but they were getting bombed so they cramponed up to just below a gendarme ... <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) an old car in disrepair (in post-1960 use occas. transf. to any old movable mechanical object, e.g. `My old bomb of a lawnmower.'). [Usually in phrase "old bomb"; ca1951: heard once in Wellington -->1957 p.c. R. Mason `No doubt older than the 1950s.']

>  An "old bomb" is, except perhaps to the owner, a bad thing, being an unreliable car; a "bomb" perhaps from its tendency to self-destruct. The term is exclusive to New Zealand and Australia. <i>(Feb 14, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>) [NOTES: Ian Gordon]

>  He didn't seem to fit anywhere, but in this bomb car with nothing better to do than picking up hitch-hikers in rural New Zealand. <i>(Aug 30, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

>  <b>McGill</b> Cathy told him she had heard several bangs, thought it was those old bombs the surfies drove, backfiring. <i>(1995,Gold in the Creek,138</i>)

>  Once his bomb had been revived, Carl would remove the clamps, wind the cables and return them to Ben, offering vague recompense, which Ben invariably dismissed. <i>(Feb 23, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  There's a lot of commitment making old bombs new. <i>(Aug 3, 2001,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> And then it was racing back to town in the Beat-Up Bomb, everybody jammed in and Basil, who had been in the A.S.C. in the Desert, skiting about his driving and weaving the car all over the road. <i>Ibid.</i> 230 ... and Basil scatters the mob in the beat-up bomb and leans out and says jump in Kiwi ... but he does roll her over on the last bend into town ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,14</i>)

>  <b>MUIR</b> It's bad enough down ont the flat with every street full of louts in jalopies and Dad taking the kids for a spin in the family bomb. <i>(1960,Word for Word,231</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Good thing those johns didn't get a decent look at this bomb of yours, said Sam, folding a wad of paper to jam the window shut. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,19</i>)

>  Some have `bombs', some are entrusted with their parents' car for the occasion. <i>(May 30, 1962,Marlborough Express,6</i>)

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> I didn't think the old bomb would do more than 30 except down hill. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,66</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> The car parked beside the tent wasn't much more than an old bomb. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,163</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> We had a job shoving her into the bomb. <i>(1967,Hangover,53</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> <i>bomb</i>: Strategic nuclear device on wheels. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,182</i>)

>  OLD bombs - ageing cars peculiar to New Zealand roads - should be scrapped, motor vehicle dealers say. <i>(Oct 10, 1979,Dominion,5/4</i>)

>  <i>old bomb</i>: old car <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bomb</i> old dilapidated car or motorbike; c.1945. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,17</i>)

>  <b>KEINZLY</b> `...all got cars Kathy - not all heaps either, a lot of them are expensive jobs which they've bought since being here.' <i>(1960,Tangahano,20</i>)

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> However, the excellent condition in which these old cars (`bombs') are kept is a great credit to the owners ... <i>Ibid.</i> 137 Eventually it had to happen - one of us bought a `bomb'. It was a game little car; although its ... blue paint invited confidence, as did its `warrant of fitness', it seemed to possess everything but reliability. <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,52</i>)

>  High prices for old cars, lots of old "bombs" on the roads. <i>(Aug 5, 1968,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  Why is it that people can buy old bombs, get a warrant of fitness, let it expire.. and get off scot free? <i>(Jan 27, 1968,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  <b>DAVIES</b> After breakfast next morning, they offered to drive him around in an old bomb they had somehow acquired. <i>(1975,Outback,106</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> specifically, to dislodge skifield snow with explosives, in order to forestall avalanches

>  "We had a day last year when we had a huge avalanche hazard and we bombed the slopes all day..." <i>(Jul 6, 2005,NZ Herald,E1</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (informal) (transitive use) fail to exploit, waste

>  .. back they lacked precision and "bombed quite a few try-scoring opportunities". <i>(Aug 12, 2005,NZ Herald,D3</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [whaling]

>  <b>GRADY</b> <i>Bombed</i> - To thrust a bomb-lance into a whale and then to electrically detonate the grenade-head. <i>(1982,Perano Whalers,226</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a high kick; a high up-and-under near the try-line

>  And rugby types jeer at players who leave the high balls (`bombs' in league) to others ... <i>(Jul 4, 1983,National Business Review,32</i>)

>  When he was playing at fullback, Mehrtens tried to ignite counterattacks by linking with Lomu or putting in bombs which had the giant wing pressuring the Springbok defences around their 22. <i>(Aug 26, 2001,Sunday Star Times,B2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> graffiti

>  Graffiti Speak... Bomb: Prolific painting or marking with ink. <i>(Nov 30, 2000,Evening Post,19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an illegal water-boiling device [prison slang]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Every night Junk used to pull out his water-boiling gadget, called a tea bomb, and make up a brew of illegal tea or Milo. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,77</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Every smoko time ... [they] would pull their bombs out of snooker and and brew up a cup of tea or cocoa. <i>Ibid.</i> 245 BOMB (n) Illegal water-boiling device. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,85</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a (running) jump into water, from a height, and usually with hands clasped around drawn-up legs, the object being to make the biggest splash and noise possible, hence the name

>  [Caption] Rangi's city cousin is having her first swim in a country swimmin' hole. No Screamin'! No pushin'! No shovin'l No bombs! <i>(Mar 4, 1993,Evening Post,32</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Juvenile delinquency. What about us? ... [10] ... Climbing over the tin fence into the town baths and heaving the stand planks into the water when we tired of bombs from the side and bellyfloppers from the board. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,9</i>)

>  His leap from the top of the 24-metre falls was supposed to be the mother of all bombs. <i>(Jan 26, 2005,Dominion Post,A5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [whaling]

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> One bomb was darted and the whale rose and floated on its back. <i>(1968,Turn of the Tide, The,34</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> excrement (sometimes `fart', as in drop a bomb). [ca1951 p.c. Brian Sutton-Smith, heard in a charade written by Wellington children: prob. from stink-bomb]



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i> to bombard animals with shots; shoot slap-happily or too freely.

>  <b>SEVERINSEN</b> <i>(1970,Hunt the Far Mountain,42</i>) [NOTES: see bush-happy]

### bomb-squasher
 
 <i>n.</i> [cf DARE bombsies a dropping shot in marbles] (See [also bum-squasher](../A#also-bum-squasher))

>  Culled dairy cows and culled beef animals are sent directly to the works as `boners' - that is, the bones are removed from the carcasses and the meat is used for manufactured products (as distinct from meat that is used for prime cuts). Farmers are paid schedule rates on the killing weights. <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,188</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates, aggies, aggotties, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cat's eyes, chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, molly-bars, mulley-bars [mullibars: large marbles], peelies, peewees, piss-wees, pretties, Scotties, Scottish alleys, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers, white alleys, and in more recent years ball-bearings, chippies, plastics, and woodies. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

>  <b>KEITH & MAIN</b> Marbles were the currency of childhood and there were an astonishing number of names for them, some referring to the material or colour, others to value or function in the game. There were agates, aggies, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cats-eye, [sic] chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, mollies, peelies, peewees, pisswees, pretties, Scotties, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers and whities. The march of technology added ball bearings - ballies or steelies - which were unbeatable when it came to smashing an opponent's miserable glassie. World War II[roman] brought `clayeys' often imperfectly spherical they were pathetic terracotta substitutes for real marbles. <i>(1984,NZ Yesterdays,288</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bomb-squasher</i> big marble, a menace to small marbles. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,15</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shooting of noxious animals with a volley of shots.

### Bombay Hills
 
 <i>n.</i> [esp. in phrases north/south of the Bombay Hills]

>  As far as I'm concerned there are some things that should stay north of the Bombay Hills. <i>(May 14, 1992,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  I wouldn't get north of Bombay Hills. <i>(Apr 22, 1992,TV3,Nightline</i>)

>  Bombay Hills Border Control. You are now entering Auckland. Please have your passport ready. <i>(Jun 1999,North & South,41</i>)

>  The last Labour Government held every metropolitan electorate south of the Bombay Hills except for Fendalton. <i>(Jun 13, 1992,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  One of the great bugbears about New Zealand TV programmes is everything's Auckland-oriented, that the country does not exist south of the Bombay Hills. <i>(Jun 9, 1992,Evening Post,TV Week : 3</i>)

>  The archival Sources on hand to genealogists north of the Bombay hills are a veritable goldmine. <i>(Jun 14, 1964,Dominion Sunday Times,21</i>)

>  But, whichever side of the Bombay Hills you come from (that volcanic line named after the 1863 immigrant ship, <i>Bombay</i>), getting in and out of these cities seems to be the major problem in common. <i>(Jul 9, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>) [NOTES: Listener and TV Times]

>  It was from the boys at New Plymouth Boys High, livid at his treatment, urging him to go back, a sign of his support south of the Bombay Hills. <i>(Jun 12, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),25</i>)

>  Their [sc. Auckland-based companies]idea of exports is sales north of Warkworth or south of the Bombay Hills. <i>(Jun 27, 2004,Sunday Star Times,D2</i>)

>  South of the Bombay Hills, the rest of the country generally cares little about the Queen City... <i>(2009,Older & Bolder</i>)

>  We all know that Auckland begins - or ends - at the Bombay hills. Let's make it official. It is time for the city to secede from New Zealand, rename itself Akarana and appoint Dame Catherine as our first head of state. <i>(Oct 1992,Metro,48</i>)

>  Wellington's funkv reggae soulsters, Southside (of Bombay Hills, that is). <i>(Aug 16, 1992,Dominion Sunday Times,6</i>)

>  Talk of taxpayers borrowing up to $2.5 billion to help fix Auckland's traffic mess suggests yet again that there is one set of rules for those unfortunate enough to live north of the Bombay Hills - and another for the rest of us... After all, there are roads south of the Bombays! <i>(Jun 21, 2003,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

>  <b>MCCASKILL</b> These periods saw the heyday of the squatter, the gold-miner, the sawmiller and the 'bonanza' wheat farmer. >  <b>MCLAUCHLAN</b> So ploughshares bit deep into the better land at the beginning of the seventies and what became known as "bonanza" wheat farming began <i>(1981,Farming of NZ,113</i>)

>  <b>O'CONNOR</b> In the 1880s New Zealand had been a major exporter of wheat in an age of large so-called 'bonanza' wheat farms. <i>(1970,Growth of NZ Farming 1890-1918, The,9</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> .. before long bonanza wheat farming reached its limits too. <i>(1990,Forgotten Worker, The,24</i>)

### boner
 
 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [stock sales and meat industry] a cull cattle beast.

>  Culled dairy cows and culled beef animals are sent directly to the works as 'boners' - that is, the bones are removed from the carcasses and the meat is used for manufactured products. <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,188</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> boner: beast in condition not good enough to be slaughtered and sold in carcass form, yet sutiable for boning-out into manufacturing meat such as sausage emulsion, mince etc. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> The [for children's nicknames, Petone, 1903, 1914] there is Slim Kidd, Skinny Smith, Boner or Cull Price, Bumper Wright. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,405</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [occupation]

Theirs is heavy work as they cut the meat from the shoulder blade, ribs and thigh bone and slice it into the different cuts. <i>(1986,NZ Yesterdays,76</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a cattle beast bred to provide manufacturing beef. 1. a. (farmers' and freezing works) [W3 a low-grade beef animal; nf OED2] an animal Usu. a cattle beast or an animal carcass intended for use as manufacturing (grinding) meat (mince, hamburger, sausages, etc.) and usu. graded as manufacturing

>  <i>Boner</i>: Carcasses of animals intended for use as manufacturing meat (mince, hamburger, sausages, etc.) and usually graded as manufacturing ... <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,33</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> It was March when the truck came to take away the old boners to the works. <i>(1967,Return to Open Country,140</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> `How are the heifers this season, Tiwha?' `Not bad, ... And I got a hundred or so for my boners ... [sic]' <i>(1969,Green River,f12</i>)

>  <i>Boner</i>: Old cows or bulls for slaughter to produce manufacturing (grinding) beef. Also young stock in very lean condition. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,19</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [of ox, cow or heifer beef] having little or no fat covering [usually slaughtered for processed meat products.]

>  <b>BARTON</b> Boner ox, heifer or cow. This grade is for carcasses with little or no fat covering. The carcasses are usually of dairy type. Boner bull. All bull carcasses are graded as boner. <i>(1972,Sheepfarming Annual 1972,72</i>)

>  <b>FOSTER & WRIGHT</b> For more than forty years he has been droving cattle from the north, and before that he was buying up 1000 boner cattle a month and taking them on the seven to ten day drive to Moerewa from Kaitaia. <i>(1983,Stockman Country,36</i>)

### boneseed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  An effective treatment that uses no herbicide has been found to control the coastal pest plant boneseed, says the Wellington Regional Council. Boneseed is flowering now, the best time for control... Boneseed is widely established throughout the coastal areas of the Wellington region, especially Wellington's south coast, Eastbourne, Pukerua Bay and Raumati... Boneseed produces clusters of small, yellow daisy flowers that cover the plant in late winter to early summer. The single-seed is very hard and bone-like in colour and texture when ripe (thus the name boneseed. Boneseed is a bushy shrub which grows to 2-3 metres high producing up to 50,000 seeds per plant. From South Africa, it was introduced to New Zealand as an ornamental plant and is now found throughout the North Island, mostly in coastal situations, and in the South Island. <i>(Sep 20, 2000,Norwester,8</i>)

>  The sunburst of bone-seed at the Miramar peninsula, the open faces of Mexican daisies in the Northland tunnel, the blush of lilac oxalis on Onslow Rd. <i>(Feb 22, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

>  Weeds included [in the Atlas] included species many people grew unwittingly, such as Japanese honeysuckle, banana passionfruit, climbing asparagus, boneseed, old man's beard, pampas grass and German ivy. <i>(Oct 12, 2000,Dominion,12</i>)

>  South Brighton is hosting its first Boneseed Bash, a day dedicated to eradicating the plant pest boneseed... Boneseed is an evergreen shrub up to 3 m high,m with bright yellow daisy-like flowers and long dull-green toothed leaves. <i>(Jan 18, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A12</i>)

>  <b>CROWE</b> A native of the South-West Cape area of South Africa, introduced to New Zealand in the nineteenth century as a garden plant suitable for covering dry banks and poor sandy soil in frost-free areas. However, birds soon spread the seed, and by 1870 boneseed was noticed escaping into the wild. <i>(1995,Which Coastal Plant?,32</i>)

>  <i>bone-seed</i> [s] <i>Osteospermum moniliferum</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,7</i>)

>  They "bonged" on Sunday mornings to get out of going to church, said one religious man. <i>(Jun 27, 1998,Dominion,13</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [(alcohol bonging)]

>  He writes about five young men who went on a week-long camping holiday ... taking with them a car-load of beer and a curious collection of plastic tubing and large funnels; the equipment needed for alcohol bonging: ... The bong comprises a plastic funnel joined at the base by a long plastic tube, it can be large or small. It can hold from one to four bottles of beer. One person holds the contraption high in the air while the intended recipient puts the end of the tube in his mouth and allows the beer to rush in a torrent into his stomach. ... Four bottles is said to test the digestive system to the limit and is almost impossible to keep down and so usually leads to what is called a `power chunder' or projectile vomiting. <i>(Mar 13, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,16</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> ?a marijuana 'cigarette' [<b>OED online:</b> Bong n. A kind of water-pipe used for smoking marijuana. Chiefly U.S. <b>wikipedia</b> http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bong Apocryphal history suggests the term "bong" returned to the U.S. with troops who had served in Vietnam, as a corruption of the Thai word baung, which refers to a hollow pipe or tube. <b>Internet search Feb 2006:</b> also used in N Amer, Austral, Canada, Sth Africa, Uk, Ireland.]

>  <b>MARRINER</b> Scored some nice skunk yesterday. Anyone fancy a bong or five? <i>(Jan 2001,Stonedogs,111</i>)

>  revolution in the bottom of a bong <i>(Oct 25, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>) [NOTES: marijuana or Polynesian?]

>  Wirangi is charged with two assaults on a female, possession of cannabis, possession of a cannibis utensil (bong) and theft. <i>(Feb 8, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,7</i>)

### bonga
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori or other Polynesian (See [also bong](../A#also-bong))

>  Other [White Club] members feel perfectly free to talk to me about `bongas', `a touch of the tar brush', `the jungle coming out in him' and `bloody Maoris'. <i>(Apr 3, 1998,Dominion Sunday Times,8</i>)

>  <b>MARRINER</b> You see, that's when the big men on campus - psycho Maoris and bongas to a number - that's when they choose which white boy they wanna make wear a dress for them... <i>(2001,Stonedogs,337</i>)

### bonies
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates, aggies, aggotties, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cat's eyes, chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, molly-bars, mulley-bars [mullibars: large marbles], peelies, peewees, piss-wees, pretties, Scotties, Scottish alleys, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers, white alleys, and in more recent years ball-bearings, chippies, plastics, and woodies. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

>  <b>KEITH & MAIN</b> Marbles were the currency of childhood and there were an astonishing number of names for them, some referring to the material or colour, others to value or function in the game. There were agates, aggies, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cats-eye, [sic] chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, mollies, peelies, peewees, pisswees, pretties, Scotties, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers and whities. The march of technology added ball bearings - ballies or steelies - which were unbeatable when it came to smashing an opponent's miserable glassie. World War II[roman] brought `clayeys' often imperfectly spherical they were pathetic terracotta substitutes for real marbles. <i>(1984,NZ Yesterdays,288</i>)

### boning foreman
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Company Directors were.. a former Railcorp executive;.. and Jamie McGregor, previously a boning foreman at Whakatu. <i>(Dec 7, 1992,Evening Post,3</i>)

### boning room
 
 <i>n.</i> the room in a meatworks in which the bones are removed from (usu.) beef carcases.

>  At Longburn the boning.room is idle because 12 boners have been dismissed. <i>(Mar 8, 1974,Dominion,1</i>)

>  Meat is processed in a boning or cutting room, after it comes (whole or in quarters) from the cooling floor or from the chiller. <i>(1974,NZ Journal of Agriculture,189</i>)

>  Across in the boning room, the sides of beef are put through a band saw, and the pieces travel down a conveyer belt to the boners, working at tables in pairs. Theirs is heavy work as they cut the meat from the shoulder blade, ribs and thigh bone and slice it into the different cuts. <i>(Jul 1986,Metro,76</i>)

### bonita
 
 <i>n.</i> bluenose (also blue bream, bream, Griffins silverfish, stone eye). (See [contrast bonito](../C#contrast-bonito))

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>BONITA</i> - Two fish species are taken in small quantities, Ray's Bream (<i>Brama raii</i>) and, chiefly in Cook Strait, <i>Hyperoglyphe porosa</i>. Both are commonly called Bonita or Bream and usually marketed as Groper. The common name Bonita is not to be confused with Bonito which is Skipjack Tuna, (<i>Katsuwonus pelamis</i>). <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,12</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BLUE-NOSE WAREHOU Hyperoglyphe antarctica</i> (Blue-nose grouper, griffins silverfish, bonita, deepsea trevalla) ... [302] They are caught regularly on hapuku long lines along with hapuku and bass and are sold as hapuku. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,301</i>)

>  [species name] blue nose .. [scientific name] <i>Hyperoglyphe antarctica</i> .. [other name(s)] bonita [taken by longline and trawling: contrast BONITO], blue bream, griffins silverfish, stone eye <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  the fish were warehou and bluenose, also known as bonita. <i>(Nov 28, 1986,Dominion,1</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Bluenose Hyperoglyphe antarctica</i> ... Alternative New Zealand names include stoneeye, bonita, bream, Griffin's silverfish. Deepsea trevalla in Australia. Widely distributed in temperate waters of the Southern Hemisphere ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,138</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] bonita 2051, 2060-2, 2079 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> Ray's bream [See 1970 cit. above]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> hapuka; as a fish-shop or marketing name; [[?does this merely mean that the less valuable bonita is marketed under the name of the more valuable and fancied groper].]

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>GROPER</i> (two species) (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Polyprion oxygeneios</i> (ii) <i>Polyprion moeone</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) New Zealand Groper; Hapuku (ii) Bass. (c) Other common names: (i) Hapuka, Stone-eye, Blue-nose, Bonita (ii) Deepsea Groper. (d) Principal localities where taken: [Mainly easten coasts, Foveaux, and Chathams] ... [27 Groper and Bass] should not be confused with the Australian and tropical groupers which they resemble superficially. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,26</i>)

### bonito
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FORSTER</b> On the third of September great numbers of flying fish were observed [in mid Atlantic], and a bonito (<i>scomber pelamys </i>) was caught, ... <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/47</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Katsuwonus pelamis </i>(Linnaeus). BONITO. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,45,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>~ n. a fish allied to the tunny. [Sp.] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family SCOMBRIDAE. Bonito (<i>Katsuwonus,</i> ... ) ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,224</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>BONITA </i>... The common name Bonita is not to be confused with Bonito which is Skipjack Tuna, (<i>Katsuwonus pelamis </i>). <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,12</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> BONITO Offshore anglers use bonito fillets for snapper bait and deep-sea anglers claim its dark-red flesh attracts almost every sporting fish up to shark and marlin. It is known as Mackerel, Horse Mackerel, Skippy, Skipper and Skipjack because of its habit of breaking the surface when feeding. The name `Skipjack' has also been applied however to at least half-a-dozen other species. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,267</i>)

>  [species name] skipjack .. [scientific name] <i>Katsuwonus pelamis </i>.. [other name(s)] bonito [taken by trolling and purse seine; contrast <i>bonita </i>] <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>BEGG</b> Latitude 8 N. ... In the afternoon a man caught a <i> Bonito </i>, a fish about 2 feet long and 16 inches in girth... <i>(Jul 6, 1859,Diary,6,1960 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> GRIFFIN'S SILVERFISH ... <i>Other names: Seriolella amplus </i>; blue-jaw, bonito; matiri (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,223</i>)

>  [tag in window] BLUEFISH (Bonito) <i>(Jan 28, 1987,Ephemera</i>) [NOTES: Cuba St, Wellington fish-shop]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>H[yperoglyphe] porosa </i> [(Richardson)] appears in Wellington markets at odd intervals. It is sold as bonito ... <i>(1939,TrRSNZ,69/232</i>) [NOTES: (Whitley and Phillipps)]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  GYMNOSARDA PELAMIS (Linnaeus). <i>Bonito.</i> ... Distribution: North Island coasts. Examples have been caught as far south as the Kaikoura Peninsula. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/118</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <i>Euthynnus pelamis:</i> Bonito. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> STRIPED BONITO ... <i>Other names: Katsuwonus pelamis </i>; striped tunny or tuna, skipjack, bonito, and sometimes, skipper. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,268</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> STRIPED BONITO [<i>Katsuwonus pelamis </i>] ... Striped tunny or tuna, skipjack, bonito, and sometimes skipper, are alternative names in use. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,52</i>)

>  <i>TUNA</i> (3 species excluding Albacore) (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Katsuwonus pelamis </i>(ii) <i>Thunnus maccoyi </i> (iii) <i>Thunnus albacores </i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (i) Skipjack Tuna (ii) Southern Bluefin Tuna (iii) Yellowfin Tuna. (c) Other common names: (i) Skipjack; Striped Bonito (ii) Bluefin Tuna; Southern Bluefin; Southern Tuna (iii) Yellowfin All sometimes referred to as Tunny. (d) Principal localities where taken: (i) A summer visitor to New Zealand ... (ii) Southern Bluefin Tuna is a large species of temperate Australasian seas and migrates south to Foveaux Strait in summer months. (iii) A widely distributed tropical species which ranges into northern waters ... in summer months. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,49</i>)

>  [index to common names 283-293] bonito, striped 2062 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Sarda chiliensis.</i>

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The only true mackerels recorded from Canterbury are the Pacific Bonito (<i>Sarda chiliensis </i>) and the large-scaled Tunny (<i>Gasterochisma melampus </i>) which may reach a length of 6 feet; but a related family includes the well-known Barracouta (<i>Thyrsites atun </i>), the King Barracouta, <i>Jordanidia solandrii </i> (often known locally as Kingfish, but not to be confused with the North Island Kingfish which is a form or Yellowtail) and the elongated, silvery-hued frost fish (<i>Lepidopus caudatus </i>) whose curious stranding habits can hardly yet be said to be satisfactorily explained. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,199</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 199]

### bonk
 
 <i>v.</i> in distress

### bonk scale
 
 <i>phr.</i> a scale of relative comfort (on a tramp?) (cf. ODE to bonk = to reach point of exhaustion)

>  "We call it the 'bonk scale'... If we think someone is slowing, we ask them where they are on the scale. A 1 means, 'I am bonking' - hitting the wall - 'dig a hole and bury me.' A 10 means, 'I'm feeling great - I'll carry you up the next hill.'" <i>(Apr 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 46/86</i>)

### bonker
 
 <i>n.</i> head

>  <b>McGEE</b> A bang on the bonker is a bang on the old bonker. <i>(1981,Foreskin's Lament,25</i>)

### bonnet find
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [pig dogs] to sense the presence of wild pigs from a basket on the bonnet of a vehicle.

>  <b>SWAN</b> After being introduced to pig hunting Charlie soon discovered he loved it. He is now a good finder and holder. He has learnt to bonnet find, ground trail and wind scent and he will often stick with his quarry for many hours. <i>(1992,Grunter Hunters, The,26</i>)

### bonnet finder
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a pig dog that senses the location of wild pigs from a basket on the bonnet of a vehicle.

>  .. among them bonnet-finder (a hunting dog that sits on a ute's bonnet and sniffs for pigs) ... <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

>  <b>SWAN</b> Colin put his bonnet finder onto the front of the vehicle and the search began. <i>(1992,Grunter Hunters, The,28</i>)

### bonnet finding
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the locating of wild pigs by a specially-trained dog from the bonnet of a vehicle.

>  <b>SWAN</b> The old Land Rover, rigged for bonnet finding and spot-lighting, which 'fairly flew' to get to the weigh-in before six o'clock. <i>(1992,Grunter Hunters, The,30</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i>

Joe claimed he had been sleeping in a hut between Gentle Annie and Bonnie Mary, about 20 miles from the scene of the crime. ... the Gentle Annie and Bonnie Mary he was referring to were not ladies, but a couple of mountains near Kuripapango, in the Inland Patea. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,51</i>) [NOTES: c1880s an anonymous tourist in 1959 L. Masters Tales of the Mails 51]

### bonser
 
 <i>n.</i> a great success or fine example. (See [bosker](../B#bosker),[bonzer](../B#bonzer),[bosca](../B#bosca))

>  The men responded with their Maori war-cry, which had to be repeated, and finshed up with "He's a bonser," for their host. <i>(Mar 9, 1900,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  The reading of the address was followed by the Contingent giving their war cry, with "He's a Bonser!" for the actor-manager. <i>(Mar 2, 1900,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  The latter [musical entertainer Percy Kehoe] is always springing musical surprises on his audiences, his "Railway medley" being "a bonser" as the pittite has it. <i>(Jan 30, 1896,Otago Witness,37</i>) [NOTES: pitite: a spectator who occupies a place in the pit of a theatre.]

>  Various classical scholars tell me William [a singer] is a "bosker" and a "bonser." I'll tell you if he is when I 've looked up the Latin dictionary. <i>(Mar 24, 1906,NZ Free Lance,16</i>)

>  OLD BOY writes - Can any of your readers enlighten me as to the origin or meaning of the very common school word now used - "Bonse" or "Bonser." Be it a large apple or a good stroke at tennis "Oh, that's a bonser!" we hear at once. Has it anything to do with "Bonanza"? <i>(Apr 10, 1901,Otago Witness,41</i>)

>  The Sister Agnes filly is a real "Bonser" - a chestnut with a blaze and near white hind fetlock; but what power. <i>(Aug 3, 1893,Otago Witness,30</i>)

>  ALEC ALAN WRITES: .. BONSE OR BONSER. - Repling to "Old Boy," probably the correct form is "Bonsor," a proper name - that of a famous English Rugby half-back, now applied as the hall mark of merit. <i>(Apr 17, 1901,Otago Witness,41</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> great, terrific. (See [bonzer](../B#bonzer),[bosker](../B#bosker))

>  "Would you like to go to a concert tonight?" asked Miss Ross. "Concert? You bet! Bonser!" he answered eagerly. <i>(Apr 15, 1897,Otago Witness,51</i>)

>  He saved enough to get into a business in Blenheim. This town was lively then, says Gett, doing a "bonser" trade. <i>(May 21, 1898,Marlborough Express,4</i>)

>  Bonser gime. <i>(Sep 13, 1913,Marlborough Express,16</i>)

>  By about 2 a.m. we had five real bonser trout. <i>(Mar 25, 1943,NZ Farmers' Weekly,15</i>)

 <i>n.</i>
