### bonza
 
 <i>adj.</i> (See [bonzer](../B#bonzer))

### bonzer
 
 <i>n.</i> a great success, a fine example (See [bonzer](../B#bonzer),[bosker](../B#bosker),[bosca](../B#bosca))

>  There are dozens of warships, transports etc. here, including the 'Queen Elizabeth' - she is a bonzer. <i>(May 12, 1915,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.nzmr.org/garland.htm A Gallipoli soldier, Greville Garland writing home to his family.]

>  <b>McGILL</b> Your shooting marble was usually a bonzer. <i>(1989,Kiwi Baby Boomers,45</i>)

>  When the big Lizzie fires it shakes the earth. She is a bonzer! <i>(Jun 8, 1915,Evening Post,8</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> great, terrific (See [bonser](../B#bonser))

>  <b>ANZAC</b> Had a boncer breakfast this morning. <i>(1915,On the Anzac Trail,149</i>)

>  <b>CADEY</b> You've made a bonza little place out of this. <i>(1933,Broken Pattern,135</i>)

>  And the first prizewinner is <b>Damien Wilkins</b>, (<i>The Miserables, Chemistry, The Fainter</i> etc). Tres, as the French say, bonzer. <i>(Dec 2007,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  Dug a bonzer big hole which we now use for putting scraps in,saves a lot of digging. <i>(Jul 1, 1915,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.thekivellfamily.co.nz and follow links to family history, diaries of Ralph Doughty, diary one, page 7.]

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> Sister had thought it a bonzer painting. <i>(1991,Always the Islands of Memory,58</i>)

>  Good old Jeff Insley, the bonsor bandy-legged baritone, made a great hit in his rendering of "Thora." <i>(Dec 1916,Roll Call,17</i>)

>  This book is chock-a-block with bonzer activities - puzzles, mazes, join-the-dots, games and more. <i>(2008,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.dymocks.co.nz]

>  Dave and Heather Turnbull of Christchurch say their experience with Bonza Financial Services was anything but bonzer. <i>(Oct 25, 2006,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: tvnz.co.nz/view/page/410965/871038]

>  An' lost some bonzer cobbers That I wants ter meet once more <i>(Dec 2, 1918,NZ Free Lance,Xmas Annual : 23</i>)

>  I would put a sign saying, "Bonza yellow car for sale $XXXX 100 yards ahead phone XYZ," on a sympathetic neighbour's fence. <i>(Dec 19, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> The tract was a prayer, a song from the soul which Effie reckoned was so bonzer the [20] words biffed out from the page. BOOHAI and BOOT edited and a reduced versions produced 7/10/89 and 6/3/90 for Oxford. <i>(1989,Then Upon the Evil Season,19</i>)

>  Miss Ariston's Concert Party.. has been down and given us a "Bonsor" (sic) entertainment. <i>(Nov 15, 1916,I Rode with the Epigrams,I/6 : 127</i>)

>  She's got bonzer muscles, mum, and can race every boy and girl in the school. <i>(Oct 2, 1947,NZ Woman's Weekly: The First 60 Years,17</i>)

>  [Caption] A couple bonzer scouts we are - followed the North Star all night and it turns out to be the tail-light of a Hurricane. <i>(Aug 25, 1941,NZEF Times,i/9: 8</i>)

 <i>n.</i> ca1964 Auckland: a big marble (See [cf. bonzer](../B#bonzer))

 <i>n.</i> (See [boohai](../B#boohai))

### boo-ay
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [boohai](../B#boohai))

### boob
 
 <i>n.</i> (humorous) big owner-operated brothel [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  Beyond the case of Club 574, they placed particular emphasis on the rights of small owner-operated brothels, or Soobs, and so the obvious joke here is that big owner-operated brothels must henceforth be known as Boobs. Club 574 is a Boob - it contracts 45 sex workers. As for the Soobs, which are typically 2 or 3 women whoring out of private homes throughout the Auckland suburbs, the city council argues they could move into the city, where there are 9500 apartment units or, more to the point, 12,000 bedrooms. <i>(Oct 30, 2005,Sunday Star Times,A5</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> prison, jail; cell; gen. Eng. but not recorded elsewhere in the combinations below

>  A good example.. is <i>boob</i> itself, meaning prison. <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 1</i>)

>  <i>Boob</i> can prefix many words, for example,.. <i>boob issue</i> for an issue of toiletries. <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 1</i>)

>  <i>Boob</i> can prefix many words, for example,.. <i>boob gun</i> for a tattoo machine. <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 1</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> Throughout the text I have made free use of 'boob' jargon, the jargon of the jail. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,8</i>)

>  <b>HARRISON</b> `You know what a boob tat is?' ... `A tattoo done in jail. Or borstal. Or D.C. ... Detention Centre. Or remand home' <i>(1981,Quiet Earth, The,153</i>)

>  His left arm (and hand) have been heavily (and badly) tattooed, first by the kids at the Owairaka boys' home and later by the men of Mount Eden jail. Edmonds calls them `boob tats'. <i>(Mar 1989,Metro,51</i>)

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> <i>boob</i> prison; mental hospital. Boob-head - a person conditioned to life in a prison or mental hospital <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,95</i>)

>  <b>BEATON</b> KATE: What is it, Boss? / DI: A drop of the ol' Boob Blue! / [65] HELEN: What is it? / DI: Old Boob Blue. / HELEN: Yeah. But what is it? ... (<i>Helen is forced to drink.</i>) / HELEN: Shit! Christ! What is that stuff? / DI: Brasso. ... We even strained it! <i>Ibid.</i> Glossary 109 <i>boob blue:</i> alcohol obtained by straining brasso polish. <i>(1984,Outside In,64</i>)

>  <i>boob boy</i>: Borstal boy <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> Even while he was being stripped and even while they shore off his yellow hair, gave him a number, a well-pissed mattress, his boob blues - he still raved on. <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,165</i>)

>  On his stomach is an excellent "boob" (prison) style tattoo, incorporating a large bulldog, with two smaller dogs underneath and a gang hand to the side. <i>(Oct 30, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),39</i>)

### boob-happy
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  He'd done nearly nine years in three three-year sessions and he was boob-happy. <i>(Nov 1953,Here & Now,44</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

### boobgear
 
 <i>n.</i> prison clothing

>  <i>Boob</i> can prefix many words, for example, <i>boobgear</i> for prison clothing. <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 1</i>)

### boobhead
 
 <i>n.</i> a prison inmate, one conditioned to prison life (See [boob](../B#boob))

>  Not boobhead university exactly, but just the steady influence of social workers and agencies and all that. <i>(2007,Best New Zealand Fiction, Vol 4, The,41</i>) [NOTES: 'The Feijoa is Brother to the Guava', David Eggleton, 2007]

>  Still pretty young and green back then but I came out a boobhead, fully fledged and staunch as. <i>(2007,Best New Zealand Fiction, Vol 4, The,41</i>) [NOTES: 'The Feijoa is Brother to the Guava', David Eggleton, 2007]

>  <i>Boob</i> can prefix many words, for example,.. <i>boobhead</i>, a chronic recidivist, or one who prefers to be in jail rather than on the <i>Outside.</i> <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 1</i>)

>  We get the lot in here... Battered wives, runaway teenagers, alkies, boobheads, hippies, pill heads and drifters. <i>(Jun 1999,Metro,94</i>)

>  Of course, when it comes to smuggling things in, the boobhead always has one advantage over the screw. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,7</i>)

>  ... the more downandout the boobheads look, the more highfalutin' becomes his [the screw's] voice. <i>(Nov 1953,Here & Now,41</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> <i>Ballad of the Third Boobhead</i> [title] <i>Ibid </i>526 Then the four boobheads caught the horns of the beast <i>Ibid.</i> 630[Baxter's MS note] A boobhead is a man who is regularly in and out of jail. <i>(1979,Collected Poems,525,1971 ed.</i>)

>  A `boobhead' is a man who is regularly in and out of jail. <i>(Jun 27, 1972,Critic,back page</i>) [NOTES: J.K.Baxter `The Boobhead and the Girl' (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> He may have been a doctor, a lawyer, ... or a university student ..., but once he's in jail he's just a boobhead, same as everyone else.<i>Ibid.</i> 245 BOOBHEAD (n) Prisoner, prisonised person. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,221</i>)

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> He lived in Mt Eden jail for many years, to become an accepted `boob-head' character. <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,79</i>)

>  Oh fuckem all, the boobheads of the world. <i>(Aug 29, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a fool; an addict

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> BOOBHEAD (n) Moron, imbecile. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,245</i>)

>  <b>WEDDE</b> `Some young pisshead ...[sic] dope fiend ... dole bludger ... sex pervert ...how did they know he wasn't a Courtenay Place bus-shelter sherry boob-head?' <i>(1986,Symme's Hole,70</i>)

### boobhouse
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> A blushing young maiden from the backblocks has nothing on the boobhouse leaders when it comes to avoiding the public eye. <i>(1953,Till Human Voices Wake Us,186</i>)

### boobslang
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Boobslang</i> (from <i>boob</i> 'prison'), also known as <i>boob jargon</i>, is a type of argot used amongst prison inmates, defined by Greg Newbold in <i>The Big Huey</i> as 'the jargon of the jail'. <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 1</i>)

### boobweed
 
 <i>n.</i> prison tobacco

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> BOOBWEED (n) Prison tobacco. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,245</i>)

### booby
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FORSTER</b> [In Atlantic] Boobies (<i>pelecanus piscator</i>); men of war birds (<i>p. aquilus</i>); tropic birds (<i>phaeton aethereus</i>). <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/31 fn</i>)

>  The great feature of the southern seas is the multitude of birds ... Huge albatrosses, molimorks (a smaller albatross), Cape hens, Cape pigeons, parsons, boobies, whale birds, mutton birds and many more, wheel continually about the ship's stern, ... <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,i/12</i>)

### boof
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [species name] stargazer .. [scientific name] <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i> .. [other name(s)] monkfish, boofs, bulldogs, churchills, flathead monk, giant stargazer, monks, muddies, spotted stargazer, toebiters, whitefish, sandcod <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Monkfish Kathetotoma giganteum</i> ... Also occurs in southern Australia. Known by a number of local names: giant stargazer, flathead, bulldog, boof, etc. The name monkfish is well established for this species in New Zealand, whereas overseas it is used for similarly shaped but unrelated anglerfishes. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,118</i>)

### boof-headed
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of sheep or dogs] acting without intelligence. [cf. Austral. boofhead a fool]

>  A dog or a sheep with no brains is called boof-headed. <i>(2000,Peter Glossary,2</i>)

### boohai
 
 <i>n.</i> a remote place or district; the backblocks [perhaps from North Auckland township of Puhoi]; up the boohai: confused, wrong, messy [up the boohai/booaye/boo-eye/booay is out of place, confused] (See [cf. backblocks](../B#backblocks),[bush](../B#bush),[cactus](../C#cactus),[wo(o)p-wo(o)ps](../W#wo(o)p-wo(o)ps))

>  Arthritis in the hands forced Titahi Bay artist Kerry Moore to 'paint' with words, and it has resulted in his first novel, <i>The Boo-Eye</i>. Set in 1950s New Zealand, in a town "10 miles from nowhere". (or in other words, in the boo-eye), three young men go on an odyssey of discovery that is both tragic and triumphant. <i>(Apr 8, 2004,Wellington Contact,38</i>)

>  "You're getting to be as bad as Dad since you joined that up-the-boohai outfit!" <i>(Sep 22, 2002,NZ Catholic,18</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> 'We'd be bollocksed!' / '- boating up the booai!'/ "We'd be pissing -' / '- into the wind!' <i>(1975,Log of a Superfluous Son, The,13</i>)

>  <i>boohai:</i> country esp. remote areas <i>up the boohai</i>: in error, in confusion <i>(1980,Lynfield College, Auckland</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>boohai/boo-ay/boo-eye</i> remote area; ... <i>up the boohai</i> to be lost or to have got something quite wrong, a nod perhaps at `puhoi', Maori for dull, slow, phlegmatic; 3. in elaborations emphasising distance, or distancing attempts by the speaker. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,18</i>)

>  <b>MEIKLE</b> Of the boys' funny sayings [in the 1930s] two live on in my memory. ... And if he was asked where he was going, he would answer: `Up the boo-ai in a matchbox hunting pukakers [pukekos] with a long-handled shovel.' <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,126</i>)

>  `Up the booeye for the rhubarb season'. Saying of the 1930s. Source unknown. <i>(Apr 30, 1984,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1984 Apr 30 letter to <i>Heinemann Dict. NZ Quotations.</i>]

>  <i>up the boohai - shooting pukakas</i> (pukekos) an amusing extension of notion of being lost, possibly in the head, Sue Budd recalls. Grant Tilly recalls its completion: <i>up the boohai shooting pukakas with a popgun</i>. `Boohai' c.1920. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,18</i>)

>  "It's quite therapeutic. If something in your life is all up the boohai you just go for a ride out on the farm." <i>(Aug 16, 2007,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

>  It's a long drive up from the buwai / By woodcocks and Kairpara [sic] Flats. <i>(1969,AUTC Song Book (3rd ed.),Song No. 36</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> Heard and used in Pukekohe, Auckland, c1922 <i>(1955,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1922]

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The Bohemians ... founded Puhoi (the name of which, corrupted, is apparently the origin of the slang term `the Boo-ay', a synonym for `the out-backs'). <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,97</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> <i>sticks</i>-`out in the sticks', `out in the backblocks', `out in the bush', `out in the booay', `out in the cactus', all have the same basic meaning of `fifty miles from nowhere'. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,184</i>)

>  <b>CASEY</b> You fancy them up here in the boo-eye, mortgaged up to their necks for the rest of their lives <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,244</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Some slang terms seems to be peculiar to certain districts. An example is the Auckland term <i>up the Puhoi</i> or <i>Boohoy</i> (its form varies a good deal) meaning `gone somewhere or other' and this is a genuine local product referring to an old German settlement on the coast north of Auckland and little, if at all, in use elsewhere in New Zealand. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/680 Examples of slang and colloquial expressions of local origin are ...<i> up the boohai</i> (and other forms) for `off in some unknown locality', an Aucklander's idiom. [Wall does not seem to completely relate these two statements] <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/678</i>) [NOTES: (Wall, NZ Speech)]

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>booeye:</i> So far OUT IN THE COUNTRY, mountains, or woods that carrier pigeons lose their way trying to find the nearest town. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,16</i>)

>  Well, out of the boo-ai comes three trampers. One was a nuggety bloke in a sou'-wester .. <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  The oldest son, John, left Puhoi when he was 16 to settle in Auckland. He found a job as a grocer's boy, and played the fiddle at night to pay his board. In 1907 he founded the National Trading Co in Customs St. `The expression `up the Buhoi' was said to have originated with him because when he was asked where he came from, that was what he replied in his strong accent - the same pronunciation of the name of our village can still be heard in Puhoi today,' said Mrs Williams. <i>(Mar 1, 1986,NZ Herald,1</i>) [NOTES: (Schischka)]

>  <b>LAY</b> Living in a shack in the middle of the boo-ai with a load of strangers, doing the washing by hand under a tilly lamp? .bf text; b. way to boohai , intensifying remoteness or distance. cf. <i>way to blazes. </i> <i>(1988,Fools on the Hill, The,220</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> When we got there, way to boo-eye up Lake Rotoiti way, we had to shovel the snow away before we put the tent up ... .bf text;.be;.bd;2. up the boohai, completely awry, astray. cf up the cactus, shoot. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,88</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Got the pricker with me. Slingin off at me he was. You're up the boo-ay he told me. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,91</i>) [NOTES: [This OEDS viii 86: error slingin']]

>  <b>MUIR</b> `She'll be right? She'll be right up the boo-ay if we're not lucky,' Arthur said. <i>(1960,Word for Word,171</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>Up the boo-ay</i> means `altogether wrong'. This seems to me a commoner meaning for <i>boo-ay</i> than `backblocks', `remote rural districts', mentio <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,122</i>)

### boohoy
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [boohai](../B#boohai))

### boojee
 
 <i>n.</i> an enema [ca1930 Marlborough: used by nurses: p.c. E.A. Orsman] (See [cf. soogee](../S#soogee))

### book
 
 <i>v.</i> to fold sealskins into layers like the leaves of books

>  <b>HOWARD</b> Incoming skins are sorted to arbitrary standards - wigs (old males), clap-matches (females), bulls, yearlings, grey or silver pups (under one year), black pups. If the cargo is intended for the European or American market, the skins are removed from the animal with an inch of blubber adhering, and in the final simple treatment are washed, heavily slated, and then folded or `booked' into rectangular shape for packing. Skins destined for China, however, are treated with more preliminary care. They are taken as cleanly as possible, and after careful washing are sent to the `beamer' to be thoroughly scraped free of the last remaining particles of flesh and blubber. <i>(1940,Rakiura,40</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a bookmaker

>  <b>SCOTT</b> If the `books' did not see the horse doing good work, they would not bite. <i>(1900,Colonial Turf,134</i>)

>  Enough was apparently lifted from the "books" to inconvenience them considerably. Ibid. 3 There was a rumour a few weeks back that the Taranaki "books" were going to extend the limits to GBP15 and GBP5 for the new season. <i>(Aug 19, 1939,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  A number of "books" from Auckland were present, but business was not brisk. <i>(Mar 24, 1883,Auckland Weekly News,10</i>)

>  One worthy "book," somewhat stout, grasping the situation of affairs, started at a brisk run after Porowini, urging him to finish, so winning much to the rider's astonishment. <i>(Mar 24, 1883,Auckland Weekly News,10</i>)

>  A bunch of [pukekos] were passed and the bookmaker ejaculated: "Look at them, protected like the 'books'." <i>(Apr 18, 1934,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  [Hdg] Collusion with Books emphatically denied.. <i>(Feb 21, 1934,NZ Truth,2</i>)

>  [Hdg] Horses withdrawn after "Books" bet wide and handsome... The fact that some of those who operate outside the law were not found with full books.. was significant to many. <i>(Feb 14, 1934,NZ Truth,2</i>)

>  This year clubs have notified through the press that a few "Books" would be permitted to operate. <i>(Feb 1, 1908,NZ Truth,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the omasum; third stomach of a beast.

>  <i>(1949,Report of Royal Comm. Sheep-Farming Industry,96</i>) [NOTES: see BIBLE (in W3)]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> [caption: ?refce ?1910, or 1930s:] Cutting Blackfish blubber for boiling down. ... The blubber is cut into blocks then chopped into `Books' to speed up the oil extractions. [the `Books' pictured appear to be about 6 x 3 inches, about an inch thick.] <i>(1982,Whaling and Sealing,72f</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (the book) commercial radio audience ratings

>  The answer lies in the ... McNair Survey ratings which commercial radio stations call the book. <i>(Jul 7, 1987,TV1</i>) [NOTES: News]

### book up
 
 <i>v.</i> to charge on an account. [ca1940 at least Will you pay cash or book it (up)?]

>  <b>MASTERS</b> As previously instructed Bill arranged for a wreath from the station people, including himself. This he booked up against the station. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,173</i>)

### book-bashing
 
 <i>n.</i> swotting [1984 16 F E36 Pakuranga Coll. 26 BOOK BASHING [F1M2]]

### bookau
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [pukahu](../P#pukahu))

### bookie
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a member of the book trade

>  But the most primitive scrap is actually happening in the New Zealand's bookie community. <i>(Jan 20, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A7</i>)

### bookvan
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal)

>  <b>TURNER</b> In New Zealand the Education department controls the National Library Service which sends <i>bookvans </i>(equivalent to the American <i>bookmobile</i>, which is also used but especially for suburban bookvans) into the backblocks. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,159</i>)

### boom
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  There was one more sound that so far had gone unrecorded - a sound as of someone blowing across the top of an empty milk bottle with the power of a fog horn and the regularity of a heartbeat. This was the male kakapo booming - sitting inside the amphitheatre of his bowl, inflated into a feathered balloon, sending out a pulsing, low-frequency love call. <i>(Mar 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 56/30</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a timber-boom across a river, etc. to retain floating logs.

>  <b>BAINES</b> It is nine miles from the dam to the `boom' at the mouth of the creek. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,177</i>)

>  At the mouths of streams a line of floating [kauri] logs have been previously fastened together with iron `dogs' and short chains; this is called a `boom', and prevents the timber getting away to sea. The logs are then fastened together in rafts, and, guided by men armed with long poles shod with iron (called rafting-spears), are conveyed to the mills, ... <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,365</i>) [NOTES: (E. Tregear: Labour in NZ)]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> Here [the felled kauri trees] lie until a `fresh' drives them down to the creek or harbour where the `boom' lies waiting. This boom is formed of a circle of logs fastened together with chains. There may be as many as fifty logs forming the chain, which sometimes encloses acres of water. The logs enter the boom, - are fastened together into a kind of raft, and towed to their destination. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,62,1940, p. 63 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PINFOLD</b> On his return [from Upper Thames] he took us to what were called `The Booms'. These were great logs of wood driven into the ground across the mouth of a river that opened out toward the sea. The bushmen ... felled the trees. Afterwards, when a good rain came and flooded the river, the `logs' would come down, and the `booms' were expected to stop them from going out to sea. <i>(1930,Fifty Years In Maoriland,60</i>)

>  <b>MANDER</b> They watched the progress of the log from the booms up the skids to the side of the breakdown platform... Halved and quartered, it was then levered ... on to greased rollers and rushed towards the big circulars, which turned it into flitches. Then the small circulars, the drag, and the goose completed its metamorphosis into the regulation strips ... <i>(1920,Story of a NZ River, The,198,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOWE</b> We had certainly expected life in Thames to be more lively than it was at the `booms' [of a timber camp]. <i>(1964,Stamper Battery</i>)

>  <b>EADDY</b> Here [the logs] would be discharged and rafted into `booms' alongside the different sawmills to await their turn of being hauled up and sawn. <i>(1939,Neath Swaying Spars,11</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> ... the [kauri] logs are placed behind booms until enough are collected to make a raft. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,112</i>)

>  <b>INGLIS</b> a `boom' of great logs <i>(1887,Our NZ Cousins,27</i>)

>  The practice obtains among bushmen of rolling the logs into the adjacent creeks, down which they are afterwards driven during freshes to the `booms.' <i>(1876,TrNZI,9/368</i>) [NOTES: (Brown)]

### boom off
 
 <i>v.</i> to get rid of; push off.

>  <b>REVANS</b> I have boomed [the lawyers] off quietly, yet they have managed to have some thirty pounds out of me. <i>(1841,Letter,172</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Revans to Chapman (TS) 172]

### boomer
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <i>Boomer</i>: very good, successful <i>(1981,Auckland Savings Bank</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  To those of you who hoped for a farce/ Another plug for your well blocked arse?/ And ye who were quiet and all sublime,/ Let the record books show it was a boomer time. <i>(Sep 15, 1986,Salient,14</i>)

>  `Dear Madame, on behalf of all those who watched this year's boomer Christmas parade, may I thank all ... who helped to make this fantastic occasion.' <i>(Apr 23, 1988,Dominion,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> anything very large of its kind (esp. a lie) (often prefixed with an intensifying or hypocoristic little). [ca1935 Havelock (mainly among older people of second generation back, but still occas.]

>  Doesn't anyone say "boomer" any more? <i>(Dec 5, 1998,Life with Barbara,15</i>)

>  [Of a battle] It was a boomer I tell ya not at all like the Saturday night do's at home. <i>(1997,Volunteers, The,5(2): 27</i>)

>  `My word, he's a boomer. Must be over a hundred pounds, by the way he tugs.' <i>(Mar 26, 1910,NZ Truth,248/8/1</i>)

>  <b>REES</b> My word, he's a boomer! Must be over a hundred pounds. <i>(1913,Merry Marauders,149</i>)

>  <i>boomer n.</i> a very large kangaroo. ... [origin uncertain] [boomer= something large] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

>  Take half a pound of bait per person ... Or take a very large one, for the season's started out a boomer. <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,120</i>) [NOTES: 1974 Hulme in 1977 NZ Listener Short Stories 120]

>  <b>LELAND</b> `She's a boomer.' REALLY GOOD. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,16</i>)

>  [Headline] BASIN BOOMER LOOMS as Australia strikes back [by winning the second one-day cricket match] <i>(Feb 18, 1982,Dominion,18</i>)

>  <i>boomer ... 2. Austral. & N.Z. inf</i>. anything exceptionally large. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,123</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 80; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 55]]

>  <b>STEWART</b> And after telling each other repeatedly over the next few days just what a boomer of a day out this was going to be, we were at the airport, ... <i>(1985,Gumboots and Goalposts,14</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> Or a very large one, for the season's started out a boomer. <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,38</i>) [NOTES: (King Bait)]

>  The budget next Thursday, the Prime Minister says, will be a `little boomer.' That threw various political soothsayers into a panic. Was he, they wondered, emphasising the word `boomer' - or the adjective? <i>(Jun 14, 1987,Sunday Star (Auckland),A13</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>boomer</i> something first-rate or successful, such as a topnotch surfing wave, but originally a large male kangaroo; eg `That paint job you did on the car, boomer, mate.' ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,18</i>)

>  <b>McGill</b> ...trussed up like a bent mummy in that boomer of a cantilevered barber's chair after Oswald Cottle wound his white sheet around you... <i>(1995,Gold in the Creek,1</i>)

### boomerang
 
 <i>v.</i> (of a plane heading to Antarctica) to return before landing

>  ...if the weather at the airfield on the ice-shelf at McMurdo isn't absolutely right, then the Hercules "boomerangs" - it turns 180 degrees and heads back. <i>(Apr 2004,Listen Hear,No. 6/1</i>)

### boomerang children
 
 <i>phr.</i> children returning to live at home with their parents

>  In this part of the English-speaking world [sc. England], "boomerang children".. are known as "kippers" - kids in parent's pockets eroding retirement savings. <i>(Feb 7, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  The phenomenon of boomerang children, who refuse to leave home or keep coming back, is on the rise. <i>(Jul 11, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),20</i>)

### booming
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HEINZ</b> Booming is ground sluicing by means of an intermittent supply of water. <i>(1952,Prospecting for Gold,48</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The Arrow is also a booming river when in flood <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,168</i>)

### boong
 
 <i>n.</i> an offensive term for a coloured person. (See [also bong](../A#also-bong))

>  Our old people did not like this mingling of blood and warned against fraternising with "those North Island Maori", those "head-hunters", those "Boongs". <i>(Oct 1998,Mana,No. 24: 58</i>)

### boonga
 
 <i>n.</i> (offensive) a Polynesian, usu. a Pacific Islander, less freq. a Maori [an elaboration of <i>boong</i>; (See [also bong](../A#also-bong),[boong](../B#boong))

>  Chinaman, boonga, slanty-eyed, fob (fresh off the boat), Sambo ... the multicultural interviewees had heard it all. <i>(Nov 23, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),78</i>)

>  do you think they would ever let a boonga be prime minister / <i>(Oct 25, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  The Labour Department sent a boy to a job which fellow workers told him was only done by `boongers' (Islanders) because it was dirty and dangerous and the pay was poor. <i>(Aug 17, 1977,Dominion,8/4</i>)

>  <i>boonga</i>: Islander <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <i>(Feb 26, 1982,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: Wellington: <i>boonga</i> used of a Polynesian by a child of 4 years in the Newtown (Wellington) kindergarten.]

>  Georgina Stirling admitted under Cross-examination in the High Court at Auckland her brother Paul started the fight with three Samoan men after calling them `boongas' and threatening to kill them. <i>(Jul 22, 1987,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>boonga</i> offensive term for Pacific Islander, adapted from equally offensive Australian `boong', applied to an Aboriginal. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,18</i>)

>  <b>MITCALFE</b> Riki spoke out of the side of his mouth like and American gangster, `Maoris is "Boongers", right? And Polynesians is "Coconuts", right? And Winston's half Boonger and half Coconut ... <i>(1985,Hey Hey Hey,148</i>)

>  GLOSSARY OF TECHNICAL TERMS: A boobhead is a prisoner; a screw is a warder; a topper is a toady or an informer. The Act or the Collar - The Habitual Criminals Act, under which persons declared habitual criminals can have their sentences increased by the Prisons Board for offences against prison discipline during their incarceration. <i>(Nov 1950,Here & Now,14</i>) [NOTES: (Ian Hamilton `NZd's Last Flogging')]

>  <b>Bernard Gadd</b> 'Get lost, boonga, this is none of your business,' he said. <i>(1981,Where to Go?,37</i>) [NOTES: Said to a Samoan character in the short story <i>Love Story.</i>]

### booreedy
 
 <i>n.</i> (offensive) (See [puriri](../P#puriri))

### Booroola gene
 
 <i>n.</i> a fecundity gene in sheep

>  Molecular biologists at Otago University isolated a marker in 1992 for the booroola or "fecundity B" gene which produces between 60 and 100 extra lambs from every 100 sheep. Agricultural scientists have said the booroola gene can lead to lambing rates of 190 per cent in merinos, but does not always mean prolific lambing. <i>(Oct 21, 2004,Dominion Post,C8</i>)

### boot boiler
 
 <i>n.</i> [1989 Charles Ransom notes: This is not found elsewhere, and so may be NZ but obsolete.]

>  <i>Boiler, boot</i>- A modification of the back boiler, L-shaped to increase its heating surface. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,16</i>)

### boot home
 
 <i>phr.</i> to give impetus or vigorous movement to (a person, thing, plan, etc): esp. boot along, boot home.

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>boot home</i> horse racing term for kicking or urging a horse to reach the finishing line first; eg `Jack might be losing but he's really booting his yacht home.' - any marked or forcible encouragement. Both NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,18</i>)

### boot lunch
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a picnic meal served from car boots or station wagon car hatches.

>  <b>GORDON</b> He did, however, give me a very old-fashioned look at the excellent boot lunch that always follows the Hawke's Bay Show's dog trial final. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,91</i>)

### boot, the
 
 <i>n.</i> a kicking attack (See [cf. boots and all](../B#boots-and-all))

>  This he does with great danger to himself from the onslaught of the tough's `cobbers', who are not particular whether it is `the boot' (it is seldom the hands) or a stone, or even a bottle that is brought into action. <i>(Mar 23, 1907,NZ Truth,92/4/5</i>)

>  [cartoon of a large boot entitled RUGBY kicking into the stomach of Professor Seagar who is entitled AUCKLAND RUGBY UNION. The whole is <i>(1913,Auckland University Carnival Book,33</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `Sonny's dead for ages. Flashing his roll after the shearing season. At a party or something. Some pakehas got him outside and into him with the boot.' <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,205</i>)

### booth licence
 
 <i>n.</i> a licence permitting the sale of liquor on a particular occasion or event, e.g. a show, race meeting, cricket match, or tournament. [Jim Cameron writes: Historical 1962-89. Previously known as a <i>conditional licence</i>. Since 1989 subsumed under <i>special licence</i>.]

### bootlace
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Boot laces.</i>-Narrow strips of skin cut off by rough shearers, generally when opening up the neck. Hence the verb, to <i>b.l. </i> <i>(Jan 13, 1934,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss. additions]

>  <b>ACLAND</b> ~ Narrow strips of skin cut off the sheep by rough shearers, generally when opening up the neck. Hence the verb <i>to bootlace</i>. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,357</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Bootlaces. `<i>The narrow strips of flesh carved off a sheep especially when opening up the neck of a fleece by a rough shearer are bootlaces,'</i> B1941: Aust and NZ rural: late 19-20C. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,997</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> BOOTLACE. A long thin strip of skin cut off a sheep, seen mostly on last side. It usually comes from wrinkled skin. <i>(1956,Wool Away!,155</i>)

>  <i>Bootlace</i>: Long thin strip of skin, usually cut from a wrinkled sheep while shearing the last side. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,52</i>)

>  <i>bootlace ... NZ & Austral.</i> strip of skin cut from sheep by careless shearer. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,80</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Several sheep went out the port hole minus a few `boot laces' from their neck. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,97</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Acland Gloss. additions <i>(Jan 13, 1934,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: see @n Acland Gloss. additions]

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Also, having the skin wrinkled does not make for quite such a good job, and you are more likely to bootlace the sheep - i.e. cut the wrinkles off in long thin strips of skin. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,41</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a thin strip of sheep's skin with wool attached [accidentally taken off during shearing.]

>  Boot laces.- Narrow strips of skin cut off by rough shearers, generally when opening up the neck. <i>(Jan 13, 1934,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),13</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Bootlace. A long thin strip of skin cut off from a sheep ... <i>(1955,Wool Away!,155</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> bootlace: a narrow strip nicked off by a rough shearer, say from the wrinkle on the neck of a Merino .. Especially with the wrinkly-necked Merinos, to open out with blades can easily produce bootlaces. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,9</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> Bootlace: Long thin strip of skin, usually cut from a wrinkled sheep while shearing the last side. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,52</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> a really tough shearing mob there could be a lot of bootlaces around the shed. No use for boot strapping, these laces are strips of skin, the result of the shearer pushing his tongs up the thick unyielding neck of an old merino. <i>(1983,Other Side of Farming, The,ii 2</i>)

>  Old high country slang..'bootlaces' were strips of skin accidentally taken off by shears. [boot lace; boot-lace] <i>(1984,NZ Journal of Agriculture,13,Jan Feb ed.</i>)

### bootleg
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  ... an accident which took place in the course of tunnel operations ... A charge had `bootlegged' on March 28 at 9 p.m. - that was, some holes had not blown completely. <i>(Apr 12, 1953,Evening Post,6</i>)

### bootman
 
 <i>n.</i> a tapdancer using boots instead of tapshoes ?

>  A stomping performance by local bootmen, acrobats mingling with the guests on the dance floor and a queue of local lovelies vying for the strongman's attention enlivened provceedings. <i>(Jan 14, 2001,Sunday Star Times,D4</i>)

### boots
 
 <i>exclam.</i> cf. boots on the board

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>boots!</i> shearing term identifying the farmer's arrival.<i> [Keith Sinclair] </i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,18</i>)

### boots and all
 
 <i>phr.</i> with no holds barred, wholeheartedly [ca1950 Wellington `You ought to have seen the kids at the party. When the grown-ups left they were into the food boots and all.']

>  Knock it off, pack a sad, have a nice one Get stuck in, boots and all, have a coke, Half a shake, how ya doin', have a drop, of mother's ruin Hell's bells, blow that for a joke. <i>(Dec 2004,NZ Memories,No 51, p19</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> We encountered a clutch of young Paradise ducks, just in the flapper stage ... so we went in boots and all. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,78</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> The next thing he'll do is counter-attack, boots and all. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,96</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> enter the fight, using every resource, disregarding all the rules of fair fighting .in 4;`Some of the gang had Mack on the ground when his brother Joe arrived. Joe went in boots and all, and that was the end of it.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],418</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> So my lover girl is babysitting again this Friday night, said Colin Harrington. And she's asked me along to help. ... Got held up last Monday night, ... but this time I'm in, boots and all. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,123</i>)

>  <i>boot ... 10. boots and all. Austral. inf.</i> making every effort. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,123</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 80; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 55]]

>  <i>3 boots and all NZ & Austral. colloq.</i> with no holds barred, wholeheartedly <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,80</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>boots and all</i> no holds barred, complete or enthusiastic commitment; eg `What better title for big lock Andy Haden's story than <i>Boots 'n' All</i>?' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,18</i>)

### boots on the board
 
<b>1</b> <i>phr.</i> (Rural) a call made by shearers when the boots of the shed boss or land owner are seen approaching the board.

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> When the boss was sighted, the catch phrase 'boots on the board' would be repeated from stand to stand along the board. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,40</i>)

>  <i>boots on the board:</i> The boss is in the vicinity. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/21</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

### booze artist
 
 <i>n.</i> [see also artist]

>  artist ... NZ & Austral. sl. (usu. with defining word) chap or fellow, person devoted to or expert in (esp. reprehensible) activity (booze artist). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,37</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 58, sense 5;]

>  sense 3] b. booze-artist <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,26</i>)

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: booze artist <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Drunken sot. I'd do it with pleasure. No-one would miss this booze artist. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,167</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> She told him about an artist who came in each day and asked her to pose for him, `Booze artist , or bull?' Arthur wanted to know. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,233</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> They reckon he was sozzled. A right booze artist they're saying he was. Bottle a night and all that. <i>(1971,Plekhov Place, The,104</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> booze artist heavy drinker of alcohol; c.1920. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,19</i>)

### booze balloon
 
 <i>n.</i> cf. spare tyre

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> That's a booze balloon around his middle region, and his shoulders slope a bit. He exercises sometimes with his mates but his figure slippage is mainly a mental one, ... <i>(1979,Skin Deep,17</i>)

### booze barn
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The result [sc. of the introduction of tavern licences] has seen the rise and then fall from prominence of booze-barn size taverns to the present trend of smaller and more intimate neighbourhood taverns, and inner city bar taverns. <i>(1990,Sale of Liquor,1-25</i>)

>  They have become booze barns run by a private company which is still in the six o 'clock swill mode . <i>(Jan 20, 1993,Independent Herald (Wellington),1</i>)

>  It's a modest little dining toom, done out in restful shades of minty green but with a distinctly dizzying red patterned carpet which would not have looked out of place in a 1970s DB Breweries booze barn. <i>(Dec 12, 2002,Dominion Post,D3</i>)

>  People don't want to know about the old booze barns of yesteryear. <i>(Oct 3, 1995,Independent Herald (Wellington),1</i>)

>  It's the late '80s in a big, smoky booze barn in Nelson and Headless Chickens' frontman Chris Matthews is not a happy chook. <i>(Jun 16, 2002,Sunday Star Times,F3</i>)

>  <b>HALEY</b> They were going flat out in the booze barn pulling in as much coin as they could while things were sweet. <i>(1989,Transfer Station,41</i>)

### booze baron
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Boag mysique is piqued by the fact... that she has worked both for booze barons and Fay Richwhite, other Stan rellies. <i>(Dec 9, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  It is the same morality as that practised by generations of booze barons who argued for monopoly on the grounds that their enormously lucrative liquor empires were operated for social good. <i>(Apr 8, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

### booze blitz
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A booze blitz across the Eastern Bay finds half the targeted liquor outlets selling liquor to minors. <i>(Jan 6, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,5</i>)

### booze bus
 
 <i>n.</i> a liquor checkpoint bus

>  [Hdg]. Booze bus off the road. Southland police's rust-ridden booze bus has failed its warrant of fitness... Mr Price said the booze bus was an effective tool for combating drink-driving. <i>(Dec 27, 2000,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  The consultant estimated the high-performance car reached speeds as high as 175kmh on Auckland northwestern notorway, even passing a police "booze-bus" checking traffiuc heading into the city during the Wednesday night incident. <i>(Jul 11, 2003,Dominion Post,A9</i>)

>  Police are out in force again with the same old anti-drink-drive props - the booze bus, random testing, sneaky unmarked traffic cars, checkpoints and confiscation of car keys. <i>(Dec 17, 2003,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  A Balclutha woman, who flagged down an unmarked police car to ascertain the whereabouts of a "booze bus", was convicted in Blaclutha District Court yesterday on a drink-driving charge. <i>(Dec 22, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>) [NOTES: Original in Evening Post 16/10/2001]

>  The motorist gave himself up at a booze bus. <i>(Feb 1, 2000,Dominion,00</i>) [NOTES: Check page]

### booze-oh
 
 <i>n.</i> an organised drinking party

>  They said they reckoned they had a right to be present at the booze-oh. The brigades men were not asked to come into the wet, and got the spike hot heavy and sudden. <i>(Jan 8, 1910,NZ Truth,4</i>)

### booze-rooster
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>JOSEPH</b> `The way she talks ... you'd think I was a regular booze-rooster.' <i>(1962,Pound of Saffron, A,253</i>)

### booze-wacked
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  booze-wacked bodgies steer the snouts of their cars/ past macho sailors <i>(Mar 1989,Landfall,169/103</i>) [NOTES: (review: of Eggleton People of the Land)]

### boozelum
 
 <i>n.</i> euphemistic or hypocoristic for `booze'.

>  Timothy was a canvasser, a sort of Autolycus or picker up of unconsidered trifles, and his good lady had got him sundry jobs which he had promptly lost through the `boozelum.' He was all right when he was sober, only he was never sober. <i>(Sep 30, 1911,NZ Truth,327/5/3</i>)

### boozeroo
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Walker's explanation was that he was on the boozeroo, and didn't know what he was doing. <i>(Jan 4, 1908,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  The face of Elizabeth Sheehan, next charged, reminded one of a lost soul plunging violently in a sea of boiling beer. She pleaded guilty to boozeroo, but denied with vehemence that she was a rogue and vagabond. <i>(Mar 5, 1910,NZ Truth,245/5/7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a boozy party

>  It's going to be a real boozeroo. <i>(Oct 12, 1970,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12/2</i>) [NOTES: [OED2]]

>  <i>boozeroo n. NZ sl.</i> drinking spree; inferior public house. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,80</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>boozeroo</i> drinking bout. - pub. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,19</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> A `good spree' would be described as a `a proper old boozeroo' this word being of the same pattern as various American words in -<i>eroo. </i> <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,89</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> A terrible bad night it was to be on guard what with all the boys still drinking to celebrate the Second Ech coming from England ... And the whole lot of them thinking: well here's for a last good boozeroo. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,75</i>)

>  Often his clique meets at a beer party. Book-titles apart, his party is not so different from the Saturday night boozeroo in the Sydenham side-street with the keg on the kitchen-sink. <i>(Sep 1952,Landfall,23</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson `Fretful Sleepers')]

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> `Meeting!' retorted Mother, witheringly. `Boozeroo you mean! ... I'm glad the old Soak had to walk home ...' <i>(1960,Ernie and the Rest of Us,106</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> He played Beethoven at boozeroos. <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,28</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a (low) pub; a party or drinking group (?transf. f. 1). [ca1951 Wellington (H.W.O.) used of @workingclass' pubs]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> Till any Scotsman with the shakes/ Can pile on your head his mistakes/ And petrify a boozaroo/ [?a pub, ? party: the group of people forming a drinking party or clientele] Reciting <i>Tam o' Shanter</i> through; <i>(1979,Collected Poems,290,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>boozeroo</i> drinking bout. - pub. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,19</i>)

### boozeteria
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We have..developed a restaurant and cafeteria scene that would have been unthinkable during the glory days of the boozeteria (carpark provided for your drinking and driving convenience). <i>(Jun 19, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

### BOP
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Bay of Plenty [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  Janie Waldon was re-elected as president and in her report she thanked all the members for their support, and also a big thank you to the BOP Community Trust for their donation, which has helped to purchase a new vehicle. <i>(Nov 4, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,8</i>)

### bop up
 
 <i>v.</i> illegally alter prison clothing

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> In the tailor's shop the altering or bopping-up of institutional gear was the main spare-time occupation. <i>Ibid.</i> 245 BOP UP (v) Illegally alter prison clothing. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,67</i>)

### boracic
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MacKENZIE</b> <i>boracic</i> soothing syrupy words meant to placate <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,95</i>)

### boradi
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [puriri](../P#puriri))

### borage
 
 <i>n.</i> a coarse, hairy, blue-flowered European herb found mainly in the North Island (See [blue borage](../B#blue-borage))

>  <b>BAKER</b> She put her cup of borage tea down... <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,171</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> VIPER'S BUGLOSS (<i>Echium vulgare</i> ), also called blue weed and by mistake, blue borage, occurs on roadsides, river-beds, and fields in both islands, though it is commoner in the south, and especially in Marlborough. It is an annual or biennial with a deep penetrating tap root root and a stem 2 to 3 ft high. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,143,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> Small local hives of wild bees produced honey with a special flower due to the mixture of nectar from white clover, viper's bugloss (known locally [on Molesworth] as blue borage) and various wildflowers. <i>(1959,Molesworth,248</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The Taylor River [Marlborough] river-fan glowing with <i>eschscholtzia</i>, the orange-yellow four-o'-clock, or bright with blue borage. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,171</i>)

>  <i>borage, [=] viper's bugloss</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,7</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Viper's bugloss is often wrongly called `blue borage' - the correct name for an ornamental plant of the same family. <i>(1980,Weeds of Crops,110</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> Viper's bugloss, or blue devil, often incorrectly called `borage', makes a sea of blue on open ground, particularly ... in the South Island. <i>(1980,Wild Flowers,32</i>)

### borax
 
 <i>n.</i> the blame; (occas. concretely, a scolding)

>  <b>COOZE</b> All the borax cannot, in fairness, be heaped upon her permed head. <i>(1946,My Little State Home in Suburbia,54</i>)

>  Frank Barnard comments: `We got borax from the members, and congratulations from the Prime Minister.' <i>(Jul 1986,Metro,80</i>)

### borax/borak
 
 <i>n.</i> banter, ridicule [(The 1905 cit. has spg <i>borah,</i> which is taken as a typographical error for<i> borak</i>, which itself is less common in NZ than the folk re-formation borax)] (See [also poke the borax](../A#also-poke-the-borax))

>  They've been taking it out on borax since. <i>(Aug 27, 1910,NZ Free Lance,13</i>)

>  Although Borak could not repeat his Winter Hurdles win at Riccarton.. the Nightmarch gelding has a jumping future. <i>(Aug 28, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/166 : 11</i>)

>  To poke the borax at Tottennham Hotspur soccer fans for getting so excited about a 3-0 halftime lead over Manchester United would be childish. (Hah, losers!) <i>(Oct 2, 2001,Evening Post,30</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> "Go on chiaking.. poke borak - it doesn't hurt me." <i>(1914,Tale of a Timber Town,88</i>)

>  If one could only think that Attic salt was a commodity that you could buy at the grocers.. it would be easier to determine how much of the eloquence poured out at smoke concerts.. and meetings of the Natives' Association is genuine and how much "borak". <i>(Apr 23, 1904,NZ Observer,4</i>)

>  Notwithstanding all the "borak" that has been poked at the Premier, Richard is plainly in earnest on the subject of stimulating the birth rate. <i>(Aug 27, 1904,NZ Observer,2</i>)

>  Now I know <i>Heartland</i> isn't meant to be <i>Hard Copy</i> but a little poke of the borax wouldn't have hurt. <i>(Dec 11, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

>  It is all very well for Opposition members to "poke borax" at this sort of thing. <i>(1945,NZ Parliamentary Debates,272/254</i>)

>  Then Alexander, who always seems to carry a packet of borax about with him, went on to say "he knew what a racing calendar was." <i>(Apr 30, 1904,NZ Free Lance,4</i>)

>  We've had the borax thrown at us, and I can't say it didn't have a detrimental effect on some of the players. <i>(Jul 26, 1992,Dominion Sunday Times,27</i>)

>  My first thought was that the gentle shepherd was "taking a rose out of me," "poking borax," "playing a little game," and so forth. <i>(Sep 17, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,21</i>)

>  Was it necessary to poke borax from across the street? <i>(Apr 23, 1995,Sunday Star Times,A15</i>)

>  Infometrics' Gareth Morgan is a dab hand at poking the borax. <i>(Jul 31, 1992,National Business Review,10</i>)

>  We need the borax and the bite that the talent can produce. <i>(Jun 1, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

>  <i>Bor&#769;.ak</i> (b&obreve.r&#769;.&adot.k), n. [Aboriginal.] Banter; ridicule; chaff. <i>[Colloq.] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2018</i>)

>  The Winton Borough Council has ... agreed to ... [do] its business in secret because the local `Herald' poked a bit of borah[sic] at a new and self-satisfied Alderman. <i>(Oct 7, 1905,NZ Truth,16/1/5</i>)

>  You mustn't joke, or borak poke/, At monarchs gone to hell. <i>(Jul 14, 1906,NZ Truth,56/1/8</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> <i>(1914,Tale of a Timber Town,88</i>) [NOTES: [see quot Chiack)]

>  <i>borak n</i>. banter, chaff.- to poke borak at, to make fun of [Abor.] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

>  <b>MARKS</b> As soon as the boss left the room (always an opportunity for us to `poke borak' at one another) ... <i>(1934,Memories (Mainly Merry),60</i>)

>  <i>borak n</i>. banter, chaff, fun at another's expense, to poke borak at, to poke borak at, to ridicule [Abo]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),611</i>)

>  <i>borak ...n.</i> banter, chaff, fun at another's expense, <i>to poke borak at</i>, to ridicule [Abo]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  <i>borak n.</i> (also <i>borax) NZ & Austral. colloq.</i> banter, ridicule; <i>poke borak at</i> make fun of. [Abor.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,80</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 55]]

>  <b>BENNETT</b> To `poke borax at' (to tease) comes from Australia, and contains a corruption of an Australian aboriginal word. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,90</i>)

### borch
 
 <i>n.</i> the small Antarctic fish <i>Pagothenia borchgrevinki</i>

>  He sits out on the Ross Sea ice in McMurdo Sound for hours each day, catching small, free-swimming fish called borch. ... <i>Pagothenia borchgrevinki</i>, named after a Russian explorer reputed to be the first man to set foot on the Antarctic continent, could well have been called Boris or Ivan because a seaman went ashore first to hold Captain Borchgrevinki's boat. <i>(Nov 24, 2004,Dominion Post,A5</i>)

### bord
 
 <i>n.</i> workings or areas driven into or off a main coal seam or at right-angles to the seam (so EDD Nhb, wYks) [???check].

>  Bords, or headings, are tunnels driven in the coal. ... lead off on both sides from the heading or main drive. <i>(1901,NZ Illustrated Magazine,?2</i>)

>  ... both stentons and bords should be blinded so as to make the air full. <i>(Nov 10, 1911,Maoriland Worker,36/6/2</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> ... drives were broken off at right angles to [the dip heading] at distances of two chains apart ; thus forming pillars on either side of the dip, two chains square. These drives were called main levels. The bords were driven about two chains off the dip, and the `stenton' would be driven from the lower level to the one above. <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,169</i>)

>  <b>BATTYE & EATKIN</b> <i>Bord</i> the mine was divided into several working areas, called <i>Bords. </i> <i>(1980,Shadow of the Valley,72</i>)

### bord and pillar
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  The system of work [in the Grey coalfield] is mainly by bord and pillar, a small portion only of this coal having been worked by the longwall method. In the bord-and-pillar system the bords are 18ft. wide, and pillars 14 yards thick. The pillars are left intact until the boundary of a section is reached, when they are worked back. The roof has in [418] main proved good, enabling a large percentage of the pillar-coal to be won. <i>(1905,NZ Mining Handbook,417</i>)

### border
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [irrigation] to surround an area with ditches or dykes.

>  After a farmer has decided on the area to be bordered it should be ploughed and worked up. After bordering it should occasionally be worked up to keep down weeds, at the same time consolidating the land to produce the firm seed-bed so desirable for grass and clover seed. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,33</i>)

### Border Leicester
 
 <i>n.</i> a breed of sheep bred for wool and meat.

### border-ditch
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a simplified form of border-dyking surface irrigation.

>  There are a number of methods [sic] of irrigating land, of which the following are the principal:- 1. Border dike. 2 Border ditch. 3. Contour. 4. Wild flooding. 5. Furrow. 6. Basin-check. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,25</i>)

>  The Border Ditch method is used where the fall of the land is over 1 ft. per chain, and can be constructed direct on to pasture land without previously working and levelling it. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,26</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> Border-ditch Irrigation. Where the fall of the land is too steep or the surface is too irregular for using the border-dyke method, or where it is not too desired to plough up the pasture, ditches are constructed over the area to be irrigated and the water is caused to flood over from the ditches. These ditches are called border ditches ... <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,187</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> The border-ditch method is not as efficient as the border-dyke method in its use of water. [border ditch] <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,188</i>)

### border-dyke irrigation
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [used elsewhere but sign. in NZ farming] a type of surface irrigation commonly used on the Canterbury plains, in which a system of races and gates is used.

>  Border-dyke irrigation is the most economical method, but uses more water than other methods and requires large flow-rates. <i>(Dec 1976,NZ Journal of Agriculture,38</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> After borrowing a further $10,000, they went into a syndicate set up to develop a 400 ha (990 acre) dry land farm, in Canterbury, into border dyke irrigation. They put in up to 140 ha (345 acres) a year of border dykes ... <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,121</i>)

>  <b>RENNIE</b> Flood irrigation, commonly called border dyke irrigation is the system used. Once installed, border dyke irrigation costs little to run. <i>(1988,Agriculture in NZ,27</i>)

>  The farm has the advantages of good soils, borderdyke irrigation and medium rainfall. [border dike, border-dyke] <i>(Nov 25, 2000,Press, The,64</i>)

### border-dyke system
 
 <i>n.</i> an irrigation system (See [border-dyke irrigation](../B#border-dyke-irrigation))

>  Using a border-dyke system to partially flood his paddocks, Ted.. can irrigate 20-30 ha a day between September and April - as long as river flows hold up. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/46</i>)

>  The border dike method is the most efficient in utilisation of water and saving of labour. <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,25</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> The advantages of the border-dyke system are the fair degree of uniformity attained in the application of water, the high irrigation efficiency attainable in a well-designed and carefully operated scheme, and the speed of the application. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,188</i>)

### border-dyked
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land] flood-irrigated from drains bordering paddocks.

>  <b>TAIT</b> Fifty acres of land was border-dyked for irrigating in the spring. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,6</i>)

>  There is also 100 acres of borderdyked irrigated pasture. <i>(Nov 21, 1963,NZ Farmer,54</i>)

>  Farmers with borderdyked land could face problems. [border-dyked] <i>(Sep 18, 1997,NZ Farmer,5</i>)

### border-dyking
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the formation of an irrigation system using border-dykes.

>  This [increase in irrigation water] is the result of increasing demand by farmers to participate in the various schemes and the preparation of further areas of irrigation by border-dyking land .. insufficient border-dyking has been done. <i>(Apr 12, 1951,NZ Farmer,7</i>)

>  A competent Manager is required to manage our Civil Engineering/Earthworks and Borderdyking operations in the Oamaru area. Experience in the following areas would be an advantage:.. Borderdyking... <i>(Jan 24, 2001,Dominion,47</i>)

### Borderdale
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred sheep developed from the crossing of Border Leicester and Corriedale breeds.

>  <b>WONZ</b> The Borderdale is a New Zealand breed developed in the 1930s ... <i>(1980,NZ Sheep and Their Wool,7</i>)

>  <b>WARMAN</b> The Borderdale is a recent addition to the list of New Zealand sheep breeds, having been developed in the South Island in response to regional farming conditions. <i>(1991,What Sheep is that?,18</i>)

>  <b>MEADOWS</b> The breed was recognised and the New Zealand Borderdale Sheep Society officially formed in 1977. The Borderdale thrives on irrigated or light pastures and in rolling hill country. <i>(1997,Sheep Breeds of New Zealand,28</i>)

>  The first draft of Borderdale lambs from the main mob of ewes is taken in early December. <i>(Nov 25, 1999,NZ Farmer,12</i>)

### bore
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Kut and Kolour salon owner ... said many of her customers had had problems with their hair since the town switched to bore water supply. <i>(May 6, 1988,Dominion,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> At Makaraka, ... an artesian bore was put down which struck water at about a hundred feet. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,157,1935 ed.</i>)

>  A recent bore put down at Clinton to a depth of 102 feet, presented ... the same features, so far as the constant inrush of water was concerned. <i>(1882,TrNZI,14/121</i>)

>  A few households and business concerns obtain excellent water from bores sunk in the old sedimentary rocks ... <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,23/98B</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> `Water, bloody water. Where is it?' another bellowed. A fruitless search ensued. No tanks. `Must have sunk a bloody bore.' <i>(1971,Plekhov Place, The,91</i>)

>  <b>SLIGO</b> Where the Army control of the road ended he listened to the water from the artesian bore. <i>(1987,Final Things,241</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sp use] to make holes in posts for a post and rail fence.

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> I was boring post-holes all day. <i>(Jul 21, 1865,Chudleigh Diary</i>)

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> Boring holes in posts for a new fence to try to keep the horses at home. <i>(Jan 12, 1869,Chudleigh Diary</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Rotorua Bore-users Association spokesperson ... <i>(Oct 19, 1986,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: National Programme]

>  <i>Bore users' view raised</i> Rotorua bore users and council representatives will meet Energy Minister David Butcher ... <i>(Mar 17, 1988,Dominion,7</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  A good supply of alkaline water at 278 feet has been secured from a trial three- inch bore in Government ground at Rotorua. <i>(Sep 25, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/170 : 5</i>)

>  <b>HINDLEY</b> The growing number of bores drawing energy from the field under Whakarewarewa has had a marked impact. <i>(1989,Rotorua,2</i>)

>  Six homeowners who bought houses ... are supplied water from a bore with a high salt content. <i>(Oct 28, 1989,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  <b>STAFFORD</b> Hot water had been extracted from the ground by hand-sunk bores since the 1920's. <i>(1988,New Century in Rotorua, The,250</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  &#65279;If these lines should meet the eye of an expert in petroleum boring, it would be interesting to know whether the facts above given would be of any assistance in enabling an expert to arrive at a reliable opinion as to the probability of finding a profitable bore in the vicinity. <i>(Oct 28, 1899,NZ Times,3</i>)

>  The soft sand formation reached by the oil bore at Midhurst at over 1800 feet has caused loss of circulation of the viscous mud used to seal the inside of the hole as it is drilled. <i>(Aug 24, 1940,Taranaki Herald,6</i>)

>  <b>STAFFORD</b> During the same month [Dec., 1895] they began sinking bores to test conditions beneath the surface and to analyse and assess the potential of the oil deposits <i>(1986,Founding Years in Rotorua, The,352</i>)

### borefield
 
 <i>n.</i> an area where bores provide water

>  In model 2 the FRI borefield is about 175m from the centre of Roto-a-Tamaheke, but is even closer to some springs on the lake edge. <i>(Jul 1983,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,61/19</i>)

>  No appeals have been lodged against the Waikanae borefield project, securing a long-term water supply for the Kapiti Coast.... <i>(Dec 4, 2004,Dominion Post,A10</i>)

### borer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HULME</b> My flat is old/ dust from borer tunnels builds/ in soft orange piles/ <i>(1982,Moeraki Conversations,10</i>) [NOTES: The Silences Between]



 
 <i>n.</i> a wood-boring insect; various wood-boring insects infesting mainly house-timbers ?mainly <i>Anobium</i> spp. or [longhorn =?].

>  Accused had travelled throughout New Zealand pretending that naphthalene crystals, costing about 6d, were useful for exterminating borer. <i>(Jul 26, 1943,NZEF Times,III/109 : 8</i>)

>  Not even a reputation as a "nutter" and a bad case of borer could keep architect Jackie Gillespie from battling the bulldozers to save a precious piece of Queenstown's waterfront heritage. <i>(Oct 1997,North & South,27</i>)

>  Says a simple country paper:- `The borer, which is so destructive to white pine, is not at all particular what he bores through. In the Southland `Daily News' office a sheet of lead in one of the walls was found simply riddled by the pest.' <i>(May 13, 1911,NZ Truth,307/1/3</i>)

>  <b>31/7/3</b> ... they went in for homes for themselves on the usual terms - 20 [pounds] down and 15s per week, and if their hearts are not broken before then or the borers have not eaten the house, in 20 years they will own their own homes. <i>(Oct 6, 1911,Maoriland Worker</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> White pine ... is too subject to the `borer.' <i>(1915,Reminiscences of Earliest Canterbury,99</i>)

>  <i>A Note on the Susceptibility of New Zealand Timbers to the Attacks of the Borer, Anobium domesticum. </i> <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> ... the borer was in the lining. <i>(1924,Follow the Call,57,1975 ed.</i>)

>  ... if possession of the Ranfurly Shield is to encourage this class of warfare, the sooner the borer consumes this trophy the better. The Bay owed its win principally to the superiority of their forwards. <i>(Jul 11, 1927,Wairarapa Times-Age</i>) [NOTES: 1927 July 11 Wairarapa Times-Age (Masterton) in 1985 Bromby Eyewitness History 150]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> In the later [eighteen] fifities houses were made with an upper story with two or three projecting windows which we called attic windows. The talk of borers in these wooden houses has only been during the last 30 or 35 years [sc from c1900]. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,56</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> ... I lived in an old house with borer ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,228</i>)

>  <b>CASEY</b> And if anybody does poke their nose underneath - well, you tell me the house that doesn't have borer in it these days. <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,16</i>)

>  <b>TEXIDOR</b> Who pulls my bach to bits and sprays it for borer? ... Who can run up a dress and knock up some scones? <i>(1987,In Fifteen Minutes,205</i>) [NOTES: a1964 Texidor in 1987 In Fifteen Minutes 205]

>  <b>TURNER</b> In New Zealand a tiny insect is called <i>borer</i> because its grubs leave tiny holes, about the size of a pinhead, in wood. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,63</i>)

>  <b>HASCOMBE</b> Suddenly a woman hisses, `There's borer in the back of this duchess.' ... What is a duchess? What is borer? <i>(1969,Down and Almost Under,35</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> <i>borer</i> : Explanation of your inability to get a long mortgage. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,182</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>borer</i> local insect with the dendrophagic habits of a TERMITE. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,17</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>borer</i> the insect that chomps through OB (outer board) in Kiwi wooden houses, though it gets nowhere with heart timbers. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,16</i>)

>  Any Wellington resident who sees a borer hanging on his dining-room ceiling.. should rush to the scullery for a broom. <i>(Feb 5, 1948,Evening Post,12</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>CADEY</b> Mrs. Briggs pronounced it free of "borer" a wood pest for which Phoebe would never have dreamed of looking. <i>(1933,Broken Pattern,232</i>)

>  Described by the police as the "Borer King,".. a salesman pleaded guilty in the Christchurch Magistrate's Court to 11 charges of false pretences. <i>(Jul 26, 1943,NZEF Times,III/109 : 8</i>)

>  A big Family Home, no Borer <i>(1951,NZ Parliamentary Debates,295/557</i>)

>  <b>BALL</b> He and the cast locked themselves away in the school hall, coming to the surface only occasionally to clear their lungs of borer droppings. <i>(1970,People Makers, The,104</i>)

>  [Advt]. Esitol Kills the Borer. <i>(Mar 16, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,65</i>)

>  Could you please send me the name of a preparation to kill borer in a piano? <i>(Dec 7, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,24</i>)

>  Restoring an organ means far more than just ridding it of borer and dust. <i>(Oct 1992,North & South,20</i>)

>  The Managing Director of Boracure (N.Z.), Ltd., said today his company had been accused of raising a 'borer bogey' in New Zealand <i>(Sep 4, 1952,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  There has been a great deal of frustration in describing the business of borer treatment in New Zealand. <i>(Sep 4, 1952,Evening Post,10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Sc. of wells

>  <b>HAAST</b> In summing up I may ... state, that the strata through which the borer has to go will not ... offer any serious obstacle ... <i>(Jun 19, 1863,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,58</i>) [NOTES: 1863 June 19 Report in 1879 Haast Geology Canterbury and Westland 58]



 
 <i>n.</i> a hagfish

>  <i>Hepatretus cirrhatus</i> (Bloch and Schneider). Hagfish (slime-eel, hag, slimy, borer). <i>Tuere. </i> <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/399</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] borer 10 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a plant borer

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> ... the pakeha joker from up the road tells me that [the tomatoes] have got the borer and need a different kind of spray. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,3</i>)

### borer bomb
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Borer bombs haven't been available for some time because they tended to kill only those insects on the wing at the time. <i>(Dec 1, 1999,Evening Post,22</i>)

>  A school hall was evacuated after they let off borer bombs to add atmosphere to the performance. <i>(May 6, 1990,Sunday Magazine (Auckland),45</i>)

>  A borer bomb was thrown into a Committee meeting.. in the Lounge. <i>(Oct 1, 1979,Salient,17</i>)

>  <i>borer bomb</i> device emitting pesticide fumes. <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,56</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; nf. 1986 NZPOD]]

>  Police made eight arrests.. after demonstrators brandishing borer bombs and aerosol cans were removed from the building. <i>(Aug 5, 1980,Evening Post,8</i>)

### borer dust
 
 <i>n.</i> nonsense

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>that's borer dust</i> that's a lot of nonsense or rubbish. <i>[Shepard] </i> <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,112</i>)

### borer hole
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOSKEN</b> I still saw beauty in every scratch and borer hole. <i>(1964,Life on a Five Pound Note,58</i>)

### borer-proof
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>ANTHONY & JACKSON</b> He immediately upset all my previous guesses by plunging into a discourse on borer-proofing houses. <i>(1951,Me and Gus,81</i>)

### boride
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [puriri](../P#puriri))

### born to the purple
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) (of an animal) of excellent breeding.

>  Born to the purple: a pedigree Jersey calf lying half hidden in the rich grass which will one day make it an outstanding producer. <i>(Aug 19, 1936,Weekly News,78</i>)

### boronia
 
 <i>n.</i> any of a group of Australian shrubs usually with yellow and brown flowers.

### borough
 
 <i>n.</i> in New Zealand, a village, township or town having a special governing body called a borough council.

>  There shall be in and for each single borough a council consisting of nine councillors. <i>(1867,NZ Statutes,31 Vic No. 24 [Para.] 29</i>) [NOTES: [also OED2]1867 Acts N.Z. 31 Vic No. 24 [Para.] 29]

>  <i>borough n. NZ</i> urban area as administrative unit in local government (<i>Ellerslie borough has seen a vast improvement in traffic enforcement).</i> [OE] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,81</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 125, sense 4; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 56]]

### borough council
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (historical) a local body elected to administer a borough

>  [Hdg] Hastings Boro' Council. <i>(Feb 15, 1913,NZ Bulletin,11</i>)

>  The late Henry Sergeant.. had left a considerable amount of landed property in Wanganui to the Borough Council. <i>(1920,NZ Parliamentary Debates,188/177</i>)

>  The Borough Council was desirous of making certain of the sections available as sites for workers' dwellings. <i>(1920,NZ Parliamentary Debates,226/866</i>)

>  The Eastbourne Borough Council is offering GBP2 for information leading to a conviction of any persons taking shrubs etc.. from the Butterfly Bush. <i>(Apr 1934,Tararua Tramper,6(6) : 1</i>)

>  The West Harbour Borough Council had, over the years, done practically nothing to improve the area. <i>(1951,AJHR,H.-28/15</i>)

>  The Whangarei Borough Council lately wanted an engineer. <i>(May 31, 1913,NZ Observer,17</i>)

>  Strictly speaking, a Borough Council is the highest unit of local government in New Zealand. <i>(1945,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(24) : 19</i>)

>  If the Brigade breaks up, the duty of providing efficient means for protection from fire will devolve on the Borough Council. <i>(Oct 8, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> The actual transition from provincial to County and Borough Councils was a very peaceful one in Taranaki. <i>(1983,Illustrated History of Taranaki,73</i>)

>  Every reference in this Act.. To a borough or a borough council shall.. be read as including a reference a district or district council, as the case may be: To a county or to a county council shall be read as a reference to a district or a district council, as the case may be... <i>(1974,NZ Statutes,II 66/1538</i>) [NOTES: The Local Authority Act 1974 abolished borough and county councils in favour of district councils]

>  Mr. Scully won the gentleman's prize; he was a fat and dissipated person with a point lace cravat, heavy nose, ginger whiskers, and a borough council. <i>(1934,Tramper,6(5)/4</i>) [NOTES: Date is a guess based on Tararua Tramper]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a (small) `corporation'; pot-belly [pun on <i>(city-)corporation</i> and (borough-)council].

### Borough Ranger
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Mr. Wall is, we understand, the Borough Ranger and there is no reason why those horses and cattle complained of should not be impounded. -Ed. W.H.] <i>(Apr 7, 1899,Wanganui Herald,3</i>)

### borra borra
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [poroporo](../P#poroporo))

### borrow
 
 <i>n.</i> spoil, etc. taken from a `borrow-pit' [1987 P.S. O'Connor p.c. from Ministry of Works documents]

### borrow-pit
 
 <i>n.</i> before roads were macadamised, step-faggots [presumably as in `step(ping)-stones' HWO] were placed on one side of the road to form a footpath, and a barrow-ditch extended from and at right angles to the footpath into the road. These occurred at regular intervals, draining the surface water from the road, and also compelling carts, &c., to keep off the footpath (P.M.); Paid W. Masters for making 76 rods of Barrow ditch ... (Dec. 26, 1752). [no etym given: cf. barrow sb3 1. a hill; a heap of stones. [gen. dial. Cum to Ken and Cor]; DARE barrow ditch Also, by folk-etym. borrow ditch; rarely barrow [EDD ref.] Cf bar ditch1 1927-1973: barrow pit Also by folk-etym. borrow pit Prob, from barrow a mound of earth; for folk-etym. see quot1950 ... 1 rarely burrow pit: a roadside drainage ditch. Chiefly West, esp. Rocky Mts 1931-1973 Map shows mainly NW: 1950 quot explains folk etym: barrow `mound of earth formed when pit was dug'; barrow = `wheelbarrow'; borrow `a pit where dirt is borrowed to be used in another.] 2 a place from which material for construction has been excavated and which, esp. in the Gulf states, often fills with water (1940-1968); cf OED2 sb1 4. dial. A mound or heap 1869, 1881 Raymond Mining Gloss. Barrow (Cornw.), a heap of attle or rubbish.] (See [borrow](../B#borrow))

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> Unfortunately the road at this point went `zag' and that is where we left it, and the Model A slid sideways into a ditch - or `borrow-pit' to use the correct roading term. <i>(1975,Men of the Milford Road,53</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

### borsalino
 
 <i>n.</i> a proprietary name

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> Anyhow, he generally wore - and this was another thing pretty common with the swagger - he generally wore a good hat. Riverina always had a borsalino ... [which] was looked upon as a sort of a prestige thing, you know. The brim was wider by far than the present-day brim, and it was one of the superior hats of the day. ... A borsalino, stetson - these were the hats that had a bit of class about them. It was similar in style to a stetson, but with a slightly smaller brim, but it was a class hat. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,73</i>) [NOTES: (Bob Edwards, bushman)]

>  [The khaki beret] is waging a battle in popularity with two opposing types of headgear. These are the borsalino and the common straw. <i>(Jun 12, 1944,NZEF Times,III/155 : 6</i>)

>  His Borsalino doesn't quite blend with the ensemble either. <i>(May 22, 1944,NZEF Times,III/152 : 7</i>)

>  Headware Highlights in Wet Weather... Hats, Tin.. Balaclavas.. Highly Unofficial, The Borsalino. <i>(Mar 20, 1944,NZEF Times,III/143 : 8</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> Riverina always had a borsalino... A borsalino was looked on as a sort of prestige thing, you know. The brim was wider by far than the present-day brim, and it was one of the superior hats of the day. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,77</i>) [NOTES: Check page]

>  <b>PASCOE</b> [He [sc. Zurbriggen] survived Mount Sefton and the streets of Christchurch to return later that year with an Italian patron, Signor Borsalino, a maker of hats. This was in 1895.] <i>(1958,Great Days in NZ Mountaineering,141</i>)

### borsalino hat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Borsalino hats, which are subject to transhipment before arrival in New Zealand, are usually found to have received the attention of sticky-fingered searchers for considered trifles. <i>(Aug 1, 1928,NZ Free Lance,18</i>)

### Borstal honey
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Before you're unlocked you put some things in a small linen bag. A knife, some bread ... and a small bottle containing syrup or, if you've been lucky, Borstal honey - which is a mixture of syrup and animal fat pinched from the cookhouse. It's a pretty foul mixture, but it replaces butter, which is always short in gaol. <i>(Nov 1953,Here & Now,41</i>) [NOTES: (Hamilton)]

### bosca
 
 <i>adj.</i> (See [bosker](../B#bosker))

### boshee
 
 <i>n.</i> (derogatory) a Maori

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> But the attitude of the settlers was not wholly a product of their environment. To a large extent their prejudice was imported with them. Those who had encountered few Maoris, like the Otago and Canterbury settlers, were as biassed as the rest. The Maori was called `nigger', `black', and in the <i>Auckland Examiner</i>, `Boshee' [subscript 44] by people who had never seen a slave. The word `Maori' conjured up in the settlers' minds a stereotype based on experience in other colonies. [fn44] Perhaps a mixture of `bosh' and `Quashee', a West Indian negro. <i>(1957,Origins of the Maori Wars,10</i>) [NOTES: [?1860s]]

### boshter
 
 <i>n.</i> (derogatory) variant of bosker
