>  `Do you like going to church?' asked a visitor of a Methodist minister's son. `My oath!' said the lad. `It's a real boshter to hear Dad talking for a solid hour and a half, and Ma never allowed to say a word!' <i>(Sep 8, 1906,NZ Truth,64/12/3</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> variant of bosker

>  Bosker, boshter, bonzer - Australian slang for splendid. <i>(1916,Anzac Book, The,36</i>)

>  ... the thrill of a bayonet charge is, as I am informed, boshter. <i>(Mar 23, 1916,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>) [NOTES: [Frank Morton]]

>  <i>boshter adj.</i> see <i>bonzer. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>boshter</i> first-rate, excellent; variant of `bonzer'. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,19</i>)

### bosker
 
 <i>adj.</i> bonzer, beaut [Obsolesc. by ?late 1930s; by then confined to mainly to the usage of old people and some children: frequent early use in <i>Truth</i>, and var. spg, suggest a newly-fashionable adoption; an old word for enjoyable or excellent: `Gore must be considered a bosker place to live the good, social life in'; (anything) excellent or enjoyable of its kind; 1953 14 M A19 Thames DHS 4 BOSKER (BOSCER) -->1960 16C F B4 Wanganui Girls C. 4 BOSKER] (See [also boshter](../A#also-boshter))

>  <b>Alister Robison</b> During the week I also had letters from Nana & Aunt Maud & also a bosker cake from the latter. <i>(Aug 20, 1916,Letter</i>) [NOTES: http://www.nzine.co.nz/features/warletters_8]

>  '..the telegraf posts appear like a stab fence. Strite!' 'That must be bosker,' said the Young Colonial. <i>(Jun 1926,NZ Railways Magazine,36</i>)

>  Who have, who have Boska fun? We have, we have <i>(1907,King's Collegian,6(1) : 8</i>)

>  "Lumme," said the bus driver to the fare alongside, "that's a bosker pipe of yourn mister!" <i>(Jul 4, 1935,NZ Railways Magazine,4/55</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> But there's no fear of Tidal Creek being disappointed. Everyone agrees it's a bosker circus. <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,100,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>KINROSS</b> `By crikey!' we said. `What a bosker cop!' <i>(1963,Please to Remember,85</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> bosker: really fine, clear. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,298</i>) [NOTES: 1964 Pearson Glossary to 1964 Sargeson Collected Stories 1935-1963 298]

>  <i> ~ adj.</i> see bonzer. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),612</i>)

>  <i>bosca adj.</i> see <i>bonzer. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  <b>GRIFFITHS</b> G.J.Griffiths notes: Aust; another form of bonzer and boshter; I fancy I have heard this very infrequently in NZ <i>(1972,Personal Communication</i>)

>  I tell people to read [`Cow Pats'] especially the young, for it shows so precisely how it is against dignity to do certain things simply because it feels `bosker'. I quote that last word because to the young - and who isn't to me, now - the use of once current slang is a little impediment. <i>(1978,Islands,21/240</i>) [NOTES: 1978 John Summers in Islands 21/240 [aj] [On Frank Sargeson]]

>  <b>GILDERDALE</b> <i>bosker See</i> bonzer. First recorded in T.T. Garland's <i>Old Joe</i> (1937). <i>(1982,Sea Change,240</i>) [NOTES: (Note on Language)]

>  <i>bosker n. NZ & Austral. obsolesc. sl.</i> good, excellent, delightful. [orig. unkn.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,81</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bosker</i> first-rate, excellent; variant of `boshter' and `bonzer'. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,19</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> [Young flapper] that's a boscar song. <i>(1922,Three Plays of NZ,36</i>)

>  `... He said: `It smells bosker.' I said: `It smells rotten.' After some more words he landed me a oner on the nose.' <i>(Apr 23, 1910,NZ Truth,252/1/6</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ... he said it made his feet feel bosker and warm. So we all stuck our feet into cow-pats, and after walking over the frost it was bosker and warm sure enough. <i>(Jan 6, 1937,Tomorrow</i>) [NOTES: (Cow Pats)]

>  Got to be quite an expert at cooking. Bosker at boiling rice. <i>(Jul 19, 1915,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.thekivellfamily.co.nz and follow links through family pages to diaries of Ralph Doughty, Diary One, page 7.]

>  Bosker, all right, take it from me! <i>(Sep 13, 1913,NZ Observer,16</i>)

>  Oh yes, we're goin' ter 'ave a bosker time this 'ear! <i>(Dec 20, 1913,NZ Observer,17</i>)

>  If a member changes places,/ He's a `bosker sewer rat.' <i>(Mar 31, 1906,NZ Truth,41/6/4</i>) [NOTES: [Bloke's patter: fashionable slang]]

>  Gore must be considered a bosker place to live the good, social life, in fact that of the giddy little town of the giddy little curate isn't in it. <i>(Sep 22, 1906,NZ Truth,66/1/6</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> `It's a `bosker' castle' [the little girl] said. <i>(1909,Galahad Jones,8</i>)

>  <b>FANNING</b> No treatise on forwards can be complete without a reference to `wingers'. ... the scrum's Satanic wings ... [Long ago elderly gentlemen] went to see the old-fashioned `wingers'. Their joy was like the thrill of the alleged Spaniard, who watched a game in the heyday of `wingerism' : `Caramba! eet ees splendida! Eet ees to me your bonsa or bosca.' <i>(1910,Players and Slayers,13</i>)

>  Bosker, boshter, bonzer - Australian slang for splendid. <i>(1916,Anzac Book, The,36</i>)

>  ... the thrill of a bayonet charge is, as I am informed, boshter. <i>(Mar 23, 1916,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>) [NOTES: [Frank Morton]]

>  Said Martin of the Dinks, `why he was the greatest `swi-up king' in the `Inverteds', and he used to give the `vin rouge' a boscar lash to leg.' ... <i>(Sep 5, 1917,NZEF Chronicles,28</i>)

>  ... it's bosker to be free as air ... <i>(Jul 10, 1919,Quick March,84</i>)

>  `Bosker day, ain't it?' Conventionally the man who says ---- is bound hot foot for eternal punishment, but the man who substitutes a rich illustration like `bosker' is still within the pale of respectability. <i>(Jul 11, 1921,Quick March,13</i>)

>  <b>PEACOCKE</b> What bosker fun. <i>(1926,His Kid Brother,54</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> Did you go to school in a belltopper hat? Mike says the boys do at Eton. He says it would be a bosker game kicking them round the yard. <i>(1934,Winds of Heaven,143</i>)

>  He says it would be a bosker game. <i>(1935,Winds of Heaven,140</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> [Slang] ... in constant use by our youngsters [in 1930s] ... bonzer, bosker ... <i>(1941,NZ Slang,51</i>)

>  But to say a thing is `corker' or `bosker' means that it's swell, while `fair dinkum' means honest-to-god. <i>(1944,US War Dept. Short Guide to NZ,38</i>)

>  They were bosker horses. <i>(1946,That Summer,31</i>) [NOTES: `Pair of Socks']

>  <b>SIMPSON</b> <i>(1945,If You'd Care to Know,97</i>) [NOTES: [see BONZER]]

>  ... his ear is fine so that the exact out-of-dateness of schoolboy argot, regional relics, as for example `bosker', and words associated with a particular class like `cobber', are registered as neatly as the rise and fall of his lines. <i>(1947,Landfall,3/219</i>) [NOTES: Robt. Chapman reviewing Sargeson's <i>That Summer</i>]

>  Oh, no, mum, ham paste is bosker for swops; nobody else has it, and we can choose any lunch we like with it. <i>(Dec 26, 1931,Exporter & Home Journal,49</i>)

>  He said: "It smells bosker." <i>(Apr 23, 1972,NZ Truth,1</i>)

>  We gave him a boscar funeral. <i>(1911,Kiwi on the Swag,14</i>)

>  I heard a Digger and a Sammy talking about the eternal feminine yesterday; they talked for about five minutes before the digger "twigged" that the "Jane" that the Sammy was talking about, corresponded to the "bosker tart" that he was describing. <i>(Aug 16, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,V/50 : 33</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a great success, a fine example. [Also bosca]

>  You're a bosker, Ma! <i>(Jan 8, 1910,NZ Free Lance,12</i>)

>  And now comes somersault Asher, / He's a record try-getting smasher, / He's a "bosker," no kid (they say he's half Yid), / But I tip him to end up a crasher. <i>(Aug 8, 1903,NZ Free Lance,21</i>)

>  Various classical scholars tell me William [a singer] is a "bosker" and a "bonser." I'll tell you if he is when I 've looked up the Latin dictionary. <i>(Mar 24, 1906,NZ Free Lance,16</i>)

>  "Well, by Jove," said the old man, "that machine's a bosker." "Can I sell you one?" asked the attendant. <i>(Nov 18, 1905,Observer (Auckland),16</i>)

>  "He's a bosker," and worthy of all the praise his fellow "All Blacks" showered on him. <i>(Sep 29, 1906,NZ Free Lance,19</i>) [NOTES: Punctuation as recorded.]

### boss
 
 <i>n.</i> a station owner, an employer (See [cf. boss-cocky](../B#boss-cocky))

>  Yesterday one of the cows died and my boss brought it home and cut it up for the dogs. <i>(Dec 16, 1924,Otago Witness,71</i>)

>  The popular impression that the "boss" has the easiest jobs received prompt confirmation from a witness in the Arbitration Court. <i>(Sep 2, 1929,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  With this accession of horseflesh [by the middle of the sixties], sporting instincts came to light, and on Christmas or Boxing Day ... the hands would run a picnic, the biggest donation being from the `boss' or owner [of the station], and several horse races would eventuate. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,35</i>)

>  ... the boss. <i>(1864,Digger's Notes,47</i>)

>  ... the boss. <i>(1864,Digger's Notes,47</i>)

>  <b>CAIRD</b> ... the owner of the station commonly called the `Boss.' <i>(1874,NZ Sheepfarming,12</i>)

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> Occasionally, during those early days, the `boss' (proprietor) would come round and scold men for not working hard enough. <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,160</i>)

>  <b>COTTLE</b> `Is the boss at home?' I shouted to him trying to appear colonial. <i>Ibid.</i> 94 I wired into the boss like one o'clock. <i>(1891,Frank Melton's Luck,33</i>)

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> The `residence' stood close by ... [where] `the Boss' and `the Missus' lived. <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,5</i>)

>  If the owner managers his place himself, he is called, and addressed as, `boss'. If not, the boss is whoever gives the men their orders. Presumably it comes (via Australia or America?) from the South African `Baas'. (C.) <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  Good weather, good shed, good sheep, good boss, and a good gang create an atmosphere of work and action, ... <i>(1955,Wool Away!,2</i>)

>  She's a big woman, the boss's wife, two pick handles across the backside and an arm that can fell a bullock, ... <i>(Dec 1986,Marist Messenger,36</i>) [NOTES: [station-owner or manager] (Joy Cowley)]

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Boss</i>. The runholder or his/her manager. The term is loosely used by the shearers to refer to whoever gives the men their orders. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,95</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> someone who has respect but is not gang affiliated

>  There are words for people who have respect, for example <i>boss</i> (someone who has respect but is not gang affiliated. <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 2</i>)

### boss bull
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bull that assumes charge of a group of bulls.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. if it so happened that a 'boss' bull went in first, the others, afraid of him, were loath to follow. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,20</i>)

>  They tussled around for a day or two, separated out the boss bull and then remained reasonably docile for quite a while. <i>(Aug 10, 1972,NZ Farmer,39</i>)

### boss calf/cow
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a herd leader that takes more than her share of feed, often high-producing at the expense of other cows, and is usually first in butting line.

>  There is also the 'boss' calf at that age and if she, and preferably another one, are taught to lead, the daily walks can be made more pleasant. <i>(Oct 1, 1949,NZ Dairy Exporter,73</i>)

>  One problem which seems to plague many farmers is the boss cow. ..they can not remember the bunting order any more and the boss cow system is broken down. <i>(Aug 1972,NZ Dairy Exporter,71</i>)

>  <b>CANDY</b> In this parlous state, the old boss cow bore scant resemblance to the authoritarian figure which had tyrannised the herd for years. <i>(1988,Every Living thing,125</i>)

>  I sat up a ladder for two hours and watched every fight that happened and worked out who were the boss cows and what the pecking order was. <i>(Dec 30, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,10</i>)

### boss cocky
 
 <i>n.</i> a leader or one who assumes or is grudgingly given leadership (often ironical).

>  The boss cocky of the squadron [of special constables] - if he was not boss he assumed that position - a bad-tempered-looking, sunbaked bushman he appeared, was particularly officious and vicious, and once or twice he had his baton raised above his head as if to strike. <i>(Nov 22, 1913,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  All these men are responsible to Mr. Robinson, who, in turn, is responsible, during the session only, to the Speaker. Out of the session he would appear to be absolute `boss cocky'. [Mr Robinson is officer-in-charge of Parliament Buildings.] <i>(Mar 5, 1910,NZ Truth,245/4/6</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> I have been baptized by the boss cocky, old Bishop Lenihan, long time ago now. <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,71</i>)

>  <i>boss cocky n.</i> farmer who employs men. hence, any one who is undisputed leader. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),612</i>)

>  <i>boss cocky n.</i> any farmer who employs men, hence, any one who is undisputed leader <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

### boss cow
 
 <i>n.</i> one which leads the herd in (for milking) [ca1925 J. Winchester (Wellington) ?]

### boss fern
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POTTS</b> (And) <i>Dryopteris decomposita</i> [get cit] <i>(1882,Out in the Open</i>)

>  <b>POTTS</b> Thus we have spleen-wort, bristle fern, bladder fern, adder's tongue, boss fern, polypody ... <i>(1882,Out in the Open,74</i>)

### boss of the board
 
 <i>phr.</i> the overseer of a shearing shed (See [cf. board walker](../B#board-walker))

>  Raddle in those days [1880s] was in great demand, and when the boss of the board was in a bad humor[sic] or perchance did not like the color of the shearer's hair he used the raddle on the sheep shorn by the shearer and the raddle sheep were counted out and the shearer not paid for them. <i>(Mar 29, 1912,Maoriland Worker,55/12/2</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> The use of <i>board</i> for the floor of a shearing shed is also slang. Whence comes <i>a full board</i> and <i>boss-over-the-board</i>. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,29</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> He acted as shepherd, wool-classer, `boss of the board' in the shearing shed, and wool scourer. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,59</i>) [NOTES: 1947 Kenneth Ross in Beattie Early Runholding 59]

>  <b>HARPER</b> Among all those men in dungarees ... was one man with a high starched collar and tie. Systematically he walked the woolshed floor, saw that every sheep was thoroughly shorn, not a tuft left, and kept an account of the tallies. He was known as `the boss of the boards.' <i>(1967,Kettle on the Fuchsia, The,92</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Each shearing season, for many years he came ... to be `boss of the shearing board'.<i>Ibid.</i> 42 Joe Ferris was head shepherd and Chris Mccauley and Jimmy Muir paddock shepherds, and Bukkie Cordie, packie. ... Murdock McKay was still boss of the shearing board. <i>Ibid.</i> 56 ... sometimes he was heard to be talking to ... the boss of the board Mr McKay. <i>(1975,Eight Daughters,25</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> ... and he acted as boss-of-the-board when Mr Knox wasn't in the shed. <i>(1981,Sunrise,43</i>)

### boss's boots
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Boss's boots</i>. Whenever the boss is sighted the shearers pass on the phrase `boss's boots' or `boots on the board' as a warning. Not only was the boss the only person in the shed to wear boots, but from the doubled-up position of the blade shearer working over a sheep, the boss's boots were the first thing he saw. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,95</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Yet I believe that if a shearer does continually cut sheep in these places he shears less in a day, as he is mentally conscious of his shearing, looking for the `boss's boots.'<i>Ibid.</i> 155 BOSS'S BOOTS. This means what it says: it is the footwear a shearer watches out for. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,4</i>)

### Bossy
 
 <i>n.</i> a pet name for a calf or cow; or a call for a calf. [ca1930s Dalton's farm: a calf name and occasional call (with Sookey) [cf DARE: check]]

### bossy
 
 <i>n.</i> a bossy person (woman)

>  <b>HARVEY</b> But the [tram-]conductor wasn't around, so the blonde lady grabbed a fag [19] and the Yank lit it for her. One or two of the old bossies [sc. old ladies] started to cough. <i>(1964,Any Old Dollars Mister?,18</i>)

### Boston bun
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>Boston bun:</i> This term refers to a sweet bun, filled with jam or cream and iced with coconut. (see: bread roll, iced buns) <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,17</i>)

### bosun bird
 
 <i>n.</i> = red-tailed tropic bird, amokura

>  <i>Red-tailed Tropic Bird Phaeton rubricauda roseotincta</i> MATHEWS, 1926 ... OTHER NAMES:<i> Bo'sun bird, amokura. </i>... The Maoris of the North Cape region prized the long tail streamers of the red-tailed tropic bird. After easterly gales it is said they systematically searched the coast for these birds. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,108</i>)

### bot
 
 <i>n.</i> esp. a cold, the flu (esp. with coughing fits); catch, have the bot to become, feel unwell [ca1938 Havelock to have the bot 1953 14-15 M A2 Thames DHS 31 BOT, THE [sickness] [M7] -->1960 16C F B3 Marton D.H.S. 31 BOT, THE -->1960 17C M B6 St Bede's, Chch 31 BOT, THE BOT -->1984 16 M E89 Pakuranga Coll. 31 GOT THE BOT -->1984 17 M E97 Pakuranga Coll. 31 THE BOT -->1984 16-17 F E6 Pakuranga Coll. 31 BOT [F3M2] -->1984 16 F E9 Pakuranga Coll. 31 BOT, THE [F3]]

>  <b>BAKER</b> Emerging originally from children's slang to bulk quite considerably in a grown-up world is the serviceable term <i>bot</i>. Its origin seems to be medical, since in early uses <i>bot</i> is rendered as a germ, doubtless from bot-fly. [52] From this comes the phrase of greeting, <i>How are the bots biting?</i> By the 1920s a <i>bot</i> is being used extensively for a troublesome person, for a persistent borrower, a financial parasite. By 1925 the noun <i>bot</i> was translated to a verb, and as a result we have <i>to bot,</i> to borrow money, to impose on others, and <i>botting</i>, the practice. <i>To bot on</i> a person is widely used. An interesting extension to the term is to be found in Australia, in which country<i> to bite</i> is often the equivalent for <i>bot</i>. On the other hand <i>cold botting</i> is a straight-out request for food at house-doors. ... Of fairly recent development in New Zealand is the phrase<i> to have the bot</i>, to be sick or out of sorts, moody or disagreeable. par <i>(1941,NZ Slang,51</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bot, have the</i>. To feel unwell: to be moody: querulous: NZ: since ca1930, B1941. Ex bot n2 (<i>Dict</i>) <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,997</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> And if you have a good rub down and wrap up warm after, nobody will catch the bot. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,144</i>)

>  After complaining of Scott-Base-like conditions at last week's Auckland club footie, old Loosehead has come down with a spot of the bot and his regular Sunday Star column will take a week's rest with the joker himself. <i>(Jul 20, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),B10</i>)

>  <i>bot n. NZ & Austral. sl. 1. n</i>. scrounger, cadger; unspecified minor ailment (<i>have the bot). 2. v.i. bot on cadge from, impose on. [orig. = parasitic worm, f. LG] </i> <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,81</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 125]]



 
 <i>n.</i> short for bottle (of liquor)

>  `It's a grim show,' I said. `You can't have a victory on one bot of Canadian.' <i>(1945,Sixes & Sevens,7</i>)

>  You had a hell of a lot of laughs smuggling the booze in [to the Majestic Cabaret], especially when it was wartime ... Then you'd con the girls into hiding a couple of `bots' in the [?their] enormous stoals[sic] and stash the grog up the chimneys ... <i>(Jul 1984,Cosmo,56</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> to have the bot, (informal) to be ill; usu. as <i>the bot</i>; A vague feeling of unwellness in human beings; the mulligrubs: 1898 Alab.; a lung or bronchial disease, specifically positive tuberculosis

>  <b>MARY O'CONNOR</b> the bot; also bot ward, the isolation ward for tubercular patients <i>(1964,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: (Wairau Hospital) ca1912]

>  `Bot' is a rare example of medical slang, meaning a germ or a tubercular patient. <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <i>bot n.</i> annoying type of fly, hence a troublesome person, particularly a persistent borrower of money; <i>to have the bot</i>, to us;have tuberculosis; how are the bots biting? how are you? <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  <i>bot n.</i> annoying type of fly, hence a troublesome person, particularly a persistent borrower of money; <i>to have the bot</i>, to us;have tuberculosis; how are the bots biting? how are you? <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Bot. See bot-fly. 2. A germ: New Zealand medical: from ca1928. Perhaps ex the bot(-fly), which, in horses, lays eggs that are said to penetrate into the animal when they hatch. 3. Hence, a tubercular patient: id: from ca1929. Cf <i>bots biting,</i> q.v. <i>bot-fly</i>. A troublesome, interfering person: Australian: C.20. In G.W. +, often abbr. to <i>bot. </i> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,83</i>)

>  He had been feeling "a bit nauseous, hot and crabby" after taking antibiotics for "a bit of the bot." <i>(Nov 4, 1992,Evening Post,2</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a smalltime financial parasite, a cadger, a bludger.

>  <b>BAKER</b> By the 1920s a <i>bot </i> is being used extensively for a troublesome person, for a persistent borrower, a financial parasite. By 1925 the noun <i>bot</i> was translated to a verb, and as a result we have <i>to bot,</i> to borrow money, to impose on others, and <i>botting,</i> the the practice. <i>To bot on</i> a person is widely used. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,52</i>)

>  <i>bot n</i>. annoying type of fly, hence a troublesome person, particularly a persistent borrower of money; <i>to have the bot</i>, to us;have tuberculosis;<i> how are the bots biting?</i> how are you? <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  <i>bot n. NZ & Austral. sl. 1. n</i>. scrounger, cadger; unspecified minor ailment <i>(have the bot). 2. v.i. bot on cadge from, impose on. [orig. = parasitic worm, f. LG] </i> <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,81</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 125]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bot </i>a germ, originally in 1920s a TB patient, latterly a minor ailment, usually cold or flu; probably derived from the botfly, whose larvae feed beneath the skin, an afflicter of horses. - <i>to have the bot</i> to feel unwell or irritable. - <i>how're the bots biting? a humorous greeting. - <i>on the bot</i> to cadge. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,19</i>)

### bot (on)
 
 <i>v.</i> to sponge on, cadge from (See [cf. cold bot](../C#cold-bot),[bite](../B#bite))

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>(1941,NZ Slang,52</i>) [NOTES: By the 1920s a bot is being used extensively for a troublesome person, for a persistent borrower, a financial parasite. By 1925 the noun bot was translated to a verb, and as a result we have to bot, to borrow money, to impose on others, and botting, the practice. To bot on a person is widely used.]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bot</i>. To borrow money; (usually <i>bot on</i>) to sponge or impose on others): Aust and NZ: since ca1925, B1941. Ex sense 1 of the n. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,997</i>)

>  <i>bot n. NZ & Austral. sl. 1. n.</i> scrounger, cadger; unspecified minor ailment (<i>have the bot). 2. v.i. bot on cadge from, impose on. [orig. = parasitic worm, f. LG] </i> <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,81</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 125]]

### bot ward
 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1949 Wellington Public Hospital (J. Greer)] (See [bot](../B#bot))

### bot-burglar
 
 <i>n.</i> a male homosexual [ca1978 Auckland p.c. G. McEwen]

### bot-nursing
 
 <i>n.</i> duty in a TB ward or sanatorium [ca1949 J. Greer Wellington Public Hospital p.c. 1951] (See [cf. bot ward](../B#bot-ward))

### Botany Bay language
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. Australian language](../A#australian-language))

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> I am credibly informed that the person this letter is for, is a cattle stealer, a swindler and a bastard. ... choice Botany Bay language. <i>(1866,Otago and the Middle Island of NZ,20</i>)

### botbomb
 
 <i>n.</i> medicine for botfly strike?

>  <b>CRUMP</b> A fair dinkum thoroughbred mongrel of a pacer ... Uncle Wally ... poured more oats, chaff, hay, good grass, time and bot-bombs into his miserable hide than all the cows were getting between them. <i>Ibid.</i> 124 Uncle Wally bought a brand-new sulky and patched up all the harness. Then he had to have a trailer and horseshoes and entry fees and more bot-bombs. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,123</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a drench or drenching equipment.

>  <b>PETER</b> botbomb: drench. <i>(2000,Peter Glossary,3</i>)

>  Equipment used for drenching: botbomb gear. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### bother and fuss
 
 <i>n.</i> a bus [ORIGIN: rhyming slang]

>  And I must have dropped my leather because when I look for it on the bother and fuss, it's gone. -->bother and fuss ... bus <i>(May 19, 1963,NZ Truth,21</i>)

### bothy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Donovan is sitting in the bush above the camp seeing to his equipment under the plastic awning of the crew's `bothy'. This shelter contains two generators ... <i>(Jun 18, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

### bottle
 
 <i>n.</i> ?a thick part on the trunk away from the ground

>  <b>REED</b> A very large tree is chosen, standing close to the skids ... He has got what they call a great `bottle' on him, so after debate it is agreed to bring him down with the axe instead of backing him up with the cross-cut. <i>(1957,Story of the Kauri, The</i>) [NOTES: 1957 Reed Story of Kauri 159 quoting 1864 Weekly News (Auckland)]



 
 <i>v.</i> 1. to hit or attack with a (broken) bottle. (cf. ODE) 2. to feed from a bottle (1981 cit. below)

>  I've been here every day since the World Cup started. I don't know what people think. No one has bottled me yet, though. <i>(Jun 22, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,3</i>)

>  Porirua's active Mongrel Mob does not escape my thoughts. They like bottling cops. <i>(Jul 14, 2002,Sunday Star Times,C11</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> ... a real tough guy smacked another guy in the face with a bottle. It was the first time most of the students had seen a guy get bottled and about half of them fled. <i>(1971,Bullshit and Jellybeans,82</i>)

>  He said that James Davys had been `bottled' by McCormick and would be scarred for life. <i>(Oct 2, 1974,Evening Post,19/3</i>)

>  On both occasions he had denied `bottling' Tuohy. <i>(Nov 25, 1980,NZ Truth,5/2</i>)

>  ... The pupils were allowed to bottle the young lambs. <i>(Nov 14, 1981,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  Then opposition supporters lost the plot and rushed on to the pitch and started bottling players. <i>(Aug 18, 2003,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  The accused told her she was standing up for a friend of hers who was going to get bottled. ... "She spoke to this girl about wanting to bottle her friend..." <i>(Sep 29, 2006,Dominion Post,A7</i>)

### bottle boy
 
 <i>n.</i> a young collector of refundable bottles after sports gatherings

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The bottle boys will have a good collection after this game. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,78</i>)

### bottle drive
 
 <i>n.</i> the organised collection of refundable bottles, esp. empty beer or soft-drink bottles to raise money for causes. [ca1940 Havelock]

>  <b>HALL</b> ... plus a bottle drive for the Girl Guides. ... JOHN. Get a lot of bottles? <i>(1977,Glide Time,17</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bottle drive</i> fund raising by collecting empty bottles from residences for the miniscule deposit; now declining. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,19</i>)

### bottle lantern
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. miners' lantern etc](../M#miners'-lantern-etc))

>  <b>McBRIDE</b> Bottle lanterns were made by knocking off the bottom of a bottle and dropping a candle in, which was held firmly in place by the narrow neck ... <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women</i>) [NOTES: 1940 McBride in Tales of Pioneer Women in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 17]

### bottle license
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRACE</b> Europeans on all sides are trying to get what they call `bottle licenses' - which, if they succeed, will mean that the Natives may be paid their rents in grog! <i>(Jun 1868,Journal,192,1928 ed.</i>)

>  Bottle licenses [sic] can be granted only in the Provincial Districts of Hawke's Bay, Otago, Westland, Marlborough, and Nelson; and in those districts there are not more than sixty. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,258</i>) [NOTES: [?relation to use of BOTTLE-STORE]]

### bottle off
 
 <i>v.</i> stop (up); make stop

>  This laconic rejoinder, to my unspeakable relief, bottles off my tormentor. <i>(Aug 1862,NZ Magazine,2</i>) [NOTES: Chapman's NZ Magazine]

### bottle out
 
 <i>v.</i> to dash, bolt out [[?a play on or mistake for bolted]. -->1907 May 18 Truth 100/6/2 One day when he went round to the back of the house from the street two men bottled out of the front door; they were bottle gatherers who had been drinking with his wife.]

### bottle shop
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. bottle store](../B#bottle-store))

>  ...will Smith have to make any changes to Bennett's idiomatic script for an audience unfamiliar with such peculiarly British terms as off-licences rather than bottle shops? <i>(Apr 18, 2004,Sunday Star Times,16</i>)

### bottle store
 
 <i>n.</i> a shop from which liquor can be bought by the bottle or in bulk. [[OED2 (a) Sth Afr. =<i>bottle-shop</i> 1862, 1950; 1944 NZ.]]

>  Bottlestore Attendant required for inner city tavern. Honesty, reliability and personality is the requiem for this position. <i>(Feb 15, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),2</i>) [NOTES: Original in <i>Evening Post</i> 11/1/1986]

>  <b>O'Sullivan</b> It had not entered his mind to stop at the bottle store as they drove past the Masonic. <i>(2006,Pictures by Goya,212</i>)

>  There are rats in the bottle store, dozens of them! <i>(1944,NZ Short Stories,104,1953 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1944 Davin NZ Short Stories (1953) 104 (J.A. Lee)]

>  <b>BOOTH</b> The boys had abandoned their kicking ... to head for the show via the bottle store ... <i>(1964,Footsteps in the Sea,64</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> We knew it was him who broke into the bottle-store of the pub one night ... <i>(1971,Bastards I Have Met,19</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> I stood up and went into the bottle store and bought three bottles of beer in a paper bag. <i>(1976,Pacific Star,42</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>bottle store</i>: Attached to every licensed hotel is not only a boozer, but a separate shop which sells your home supplies of booze. In other words, a LIQUOR STORE or PACKAGE STORE. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,17</i>)

>  ... will he feel obliged to negotiate with the dairy or the bottle-store down the road to rid his union of a possible undeserved appellation? <i>(Nov 7, 1983,National Business Review,6</i>)

>  <b>OVENDEN</b> Frank bought a jug of beer from an ancient woman who came out of the bottlestore to serve them ... <i>(1984,Ratatui,187</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bottle store</i> the room in the pub that sells bottles, and indeed cans, of beer and wine and spirituous liquors; known as a `bottle department' across the Tassie. <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,16</i>)

### bottle top
 
 <i>n.</i> a policeman's hat

>  ...Bluey got smart and ... knocked a constable's bottletop off his head, ... <i>(Oct 1952,Here & Now,19</i>)

### bottle-brand
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to mark sheep with a substance for branding (identification) purposes. [bottle brand]

>  We had a dispute as to the 65 [sheep]. The plaintiff ought to have kept the sheep properly. I wanted to bottle-brand the 65; but he wouldn't consent, and I raddle-marked them. They were counted in my 13,651, but they were not bottle-branded. <i>(Mar 21, 1868,Otago Witness,6</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a substance, usually made up of tar and pitch used to brand sheep, the mark made on a sheep, and the activity involved in dispensing the brand. [also bottle brand]

>  TWO POUNDS REWARD. Lost from Timaru, on the 14th inst., SIXTY-NINE WETHERS, branded Prince of Wales' Feather, and also Bottle brand ... <i>(Feb 18, 1865,Timaru Herald,5</i>)

>  STRAYED from M EWEN'S paddock, Cambridge road, on the 2nd instant. two Lincoln EWES, red mark on back of neck, and blue bottle brand on rump. <i>(Feb 12, 1876,Waikato Times,3</i>)

>  He did, immediately after returning to town, write to me to be good enough to inform him when I mustered, so that he might be present, and claim any sheep not marked with the bottle-brand .. The bottle-brand was of pitch and tar, and would have been as visible as the station-brand. <i>(Mar 21, 1868,Otago Witness,6</i>)

### bottle-branding
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the marking of sheep with pitch and tar for identification purposes.

>  There was a dispute between us as to 65 sheep - whether they were included in the first 7000, and had escaped bottle-branding, or whether they were other sheep which had not been brought into the yards amongst the 7000. <i>(Mar 21, 1868,Otago Witness,6</i>)

### bottle-doctor
 
 <i>n.</i> ?a doctor who makes up his own prescriptions; a doctor who lived off a practice of (indiscriminately) prescribing medicines (See [cf. aspro doctor (at aspro)](../A#aspro-doctor-(at-aspro)))

>  Long years ago he had begun to practise as a conventional bottle-doctor - and moved with the times until the writing of a prescription began to prompt him to pause ... <i>(1975,NZ Short Stories,3/2</i>) [NOTES: Sargeson `A Final Cure]

### bottle-dress
 
 <i>n.</i> humorous, apt WW2 pun, referring to the number of bottles of beer that can be carried in a battle-dress tunic

>  <b>BRUNO</b> An O.S. `bottle-dress' jacket. <i>(1948,Maleesh George,5</i>)

### bottle-jack
 
 <i>n.</i> a large jack used for lifting houses, etc. [Jim writes : not in DNZE. Collins tags it as NZ.. See ref. by Ian Gordon in <i>Listener</i>. But OED defines it as "a kind of lifting jack" with an 1871 citation - is it the same device?]

### bottle-mark
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to mark sheep with a staining substance for branding (identification) purposes. [bottle mark]

>  .. eventually I consented to have the sheep in the yards counted and bottle-marked, the sheep being counted out after they had been so marked in a small pen, where they were put on lots of about 100. <i>(Mar 21, 1868,Otago Witness,6</i>)

### bottle-o
 
 <i>n.</i> a spherical, glass stopper of an old-fashioned patent soft-drink bottle used as a marble; =bottley, glassy.

>  <b>PARK</b> He took a green bottle-o from the unresisting hand of an open-mouthed, dismayed child ... [177] whatcher let him take the glassy for? ... <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,176</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bottle-oh</i> dealer in empty bottles, c.1900s, known from his or her cry. - a green marble. Both NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,19</i>)



 
 <i>exclam.</i> [bottled (as Orsman) sic in 1907 citation?? (bolted??)]

>  One day when he went round to the back of the house from the street two men bottled out of the front door; they were bottle gatherers who had been drinking with his wife. <i>(May 18, 1907,NZ Truth,100/6/2</i>)

>  ... he dabbled in the `bottle-O' profession. <i>(1915,Countess of Liverpool's Gift Book,92</i>) [NOTES: (Poulton)]



 
 <i>n.</i> [[`Give me your bottles before the day-goes'.]]

>  <b>HYDE</b> The only other queer person in Calver Street [Newtown] was old Nigger Jack, who drove about with a dusty sack round his shoulders, chanting, `Bottle-oh, bottle-oh,' in his strong, sad voice. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,36,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <i>bottle-oh n. NZ & Austral. sl.</i> collector of empty bottles. [his cry] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,82</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
 <i>n.</i> a person who collects empty bottles for sale; a dealer in empty bottles

>  You would have laughed had you seen the bottle-oh carts lined up plying for hire. <i>(Oct 10, 1917,Wanganui Herald,4</i>)

>  [Advt] Sacrifice of Modern Five-Roomed <i>(Dec 2, 1916,Press, The,6</i>)

>  If you don't know Sydney Claridge - Sid the bottle-o, to you - then you don't know your Wellington. <i>(Sep 30, 1953,NZ Observer,12</i>)

>  [Hdg] Bottle-o's On The Job Again. <i>(Jan 6, 1948,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> He took a green bottle-o from the unresisting hand of an open-mouthed, dismayed child... 'Gimme back me marble', yelped the dismayed one. <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,176</i>)

>  The `Bottle-O' belted the back gate and called for the `dead marines' ... hunter of beer shells. <i>(Sep 30, 1905,NZ Truth,15/7/6</i>)

>  <b>FANNING</b> The fire brigade horse harnessed to a dust-cart, the cavalry charger yoked to a `bottle-oh's' decrepit vehicle, can be galvanised by the old sounds ... <i>(1910,Players and Slayers,50</i>)

>  ... we find our old friend the bottle-o doing a great business. Does he buy the bottles? No, ... he gets them baksheesh, ... <i>(Nov 15, 1918,Kia-ora Coo-ee,Second Series 5/14,1981 ed.</i>)

>  The ... cavalry charger yoked to a `bottle-oh's' decrepit vehicle, can be galvanised at the old sounds ... <i>(Aug 11, 1919,Quick March,85</i>)

>  He was dressed in a dilapidated tweed suit, ... and he displayed the manners of a bear or a bottle-oh. <i>(1930,Short Stories (various authors),194</i>) [NOTES: `Maori Mac']

>  <b>HYDE</b> `Our Dady <i>does </i>drink . . . [sic] he's got lots and lots of bottles, and we sell them to the bottle-oh.' <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,337</i>)

>  <i>bottle-oh n</i>. collector of empty bottles. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),612</i>)

>  <i>bottle-oh n. </i>collector of empty bottles. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  <i>bottle-oh n. NZ & Austral. sl.</i> collector of empty bottles. [his cry] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,82</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>BRUNO</b> He was bottle-oh [that is collected the empties at a picture theatre] <i>(1944,Desert Daze,10</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bottle-oh</i> dealer in empty bottles, c.1900s, known from his or her cry. - a green marble. Both NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,19</i>)

### bottle-o-er
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The King of Bottle-o-ers. <i>(1915,Countess of Liverpool's Gift Book,92</i>) [NOTES: 1915 Poulton in The Countess of Liverpool's Gift Book 92]

### bottle-o-ing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... a rank failure at bottle-o-ing <i>(1915,Countess of Liverpool's Gift Book,93</i>) [NOTES: (Poulton).]

### bottle-washer
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [chief cook and bottle-washer](../C#chief-cook-and-bottle-washer))

>  With coffee, serve Christmas cake and a big bowl of chocolate scorched almonds. Happy Christmas to the chief cook and remember the chief cook never has to be the bottlewasher. <i>(Dec 21, 2002,Dominion Post,F4</i>)

### bottlebrush
 
 <i>n.</i> an Aust. shrub with dense, coloured flower heads consisting of stamens all around the stem; usu. in NZ applied to the introduced Aust. genus <i>Callistemon</i> [or the Myrtaceae or Protaceae families] used as ornamentals, with brilliant red flowers reminiscent in shape to the brushes used to clean out (?orig. wine) bottles [cf the similar decriptive application to <i>pohutukawa</i>

>  <b>MATHEW</b> ... and largest of all [the flowering trees], the `Pohutokawa' which grows quite down to the coast, covered with large crimson tufted blossoms shaped like a bottle-brush.] <i>(1873,Autobiography</i>) [NOTES: 1873 S. Mathew Autobiography in 1940 Founding of NZ 206]

>  <b>WHITE</b> The name the Bottle-Brush, by which the Callistemon, another Australian shrub, is commonly called, helps to describe both the pink and the red. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,12</i>)

>  <i>bottle brush n.</i> name given to a genus of shrub and trees bearing brush-like flowers; the flowers themselves. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),612</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 82; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 56]]

### bottlenose
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FORSTER</b> Great Shoals [295] of a cetaceous fish, whose nose was quite blunt & round as a bottle & the body slender, passed us; our Sailors called them <i>bottlenoses</i> but the[y] were not what Dale calls a <i>Bottlenose</i>, which is the <i>beaked Whale</i>: for this fish has no beak at all, was very slender, about 3 yards long, quite black & all had a white spot before the dorsal fin. [fn] The Bottle-head or Flounder's Head in Dale, <i>History and Antiquities of Harwich</i> ... (London, 1730), p.411, ... is the Bottle-nosed whale (<i>Hyperoodon ampullatus</i>), a species named by Forster in a footnote ... <i>(Jun 8, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/294</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> [Of a black cetaceous fish, Cook Strait] It seemed to be about three yards long, and was slender and blunt-headed, from whence our sailors called it the bottle-nose, a name which Dale applies to a very different fish, the beaked whale, of which the beak or nose resembles the neck of a bottle. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/233</i>)

### bottlenose(d) dolphin
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Bottlenose dolphin often work the shallows, and have been observed chasing kahawai and forcing them to strand themselves high and dry - a scaled-down version of the orca/eagle ray hunt. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/20</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BOTTLENOSED DOLPHIN ... <i>Other names: Tursiops truncatus</i>; cowfish. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,302</i>)

>  As we pass the Shelter Islands we catch a glimpse of a bottlenose dolphin and a Fiordland crested penguiin with a distinctive yellow stripe over each eye. <i>(Dec 17, 2010,Waikato Times,14</i>)

### bottlenose(d) grampus
 
 <i>n.</i> (dolphin) [see grampus]

>  Eleven bottle nose grampus came on shore at Waitangi. 30 ft. long ... <i>(Dec 19, 1884,Chudleigh Diary,331,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> Some of the oil (black fish or bottle-nosed grampus) was still in the workshop in my time, until my brother, Reg tried it out on his mustering boots one day and was begged ... to leave them outside because they made her feel ill. <i>(1985,Last Entail Male,25</i>)

### bottler
 
 <i>n.</i> someone or something that is considered outstanding, excellent; something superior, splendid, excellent of its kind; corker, beaut; (or ironically) for soething or someone eccentrically out of the ordinary, dag [1951 14 M 14 St Bede's, Chch 4 BOTTLER [someone/something liked] ;1960 17C M B6 St Bede's, Chch 8 BOTTLER [something good]]

>  [Hdg] "A Bottler"... On cleaning the fish he discovered it had swallowed a beer bottle shortly before being hooked. <i>(Feb 26, 1945,NZEF Times,3</i>)

>  For a series opener, last night's was a bottler. <i>(Aug 6, 2004,Dominion Post,B19</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> But I was mighty curious all the same - that must have been a real bottler of a tangi. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,100</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bottler</i>. 2. Expression of high praise or deep delight: NZ juvenile: 20C. B1941. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,997</i>)

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>bottler </i> <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Give me the Gurkhas any day. They're bloody bottlers. They love it. <i>Ibid.</i> 181 Ya know old Donovan? He was a bloody bottler. A real beaut. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,179</i>)

>  [A Paraparaumu correspondent writes] Concerning the book `Lolita', your columns on Thursday contained a `bottler.' <i>(Jan 17, 1960,Dominion,8</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> A really good thing or person. .`My bike's a real bottler.' ;We would've spent the night drifting if Herbie hadn't brought his bag of tools and some spare parts. He got us going once before, he's a little bottler, is Herbie. <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS]</i>)

>  <i>(Oct 15, 1966,Dominion,3/1</i>) [NOTES: [Aviemore Visitors' Book] see FANTABULOUS]

>  <i>bottler n</i>. exceptional person, usually in a derogatory sense. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> [Boys speak] `Arthbutt's [sledge] is a bottler.' `Yeah, but he didn't build it. I reckon ours is faster, ...' <i>(1979,Supper Waltz Wilson,46</i>)

>  <i>bottler n. Austral. & N.Z. inf.</i> an exceptional or outstanding person or thing. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,126</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 82; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 56]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bottler</i> object of admiration or great approval; eg `Thanks for all your help, mate. You're a bloody bottler.' NZA <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,19</i>)

>  [Hdg] Bottler of a Journey. <i>(Jan 10, 1968,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Sunday being popularly described as a "bottler," it is safe to say that Saturday was satisfactory for a "schooner". <i>(Apr 14, 1952,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> In the preserving season places where teenagers are meeting are suddenly all male - the girls are bottling. 'She's a bottler' is the highest praise a man can give about a woman. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,114</i>)

>  [Hdg] Day's Slick solo show is a bottler. <i>(Nov 26, 1995,Sunday Star Times,D4</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> That must have been a real bottler of a tangi. <i>(1996,Boss's Story, The,100</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Of children's terms.. we may note snitcher, snitch, whopcacker, bottler and snozzler, descriptive of something superlative or excellent, both as nouns and adjectives. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,51</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> an awkward or extremely difficult problem

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> A very awkward proposition or problem. .. `We can only go at a snail's pace, if anyone as much as sneezes, the whole structure could settle back again, and that'd be the end. It's a little bottler.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],97</i>)

### bottles
 
 <i>n.</i> a drinking game

>  Other well-known [drinking] games are Hokonui Swindle, Bottles, Fluffy Ducks, Bunnies are Hovering, Cardinal Huff, All Blacks, Matches, and The Amazing Grimaldi Brothers. All these games involve either a complicated intellectual exercise, tongue twisters, mathematical calculations or aerobatics in a confined space; High Candelabras for example, involves intoxicated individuals attempting to traverse the four walls of a room without touching the floor. <i>(Mar 13, 1988,Dominion Sunday Times,16</i>)

### bottlie
 
 <i>n.</i> a spherical, glass stopper of an old-fashioned patent soft-drink bottle used as a marble, cf bottle-o; clear glass marble. [ca1910 W.H.B. Orsman -->ca1938 Havelock]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates, aggies, aggotties, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cat's eyes, chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, molly-bars, mulley-bars [mullibars: large marbles], peelies, peewees, piss-wees, pretties, Scotties, Scottish alleys, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers, white alleys, and in more recent years ball-bearings, chippies, plastics, and woodies. ...When I first went to school, bottlies were good currency, being classed as two-ers, but usually sodawaters and black-bottlies counted as the equivalent of six-ers. ... (Petone, Wellington, 1913) <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

>  <b>KEITH & MAIN</b> Marbles were the currency of childhood and there were an astonishing number of names for them, some referring to the material or colour, others to value or function in the game. There were agates, aggies, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cats-eye, [sic] chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, mollies, peelies, peewees, pisswees, pretties, Scotties, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers and whities. The march of technology added ball bearings - ballies or steelies - which were unbeatable when it came to smashing an opponent's miserable glassie. World War II[roman] brought `clayeys' often imperfectly spherical they were pathetic terracotta substitutes for real marbles. <i>(1984,Yesterdays in Maoriland,288</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bottlie</i> marble stopper from neck of aerated water bottle, prized by marble buffs. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,19</i>)

>  I could spin a top `overarm' and shoot alleys, glassies and bottlies with the best of the boys. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,7</i>) [NOTES: (Dorothy Butler)]

>  Bottlies - the glass marble stoppers from the tops of bottles. These worked perfectly well but were not considered to be high class. <i>(1987,Alfredton,75</i>) [NOTES: Quote from c1935]

>  <b>BUTLER in GIFKIN</b> I could.. shoot alleys, glassies and bottlies with the best of the boys. <i>(1988,Through the Looking Glass,7</i>)

>  Marbles was another recreational pursuit... Silveries and bottlies being the most prized marbles. <i>(2008,NZ Memories,73/12</i>)

### bottlo
 
 <i>n.</i> ?container for fragile parcels

>  <b>SLATTER</b> There was [postal] work [in the 1960s], hard satisfying work. Unloading parcel bags, huge cumbersome containers nicknamed pre-fabs, from a three-door Z. There was slots, there was lumps, there was packing fragies in bottlos, there was the tying down of the north line for a clearance, the scissors, lead seals and squeezers. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,85</i>)

### bottom
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> The Australian diggers soon found out, that hitherto only the superficial deposits had been worked, and that they had not yet come to the `bottom', where ... the richest treasure was to be expected. They began `deep sinkings', and after having dug to a depth of about 130 feet ... struck a second gold-bed. <i>(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles),113</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  The first hole was imperfectly bottomed, owing to the quantity of dead wood. <i>(Jan 28, 1855,Nelson Examiner,2</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering or grazing] the lower slope of hills or valleys.

>  Romney Marsh are perhaps engaging more attention .. not liable to take foot-rot on the dampest bottoms. <i>(Nov 30, 1867,Weekly News,18</i>)

>  .. with alluvial flats in the bottoms of the valleys. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,3</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Walking had always been my long suit and the thought of doing the job from the bottoms just didn't appeal to me. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,181</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. it's all good, open tussock and carpet grass country with grand bottoms. <i>(1972,Sheep Thief,24</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> As it happened they had to straggle-muster the Glen May bottoms the day after I arrived. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,58</i>)

### bottom beat
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mustering area that is the lowest on the hill or mountain and easiest to muster.

>  <b>STRONACH</b> The beat on the top of the hill is known as the 'top beat' and the one along the bottom as the 'bottom beat.' <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,24</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> An easy beat is one of easy walking, usually the bottom or top beat, though the latter may entail a long initial climb. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,6</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> I was given the bottom beat because I'd done the top the day before. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,83</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> The man on the bottom beat got into trouble. Some sheep had been smothered through a poor bit of shepherding ... <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,84</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> If the 'face' was not large, only a 'top beat' and 'bottom beat' would be necessary, but usually the 'spurs' had to be 'sidled' or traversed across by several men. <i>(1990,Forgotten Worker, The,64</i>)

### bottom flat
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a river terrace or flat land near a river that was once river bed.

>  <b>GORDON</b> .. there was just too much water. I don't know how much went over the bottom flats. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,182</i>)

>  Typical of many Taranaki hill country properties, the terrain varies from wide creek bottom flats to rolling medium hills, on into very steep country. <i>(May 31, 2002,Dominion,16</i>)

>  Okirae Rd divides the farm between the Renegour block and the bottom flats. <i>(Jul 26, 2002,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

### bottom liner
 
 <i>n.</i> [one who works from the base of a problem, see 2011 cit.]

>  <b>Jane Bowron</b> Sure it's quake porn but people are naturally curious and I'm a bottom liner, as in: see how bad it is, absorb it, and then start getting on with the business, but I fully understand those who don't want a bar of looking at any of it. <i>(Apr 25, 2011,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### bottom man
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shearer at the furthest end of the board from the wool table in a woolshed.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. those bottom men were hardly 'drummers.' <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a musterer with the lowest or bottom beat.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> As they approached the far end of the block, the two bottom men hung fire ... <i>(1949,High Country Days,72</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> .. the sheep may run down and across the creek before the bottom man can catch them. <i>(1951,NZ High Country,23</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> If there is any extra work at the end of the day, such as killing a sheep or driving the mob on round the hill, the bottom man gets the job, not the top man. <i>(1970,Many a Glorious Morning,92</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> As it happened the bottom man, Jack McIver, had them safely held ... <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,17</i>)

### bottom paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a low-lying paddock or one beyond a gorge or terrace.

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> Two other workers and I used to take some lunch down to the bottom paddock where the white-face cattle were. <i>(1953,Short Stories (various authors),18</i>)

### bottom side
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sheep handling] a position or area below a mob of sheep being worked on a hill.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> To head on the bottom side is to run below them when actually running out to 'head' or catch sheep. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,168</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of a sheepdog] working below his sheep on a hill. (See [bottom side head](../B#bottom-side-head))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Bottom-side work: This refers to a dog working below his sheep on hill country. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,168 Glossary</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> bottomside: adjectival term indicating that a dog has had to work from below his sheep. The opposite, of course, is a top-side run. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,Glossary 9</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> topside run: the more usual and opposite of, bottomside run. [bottomside; bottom-side] <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,86</i>)

### bottom side head
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sheep dog work] the working of sheep from below when heading on a hill.

>  <b>STACEY</b> Arthur Smith started his dog Tommy down the river bed for a bottom side head. He then completed his head and stopped in the shade of the only bush for miles. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,67</i>)

### bottom-dogger
 
 <i>n.</i> (Commerce) a person who occupies lowest status in an economic sense.

>  Now that we are studying budgets I should like to give members the budget of one of the families I have just referred to as bottom-doggers. I think this should be interesting to honourable gentlemen who live in comfort ... <i>(Nov 1921,NZ Parliamentary Debates,192/283</i>)

>  None of your Civil servants or Arbitration Court Judges are called on to throw away a day's pay, but when you come down to the poor old bottom-dogger you say to him "Oh, you have got to keep up Anzac Day, it is the law of the country." <i>(Nov 1921,NZ Parliamentary Debates,192/282</i>)

>  A hungry down-at-heel bottom-dogger can not see the beauties of Auckland like a fat sleek tourist doing the Dominion on a banking account. <i>(Mar 14, 1923,Maoriland Worker,2</i>)

>  From wharf-truck to Mayoral chair, from bottom-dogger to chief citizen of the Empire city [Wellington], is something to be proud of. <i>(May 3, 1912,Maoriland Worker,7</i>)

>  Mr Howard said he proposed to look at the Budget from the point of view of the bottom-dogger, the worker. <i>(Nov 16, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,7</i>)

>  Mr E J Howard claimed that the Federation of Labor [sic] was now the stronghold and only hope of the "bottom-dogger". <i>(Jan 24, 1913,Maoriland Worker,5</i>)

>  What bottom-dogger's son was ever apprenticed to a trade which provided him with L30 in cash on starting, five shillings and sixpence a day for his first four years, and a "GUARANTEED POSITION" at the end of that time ... <i>(May 23, 1913,Maoriland Worker,5</i>)

### bottom-fisher
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  .. larry Johnston, a Greymouth long-line bottom-fisher who had parked up his commercial boat to chase the excitement. <i>(Aug 12, 2005,NZ Herald,D23</i>)

### bottom-land
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) riverflat. (See [bottom flat](../B#bottom-flat))

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Every two years Jim Stevenson and a team of well-chosen musterers headed out for a week to round up some of the horses ranging the Waiau River bottom-lands. <i>(1993,Station Country I,49</i>)

### bottoming
 
 <i>n.</i> [-->[AND 1856; OEDS2 2. Austral. In mining, the process of reaching bedrock 1890 (Boldr).]]

### bottoms
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Walking had always been my long suit and the thought of doing the job from the bottoms just didn't appeal to me. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,181</i>)

### Bouai
 
 <i>n.</i> [idiosyncratic spelling?] (See [boohai](../B#boohai))

>  <b>Simpson</b> Another New Zealand expression is `Up the Bouai' (pronounced boo-eye). There was a place near Auckland city which many years ago was somewhat isolated, and it was called the Bouai. The word is not Maori, for there is no `b' and no `h' sound in the original Maori tongue. Once more I cannot tell you where the word came from, but to go `up the Bouai' has become fairly general in use. Today, if a person comes to town from any lonely place in the forests or mountains, and a friend asks him where he has been, most likely the answer will be, `Oh, I've been up the Bouai.' It is a general word for an isolated region. <i>(1945,If You'd Care to Know,101</i>)

### bough shed
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a makeshift shelter made from tree branches, in which sheep are shorn.

>  <b>OGONOSWKA-COATES</b> On chilly days the men might build a bough shed, a leafy lean-to cut from the top of nearby trees. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,14</i>)

### bought biscuits
 
 <i>n.</i> commercially-manufactured biscuits as opposed to home-made biscuits

>  Despite their mother's prowess in the kitchen, Clark's sons Jack and Sam say they prefer bought biscuits to the home-made variety... <i>(Dec 4, 2003,Dominion Post,D5</i>)

### bought the farm
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  This best of British men.. has tossed the towel in after Charlie, his affable sidekick, bought the farm trying to defend his old mate. <i>(Jul 9, 1998,Dominion,23</i>)

### bought-in
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of stock ] not bred on a property, but purchased for breeding or fattening purposes.

>  ..he will not try to replace the poor producers with bought-in cows. <i>(Sep 26, 1925,NZ Dairy Exporter,21</i>)

>  I used to aim at a gross profit of GBP3 per head from bought-in steers, but in recent years that has not been possible. <i>(May 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,503</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> Laid down in English permanent pasture, it is cropped regularly in chou moellier to assist in fattening of home bred lambs and bought-in stock. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,48</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> Bought-in sheep should be isolated until they have been over the shearing board for foot paring and through the foot-bath. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,118</i>)

>  Bought-in cattle wintering on the final stages of a chou moullier crop get a supplementary ration of hay. <i>(Sep 27, 1962,NZ Farmer,3</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> These are all bought-in cattle. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,36</i>)

>  Of the three rams he used one year, 51% of the progeny from a bought-in ram were affected by ryegrass staggers ... [brought in] <i>(Aug 31, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,21</i>)

### bouilli
 
 <i>n.</i> an old word for stewed meat preserved in a tin as a common fare in emigrant ships and early settlement; compare <i>bully beef</i>; bouilli tin; a usu. early 19C. spg of a usual emigrant commodity: tinned boiled or stewed meat, esp. beef (OED 17C.), often, as emigrant-ship, or early colonial fare, `soup and bouilli'. Now usually<i> bully-beef</i>.

>  <b>SAM STEPHENS</b> [Surveyor] Dinner off(?) preserved soup & bouilli. <i>(1843,Letters & Journals,171</i>)

>  Sent him my Shakespeare, some brandy and bouilli soup. [Canterbury] <i>(Feb 18, 1849,Torlesse Papers,57,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> Our biscuits [on board ship] were of the coarsest ... our meat was a stated allowance of salt pork and salt junk with an occasional tin of soup and bouilli. <i>(1940,Early Adventures in Otago,22</i>) [NOTES: [ca1850] 1940 E. Smith Early Adventures in Otago (ed Downie Stewart) 22]

>  <b>IMPEY</b> We enjoyed a good dinner of soup and bouilli. <i>(1850,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1850 Impey's Journal in 1933 MacDonald Pages from the Past 211]

>  <b>HODGKINSON</b> [Dietary on board emigrant ship includes] soup and bouilli. <i>(1856,Description of Canterbury,26</i>)

>  <b>BODELL</b> (Population principally Diggers [with] good payable gold.) ... At last we managed to get a hot pannikin each of what I called Boilly Soup a Mixture of Tea Sugar Biscuit and Pork fat, anything better than hunger. <i>(1890,Soldier's View of Empire, A,176</i>) [NOTES: c1890 Bodell A Soldier's View of Empire (1982) 176]

### bouilli tin
 
 <i>n.</i> a tin which has contained bouilli (useful as a utensil to early colonists and perhaps reinforcing the development of the form billy), bouilli tin.

>  A bouilli tin (that is a tin in which six pounds of preserved meat is packed for the voyage) to put my small things in. <i>(1849,Australian & NZ Gazette,35/57</i>) [NOTES: (Diary Overland Journey Wellington to New Plymouth)]

>  <b>PRATT</b> There was another of these low chimneys the architect of which ... had inserted a boulli soup tin (minus the bottom) in the clay ... as an aperture. <i>(1877,Colonial Experiences,15</i>)

>  <b>NORMAN & MUSGROVE</b> ... we found a soup-and-bouillie tin. <i>(1866,Voyage & Proceedings of HMCS Venture,29</i>)

### boulder bank
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We were caught in a fog at Pepin's island and had to creep in, sounding all the way for fear of running on the Boulder bank. <i>(1875,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,11/504</i>)

>  Our port of shipment in the early "fifties" was the "boulder bank".. to whence also came all the bullock drivers and shearers' cheques, to be "knocked down" on their periodical "bursts". <i>(Oct 30, 1890,Otago Witness,32</i>)

>  We made D'Urville's Island in a week, and were inside the "boulder bank" of Tasman Bay nine days out from Port Cooper. <i>(Oct 23, 1890,Otago Witness,30</i>)

>  <b>Hochstetter</b> &#65279;The harbour of Nelson... owes its formation to a most singular "boulder-bank" which extends eight miles along the coast, forming a natural dam. <i>(1867,New Zealand (Heaphy),470</i>)

### Boulder Bank
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> [Nelson Harbour] owes its formation to a most singular `boulder bank'. <i>(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles),470</i>)

>  From here, one can look across to the northern end of the Boulder Bank where 100 years ago, moa-hunters used to drive moa, trapping them at the peninsula's end. <i>(Oct 2, 2004,Dominion Post,E 16</i>)

>  The aptly named Boulder Bank is home to just a handful of baches and a unique way of life. <i>(Feb 4, 2003,Dominion Post,A9</i>)

>  A man name Burke, by trade a shoemaker, was drinking, it is said, at an unlicensed house at the Boulder Bank on Tuesday last. <i>(Jan 10, 1855,Nelson Examiner,3</i>)

>  Walking from the northern end of the bank, I passed large colonies of terns gathering noisily on the ocean side of the Boulder Bank. <i>(Feb 8, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

### boulder bash
 
 <i>v.</i> to slog along over boulders (on stream beds) (See [cf. boulder hop; bash v](../B#boulder-hop;-bash-v))

>  We boulder bashed out to Klondyke Corner. ... Canterbury Mountain Club Hut was reached after a short bush bash. <i>(1978,Manawatu Tramping Club Jubilee,90</i>)

### boulder hop
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [mustering and tramping] to cross a creek or river by jumping on to large rocks in the river bed. (See [cf. sense 2](../S#sense-2))

>  <b>MORRIS</b> .. the 'Fang Hill' creek always icy cold that we had to boulder hop. <i>(1997,Different Worlds,18</i>)

>  Cairns mark the route, which boulder-hops beside the river (past some delicious pools) until it sidles up a scree face... <i>(1990,Tramping in South Island Forest Parks,211</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to make one's way up a river bed by walking on boulders (when the banks are too steep to be tracked) (See [cf. rock-hop](../R#rock-hop))

>  After tea we boulder-hopped up to the wee Macgregor moraine. <i>(1948,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,4/18/299</i>)

>  <b>GLOVER</b> There were ... a host of others who were boulder-hopping and climbing in country that was [78] little known. <i>(1962,Hot Water Sailor,77</i>)

>  ... Pete crawled through the Wild Irishmen and forded the creek beside the shingle slip just below the confluence. Then he boulder-hopped along towards the burn. <i>(Apr 19, 1971,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  That afternoon a trip was made to Bell's Falls [sic] where 4 of the party climbed up the side of the waterfall and boulder hopped back to the hut. <i>(Jul 1967,Footprints,24/2</i>)

### boulder hopper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The bull Thar was panicking and even though I was a great boulder hopper, he was world class, leaving me in his wake. <i>(2001,Good Bastards: the larrikin's guide to success,256</i>)

### Boulder Lake daisy
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Clemisia parva</i> and <i>Clemisia rupestris</i> [Bolder sic in citation?]

>  The Bolder Lake Daisy, <i>Clemisia parva</i>, and <i>Clemisia rupestris</i> are two examples of plants with limited distribution elsewhere. <i>(2003,Tramping in South Island Forest Parks,62</i>)

### boulder-hopping
 
 <i>n.</i> travelling on a riverbed by stepping from boulder to boulder [some citations below are of a verb to boulder-hop] (See [cf. boulder-scrambling](../B#boulder-scrambling),[rock-hopping](../R#rock-hopping))

>  Beyond here is only a marked route requiring some bush-bashing and boulder-hopping beside the river, with bluffs to bypass beyond Kakapo Creek. <i>(1990,Tramping in South Island Forest Parks,119</i>)

>  ... all `beaches' obliterated and boulder-hopping a necessity. <i>(Dec 26, 1932,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1932 Dec 26 Diary in 1933 NZ Alpine Journal V/20/233]

>  The going up the north branch was much better ... and we were able to stay on the east bank all the way, travelling along flats or the hillside, or boulder-hopping; ... <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,48</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> Boulder-hopping alongside a river also requires that essential rhythm and, like skiing, a certain minimum speed so that you have enough momentum to recover from a stumble. <i>(1988,Hills, The,31</i>)

>  However, I staggered out, held together by much Elastoplast, sheer stubbornness and a sense of intense relief that there was not more full-on rock climbing, abseiling or boulder-hopping fast-running rivers - well, at least not until next year. <i>(Mar 22, 2003,Dominion Post,F13</i>)

>  <b>SHAW</b> The remaining two hours to Grey's Hut.. is more strenuous and requires some bush-bashing and boulder-hopping. <i>(1991,NW Nelson Tramping Guide,29</i>)

>  Our boulder hopping soon became impossible as the river ran into a steep gorge <i>(1932,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,I/11</i>)

>  Boulder-hopping up the river was a case of the "blind leading the blind." <i>(1933,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,II/5</i>)

### boulder-jumping
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. rock-hopping](../R#rock-hopping))

>  <b>SEVERINSEN</b> I tried to reach base by boulder-jumping and cliff-climbing along under the bluffs on the seashore. <i>(1970,Hunt the Far Mountain,116</i>)

### boulder-scrambling
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [boulder-hopping](../B#boulder-hopping))

>  ... it was a mixture of boulder-scrambling and `bush-hiking' interspersed with a few shingle flats. <i>(1937,NZ Alpine Journal,7/24/57</i>)

### bouldering
 
 <i>n.</i> scaling smooth boulders

>  We also complete a couple more abseils and even try some "bouldering" - scaling large smooth boulders with very few hand and footholds. <i>(Oct 25, 2003,Dominion Post,E12</i>)

### bounce
 
 <i>v.</i> to deceive

>  <b>MARSHAL</b> His' companion `bounced' (or <i>lied to</i>) the New Zealanders on this occasion [i.e. on a matter of trade]. <i>Ibid.</i> 190 Shall I bounce them? Shall I tell them a lie ... ? [Sydney-appointed interpreter speaks]. <i>(1834,Narrative of Two Visits,153fn</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i>

>  Te Atatu mussel curer, ... Nathan, 28, explained ... to be `bounced' was to be arrested. <i>(Jul 30, 1963,NZ Truth,5</i>)

### bounce the ball
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>BAKER</b> <i>To bounce the ball</i>, to test public feeling on a matter, to try the market (a phrase popularised, I understand, by a well-known New Zealand politician [the Hon R. Semple] who also introduced this country to the term <i>snivelling snufflebuster</i>) <i>(1941,NZ Slang,53</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bounce the ball</i>. To test public opinion or sentiment; test the stock market: NZ political coll: since ca1920. B1941. Ex the preliminaries usual among footballers. [i.e. Aust. Rules] <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,998</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bounce the ball</i> political test of public opinion; eg `D'ya think Douglas is bouncing the ball again with all these rumours of a GST rise?' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,19</i>)

### bouncer
 
 <i>n.</i> a dead parachutist

>  In parachuting jargon parachutists are "meatbombs" and those whose parachutes don't open become "bouncers". When a body hits the ground from about 3050m, it is said to bounce. <i>(Feb 10, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

### bouncinette
 
 <i>n.</i> "A metal frame with netting stretched over it and gently sloped so that a baby may recline or bounce in it. <i>Prop</i>" (Macquarie 1981). [Jim writes: This article is used and known as such in NZ. I bought one under that name in 1969 and my daughter-in-law did also in 1994.]

### bound
 
 <i>v.</i> to cut blubber for the try-pot

>  <b>RHODES</b> The third mate having a slight quarrel with one of the men in the blubber room about a steel to sharpen the blubber or bounding knife, and the work being retarded by his not bounding, the captain took the knife and bounded ... <i>(1836,Whaling Journal,15,1954 ed.</i>)

### boundary
 
 <i>n.</i> the perimeter or limits f a rural property, cf NZ town boundary = ?US city limit.

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> [The Station owner] has some excellent paddocks fenced in. The run is bounded on one side by the Turakina river, on the other three by a good post-and-rail fence. The sheep require but little looking after, as they cannot get over the boundary. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,25</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> I have been given charge of one of the sheep-runs [of the Wairarapa station]. My duties are to look after the boundaries, as the fences are often knocked about by wild pigs. Occasionally a run is visited by wild dogs - Maori mongrels gone wild. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,27,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Bound&#769;.a-ry</i> (bound&#769;.&adot.-r`y0,<i> n. [Add.] Boundary rider,</i> a man employed at a station (see STATION) to ride round the boundaries and keep the fences in order. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2018</i>)

>  <i>boundary ... NZ & Austral.</i> limits of sheep or cattle station. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,82</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 127, BOUNDARY RIDER]]



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> As my time is not fully occupied with `boundary' work, I have taken a contract to split posts for fencing. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,33,1935 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BOOTH</b> A shepherd resides at some convenient place on the boundary, whence it is his duty to walk or ride this boundary at least once a day, and see that no sheep have crossed it [Canterbury, c1860]. <i>(1912,Five Years in NZ,26</i>)

>  <i>boundary n</i>.- bound'ary ri'der n. an employee on a station whose duties are to ride round the boundary fences and keep them in order and generally to patrol the station. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> From then on they rode boundary, hundreds of miles of it, to ensure that the Molesworth cattle stayed on Molesworth. <i>(1969,Molesworth,181</i>)

### boundary dog
 
 <i>n.</i> an old word for a dog chained permanently on the boundary of a sheep-run to prevent stock straying; a dog chained to a break in fence, or to a gateway, to prevent sheep from passing through; fence dog.

>  <i>Boundary dog</i>. - A dog chained to his kennel at a gateway on a road to keep the sheep from passing through. Now that most roads are fenced, boundary dogs are only used temporarily. [1890-1910] <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>McLEOD</b> One of the first and most arduous of the sheepdogs [sic] tasks was the job of `boundary dog'. <i>(1972,High Country Anthology,213</i>)

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> The first [dog] seen [past the Rangitata] was a boundary-dog, chained to a break in a fence, to prevent sheep straying from one run to another. <i>(1876,Colonial Travel,196</i>)

>  For fences were not, ...and kennelled boundary-dogs strung off the invisible line that severed the runs throughout the plains. <i>(1987,Happy Endings,105</i>) [NOTES: 1902 Lancaster Our Lady of the Plains in 1987 Happy Endings 105]

>  The cruel fate which had condemned him to the living death of a boundary dog, had not yet wholly ... embittered him. <i>(1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,278</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dog housed near a boundary prior to fencing, or chained to a gateway in a boundary fence to prevent stock wandering, prior to roads being fenced. (See [dog shepherd; fence dog](../D#dog-shepherd;-fence-dog))

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> The first [dog] seen was a boundary-dog, chained to a break in a fence, to prevent sheep from straying from one run to another. <i>(1876,Colonial Travel,196</i>)

>  The boundary dog came in for special consideration [from the SPCA.] <i>(Apr 1908,NZ Farmer,324</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> At fords, or where rivers were shallow and easily crossed by stock, boundary dogs might be chained ... <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,56</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> They [gates] were the successors to the poor old boundary dogs tied to the fences on station boundaries to prevent sheep passing where the road went through. <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,157</i>)

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> Runholders who did not employ line riders used boundary dogs. <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,6</i>)

>  <b>BRITTAIN</b> Gradually large blocks of land were fenced with heavy No 4 gauge wire and the boundary dogs could be released from duty. <i>(2000,Lake Coleridge,94</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> In station diaries I often came across the entry 'Rode out to feed and water the boundary dog.' <i>(1976,From the Beginning: chronicles of a county,131</i>)

### boundary fence
 
 <i>n.</i> a fence on the perimeter of a rural property

>  <b>PAUL</b> The boundary fence which separates the poor man from the rich. <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,27</i>)

>  For the erection and maintenance of party and other boundary fences ... <i>(Nov 2, 1861,Memorandum by Native Secretary</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Nov 2 Sir George Grey Memorandum in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 112a]

>  <b>BARKER</b> After we passed our own boundary fence we came upon a very bad <i>track</i>this is the name by which all roads are called, and they do not deserve a better, - but it was the only path to our destination. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,76,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>G. W. WILSON</b> ... one of Dalgety Blackwood's Stations is in front of us and immediately to our left is the home station of another settler ... who has taken the trouble of planting a Hawthorn Hedge all away along the Boundary Fence, ... <i>(Jan 25, 1873,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Jan 25 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 149]

>  First, the fellows hunting horses off Moores country have smashed the boundary fence all to pieces in fifteen or twenty places ... <i>(Jan 27, 1878,Chudleigh Diary,266</i>)

>  Mae came to terms about our boundary fence. <i>(Sep 13, 1879,Chudleigh Diary,282</i>)

>  The [Fencing] Act deals with the nature of boundary fences, the cost thereof and its apportionment, and the obligations, rights, and duties of settlers in connection therewith. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,159</i>) [NOTES: 1895 Fencing Act in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 159]

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> Or he would take them riding over the back hills to look at a boundary fence. <i>(1934,Winds of Heaven,181</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> ... Brown sent him [sc. the neighbour] a notice in writing to the effect that a boundary fence must be made between them. <i>(1938,High Country,96</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> `Bad fences make bad neighbours,' is one of the truest sayings ... for many quarrels have arisen from boundary fences not being stock-proof. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,105,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> `What's that queer old wreck over by the boundary fence, Uncle Ted?' <i>(1948,Tidal Creek,91,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> He seldom spoke and when he did it was usually to call down the wrath of heaven on the wild pigs that ignored his boundary fences and slashed his lambing percentages every spring. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,178,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> [see bushline] <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,82</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> `Found two sheep worried inside the boundary fence,' he muttered. <i>(1968,Turn of the Tide, The,62</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> ... they found the dogs had about one hundred and fifty ewes mobbed against a boundary fence, and were worrying for all they were worth. <i>(1970,Tat,61</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> Here, up and over the broken bank to the level above the boundary fence, his father had constructed a rough-and-ready hen run. <i>(1981,Hot & Copper Sky,12</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> The boundary fence on Tony Schluter's property was in pretty good shape, stock proof for 99/9 per cent of the way around the 800 odd acres. <i>(1986,Diary of a Pig Hunter,21</i>)
