>  <b>SLIGO</b> He could look over his land, well enough kept what with a manager and then ... the homestead! He climbed the boundary fence. <i>(1987,Final Things,12</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fence delineating one property from another [also colloq. & fig. a social barrier.]

>  <b>PAUL</b> The boundary fence which separates the poor man from the rich. <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,27</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND</b> These gates are usually on boundary fences at right angles to the road and between stations. <i>(1947,Holiday on Horseback, A,22</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> We climbed the boundary fence and our hobnails bit into the smooth-stalked danthonia grass ... <i>(1962,Mutton on the Menu,144</i>)

>  Construction will involve clearing, laying and erecting a conventional 7 wire post and batten boundary fence including floodgates and gates. <i>(Aug 4, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,21</i>)

### boundary hut
 
 <i>n.</i> a hut on the boundary of a rural property housing a boundary-keeper.

>  <b>PINNEY</b> The old mustering and boundary huts were of stone and the fences were rabbit-netted. <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,68</i>)

### boundary keeper
 
 <i>n.</i> an old word for a shepherd who patrolled sheep-run boundaries doing the work of a boundary dog; see cit 1933 (See [boundary n](../B#boundary-n))

>  <b>BRERETON</b> His next effort to raise money entailed a three-days' walk to the Wairau where he had four months' work as `Boundary Keeper' to a runholder. <i>(1947,No Roll of Drums,21</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A base camp is set up at Butlers hut and the boundary-keeper spends night about at the homestead and the hut. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,60</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Before fences were erected, the man who patrolled the edges of the run was a boundary-keeper if on foot or a boundary-rider if mounted. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,12</i>)

>  Lonely boundary keepers who kept boundary in some river gorge and did not see another person for months at a time, also felt the need for poetry. <i>(Oct 12, 1956,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  Boundary keeper A shepherd who keeps sheep from passing an unfenced boundary. He goes along the boundary once or twice a day or, if he has a convenient lookout, watches the sheep all day. In the old days any boundary keepers lived on their boundaries all the year and never saw anyone, except at mustering or by chance. The Australian word `boundary rider' has never been used in New Zealand; in fact, a boundary rider's work is different. He looks after the fences and sheep in a large paddock. (C.) <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person who keeps watch of stock either within a locality on a property or between two properties. (See [boundary shepherd](../B#boundary-shepherd))

>  The introduction of wire fencing into the tussock sheep country had two significant results: it lessened the labour requirements of the runs by replacing shepherds and boundary keepers, and it lessened the incidence and checked the spread of scab. <i>(Oct 21, 1965,NZ Geographer,2 / 151</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> A man detailed to keep stock within an often unmarked boundary. Even a musterer on an outside beat may be sometimes so termed. In the very early days such a man had to lead a very lonely existence, year in year out, just 'looking' his beat day after day. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,9</i>)

>  <b>THORNTON</b> Two local factors in the late 1860s and the 1870s were also very significant: the availability of cheap wire fencing (early runs had no fences and relied on boundary keepers) ... <i>(1986,NZ Heritage of Farm Buildings,9</i>)

>  .. David Sutherland, a shepherd who worked for the Murray family on Braemar and moved to Godley Peaks with Bruce Murray to become a boundary keeper. <i>(May 1994,NZ Historic Places,36</i>)

### boundary line
 
 <i>n.</i> a surveyor's line cut to mark the boundary of ownership between Maori and alienated land (or land to be alienated); or to mark the boundary of a rural property.

>  <b>T.S. GRACE</b> The war at Taranaki commenced by a land agent inducing a Chief to cut a boundary line (for the purpose of sale) through land of which he was, at most, a part owner only! <i>(Mar 24, 1858,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Mar 24 T.S.Grace letter in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 77]

>  Robertson cutting my boundary line. <i>(Aug 20, 1884,Chudleigh Diary,329,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MITCALFE</b> The pakeha could not understand why Te Whiti opposed the roads that came cutting through reserves, tribal lands, gardens and burial places alike. Te Whiti sent the fencers of Parihaka to restore every boundary line, regardless of road and roadmaker. <i>(1986,Look to the Land,29</i>)

### boundary peg
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Request for such information] as will lead to the conviction of parties removing, obliterating, or destroying Trigonometrical Stations, Boundary Pegs of Sections, or any other marks connected with the surveys. <i>(Aug 16, 1851,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)

### boundary post
 
 <i>n.</i> boundary peg.

>  I ... was occupied two hours in searching amongst the tutu and fern for the boundary posts of the section required; ... <i>(Jun 14, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  <b>McNEISH</b> Sometimes at a boundary post a lone dog would rush forward barking excitedly. <i>(1970,Mackenzie,68</i>)

### boundary rider
 
 <i>n.</i> a stockman who patrols boundaries or fences (See [but cf. the Acland quotation at boundary keeper](../B#but--the-acland-quotation-at-boundary-keeper))

>  <b>SEVERINSEN</b> [At the Mararoa] Allan deposited us, then drove on to leave mail and supplies for the boundary rider our Burwood Station [which] is probably the only station in New Zealand to warrant a boundary rider : all summer a Burwood shepherd rides herd on thousands of ewes on unfenced open ranges. <i>(1970,Hunt the Far Mountain,86</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> The two front rooms were taken by Edward Kirk, who had come out with his young wife from England and worked as a boundary rider and ploughman at Roy's Hill, then drained land and planted trees along roadsides and in plantations for Tanner. <i>(1984,City of the Plains,43</i>)

>  <b>HOOD</b> ... boundary rider. <i>(1875,Chowbokiana,89</i>)

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> [We were] sent to learn the art of sheep-farming at different out-stations. At these out-stations are shepherds and boundary-riders stationed. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,32</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,12</i>) [NOTES: [see boundary keeper] [c1850s]]

>  In the succeeding two years the ex-coke-shoveller, boundary-rider, stockman, boiling-down works labourer, shearer, rouseabout, ... examined a mining boom ... <i>(Feb 10, 1921,Quick March,36</i>)

>  <i>boundary n.</i>- bound'ary ri'der n. an employee on a station whose duties are to ride round the boundary fences and keep them in order and generally to patrol the station. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Before fences were erected, the man who patrolled the edges of the run was a boundary-keeper if on foot or a boundary-rider if mounted. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,12</i>)

>  <i>boundary rider n.</i> man who rides round fences of a station to keep them in repair. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a boundary keeper who usually is on horseback, keeping watch on boundaries between stations or on mobs of sheep from straying from a particular area. (See [boundary keeper](../B#boundary-keeper))

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> Boundary rider employed between Tamumu and Omakere at GBP74 a year. <i>(Apr 4, 1870,Johnston Diary</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Crops and herds and flocks are confined where once 85,000 sheep roamed with only boundary riders to hold them. <i>(1976,From the Beginning: chronicles of a county,99</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> The solitary life of a boundary rider was commonplace in New Zealand from the late 1850s and early 1860s. <i>(2001,Riding Country,101</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> In 1958 Frank Hore, who had taken on Nokomai eight years before, ended the days of a boundary rider spending a full winter out at the Nevis hut. <i>(2001,Riding Country,105</i>)

### boundary riding
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the containment of sheep in a single mob away from snowy tops, or other natural dangers, or away from a boundary. (See [boundary keeping](../B#boundary-keeping))

>  <b>KERR</b> In three years' boundary riding he never caught a cold, but he caught one in Dunedin and had to take to his bed for a fortnight. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,53</i>)

>  <b>TIFFEN</b> .. I had the job of living in a tent and supervising outstation work - boundary riding, I suppose you would call it. <i>(1845,Journal,68</i>) [NOTES: in Cresswell (1949) Early New Zealand Families]

### boundary shepherd
 
 <i>n.</i> (a dog)

>  These dogs, called "boundary shepherds," are placed wherever two roads meet to act as deterrents towards sheep which would otherwise cross the boundary line. <i>(Sep 4, 1890,Otago Witness,35</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> = boundary keeper

>  <b>RIVES</b> One of Caverhills's boundary shepherds who was on the look-out for me set me right and walked about four miles with me. <i>(Mar 3, 1875,Jottings on the Spot,9</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> He was a regular Cockney ... but like many ??? soon learnt enough to be of service as a boundary-shepherd. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,78</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shepherd detailed to keep stock in one place or from crossing a boundary. (See [boundary keeper](../B#boundary-keeper))

>  <b>HOBHOUSE</b> Morally one must rejoice at the prospect of releasing boundary shepherds from their wretched and perilous isolation. <i>(Nov 4, 1859,Hobhouse Letters and Papers</i>)

>  <b>JAMIESON</b> In time we reach the hut where father's boundary shepherd lives. <i>(1916,Old Memories,46</i>)

>  <b>KENNINGTON</b> Few men could endure the isolation of this occupation for long, thus leading to a high turnover in boundary shepherds. <i>(1978,Awatere: a district and its people,105</i>)

>  Wanted - a Boundary Shepherd, must have a Horse and a Dog. <i>(Dec 28, 1872,Hawke's Bay Herald,1</i>)

### boundary stake
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. boundary peg](../B#boundary-peg),[boundary post](../B#boundary-post))

>  <b>ALEXANDER</b> A boundary stake; that is, a staff driven into the ground to mark a boundary. <i>(1873,Bush Fighting,23</i>)

### boundary stone
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a stone that marks a boundary, used by Maori.

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Here exist several deep pits, near which used to stand a couple of boundary-stones - pou-rohe ... <i>(1921,Tutira,78</i>)

### boundary walker
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a foot shepherd employed to keep the boundary or to watch a flock before properties were fenced.

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> Shortly after the young man had resumed the monotonous duties of a boundary-walker at the mountain hut. <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,98</i>)

### boundary watcher
 
 <i>n.</i> = boundary rider

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Before fences were constructed, boundary watchers were employed to patrol the run boundaries, ... <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,45</i>)

### boundary-keep
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to keep boundary.

>  <b>MCCASKILL</b> The dry stock may have to be 'pinched' .. and must be closely boundary-kept for about six weeks. <i>(1969,Molesworth,193</i>)

>  <b>MACGREGOR</b> A Dog's Life 40 He was boundary keeping while doing a little fencing, and every day he had to hunt the sheep back out of the snow country. <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,40</i>)

### boundary-keeping
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) keeping watch on sheep or cattle, usually in dangerous snow country of the South Island.

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Others in at Molesworth, only Angus here. Boundary-keeping. <i>(May 21, 1908,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> The long mustering days are in some cases shortened and the necessity of winter boundary-keeping lessened by the progress in aviation. <i>(1965,Beyond the Skippers Road,160</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> He was first at Rees Valley boundary-keeping in 1903. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,175</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> As with most high-country stations of this sort, boundary-keeping after the autumn muster is a regular practice. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,179</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR & TROTTER</b> The main thing with boundary keeping was to get out early in the morning before the sheep got going back up to the tops. [boundary-keeping] <i>(1994,From Caithness to Southland,30</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [first two citations below are of a verb to boundary-keep]

>  <b>WILSON</b> Stations here must be easily worked for on this immense place there are 13 shepherds but the run is very well fenced in and there are only three men boundary keeping. <i>(Feb 2, 1873,Diary,165</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Feb 2 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 165]

>  <b>HARPER</b> Jack is boundary-keeping. <i>(1934,Windy Island,189</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> Quite apart from the long, expensive business of dipping scab-infected sheep, the lack of wire fences entailed constant vigilance in tailing and boundary keeping. <i>(1938,High Country,85</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> At one time boundary-keeping after the autumn muster was a major job, but over the years, a big proportion of the block has been snow fenced. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,30</i>)

### boundary-ride
 
 <i>v.</i> to patrol the perimeters of a property or area to control stock.

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> In Australia, sheep are either in large paddocks which are boundary-ridden, or else they are shepherded and yarded every night. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,36</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <b>CURNOW</b> The bishop boundary-rides his diocese. <i>(1939,Not in Narrow Seas,6</i>)

### bounding
 
 <i>n.</i> cutting blubber for the try-pot [citation also includes the verb to bound]

>  <b>RHODES</b> The third mate having a slight quarrel with one of the men in the blubber room about a steel to sharpen the blubber or bounding knife, and the work being retarded by his not bounding, the captain took the knife and bounded ... <i>(1836,Whaling Journal,15,1954 ed.</i>)

### bounding knife
 
 <i>n.</i> a blubber knife, boat knife

>  <b>RHODES</b> The third mate having a slight quarrel with one of the men in the blubber room about a steel to sharpen the blubber or bounding knife, and the work being retarded by his not bounding, the captain took the knife and bounded ... <i>(1836,Whaling Journal,15,1954 ed.</i>)

### boundy
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Wife came to Taupeka. Dixie carried her splendidly. She is too boundy for Emma. She bounded her off a few days ago all for fun. <i>(Nov 30, 1892,Chudleigh Diary,382,1950 ed.</i>)

### bounty
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> Some of them are back in the bush trapping 'possums. There's a bounty on 'possums. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,155</i>)

>  The Government last year introduced a bounty of 2s. 6d. for every token representing an opossum killed. <i>(Dec 1952,Tararua Tramper,7</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> ... the rabbit was no inconsiderable adversary. He was provided with a home, with food and with a small wage, but received also a bounty for each tail. <i>(1952,Julian Ware,17</i>)

### bounty bar
 
 <i>n.</i> [from the name of a (UK) chocolate coconut bar]

>  I just finished a stint playing a radical Maori doctor (to Dr Ropata's bounty bar). <i>(Aug 15, 1993,Sunday Times (NZ),25</i>) [NOTES: an Uncle Tom, black on the outside, white on the inside]

### Bounty Island shag
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment)

>  Captain Bollons has presented to Canterbury Museum a specimen of the Bounty Island shag, Phalacrocorax ranfurlyi, which he obtained on his recent trip to the southern island in the Hinemoa. .. The Bounty Island shag was named after Lord Ranfurly, and it is the last bird added to the list of birds of New Zealand, which, for all practical purposes, is now complete and closed. <i>(Aug 6, 1903,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BOUNTY ISLAND SHAG <i>Phalacrocorax carunculatus ranfurlyi</i> <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,70</i>)

>  <i>84.a. Leucocarbo campbelli ranfurlyi</i> Ogilvie-Grant, 1901 <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,33,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Bounty Island Shag Leucocarbo ranfurlyi </i>OGILVIE-GRANT, 1901 ... <i>Fewer than 600 birds at the Bounty Islands in 1978. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,120</i>)

### bourbon
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>COD, ROCK </i>(a) Scientific name: <i>Lotella rhacinus</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Rock Cod; Kelp Cod. (c) Other common names: Parrotfish; Soldiers, Kelp, Kelpies, Moreporks, Marblefish, Maori Chief, Wrasse, Bourbon, Guffy, Cloudy Bay Cod, Rasp. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,18</i>)

### boutique farmer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The family farm is either getting bigger, to gain greater economies of scale and productivity, or smaller, to accommodate the lifestylers and boutique farmers. <i>(Mar 22, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

### Bovril
 
 <i>n.</i> occas. euphemism for bull-shit [ca1938 Havelock children Children's joke: American, Frenchman and Maori swapping stories of mighty feats : American knew a man who rubbed two trees together: result, matchsticks; French; two cliffs : French chalk; Maori two bulls: <i>bull-shit</i> or <i>bovril.</i>]

### bow
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Bow</i> (1) The bows are the curved parts at the top of the handle of a pair of sheep shears. They give the shears their spring open after the shearer has made his blow. All shears are now made with double bows; but until about the 'seventies single bows were the only kind. (2) The iron that holds the yoke to a bullock's neck (see <i>bullocks</i>). ([1890- 1910]) <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BURDON</b> The sheep they shore were not so well woolled on the belly and carried less wool on the points than they do now, so that fewer short blows were required to take off the trimmings; but the shears they used were one inch and a half shorter in the blade and, being joined by a single instead of a double bow, required a wrist of iron to drive them continuously throughout a summer's day. A piece of raw hide known as a knocker is now used to prevent the shears clashing when closed, but before this was introduced the clack and snap of steel meeting steel was a noise inseparable from any busy shearing shed. <i>(1938,High Country,84</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> So most of us stick to `the blades,' the big shining steel shears eight and a half inches long, with a double spring `bow' on the end to keep them apart. <i>(1951,High Country,26</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Bow</i>. The curved top of the handle of the shears which incorporates the spring. Until the late 1870s shears were made with only one bow and the extra tension made them more difficult to operate. Now all shears have double bows. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,95</i>)

>  <i>bow</i>: The curved handle top of hand shears. The grip is against the bows which causes the blades to spring open after each cut. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/22</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]



 
 <i>n.</i> an iron contrivance in the shape of a double bow which fitted to the underside of the necks of a pair of draught bullocks and was fastened to the yoke across the upper side of the pair's necks.

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> ... you would have only to buy another [bullock] plough and two yokes and bows. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,261</i>)

>  The Working Bullocks, with Bows, Yokes, and Chains, can be warranted as most unexceptional, being in good condition, and perfectly quiet. <i>(Aug 2, 1851,Lyttelton Times,8</i>)

>  <b>W H S ROBERTS</b> I yoked up the bullocks. The bullock yoke was a square piece of white pine smoothed and hollowed out to fit the necks of the two bullocks, with holes for the iron bows which went round the neck and through the yoke, being fastened above with a key tied with a bit of greenhide; in the centre a bolt with a ring called `the start' passed through the yoke, to which the chain was fastened from the sledge. <i>(Sep 19, 1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Sep 19 W.H.S. Roberts diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 42]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> In the small river Ashburton ... we had a little misunderstanding [105] with the bullocks; the leaders ... slewed sharply round, and tied themselves into an inextricable knot with the polars [sic], while the body bullocks, by a manoeuvre not unfrequent, shifted, or as it is technically termed, slipped, the yoke under their necks, and the bows over; ... <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,vii 104-105</i>) [NOTES: 1860 in 1863 Butler First Year ch vii 104-105]

>  The iron that holds the yoke to the bullock's neck. <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: (ca1890-1910) 1933 Sep 16 Acland Glossary Press 15 (2)]

>  [<i>T. novae-zelandiae</i>] It has been called the `bowing-bird' by the settlers, from its habit of bowing its body when approached. <i>(1872,TrNZI,5/127</i>) [NOTES: (Travers)]

>  ... a suitable piece of kauri ricker ... shaped at each end to receive the ends of the bows or loops by which the yokes were fastened to the bullocks' necks. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,182/3</i>) [NOTES: [ca1900]]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> BOW: Term used for front of horse-drawn vehicle. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,185</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 185]

>  Bullocks were also driven in sledges (two or four bullocks). The iron iron which passed under the bullocks' necks to keep the yoke in place was called the<i> bow</i>. The <i>bow</i> was held to the yoke by a <i>yoke key</i> which passed through a hole in it. (C. [Butler A First Year in Canterbury Settlement], p.95, etc.) <i>(Sep 23, 1933,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  They were returning [in a bullock dray] ... when a carelessly tied `key' that held the `bow' into the `yoke' dropped out and let one bullock free. <i>(1940,Tales of Pioneer Women,151,1988 ed.</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a curved spring at the top of each blade of a pair of handshears.

>  Bow - The bows are curved parts at the top of the handle of a pair of sheep shears. <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> So most of us stick to 'the blades', the big shining steel shears 8 and a half inches long, with a double spring 'bow' on the end to keep them apart. <i>(1951,NZ High Country,26</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> bow: (i) curved spring at the top of each blade of a pair of hand shears. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,9</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Bow. The curved top of the handle of the shears which incorporates the spring. Until the late 1870s shears were made with only one bow and the extra tension made them more difficult to operate. Now all shears have double bows. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,95</i>)

### bow-tie
 
 <i>n.</i> a married woman's boy-friend; fancy man

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> `Come off it. You wouldn't like your sister-in-law to park her ears to all I know about you. You and your bow-tie.' <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,125,1976 ed.</i>)

### bowcaker/bowkaker
 
 <i>n.</i> a pukeko

>  The paddocks close to the river had not been stumped or ploughed and gave excellent shelter for rabbits, hares, ducks and bowkakas (Pukeko). <i>(May 2007,Carterton Crier,6</i>)

### Bowen method/Bowen technique
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a style of shearing developed by Godfrey Bowen [involving the use of the knees to control sheep during shearing.]

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> What is known as the Bowen method is taught in wool-producing countries the world over. <i>(1966,Sixty Million Muster,72</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Many years later, with certain modifications, this style has become known as the Bowen technique and has been taught throughout the world. [Bowen method] <i>(1991,Off the Sheep's Back,56</i>)

### Bowen technique
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Because my left arm was often numb and useless, I developed a style of holding the sheep entirely wih my legs, keeping it on the point of `balance'. Many years later, with certain modifications, this style became known as the `Bowen technique', and has been taught throughout the world. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,56</i>)

### bowerbird
 
 <i>n.</i> any of various Australian birds which live in scrub, the males of which build an elaborate, walled enclosure (a bower), to attract the females.

### bowie
 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1953 at least: Marlborough 1953 `Universal in the Pohangina district': N.T. Moar] (See [pukeko](../P#pukeko),[bokaka](../B#bokaka))



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a lamb suffering from bowie or bentleg.

>  These latter lambs were from one and a half to two and a half months old and were thus slightly older than the bowies concerned. <i>(1954,NZ Veterinary Journal,2 122</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) osteodystrophy in sheep and cattle grazed on native pastures, mainly in Marlborough and Canterbury back country. (See [bent leg](../B#bent-leg))

>  Bowie has been under the notice of this Department [Agriculture] for a good many years, and as long ago as 1930 was the subject of a report to the Director .. Bowie has a fairly widespread distribution in the South Island. Bowie has been known in the Wairau Valley for some fifty years .. Such animals also recover if shifted soon enough on to topdressed paddocks on bowie properties. <i>(1954,NZ Veterinary Journal,2 118</i>)

>  <b>NZ VET ASSN</b> Osteodystrophic diseases of sheep in New Zealand. 'Bowie' or 'bent leg' ... <i>(1971,Diseases of Domestic Animals in New Zealand,215</i>)

### bowing-bird
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Thinornis novae-zelandiae.</i>

>  <i>Thinornis novae-zealandiae</i>. I only found this bird on Mangare and parts of the coast of Pitt Island. It has been called the "bowing-bird" by the settlers, from its habit of bowing its body when approached. <i>(1873,TrNZI,V/217</i>)

### bowl of fruit
 
 <i>n.</i> a suit [ORIGIN: rhyming slang] (See [cf. box of fruit](../B#box-of-fruit))

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> BOWL OF FRUIT (n) Suit <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,245</i>)

### bowler
 
 <i>n.</i> (of a house for sale) only fit for bowling over

>  "Handyman's dream", for instance, means a complete refurbishment by a professional; "harbour glimpse" means bring a stepladder; and "quaint colonial" means it's a bowler. <i>(Nov 27, 2004,Dominion Post,E12</i>)

### bowling place
 
 <i>n.</i> ?dice-playing shanty; ?ten-pin saloon after the US.

>  <b>THATCHER</b> In all houses of amusement,/ Your nose of course you'll poke; At bowling places roll for drinks,/ And a short black pipe you'll smoke;/ Fakements pick up not a few, ... <i>(1875,NZ Songster,No. 5/64</i>) [NOTES: The Leary Boy]

### bowling-green mind
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> The `bowling-green mind' of many adults, their salacious interest in filthy stories and jests, demonstrates how these interests [in sexual, excretory, and other bodily functions] , unreleased and untutored in childhood, carry right on through life in their early and most primitive forms. <i>(1953,Unorganized Games,2/658</i>)

### bows
 
 <i>n.</i> ?the hoops over a wagon over which the tilt goes.

>  <b>MASTERS</b> I took my primitive dray, now made like a large basket, with bows and a tilt over it. <i>(1871,Autobiography,45</i>)

### bowser
 
 <i>n.</i> a petrol pump (chiefly Aust and NZ), also, a petrol tanker for fuelling aircraft .. etc; 1934; Petrol pump (at garage, etc) [also bowzer]

>  A similar danger frequently occurs during the time a tank is being filled, especially in the case of underground tanks of the kind popularly referred to as `bowser' tanks (although not necessarily made by the firm of Bowser) which are connected to kerbside pumps. <i>(1929,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,10/214</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> old-fashioned word for petrol pump, after Mr Bowser, American manufacturer of only petrol pumps available in Australasia in 1930s; appropriately, `to bowse' means to come with a rush, a drinking vessel or a drinking bout; RAF called man in charge of a towed petrol tanker a `bowser king.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,19</i>)

>  Motorists may be losing at Petrol Bowser. <i>(Jan 18, 1968,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  Kiwi software pumps up Asian bowsers <i>(Sep 1, 1999,Independent (Auckland),17</i>)

>  We carried chains to cope with the mud when rain turned the roads into quagmires, and we always carried two tins of benzine, as 'bowsers' were few and far between. <i>(Aug 2002,NZ Memories,37/16</i>)

>  [Hdg] Kiwi software pumps up Asian bowsers. <i>(Sep 1, 1999,Independent (Auckland),17</i>)

>  <b>VANCE</b> Night was fast approaching - no bowser for miles. <i>(1976,Bush, Bullocks & Boulders,224</i>)

>  <b>MCALLISTER</b> Bowsers were then [sc. c1920 unknown. <i>(1976,Old Taranaki,119</i>)

>  <b>WATSON</b> It's Erin Carter, disgruntled farmer, crossing the Otago river. Erin Carter born in March 1932, brought up on his father's farm in Northland, near a tin pot village where there's only the store with the red benzine bowsers in front of it, and across the metal road, the cream stand under the wattle trees. <i>(1986,Address to a King,59</i>)

>  <b>IRELAND</b> A local priest told my father in front of me, at the bowser in the forecourt of the old bus barns, that he would 'personally see to it when the Japs get here, you're the first person in Takapuna put up against a wall and shot'. <i>(1998,Under the Bridge & Over the Moon,74</i>)

>  <b>LORETZ</b> We gathered empty benzine tins, and there were plenty for the bowsers had not yet come. <i>(1976,Moments of Life,9</i>)

>  The petrol bought at the bowser today was not bought yesterday as crude oil. <i>(Jun 8, 1992,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Ross Motors had an honesty bowser for petrol. <i>(2001,Good Bastards: the larrikin's guide to success,64</i>)

>  This was seen as a glorious opportunity to do a troop good deed, so we offered to push the tiny vehicle to the nearest bowser (petrol station). <i>(Jun 2004,NZ Memories,No. 48/21</i>)

>  Heading home, I'm low on fuel and, with no bowser for miles, drive at a snail's pace, getting back in the dark to an angry Benecio tapping the cat watch on his paw, demanding: "What time do you call this?" as he awaits dinner. <i>(Jul 4, 2011,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Fletchers forsees battles of the bowsers <i>(Nov 21, 1997,Independent (Auckland),1</i>)

>  The Government will be unpopular enough if its 4c a litre petrol tax increase goes through to pay for unplugging Auckland's jams; it won't upset motorists more by playing wowser at the bowser. <i>(Feb 16, 2002,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  Off I roared, stereo loud, warm breeze encasing me with those wonderfully familiar country smells, my thoughts in the 70s, my foot on the pedal ... the fuel gauge on empty ... middle of nowhere and I suspected equidistant to the nearest bowser, as they say in the country. <i>(Mar 8, 2001,Evening Post Flair,2</i>)

>  <i>bowser n</i>. a petrol pump. [origin uncertain] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

>  <b>ADAIR</b> .. they came to a petrol pump - a `bowser' Alec called it. <i>(1935,Wanted A Son,228</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> A pump at a filling station is a <i>bowser ...</i> <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,87</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Tobacconists selling magazines, pubs selling cigarettes, petrol bowsers selling sweets, ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,151</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Food, cried Jack, and a petrol pump! They pushed the Ford the last thirty yards to the bowser. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,101</i>)

>  <b>DICK</b> `Well, the only time any car of mine has ever been in a panel beater's was the time Mum had to back into the only bowser in this enormous Mackenzie Country. ...' <i>(1964,High Country Family,108</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> They knew the names of the drivers, too .. overheard them at the bowzer when the trucks stopped ... <i>(1965,Power of Joy,34</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> [The country ...] / Decorated with a necklace of petrol bowsers. <i>(1979,Collected Poems,432,1968 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DUGGAN</b> How's it going, then, Adam? I hear you've doing a stint on the bowsers, Saturday. <i>(1970,O'Leary's Orchard,95</i>) [NOTES: (`Apple for Flowers')]

>  <b>LELAND</b> No, this has nothing to do with beer. A bowser is a GAS (petrol) PUMP. `Pull up to the bowsers and I'll fill her up'. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,17</i>)

>  <i>bowser .. 2. Austral. & N.Z.</i> a petrol pump at a filling station. [orig. a U.S. proprietary name] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,128</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 83; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 57]]

>  <b>TUWHARE</b> The car never played up at all. / And after we'd given it a second / gargle at the all-night bowser / it just zoomed on ... <i>(1982,Year of the Dog,10</i>) [NOTES: (`Heemi')]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> When it reached a certain gallonage per month, Caltex supplied .. an underground tank and bowser. The bowser was hand-operated ... <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,152</i>)

>  The battle behind the bowsers has now passed beyond the fields of "additives" with algebraic names and is being waged on the question of higher-octane motor spirit. <i>(Feb 17, 1954,NZ Truth,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a new entrant [1951 14-16 M 59 Wellington H.S. 25 BOWSERS]



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  BP ends up looking like a bowser wowser after its initial 7c a litre hike. <i>(Jan 27, 2001,Evening Post,16</i>)

>  "Bowser" Toogood.. was best man. <i>(Sep 1, 1938,NZ Observer,17</i>)

>  She fossicked out two gin bottles, took them down to her bowser attendant, and had them filled. <i>(Aug 13, 1945,NZEF Times,IV/216 : 7</i>)

>  <b>GRAEME-HOLDER</b> Midnight had passed when the car pulled into a filling station in Palmerston North. Extra bowser attendants worked the pumps diligently. <i>(1933,Restless Earth,100</i>)

>  <b>GRAEME-HOLDER</b> 'O.K.!' said the bowser-hand as they paid him. <i>(1933,Restless Earth,103</i>)

>  [Caption] At a Bowser station in the Western Desert. <i>(Jul 21, 1941,NZEF Times,I/4 : 4</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a dispenser attached to inverted spirit-bottle in a hotel bar which automatically measures and dispenses a fluid ounce or `nip'. [?1950s A recent commentator [on the six o'clock swill] suggested, only just satirically, that the logical development of the present bar would be `a huge beer bowser with numerous coin-in-the-slot nipples on the end of plastic hoses.]

>  <b>WILSON</b> The barman .. picked up a glass which he held under the bowser of a hanging bottle. <i>(1957,Strip Jack Naked,23</i>)

>  [Here, as attrib] One licensee said there was a growing embarrassment at the necessity to place inferior brands of spirits on sale in bars. ... He predicted that wartime rationing with bowser sales restricted to a one-hour period may have to be re-introduced. <i>(Apr 24, 1958,Christchurch Star-Sun,3</i>)

>  <b>GEBBIE & McGREGOR</b> He's [sc. the sophisticated cocktail barman] probably ... filled up an empty bottle of black-label Johnnie Walker with local whisky and popped the bottle back up on the bowser. <i>(1979,Incredible 8-Ounce Dream,10</i>)

### bowser station
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) a petrol station

>  Forbes grew up in Longburn, a rural village south of Palmerston North, and spent his holidays on a farm in Matamau, just north of Dannevirke - "just a country school and a bowser station". <i>(Sep 2003,North & South,45</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> ... On the first corner was a building of flats with a bowser station on the ground floor ... <i>(1935,Little Country, The,410,1937 ed.</i>)

### bowserised
 
 <i>adj.</i> (obsolete)

>  [Advt] Bowserized... All Benzine and Oils are now retailed from the Famous Bowser Steel Tanks. <i>(Aug 27, 1918,Hawke's Bay Herald,6</i>)

### bowyang
 
 <i>n.</i> the ties used below the knee to keep trouser legs free and from dragging on the ground, often used by shearers (usu. pl) (?obs in general use: extant in shearers'); string or a leather lace tied around the trouser leg below the knee.

>  This is how the toiling and moiling son of the soil, the chap who wears bowyangs and wields a pick, and swears and sweats in so doing, particularly when in camp, loves to while away an idle and sometimes expensive hour in `heading 'em', ... <i>(Jan 20, 1912,NZ Truth,343/7/3</i>)

>  A seedy-looking man in bow-yangs ... approached Bill and whispered something. <i>(Jul 10, 1919,Quick March,21</i>)

>  Oh you [bush-felling] contractors in black singlets and bowyangs, fuelled on spuds and mutton and pumpkin and rice and spotted dog or deadman's arm and black billy tea: perfect toast sopping with butter and Cocky's Joy (treacle): come into our art galleries, onto our stamps, wrap within songs, ... <i>(Feb 3, 1985,NZ Times,5</i>) [NOTES: (Jim Henderson)]

>  Jacky Howes and bowyangs were all part of the [shearing] job. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,58f caption to illustration</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> He missed his bowyangs dreadfully. <i>(1927,Old Savage,121</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> ... the shearers at the pen gates arrayed in their working clothes, with bowyangs (a strap over their trousers, below the knees, which allows them to bend with more comfort), and moccasins on (a kind of shoe made out of wool-pack, after the style of the foot-covering [76] of the Red Indian) ... <i>(1927,Sheep & Wool Industry,75</i>)

>  <i>Bowyang</i>.- A piece of string, flax, or lace tied round below the knee to keep the trousers from dragging on the knee. Shearers often wear them. (C.) <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BOWEN</b> `Bowyangs' are essential to shearing, more so than plus-fours are to golf. Straps are better than twine and will not cut off blood circulation. <i>Ibid.</i> 155 BOW-YANGS. Straps or string under the shearer's knees around the trousers. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,15</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Charles Whebby stayed in Mahakipawa until the early thirties. One day he mounted his hack ... and embarked for Wellington. He rode through the capital city ... dressed as usual in bo-yangs and hard knocker. <i>(1962,Linkwater,124</i>)

>  <b>DICK</b> Then, with bowyangs and moccasins suitably adjusted, and chatting all the while with Allan and the men, he ambled into the pen, ... <i>(1964,High Country Family,71</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> bowyangs: bands tied about the trouser-legs. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,298</i>) [NOTES: Pearson Glossary]

>  <i>bowyang n.</i> strap or string tied below the knee of a worker's trousers to prevent them from dragging on the ground. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. (Aust.-NZ Suppl.),612</i>)

>  <i>bowyang ...n.</i> strap or string tied below the knee of a worker's trousers to prevent them from dragging on the ground. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  BOW-YANGS: Laces tied around the trouser leg below the knee. <i>(Feb 21, 1968,Straight Furrow,20</i>) [NOTES: [Shearer's language]]

>  <i>bowyangs </i>pl. <i>n. Austral. & N.Z. sl.</i> a pair of strings or straps worn around the trouser leg below the knee, orig. esp. by agricultural workers. [C19: < E dialect <i>bowy-yanks </i>leggings] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,128</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 83]]

>  Bowyangs ... some used them - reason for bushmen wearing them [not the usual one] ... bushmen had a very healthy respect for the weta ... these things sometimes jump at you - bowyangs a protection against a weta climbing up the leg of one's pants. <i>(Nov 12, 1983,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: 9-15am: Spectrum Documentary: G.W. Johnson's working life 1940-46]

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> bowyangs pair of strings or straps secured around each trouser leg below the knee <i>(1984,Blackball 08,242</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> If bowyangs were not worn, the weight of the wet trousers suspended entirely by the hips would become very tiring by the end of the day. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,61</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>bowyang</i> trouser straps below knees, worn mostly by labourers. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,20</i>)

>  The afore-mentioned mere males, one old, with grey whiskers and wearing bowyangs.. procured liquor for Jane. <i>(May 14, 1910,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  <b>HUGHES</b> As was common those days he wore bowyangs and a pink woolen singlet with short sleeves. <i>(1985,Hyacinths & Biscuits,136</i>)

>  The Infantry called us [sc. Pioneers] "bow-yangs." <i>(Apr 18, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,II/16 : 78</i>)

>  Bow-yangs. - An English term introduced into France by Lieut. Col. Charters. to indicate labourers, who wear leather straps below their knees, to keep the bottoms of their trousers out of the mud. Not supplied by Ordinance. <i>(Apr 18, 1917,Chronicles NZEF,II/16 : 79</i>)

>  To the curious it may be explained that "boyangs" denotes the ligatures with which navvies are wont to tie their trousers just below the knees. <i>(Jun 29, 1912,NZ Free Lance,15</i>)

>  Boyangs are not an issue. <i>(May 8, 1944,NZEF Times,III/150 : 4</i>)

>  <b>henderson</b> Just below each knee a small strap. These knee-straps are called 'bowyangs'. <i>(1967,Retreat from Apartheid,70</i>)

>  He cut his flax bowyangs to let the water drain from his dungarees. <i>(Jan 15, 1923,Aussie (NZ Section),V</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> The customary dress was the old Blucher boots... and Tairoa trousers, which were made of a kind of heavy white duck; these were tied with bowyangs below the knees. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,155</i>)

### box
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> <i>(1906,Introductory Classbook of Botany</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>Veronica buxifolia</i> var. <i>odora </i> <i>(1908,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: (And)Cockayne Report on a Botanical Survey of the Tongariro National Park]

>  <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: (And) Cockayne Report on a Botanical Survey of Stewart Island]



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [box v](../B#box-v))

>  When two mobs of sheep get mixed accidentally it is called a box. (1) When you put two mobs of sheep together on purpose, you box them. (2) When they join by accident they are said to <i>get boxed</i>. In fact, the active voice of the verb signifies intention; the passive, accident. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>McLEOD</b> You see we're driving through the middle of a ewe block and `boxes' are frowned on. <i>(1972,High Country Anthology,22</i>) [NOTES: Goulter `On the Tops']

>  <b>McLEOD</b> <i>Box</i>: Mixing of two mobs of sheep either accidentally or on purpose. <i>(1972,Mountain World,310</i>)

>  <i>box ... 14. Austral. & N.Z</i> an accidental mixing of herds or flocks. ... <i>18. out of the box. Austral. inf. </i>outstanding or excellent. ...<i> 21. Austral. & N.Z</i>. to mix (flocks or herds) or (of flocks) to become mixed accidentally. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,128</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 83; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 57]]

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> That swiftly darting 'twixt the flocks,/ Just barely saved a three-fold box./ <i>(1883,Castle Gay,174</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to settle (as if `boxed' to be stored away as finished with).

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> It's a regular German stove with a flat top ... I should think it would box it - the cold I mean. [Murry's fn] To `box' something, in K.M.'s private language, was to settle it satisfactorily. <i>(Nov 24, 1919,Letter,409</i>) [NOTES: 1919 Nov 24 Mansfield Letters in 1951 Murry 409]



 
 <i>n.</i> part of a wool-press

>  <b>CARR</b> Pressing. - When there is sufficient wool in one bin, the pressers (generally two) get to work. One gets into the `box' as the lower portion of the patent presses is called, while his mate ... hands him ... the fleeces. These are placed in tiers, and tramped down until the lower box is full. An upper box is then swung ... or [16] lowered on to the lower one, and the process repeated. When full, the `monkey' or cover is put into position, the spears (wire rope) affixed, and the two men lever the cover down until the top box is clear. <i>(1913,Country Work,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> glory box

>  <b>KIDMAN</b> She wanted to leave [school] and start getting her box together. ... they were going to get married ... <i>(1983,Paddy's Puzzle,64</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> put in a place of confinement for punishment

>  <b>BEST</b> Reported convict for insolence to a sentry ... [he] was boxed ... July 13 convict's ... meat was stopped and he was put in the box [during voyage to Australia and NZ]. <i>(1837,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1837 Best Journal 1837-1843 ed Taylor 1966 81 July 12]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>EVANS</b> Can the miners erect a small dam, thereby to save the night water, to work a box with during the day ... <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,18</i>) [NOTES: 1870 Nov 30 in 1962 Evans Waikaka Saga 18]

>  <b>NOLAN</b> <i>Box</i>: abbreviation for sluice box. <i>(1983,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,111</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to confine stock in a corner or small area where they could smother.

>  <b>BEAMISH</b> Boxing of stock: Allow stock to move away from a gateway. Don't 'box' them into a corner. <i>(1999,Whana Valley Walk,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The battery has three stampers in each box, double cams, quicksilver placed in battery boxes, grating perpendicular, with round holes. The copper-plate tables are about 12ft. long by 5ft. wide, and raised about 14in. above the blanket boards; ... [The blankets] appear to be ordinary grey blankets of a very inferior description. <i>(1868,Thames Miner's Guide, The,93</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to mix mobs of sheep accidentally; usu. in quasi-aj or quasi-av constr. be, get boxed (See [box n](../B#box-n))

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> At first the diary describes this as `got mixed', but in 1864 I noted the use of the colonial term `boxed.' In 1868, however, I noted a reversion to `got mixed.' <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,84</i>) [NOTES: >?orig. SI station usage: the Puketoi diarist uses at first <i>join</i> as in 1859 July 24 Puketoi Station Diary [Hocken TS] Hume over, said that Wedder & ewe flocks had joined. but later uses join for the deliberate mixing of animals; e.g. putting rams with the ewes. cf Beattie's comment: 1864 Puketoi Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 84]



 
 <i>v.</i> to join or mix (either accidentally or for some purpose) mobs of sheep.

>  Grant unwell; lambs boxed [Apr 20] Fine. Drafted lambs out of John Murray's ewes & joined rams to both flocks <i>(Apr 19, 1864,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: [first use by diarist W. Baird Ogilvy][Hocken TS]]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> He's allowed a mob of your sheep to cross the boundary, and now they're boxed with ours. ... [149] ... Gilbert concluded that as the sheep were ... irretrievably `boxed', nothing could be done; ... <i>(1881,Waitaruna,148</i>)

>  To hear the drover at his best - or his worst - it is desirable to be near him when a young dog is making a mess of things at a critical moment, or the mob gets `boxed' with another mob round the corner. <i>(Dec 31, 1910,NZ Truth,288/6/3</i>)

>  When two mobs of sheep get mixed accidentally it is called a box. (1) When you put two mobs of sheep together on purpose, you box them. (2) When they join by accident they are said to <i>get boxed</i>. In fact, the active voice of the verb signifies intention; the passive, accident <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> When two mobs ran into each other and intermingled, they were said to be `boxed.' <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,12</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> ... it was Andrew's job to patrol the rugged boundary each day, hunt the sheep back to keep them from getting boxed, and also to prevent their being menaced, destroy as many as possible of the wild dogs and pigs that inhabited the area. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,118</i>)

>  You wouldn't notice Dan, the yellow backing dog until the mob got boxed at a gate way ... he'd be up over their backs barking and grinning and barking here and there ... <i>(1976,NZ Short Stories,126,1966 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Middleton `Saving the Breed')]

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> Be careful not to hunt them [sc. escaped wethers] further back ... They'd get boxed with the ewes if they got out that way. <i>(1970,Tat,95</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> <i>Box</i>: When two mobs of sheep intended to be kept separate are mixed. Somebody is usually very unpopular. <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,253</i>)

>  <i>box ... 14. Austral. & N.Z </i>an accidental mixing of herds or flocks. ... <i>18. out of the box. Austral. inf. </i>outstanding or excellent. ... <i>21. Austral. & N.Z</i>. to mix (flocks or herds) or (of flocks) to become mixed accidentally. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,128</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 83; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 57]]



 
 <i>n.</i> a high-sided truck for carrying coal

>  ... the rock [is] immediately loaded into trucks or `boxes' and drawn to the burning-ground ... <i>(1906,TrNZI,38/480</i>)

>  `Coal, yo ho') Let the boxes go, let the jig chains rattle away ... <i>(1900,NZ Illustrated Magazine,2/507</i>) [NOTES: (McMahon[???]]

>  He swung the last box of coal round on to the rails and pushed it up the incline, trundling it along the unevenly fixed rails while his mate stayed to fix the jig - which controlled the boxes going to and from the coal-face fifteen feet above them at the side of the rails. <i>Ibid.</i> 420 box: A metal truck used in coal-mines. <i>(1963,Coal Flat</i>)

>  <b>THOMPSON</b> Two shifts a day she [sc. the mine-horse] would drag upwards of 200 half-tonne boxes, six at a time, into the lay-by, where Frank ... would jig them down to the next level. [41] About once every three months she would ... [run] wide on one of the corners in such a way as to pull all the boxes off the line. <i>(1980,All My Lives,40</i>)

>  The wheeled vehicles that transport the coal (trucks, tubs, or skips elsewhere) are in the Strongman called <i>boxes</i>, are linked together in a string they become a <i>race of boxes</i>. This <i>race</i> is the same word you hear in a railwayman's <i>rake of wagons, rake</i> being the northern-dialect form. <i>Race</i> was usually the southern form, used by miners in Cornwall. It looks as if a Cornishman at one time got loose on the West Coast. <i>(May 2, 1981,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>) [NOTES: [I.A. Gordon: Strongman Mine]]



 
 <i>n.</i> ?the opposing 25; ? the area in front of an opponent's goalposts; ? the `gap' not covered by the fullback.

>  <b>SLATTER</b> They practised five yard scrums on attack and on defence, the up and under, the kick into the box, the reverse kick to the blindside wing. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,141</i>)

>  <b>HOWITT</b> In 1953 Pat Vincent kicked `into the box' and upset our fullback. This time Alan Clark, Don McIntosh and myself took turns at playing spare fullback. ... I remember hearing their captain ... say to Vincent `For goodness sake kick into the gap' and Vincent replied `I can't - they've blocked all the gaps!' <i>(1975,NZ Rugby Greats,42 ed.</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an accidental or purposeful mixing of stock from different groups.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> A few more minutes and Bill would have been there to help him; now he had had a box, and the sheep would all have to be drafted ... <i>(1954,On the Hill,26</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [shepherding and mustering] to combine mobs of stock accidentally or by design.

>  Lambs boxed. <i>(Apr 19, 1864,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  - it is complained that they [sheep] are not only eating all the feed on the roads on which they are herded, but that they break fences and box with other sheep. <i>(Mar 1907,NZ Farmer,235</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> To avoid boxing mobs, the ewes and docked lambs were held in open country until dark. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,51</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> .. as their time approaches and the early lambers have become reduced to a small mob the two lots can be boxed and the routine continued. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,49</i>)

>  The three of us are going to box our elite sheep at tupping and use a very good ram. <i>(Nov 13, 1975,NZ Farmer,52</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The musterers made a practice of always having a mob of decoy cattle on hand with which to box any wild ones. <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,220</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> box: separate lines of sheep or cattle that have been mixed together. There is a delicate nuance in the use of this word: if they have been mixed deliberately they have 'been boxed'.. If it all happened by accident, to be sure they 'got boxed.' And the latter case is inclined to occasion some pretty sulphurous language to foul the air. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,9</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> The next day, from the Opouri and other side gullies, we boxed the cattle, about 200 in all. [box up] <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,42</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> for driving (to a sale, etc)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> I was no longer asked to help with the droving of stock to the sale. If one of my sisters could not help he would `box' with a neighbour. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,84</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BARKER</b> [Shepherd speaks] He couldn't cross [his mob] over a river ... and never a week passes without his getting boxed. That's mixed-up ma'am. We calls it boxing when your sheep go and join another mob feeding close by, and you can't tell one from another except by the brand or the earmark. <i>(1872,Xmas Cake in Four Quarters, A,ch. iii</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i> to mix mobs of sheep either accidentally or for a purpose, also box up.

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> He `boxed' more than half his mob with your sheep. <i>(1913,Sodger Sandy's Bairn,51</i>)

>  <i>box v.t.</i> to mix together sheep that ought to be kept separate.- box'er n. a bowler hat. [origin uncertain] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

>  Once I left the gate open and boxed some sheep. <i>(1930,NZ Short Stories,171</i>) [NOTES: G.B.Lancaster]

>  <b>McLEOD</b> ... He then emerged into an open plain, where he must try and hold his mob to box them with those emerging from some other valley. <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,52</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> When one of the girls told her father she had brought a few wethers out ..., her father instructed her, `After lunch box them up with the sheep in [96] the Letterbox [paddock] and put them in the Chaffcutter.' <i>(1975,Eight Daughters,95</i>)

>  <b>PINNEY</b> In 1886 there was a bad drought ... Middleton boxed the 9,000 cull ewes with the wethers, and in February pushed them well into the back country, which was best in such a dry year ... <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,26</i>)

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> Now, mind yourselves, for if you box [fn Shepherd phrase for join or mix.]/ You'll play the mischief with the flocks. <i>Ibid.</i> 172 When he beheld in act to fuse/ With his, an adverse flock of ewes./ One startled glance told plain enough/ That he had boxed with Harry Ruff. <i>(1883,Castle Gay,167</i>)

>  <i>Box, v. t. [Add.] 4. </i>To mix (two or more flocks or herds) in confusion; - used also figuratively. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2018</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>v.</i> [ca1953 Wellington paint dealer: You've got to box the paint well by pouring half of it off first.]



 
<b>4</b> <i>v.</i> to become bluffed, to get lost occas. constucted with <i>up</i> and perhaps affected by boxed up `coped up'.

>  ... all the mercies we had to be thankful for ... possession of `tucker' galore, and ... (as the weather became worse) how we were safe on this side of the Godley and not boxed up in Scone Creek. <i>(1936,NZ Alpine Journal,6/23/228</i>)

>  If you do get lost don't get rattled ... You are not the first to get boxed - almost every bush traveller has experienced the same sensation. <i>(1939,NZ Alpine Journal,8/26/45</i>)

### box drain
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ditch with fascined sides.

>  <b>TAIT</b> Alexander Mason brought in the swamp land by swing plough, installing box drains ... <i>(1961,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol III,72</i>)

### box gurnard
 
 <i>n.</i> = scaly gurnard [[why box? shape?]]

>  [species name] scaly gurnard .. [scientific name] <i>Lepidotrigla brachyoptera</i> .. [other name(s)] box gurnard <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### box iron
 
 <i>n.</i> [As I remember them, not always as OED2 `with cavity for hot coals in them', but mainly squat shape with sides taller (or the iron deeper) than the ordinary flat-iron, and often `double ended' rather than triangular, and differing from a flat-iron in having detachable handles. (HWO)]

>  <b>HEINZ</b> The first ironing that I remember was done with what we called a `box iron' - steel frame much like the shape of the present-day iron, although rather shorter and deeper. <i>(1974,Bright Fine Gold,17</i>) [NOTES: 1940 McBride in Tales of Pioneer Women in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 17]

### box of birds
 
 <i>phr.</i> cheerful, happy, very good; fine, fit, in health, `chirpy' (often as a reply to a greeting) [she's a box of birds: she's right, indicating that all is well.]

>  Thomas the goose has found true love at his new home. David Austin.. said yesterday he was "a box of birds" at his new home. <i>(Sep 9, 1998,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  "You OK, Wallie?" "Me? Box of birds." <i>(Nov 13, 1991,TV3,Shark in the Park</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> `Howya doin?' he asks as he leaves. `All right. How's yaself?'`Boxa birds,' he tells me with a wink ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,144</i>)

>  <b>CURNOW</b> George: How are you, Bill? Bill: Box o' birds. And you? George: I'm fine, thanks. <i>(1972,Four Plays,140</i>) [NOTES: (The Overseas Expert)]

>  box: <i>a box of birds, like a box of birds</i>, fine; <i>one right out of the box</i>, a day or season of especially good weather. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,298</i>) [NOTES: Pearson Glossary]

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> `Good morning, Phil. How are you, today?' `Box of birds, Miss. How would you be yourself?' <i>(1978,Talkback Man,164</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> `How are you today?' `I'm a box of birds today'. (I'm on top of the world, RIGHT AS RAIN.) <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,17</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> Yeah, he's all right. Box of birds. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,216</i>)

>  She is still a box of birds. <i>(Apr 23, 1947,NZ Observer,19</i>)

>  <b>Tom Fitzsimmons</b> Asked how he was feeling two weeks after the episode, Mr Henare replied: "Box of birds, mate, box of birds." <i>(Jul 12, 2010,Dominion Post,A9</i>)

>  "How are you?" "Box of birds". "I see, all crap and fathers." <i>(Feb 2003,TV2</i>) [NOTES: Shortland Street, Feb. 2003]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> You're looking good Bert, he said, and Bert said he was a box of birds. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,197</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `I thought you despised the sacred calf. How's your father?' `A box of birds. ...' <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,44</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> `How yer gettin' on mate? Boxa birds, eh?' `Boxa birds all right' mate. All shit and feathers, eh.' <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,133</i>)

>  <i> a box of birds NZ & Austral. colloq.</i> excellent, very well, fine <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 57]]

>  <b>KNOX</b> `Are you all right Basil?' `Yes, fine - I'm a box of birds - vultures in a coffin.' <i>(1987,After Z-Hour,68</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>box of birds</i> fighting fit, NZ Services, WWII; fit and well in civilian life. Sometimes additions <i>and all singing, all feathers and shit;</i> eg `G'day, how are ya?' `Orrh, champion, mate. Boxa birds.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,20</i>)

>  <b>DOWNES & HARCOURT</b> 'Cheerio, mum and dad, I'm in the pink, just a box o' birds.' <i>(1976,Voices in the Air,124</i>)

>  "A guy could be standing on a chair with a noose around his neck and the phone goes and his mate will say, 'Hows it going?' And he'll say, 'Oh, box of birds, box of fluffy ducks." <i>(Aug 15, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  <b>No 51 p19</b> On the ball, warts and all, getta life, fishwife On the box seat, sitting pretty, what a winner Dinkie Di. She'll be right, a box o' birds, tonights the night Stone the crows, and done like a dinner. <i>(Dec 2005,NZ Memories</i>)

>  She's a box of birds now and an awful lot brighter. <i>(Nov 21, 2003,Dominion Post RaceForm,2</i>)

>  <b>HAWES</b> ...he felt great - boxa birds! <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,166</i>)

>  We're keeping the rafts in sight, everyone's a box of birds. <i>(Oct 28, 1991,Dominion,1</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> Friday, and I was `a box of birds.' <i>(1943,Passage to Tobruk,126</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> <i>(1943,Penguin New Writing,17/73</i>) [NOTES: (OEDS)]

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> ... be a box of birds. <i>(1944,Troop Target,86</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `Glad to see you're alive. You're looking fit.' `A box of birds, a box of birds.' <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,194</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Box of birds, a<i> Fighting-fit: NZ troops': 1939-45. J.H. Fullarton, <i>Troop Target,</i> 1943. Singing with health and happiness. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,998</i>)

>  Hans was the clever one, who already knew all the expressions of New Zealand slang. `I'm a box of boids', he would say, or `O'ill say', whenever he could. <i>(Dec 1950,Here & Now,19</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `You're sure you're all right? Do you feel all right yourself, I mean?' `A box of birds. Except that they're not chirping much just now? <i>(1956,Sullen Bell,29</i>)

>  `A BOX OF BIRDS' Sir, - I am aware that idioms in any language are often difficult to analyse, but there is one phrase used in New Zealand which leaves me completely puzzled. It is the expression `a box of birds,' given in reply to the greeting `How are you?' and is evidently intended to mean a state of well-being. This appears to be so completely irrelevant that I would be pleased if any of your readers could elucidate, if that is possible. The term suggests to me something to be prepared for the dinner table, but I am willing to be corrected. - I am, etc. KIWIPOM. ? 1957 Evening Post (?p) [To feel like a `box of birds' is to feel happy and chirpy - Ed.] <i>(Dec 1957,Evening Post</i>) [NOTES: month guessed at; check]

>  <i>BOX OF BIRDS</i>: After hearing this from another New Zealander, I tried out the phrase on many people in the South of England and London. Not one person had heard of it, and most were unable, from first hearing (in context), to judge its actual meaning or inference. <i>(Dec 13, 1957,Evening Post</i>) [NOTES: G.J. Griffiths Letter]

### box of fluffies
 
 <i>phr.</i> (See [box of fluffy ducks](../B#box-of-fluffy-ducks))

### box of fluffy ducks
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  Thanks to Athol, who finally gets into the action a bit himself, everything ends up like a box of fluffy ducks. But it's just not my cup of tea. <i>(Feb 19, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  It is clear from the demeanours of backbench seniors.. that a dousing of patronage has not left them feeling like a box of fluffies. <i>(Mar 22, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  What Letterbox Lambs do have is a sense of humour. In its earlier days it was more overt, with several of the songs on their debut ep Box of Fluffies veering close to having a joky quality. <i>(Mar 26, 2001,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  [He] said the break would not deter her from standing at the next election. "She's a box of fluffy ducks. She will be standing." <i>(Mar 4, 1993,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>box of fluffies/box of fluffy ducks</i> engaging variants of preceding entry [a box of birds]. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,20</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> I'm a box of fluffy ducks? I'm just as WELL/HAPPY/SUCCESSFUL as can be. (see: box of birds) <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,17</i>)

>  But Riggir, New Zealand's "Beautiful Lady" of country music, is a box of fluffies when she rescues me from her little dog Amy, who has come to meet me... <i>(Jun 5, 2003,Dominion Post,D1</i>)

>  "A guy could be standing on a chair with a noose around his neck and the phone goes and his mate will say, 'Hows it going?' And he'll say, 'Oh, box of birds, box of fluffy ducks." <i>(Aug 15, 2005,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>) [NOTES: cross-referenced with <i>box of birds</i>]

>  "How do you feel?" "Box of fluffies." "All crap and feathers!" <i>(Dec 13, 2002,TV2</i>)

### box of fruit
 
 <i>n.</i> a suit [ORIGIN: rhyming slang] (See [cf. bowl of fruit](../B#bowl-of-fruit))

>  I decked myself out in a box of fruit, with knife creased terrace of houses, polished up the mary lous, locked up the shovel and headed for the racetrack. <i>(May 21, 1963,NZ Truth,19</i>)

### box off
 
 <i>v.</i> to make redundant [prob.f. farming, to separate out as from a large flock; with poss. pun on <i>box</i> `a (cargo) container' so perhaps `to put in a box,' that is, to stack away].

>  [Hdg] CONTAINERS COULD BOX OFF 1000 WATERSIDERS About 1000 watersiders could become redundant within the next 18 months. <i>(Oct 2, 1972,Dominion,1</i>)

### box on
 
 <i>v.</i> to persist or persevere; often box on regardless [Internet search Jan 2006: N Zealandism; 1 Austral. citation]

>  They're boxing on as best they can. The full impact is probably yet to hit home. <i>(Jan 5, 1999,Dominion,1</i>)

>  The Seddon bussinessman said yesterday that he would "box on" after his legal team filed proceedings... <i>(Jul 19, 2005,NZ Herald,C3</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I'd better slope off. I'll box on somewhere. Get out of this place before that silly bloody Croxley comes back nagging at me. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,239</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Kevin boxed on in his own way, the honest teacher, the man with a conscience, ... and no hope of becoming a headmaster. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,107</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>box on/ box on regardless</i> keep going, endure; eg `I know we've no chance of winning, but just box on regardless.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,20</i>)

### box out of the ring
 
 <i>phr.</i> of a married woman, to have an illicit sexual encounter.

>  <b>TUCKER</b> Four of us have a mousy brown hair colouring, but I have one sister who had beautiful titian red hair. Once I became interested in genetics I began to wonder how `Ginger' had turned up. My mother was a very dignified English schoolmistress and I certainly could not suspect that she had `boxed out of the ring'. <i>(1978,Thoroughbreds Are My Life,61</i>)

### box up
 
 <i>v.</i> -

>  ... all the mercies we had to be thankful for ... possession of `tucker' galore, and ... (as the weather became worse) how we were safe on this side of the Godley and not boxed up in Scone Creek. <i>(1936,NZ Alpine Journal,6/23/228</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to mix different mobs, either intentionally or unintentionally.

>  If you heeded the facial eczema warning and boxed the ewes up, many farms could be 100 per cent certain of a severe salmonella outbreak. <i>(Sep 24, 1970,NZ Farmer,46</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> It's as if all the sheep you've ever seen have been boxed up into one flock and plonked down in front of you. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,109</i>)

>  <b>PULLEN</b> Not long after, our mob got boxed up with one of Bruce Udy's. <i>(1996,On the Road Again,45</i>)

>  .. it is only during the tupping season that it will be necessary to keep the ewes separate .. The ewes can then be boxed up again, and run in convenient sized mobs. Before boxing them up, however ... <i>(Mar 1918,NZ Farmer,271</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Not a single sheep [in three mobs] got boxed up. <i>(1990,Matawhero, Elsewhere and Other Things,70</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) boxing (See [box v](../B#box-v))

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Many a `box up' occurred while the drovers were arguing who had the `right of way' [for their mobs]. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,53</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mixing of sheep from two or more mobs into one.

>  <b>HUTCHINSON</b> Silently, relying only on their instinct to 'head' sheep, they had worked through the night, keeping the escaping wethers under control, so averting the threatened box-up. <i>(1981,Hutchinsons of New Zealand, The,48</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Many a box up occurred while the drovers were arguing who had the right of way. <i>(1991,Off the Sheep's Back,53</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a mess up; state of confusion

>  <b>BAKER</b> When flocks are mixed they are said to be <i>boxed</i>, and from this peculiar use of the verb we have derived the term a <i>box-up</i>, a state of confusion, and <i>to be in a box</i>, to be in a confused state of mind. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,39</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>box up</i> state of confusion; eg `It's a total box up, nobody seems to know what his position is, or what he's s'posed to do.' ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,20</i>)

### box-fish
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Box Fish <i>Paracanthostracion lindsayi</i> (Phillipps) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,413</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 413]

>  <b>WILSON</b> BOXFISH <i>Paracacanthostracion lindsayi</i>: Two species of Boxfish inhabit in New Zealand waters and both make their appearances from time to time, usually as curiosities taken from fishermen's nets and displayed in fish shop windows. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,31</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Box-fishes. <i>Ostracion eco</i> n. sp. <i>(1932,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,13/233</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Box Fish <i>Ostracion eco</i> Phillipps (Syn. <i>O. hexagonus </i>Phillipps, preocc.) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,413</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 413]

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family OSTRACIIDAE. The box fish (<i>Ostracion</i> ... ) and trunk fish (<i>Paracanthostracion</i>) have the head and body completely encased in an armoured box of welded hexagonal plates. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,233</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>BOX FISH Ostracion cubicus (Ostracion eco)</i> ... This distinctive little fish is enclosed in a rigid box like armour of bony plates that protects all but the eyes, mouth, fins and tail. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,315,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] boxfish 2347, 2352 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> He spent the next three years shearing, mustering, and boundary-keeping on Coronet Peak [station]. <i>(1965,Skippers Road,19</i>)

### boxed corner
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SALMOND</b> boxed corner boards fixed over weatherboards at outside corners of a house to exclude water - cf <i>angle stop</i> corner <i>(1986,Glossary of Building Terminology,231</i>)

### boxed-up
 
 <i>adj.</i> jailed

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>boxed-up</i>, adj. In prison; gaoled: N.Z.:C.20 <i>(1961,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1012</i>)

### boxer
 
 <i>n.</i> the person in charge of (the bets in) a two-up game cf <i>ringie</i> (See [cf. Partridge 8e](../P#partridge-8e))

>  The "boxer" - the man in charge of the school - without hesitation or ceremony, laid out the owner of the double-header. <i>(Jun 9, 1920,NZ Free Lance,24</i>)

>  <b>LAWSON</b> Percy Gray was the spinner, and Alf Burke, the boxer - you know the man who takes the bets [c1880s]. <i>(1951,Gold in their Hearts,90</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a bowler hat (See [cf. hard hitter](../H#hard-hitter))

>  Started Mr and Mrs Acland and their belongings in their carriage. Mrs Harper and son in boxers [ed note bowlers, i.e., hats]. <i>(Jan 27, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,74,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Box&#769;.er</i> (b&obreve.ks&#769;.&etilde.r),<i> n</i>. A kind of stiff round hat; - called in England <i>bowler</i> or <i>deerstalker. </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2018</i>)

>  Another man who wore a `boxer' hat ... he saw the accused and the `bloke' in the `boxer' ... <i>(May 5, 1906,NZ Truth,46/8/5</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a gratuity or percentage of winnings paid to the person in charge of a two-up ring. --

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> ... when the ring-keeper said `Up and do 'em', I collected 200 francs. Of course I had to give the ring-keeper a boxer. <i>(1935,Slang Today,287</i>)

>  A `boxer' is a gratuity to the `ringie' at two-up, a game supplying the phrase `two ladies on bicycles,' two pennies showing Britannia. <i>(Apr 4, 1938,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: McNab NZ Slang]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Boxer.</i> A gratuity; esp. to the `ringie' (in two-up) from the winning betters: NZ and Austr (-GW+), ex <i>Christmas box. </i> <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,87</i>)

### boxhead
 
 <i>n.</i> a fool

>  <b>PARK</b> `She says not to be big boxhead. That is Tuhikaramea, my sister's boy. He my boy now.' <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,67</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> `What a cruel thing to say box-head,' Jackpot returned sadly. <i>(1981,Fish Factory,75</i>)

### boxing
 
 <i>n.</i> the inadvertent or purposeful mixing of drafts of farm animals (See [box v](../B#box-v))

>  <b>BARKER</b> [Shepherd speaks] He couldn't cross [his mob] over a river ... and never a week passes without his getting boxed. That's mixed-up ma'am. We calls it boxing when your sheep go and join another mob feeding close by, and you can't tell one from another except by the brand or the earmark. <i>(1872,Xmas Cake in Four Quarters, A,Chapter iii</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Fear of boxing was the origin of this custom of giving notice. [sc. of driving sheep through another's property: see sheep notice] <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,152</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Boxing - Sometimes different lots of sheep would fail to keep separate, and entanglement followed. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,84</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> technique of fighting used by deer of the same sex who are in dispute in a pecking order. [citation also includes verb to box]

>  <b>LENTLE & SAXTON</b> boxing ; technique of fighting used by deer of the same sex who are in dispute in a pecking order. Hinds often box one another. Stags do also, particularly when in velvet. Contestants rear up on their hind legs and approach each other with downward-kicking forelegs. <i>(1991,Red Deer in New Zealand,182</i>)

### boxthorn
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Lyceum ferocissimum</i>. OED: box-thorn, common name for shrubs of the genus Lycium, esp. L. barbarum. Also BOX-TREE, BOX-WOOD. (See [African boxthorn](../A#african-boxthorn))

>  I learnt how to eradicate the aggressive box thorn on the cliffs of Red Mercury Island (weed-killing abseilers). <i>(Jan 10, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),67</i>)

>  Commercial beekepers in Taranaki have been fighting a losing battle in recent years in regard to nectar Sources, with boxthorn and barberry being pulled out, less gorse and wild plants and the planting of new varieties of clover that have fewer flowers and more foliage. <i>(Aug 30, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,18</i>)

>  The quail would be at the very end of our northward journey in a copse of poplar and boxthorn where the wind had scoured sand away from a dune base and exposed the water table. <i>(Jul 12, 2003,Dominion Post,F9</i>)

>  He may have hidden out for a while - some of the boxthorn clumps form deep caves - agonising over what to do next. <i>(May 27, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  <b>BALL</b> He'd thicken my lip and throw my schoolbag into the boxthorn bushes <i>(1970,People Makers, The,145</i>)

>  Pampas grass, planted for shelter when the Agriculture and Fisheries Ministry ran the island as quarantine station in the 1970s, has been sprayed and the department is removing boxthorn and other introduced species. <i>(Oct 12, 2000,Dominion,9</i>)

>  <b>CROWE</b> Boxthorn is a native of the dry, eastern regions of South Africa. It was introduced to New Zealand as a hedge plant, particularly in Taranaki, but by last century had begun to spread in that area to form immense thickets. <i>(1995,Which Coastal Plant?,10</i>)

>  <b>HILL</b> Boxthorn apparently used to be planted around African villages to keep marauding lions at bay. <i>(1988,More from Moaville,55</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  And yes, it is a fact that for 50 metres up the road from us, the tornado skinned roofs off houses before sidestepping another 100 metres into the country, to chew up boxthorn hedges and macrocarpa shelter-belts. <i>(Oct 15, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  What was once boxthorn-covered farmland near the cottage, with its raupo-lined pond,. is being tamed as the couple plant natives in accordance with a planting guide. <i>(Mar 8, 2002,Dominion Post,F5</i>)

### boxthorn hedge
 
 <i>n.</i> a hedge of African boxthorn, common in Taranaki

>  They are also using riparian management principles to plant out a boundary fence with a neighbouring property - getting rid of the traditional boxthorn hedge shelter belt. <i>(Aug 30, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  Boxthorn hedges used to be all through the [Hurleyville] district. <i>(Jul 24, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

### boxwood
 
 <i>n.</i> = golden tauhinu or hebe spp.

>  <i>boxwood [=] golden tauhinu</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,7</i>)

>  <b>DEWAR</b> The grass playing area [of the Southland school] was backed by a great area of `boxwood' or native heather. <i>(1953,Chaslands,19</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (SI) ? = hebe

>  <b>ALLAN</b> The name <i>koromiko (korokio</i> in the Far North) is commonly used for the larger willow - lvd [Hebe] spp. In the high country the name `boxwood' us. refers to some small-lvd <i>Hebe. </i> <i>(1961,Flora NZ,1/887,1982 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> For several days they travelled over miles of silver tussock country with, here and there, clumps of pampas grass or boxwood growing in patches of peaty swamp. <i>(1970,Tat,59</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> maire (See [contrast box n](../C#contrast-box-n))

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Box-wood </i>... a New Zealand wood, <i>Olea lanceolata</i>, Hook., <i>N.O. Jasmineae</i> (Maori name, <i>Maire</i>. Used by the `Wellington Independent' (April 19, 1845) for woodcuts, and recommended as superior to box-wood for the purpose. See also <i>box. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,51</i>)

### boy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Up Auckland way the crusade in favour of cheap domestic labor[sic] has resulted in a scheme for the importation of a number of black `boys' from the islands, said `boys' being men who are sent to the Dominion under contract by their relatives or owners with whom a regular slave or `blackbird' traffic will be initiated. <i>(Sep 9, 1911,NZ Truth,324/4/3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The police were kept pretty busy among the "boys", who, owing to the excessive heat, got beyond the playful into the irritable mood, and consequently went further than was allowed by law. <i>(Jan 23, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a male servant or porter in early colonial NZ, and elsewhere; a Maori male (as a servant, porter, convert, attendant).

>  Pinney, cook, signed articles; also William Brown and three Mawrie boys. <i>(May 1, 1842,Piraki Log,126,(ed. Anson 1911) ed.</i>)

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> After a tedious talk, ... they [sc. Maoris] pointed out three boys, ... who should accompany me, and these I engaged at 4s. a day (forward). <i>(Apr 9, 1844,Diary,206</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Apr 9 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 206]

>  My boy stuck manfully (and boorish-like utterly unconscious of danger) to the donkey's tail. <i>(Aug 16, 1849,Torlesse Papers,94,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> We had not been long here before my boys had dressed the pig in the Maori fashion, and the old man had dug up some potatoes and prepared a hangi - an oven of hot stones. <i>(Mar 1851,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1851 Mar T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 10]

>  <i>boy n.</i> a native servant or labourer. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)
