>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealanders have coined or adapted many expressions to meet local requirements, as illustrated by the following: ... <i>boy</i>: native servant; recorded in 1840 <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),117</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> After breakfast our boys urged us to move on. ... we acordingly packed our baggage and by seven o'clock were all on the road. <i>(Nov 6, 1827,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,82,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Our Settlement has been in an unsettled state for several days from the circumstance of my elder boy's taking a young female to wife who was designed for another person. <i>(May 14, 1829,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1829 May 14 C. Baker Jnl in 1986 Baker Never the Faint Hearted 57]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> [a Maori servant or man] The boys preparing a <i>Wata,</i>a high stand for [245] native food, about 16ft. long and 10 wide, 16ft from the ground, to preserve it from the English rats which are now beginning to infest all parts of the land ... <i>(May 16, 1832,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,244,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> At 3 the natives were served with their repast of beef potatoes and stirabout. As our boys have had some experience in this important duty ... our visitors were ... surprised at the order and expedition with which this assemblage of New Zealand rank was supplied, as the feast consisted of bout 800 dishes constructed of a plant similar to the flag. <i>(May 17, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,314,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> The Boys often showed what [fern] was best to eat. <i>Ibid.</i> 40 I was certainly ashamed that Europeans could degrade themselves so before their New Zealand Boys, but so they did. ... <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),21</i>)

>  <b>STRAUBEL</b> ... his wife, a New Zealand woman, 3 children and two New Zealand boys. <i>(1836,Rhodes Whaling Journal,34,1954 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1836 (1954)ed Straubel Rhodes Whaling Journal 34]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> .. we rested at ten in a little shed constructed of palm leaves (the nigau) where the Waimate boys had passed the preceding night <i>(Nov 13, 1839,Journal,2/167</i>) [NOTES: [Maori (?bearer) (Taylor uses <i>boy</i> for his bearers and guides at least up to 1858 : also native)]]

>  My boys, as native attendants are styled by both natives and white men ... <i>(1840,NZ Journal,23/286</i>) [NOTES: (E.J. Wakefield)]

>  ... gangs of <i>boys</i> (as the New Zealanders of all ages call themselves when in the service of Europeans) <i>(1841,New Zealand, S. Aust. and NSW,293</i>)

>  [Henry Williams] had not given them [sc. Maoris for land] even a fish-hook or a head of tobacco, except in payment to the boys who carried his things, or to those who became converts to the mihanere (missionary) creed. <i>(Apr 19, 1841,GBPP,(No.311) 129</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Apr 19 Joseph Somes to Lord John Russell in GBPP House of Commons 1841 (No.311) 129 [=Maori porter] [NZ letter of 23 Aug 1840]]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> . ?esp. among Ma. youths,, often hey boy (the usage differs from the general Eng (1917) coll intensive or exclamation (boy oh) boy!, itself common in NZ, esp. among secondary schools: cf school questionnaire evidence omitted here). [ca1940 at least Hey boy, what ya doing there boy, eh?]

>  Say, there was a sheila down my section today; boy, I bet a joker could make her on a cup of coffee. <i>(1948,Landfall,6/110</i>) [NOTES: (Ballantyne)]

>  <b>CRUMP</b> I'd ask Mori for his tally and he'd reply, `Found a beauty huhu log, boy.' <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,45</i>)

>  <b>BALLANTYNE</b> `I'll dong you, boy!' he shouted. <i>Ibid</i> 6 `You know, all right, but I'll tell you, boy.' <i>(1968,Sydney Bridge,6</i>) [NOTES: [as a term of address, ? chiefly between children]]

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Hit the sack, eh, boy? We're going to be flying in the morning.' <i>(1976,Pacific Star,70</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BEATON</b> SANDY: Lav freaked an' called in Cowface. KATE: Cowface was boilin', boy. <i>Ibid</i> 27 <i>(1984,Outside In,23</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> , esp. (be) one of the boys, be one of the gang, remain in the ranks. [ca1940-1950 (Army) He refused stripes to be with the boys.]

>  <b>SKEY</b> He goes on Sunday to the `pub'/ And sits among `the boys' <i>(1889,Pirate Chief and the Mummy's Complaint..., The,195</i>)

>  <b>DOWSING</b> ... me and the boys (in the colony, by a strange perversion of the English Langugage, men are called boys) <i>(1894,He,2</i>)

>  <b>FOSTON</b> They became very popular with the `boys' in the camps throughout the `back country.' <i>(1911,Bell Bird's Lair,89</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> Alan [from a wealthy family] had proven himself to be one of the boys. <i>(1943,Passage to Tobruk,90</i>)

### braceman
 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining]

>  <b>VOGEL</b> [Wages on goldfields] bracemen ... 8/4, feeders ... 6/-, amalgamator ... 9/- <i>(1875,Official Handbook of NZ,259</i>)

>  Brace and chambermen not to be employed in any other occupation. <i>(Sep 29, 1911,Maoriland Worker,30/6/2</i>)

He had to remove and tip the full trucks and replace them with empties as they arrived in the cage, and had to load men and materials going down the shaft. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),313</i>) [NOTES: (Glossary of Waihi Mining Terms)]



 
 <i>n.</i> [entered as brace-men (word not in cit.)]

>  [Auckland Seamen]. 8. That this award shall come into force on the 1st of February next, and remain in force until the 28th of February, 1899. 9. That this award shall be binding upon the union and its members and upon the said firms and each of them, save that Messrs. Leyland and O'Brien, so long as they pay the rates of wages to seamen and firemen they at present pay, shall not be bound by it, but if they discontinue to pay such rates they shall henceforth be bound. <i>(1900,Awards etc.,I. 46</i>) [NOTES: 1899 award.]

### bracken
 
 <i>n.</i> fern

>  The best known examples [of specialised habitats] are bracken (<i>Pteridium esculentum</i>), which occurs in areas that have been burnt or cleared, and pig fern (<i>Paesia scaberula</i>) which forms extensive colonies on reverting pasture. <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/78</i>)

>  Bracken root was a staple food for pre-European Maori, and there are many Maori words relating to different stages of its growth that were used for different purposes. <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/77</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> When we came to a long boundary fence with a gate, we tied them [sc. the horses] there and getting out the oilskins, lay down on the springy bracken for an hour-and-a-half's rest. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,134</i>)

>  We thought bracken beds might be a good way of stopping DPBs procreating, but it was pointed out that they would have been gathering the bracken from the roadside, and not paying Transit New Zealand for it. <i>(Dec 28, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),124</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Where the previous forest cover is completely removed ..., the first coloniser is bracken fern (<i>Pteridium esculentum</i>) [107] followed in order by two small-leaved light demanding species - the shrub manuka (<i>Leptospermum scoparium</i>) and the small tree kanuka (<i>Kunzea ericoides</i>). <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,106</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [bracken](../B#bracken))

### bracket
 
 <i>n.</i> set of songs [As in the phrase: Moira will now sing an Irish bracket.]



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  297... Where practicable an announcement of the horses which are bracketed shall be broadcast over the public address system on the Racecourse. <i>(1986,NZ Rules of Racing,117</i>)

>  Charlie Chan.. is a useful improver.. and bracketed with the consistent Sandusky, the combination will find strong support. <i>(May 11, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,76</i>)

>  Great Chum.. will be bracketed with Argotis. <i>(Jun 1, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,76</i>)

>  In the Marton Cup, Betterman and Skydonna will be bracketed. <i>(Dec 30, 1944,Wanganui Chronicle,7</i>)

>  <b>McCARTHY</b> The Rules of Trotting... say that all horses shall be bracketed: (a) Which are owned wholly or partly by the same owner... (f) Which the stipendiary stewards order to be bracketed <i>(1970,Horse Racing, Trotting & Dog Racing in NZ,233</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> ca1945 As a totalisator or betting convenience, to pair horses in certain circumstances so that if either is placed a dividend is paid on both. The circumstances are these: a) horses beyond 18 in any race are paired (bracketted) with a number, and hence a horse, already given (e.g. in a race of 20 entries, the last numbers would be 17, 17a, 18, 18a); b) similarly, when entries are more than can be safely run at one time and the race must be run in two divisions, each horse in the first division is paired (bracketted) with each horse in the second; c) (formerly) horses of the same ownership (galloping) or of the same ownership and trainership (trotting and pacing), running in the same race were bracketted.



 
 <i>n.</i> to couple in a horse race for dividend payment purposes the horses of the same owner or trainer [(revise this definition use HNZD2) racing in the same race.]

>  More often the stable is represented by a bracket, which.. is invariably well supported... Best recent form of likely opponents for the Smith bracket in the Victoria Handicap has been shown by Snowflake.. and Single Direct. <i>(Oct 22, 1947,NZ Observer,28</i>)

>  294. In the event of there being a greater number of starters in a race than there are numbers available on the totalisator the Stewards or Committee may create a sufficient number of brackets by including two horses for each bracket so required beginning with the two lowest weighted horses and working up from them. <i>(1986,NZ Rules of Racing,117</i>)

>  With Crocus and Nervie's Last, F.J. Smith has a powerful bracket. <i>(Oct 19, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,76</i>)

>  L.F. Berkett has a strong bracket in the Whitford Handicap. <i>(Mar 21, 1946,NZ Farmers' Weekly,26</i>)

>  The Epsom Handicap attracted a field of eight, the bracket Tradesman and Roi Alto being the popular choices. <i>(Jan 5, 1949,Auckland Weekly News,51</i>)

>  294. In the event of there being a greater number of starters in a race than there are numbers available on the totalisator the Stewards or Committee may create a sufficient number of brackets to meet the position by including two horses for each bracket so required beginning with the two lowest weighted horses and working up from them. <i>(1986,NZ Rules of Racing,117</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  Injured Jones bracketed for test [headline] ... His injury is not regarded as too serious but it will still be touch and go whether he is fit in time to regain his test spot from Mike Brewer, who's also been named as a standby. <i>(Jul 15, 1988,Dominion,28</i>)

### bracket creep
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Bracket creep - incomes rising through rate thresholds - is a surreptitious sliding tax increase because the share of a person's income going to tax rises when the income tops a threshold. <i>(Feb 22, 2006,NZ Herald,C4</i>)

>  But his big push was a big move to address bracket creep -- the effect by which inflation and wage increase push a larger proportion of workers' income into a higher tax bracket rate as their nominal income increases, thus boosting government revenue with no overt political action by the Government. <i>(May 27, 2004,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

### bracketing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McCARTHY</b> In horse racing, bracketing is the coupling of two or more horses under one number on a race card and on the totalisator. The bet is recorded against the number, not the individual horse. ... Bracketing policies differ between racing and trotting. <i>(1970,Horse Racing, Trotting & Dog Racing in NZ,233</i>)

### braided river
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wide riverbed created by melting glacial action that contains single streams that join and separate [sign. in South Island for stock and human drowning, 1800s]

>  Braided rivers are special. They are found only in northern Asia, Alaska, Iceland and New Zealand, and are created by the chaos of melting glaciers. <i>(Aug 2002,Forest & Bird,305/38</i>)

>  Meridian Energy's ludicrous Project Aqua would see the removal of 77 percent of the water from the mighty Waitaki River, one of the world's great braided rivers. <i>(May 10, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  The glaciers also carved out the valleys where now flow two braided rivers, the Ashburton South branch and the Rangitata. Braided rivers are special. They are found only in northern Asia, Alaska, Iceland and New Zealand and are created by the chaos of melting glaciers. <i>(Aug 2002,Forest & Bird,28</i>)

>  Wild, braided rivers are rare. They appear in only half a dozen countries in the world. <i>(Mar 8, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  I'm really pissed off with the extent to which we're supposed to sacrifice our last great braided river so that people can heat their towel racks, their spa pools and run their electric toothbrushes and hairdryers. <i>(Sep 21, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),21</i>)

 <i>n.</i> used occas. in 19C.

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> BLACKBERRY (<i>Rubus fruticosus</i> and [<i>R.laciniatus</i>]), occasionally called bramble, is commonest in the middle and south of the North Island, and on the West Coast of Nelson. ### branch
 
 <i>n.</i> any of the various streams (?or beds) of a plains river

>  We had great trouble to cross the streams ... Huge stones ... were constantly rolling down nearly every branch we crossed. <i>(Jan 12, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,160,1950 ed.</i>)

>  The river was very high ..., having got over that you had to walk some way and then get into a bulock dray and cross a branch that people at home [sc. England] would look at a long time ..., the bullocks sticking up in the middle each time. <i>(Nov 4, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,203,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> We ... arrived towards evening at a branch of the Rakaia which, coming from the west, unites here with the main stream. <i>(1960,Over the Whitcombe Pass,21</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Lauper in 1960 Pascoe (ed) Over the Whitcombe Pass 21]

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> About five and thirty miles from our hut, at one of the most dangerous crossings of the river, - increased now by swollen branches which had joined it on all sides, - a man was camped ... <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,170</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Having decided to ascend, first the principal branch of the Rakaia, of which the original name has been preserved, I followed the track leading from Mr. Neave's home station along the base of Mount Aegidas [sic] to the Mathias branch. [this sense `a lateral extension' of a main trunk] <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,124</i>)

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> ... I saw this morning that they [a clump of trees] appeared to be cut off from the bank by a sort of small branch of the river. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,72</i>)

>  <b>FRANCIS</b> Do you remember old Shirty and the time his dogs got away up the top branch? [?lateral extension] <i>(1983,Wildlife Ranger,56</i>) [NOTES: [sc of a river]]

>  <b>POTTS</b> <i>(1882,Out in the Open</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

It is stamped on to sheep with paint applied with a<i> branding iron</i> or <i>brand.</i> (2) and on to cattle and horses with red-hot iron which leaves a mark on the skin. ### brandy

### branks
 
 <i>n.</i> a kind of bridle or halter ?with a piece of wood on each side instead of leather joined to a halter.

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Mr. Andrew Liddell, who lived to be over a hundred, and who was a bullock-driver from the age of sixteen until he was twenty-two, had much to tell me of this adventurous portion of his long career, ... `You ask how the bullocks were attached to the vehicle, and as bullock-driving is now a lost art, I had better try and explain ... Very seldom was harness used, and then without winkers, and it was called branks. The pulling strain came on the bows, and a yoke joined two bows and went over each neck of a pair of bullocks. If there were five pairs, say, a chain ran from the yoke of the first pair to the yoke of the second pair, and another chain from the second to the third, and another chain from the third to the fourth. These chains were lighter between the first pairs and heavier as you got further back. The two rear bullocks were yoked to the pole of the dray or waggon. As there were no reins, the driving was all by commands and cracking the whip. The calls were `Get up' or `Gee up' to start, `Whoa' to stop, `Come here' to turn to left, and `Gee off' to turn right. If you came to a sharp corner, you would often say persuasively, `Way! Way! Come here.'' There is a general impression that bullocks could not be successfully driven without roaring oaths and curses at them, but this idea is a relic of early-day humour. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,42</i>)

 <i>n.</i> the policies of Don Brash
