>  She.. doesn't feel she's had much option after the latest Brashism suggesting there is a perception that Maori who make it into university on special-admission schemes graduate with inferior degrees. <i>(Mar 3, 2004,NZ Herald</i>)

>  ...in a Brashism reminiscent of the days when he urged people to sell their homes to move into rented property for marginal economic advantage, he adds a further string to the Anzac dollar's bow: when Australia was buoyant and New Zealand was depressed, "people could move". <i>(Mar 27, 1999,Dominion,16</i>)

### Brass Monkey (Rally)
 
 <i>n.</i> an annual hardcore motorcycle rally in the South Island

>  About 400 rugged - some would say crazy - Wellington motorcyclists recently donned leather and thermals for the annual pilgrimage to the Brass Monkey Rally in Otago... For Ranfurly, the Brass Monkey is an economic lifesaver. <i>(Aug 20, 2002,Dominion Post,D3</i>)

>  The possibility of chilly conditions is almost an attraction, as the growing event is one of several wintry rallies, such as the Cold Kiwi at Waiouru and the Brass Monkey in Central Otago, that attract large numbers of hardy riders to camp out and socialise. <i>(May 9, 2003,Dominion Post,A4</i>)

>  Rejoining the trail, we pass Oturehua, renowned for its mid-winter "Brass Monkey" motorcycle rally and historic Hayes' Engineering workshop. <i>(Jul 2, 2005,Dominion Post,E19</i>)

### brass razoo
 
 <i>n.</i> variant of razoo q.v. [also Australian] (See [razoo](../R#razoo))

>  In fact, despite a schedule which will take them around the world in well over 80 days, they don't receive a brass razoo from any funding agency, state or not. <i>(Jun 1, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B18</i>)

>  <b>Gee</b> 'People would say I was your mistress,' I said. 'Do we give a brass razoo what people say?' <i>(2005,Blindsight,133</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> HUGH. Don't you get anything? JIM. Not a brass razoo. <i>(1977,Glide Time,55</i>)

>  Some of the most generous, wonderful people I've come across haven't got two brass razoos to rub together. <i>(Jul 15, 2004,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

>  `The United States Air Force didn't give us a brass razoo for financing the trip,' Sir Edmund [Hillary] added. <i>(Feb 1, 1966,Evening Post,21/3</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> Every penny gone, not a brass razoo left. Next day back to the wharf! What a hakari. <i>(1969,Awatea,115,1978 ed.</i>) [NOTES: Appendix II]

>  <b>STEWART</b> This place may owe me something, but I don't owe it a brass razoo. <i>(1981,Hot & Copper Sky,295</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> And when everything was sorted out, the Old Man came out of that contract without a zak to show for it. ... at the end of seven months' bushwork we didn't have a brass razoo. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,68</i>) [NOTES: (Bob Edwards, bushman)]

>  [Advt] No Deposit/ Not a cent/ Not a sausage/ Not a brass razoo <i>(Jan 6, 1986,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> brass razoo a small amount of money; not have a brass razoo to have no money. Originally a gambling chip in Australasia; eg `Sorry, chief, I'd like to help you, but I don't have a flipping brass razoo.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,20</i>)

>  Whether it is a pint in one of Courtenay Place's intimate "trend bars" or a couple of cold ones in a more traditional pub setting, you know it is going to cost you a brass razoo or two. <i>(Nov 20, 2002,Capital Times,22</i>)

### Brass, the
 
 <i>n.</i> the Brass Monkey Motorcycle Rally (See [Brass Monkey (Rally)](../B#brass-monkey-(rally)))

>  I did "the Brass" in 2000, drawn more by the lore of the event than the allure of standing around in sub-zero temperatures discussing carburettor adjustments with petrolheads. <i>(Sep 2004,NZ Geographic,No. 69/105</i>)

### brat camp
 
 <i>n.</i> [Internet search Feb 2006: also used in UK. 700 hits for N Amer; 600 for NZ.]

>  United Future wants wayward teenagers placed in "brat camps" run by the Army. <i>(Jul 18, 2005,NZ Herald,A4</i>)

### brat pack
 
 <i>n.</i> a group of young cabinet ministers in the National government of the late 1990s [(transferred use)]

>  Late last year, the other members of English's brat pack - Roger Sowry, Nick Smith and Tony Ryall - began pushing for the removal of Wyatt Creech from the deputy prime ministership... But English, like others in the so-called Shipley "brat pack", is conscious the government can deliver real benefits only if there is longterm growth in the economy. <i>(Feb 7, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  Jenny Shipley's Young Guns - the Brat Pack, as the media rather flatteringly called them - are technically young, as in below 40. That is young in National Party circles. <i>(Feb 13, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  Tony Ryall, the only member of National's "brat pack" not to be made a minister, is settling in for a regime of passive-aggression in his chairmanship of the finance and expenditure committee. <i>Ibid.</i> 34 Socially, English remains bonded to National's erstwhile "brat pack" - himself, Smith, Sowry and Tony Ryall - who once presented themselves as an update of Labour's fish'n'chip gang by posing for a publicity short while supposedly plotting at McDonald's. <i>(Mar 22, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  "The brat pack was wetting its nappies," observes one seasoned minister caustically, referring to the quartet of up and coming ministers led by Bill English... Instead, it is the brat pack who, along with Shipley, are intended to be National's energetic, new public face. English in Finance, Nick Smith in Education, Tony Ryall in Justice and Sowry in Social Services are attempting to make the running by a series of announcements in their portfolios. <i>(Apr 18, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  [Bill English] has become angrier and angrier watching Labour campaign against himself and his closest colleagues, in particular the three other (comparatively) young ministers known as the Brat Pack. <i>(May 1, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  Whether Shipley's ministerial brat pack succeeds in reshaping the political debate on social policy, National may need an even more powerful circuit-breaker to shift voting sentiment... Who better to turn to than someone who entered Parliament while the brat pack was still in short pants?.. Bratpack minister Tony Ryall picked up the portfolio from McCully in January. <i>(May 2, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  But the approach of National's new breed of Ministers - the so called bratpack headed by Mr English and Mr Sowry and including Nick Smith and Tony Ryall - is drowned out by the cacophony of ills that continue to plague the Government. <i>(May 2, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C2</i>)

>  But the approach of National's new breed of Ministers - the so called bratpack headed by Mr English and Mr Sowry and including Nick Smith and Tony Ryall - is drowned out by the cacophony of ills that continue to plague the Government. <i>(May 29, 1999,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  With Bill English and Mr Sowry in the two top spots, the "brat pack" has truly arrived... The brat pack's ties extend to family holidays at Christmas. <i>(Oct 10, 2001,Dominion,13</i>)

>  What Smith's grudging resignation showed, however, was that the Brat Pack's days are over. <i>(Nov 29, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),12</i>)

>  The "brat pack" - a tight-knit group who entered Parliament together in 1990 - are resented by some for the influence they wield and their monopolisation of the senior roles in Parliament. <i>(Jul 8, 2002,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  And yet it represents a remarkable triumph for the "brat pack" - a moniker I'm afraid I [<i>sc.</i> Michael Laws] applied to English, Sowry, Tony Ryall and Nick Smith in early 1990 and was soundly slapped down for. <i>(Oct 14, 2001,Sunday Star Times,A9</i>)

>  National's arch young fogey. Tony Ryall, a member of the party's laughably unhip "brat pack" had just claimed - falsely - that his local hospital was full of dope-crazed maniacs while trying to defend his Prime Minister's ill-judged attack on the Green's marijuana policy <i>(Dec 4, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

>  What Shipley does have going for her is a smoothness in presentation which is head and shoulders above all of the brat pack at present. <i>(May 14, 2000,Sunday Star Times,C11</i>)

>  Nick Smith's forced resignation as National Party deputy leader marks another step in the decline of the 'bratpack" - the tight-knit group of four young National MPs who entered Parliament together in, or just out of, their twenties, holidayed together and rose through the ranks together. All four were first elected in 1990... Mr English was No 1; his mates Roger Sowry, Nick Smith and Tony Ryall were Nos.2, 3 and 5...But the bratpack - a label advertising guru Kevin Roberts attached to them - is history as a unit. This is an idea the bratpackers themselves have been trying to foster for several years. <i>(Nov 19, 2003,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  Nick Smith's forced resignation as National Party deputy leader marks another step in the decline of the 'bratpack" - the tight-knit group of four young National MPs who entered Parliament together in, or just out of, their twenties, holidayed together and rose through the ranks together. All four were first elected in 1990... Mr English was No 1; his mates Roger Sowry, Nick Smith and Tony Ryall were Nos.2, 3 and 5...But the bratpack - a label advertising guru Kevin Roberts attached to them - is history as a unit. This is an idea the bratpackers themselves have been trying to foster for several years. <i>(Nov 19, 2003,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

### bratpacker
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [brat pack](../B#brat-pack))

>  It is more likely that English and his court - which, incidentally, is rather wider than the other three Brat packers - have barely begun their attempt to reorient the government's direction and image. <i>(Jun 5, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  Mr English, 39, and Mr Sowry, 42, have been firm friends since entering Parliament in 1990 with fellow bratpackers Tony Ryall and Nick Smith. <i>(Oct 10, 2001,Dominion,13</i>)

>  <b>A2</b> Fourth-ranked National MP Gerry Brownlee is the only non-brat-packer among the party's top five MPs. <i>(Jul 8, 2002,Dominion Post</i>)

>  But the bratpack - a label advertising guru Kevin Roberts attached to them - is history as a unit. This is an idea the bratpackers themselves have been trying to foster for several years. <i>(Nov 19, 2003,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  The most hot-headed of the four so-called bratpackers learned that the company had spent $23,000 on three rugs. <i>(Nov 20, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

### brattice
 
 <i>n.</i> a partition of wood or strong hempen sheeting, placed in the shaft of a pit, &c., for the purpose of ventilation.

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> ... a piece of brattis ripped down from the stenton. <i>(1928,Dawn Beloved,172</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> <i>Brattice: </i>A curtain erected across an airway to control ventilation. See also curtains. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),313</i>) [NOTES: Glossary of Waihi Mining Terms]

>  <b>BATTYE & EATKIN</b> Coarse canvas screen hung at intervals through the mine to contain foul air and distribute fresh air from the ventilation <i>system. </i> <i>(1980,Shadow of the Valley,72</i>)

>  <b>BEARDSLEY</b> brattice partition of wood used to control ventilation in a mine <i>(1984,Blackball 08,242</i>)

### brave
 
 <i>n.</i> applied to a Maori warrior

>  <b>FEATON</b> The Maori braves ... <i>(1879,Waikato War,35</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

### braxy
 
 <i>n.</i> an inflammatory disease of sheep.

>  <i>Braxy</i>.-A fatal disease of sheep. Sheep that die of it make wholesome mutton, and in Scotland are prepared (salted, I think) and eaten. I do not know that we have exactly the same disease in New Zealand; certainly we have not the custom of eating the victims of it; but it is a stock joke on stations to point out any stinking carcase to a Scotch shepherd and say: `There's a nice bit of <i>b</i>. mutton for you, `So and so.'' <i>(Jan 13, 1934,Press, The,13</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss. additions]

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>Braxy </i>a fatal disease of sheep. Sheep that die of it make wholesome mutton and in Scotland are prepared (salted perhaps) and eaten. We may not have exactly the same disease in New Zealand and certainly we do not have the custom of eating the victims of it; but it is a stock joke on stations to point out any stinking carcase to a Scotch shepherd and say, `There's nice bit of braxy mutton for you, So-and-so.' <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,358</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [meaning extension] a dead farm animal found in a paddock.

>  <b>SHARPE</b> I reckon that braxy mutton cost the man who was selling Hungry Hills the value of two or three hundred sheep. <i>(1962,Country Occasions,131</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> I told him the word was 'braxy' but that the word didn't apply to all dead sheep. <i>(1966,Sixty Million Muster,38</i>)

### braya
 
 <i>n.</i> [[omit as mere transliteration of name]]

>  <b>FEATON</b> BRAYA NOVAE ZELANDIAE (<i>Hook.F.</i>) ... The `NEW ZEALAND BRAYA'.- This remarkable little Alpine plant is found in <i>debris</i> of schist ... in the Wanaka Lake district. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,20</i>)

### brazil
 
 <i>n.</i> a[n ?inferior] tobacco [nf dicts: ? a tradename; ?from Brazil; ?f. OED2 brazil--> sb2 (dial.) an inferior coal].

>  Tobacco is the <i>currency</i> of the Island: you could no more travel without it than in Europe with <i>`the ready'</i>; nothing however but American negro-head will pass; Brazil they [sc. Maoris] will scarcely accept as a gift - ... <i>(Sep 9, 1834,NZ Independent,31,1979 ed.</i>)

### breach
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> ... if we were sprung drinking we could be breached. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,240</i>)

### bread and butter
 
 <i>n.</i> a ball (one with basic refreshments)

>  Bread and butter Ball [sic] at the Athenaeum in the evening. 2/6 tickets ... [306] The Bread and Butters are so called because bread and butter is the only eatable provided - tea and coffee are the only drinkables. <i>(Sep 12, 1857,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/305-306,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

### bread and butter dog
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [mustering] a sheepdog that works extremely hard, but does not shine or win prizes.

>  <b>KERR</b> Musterers often talk of 'a bread-and-butter dog' - this is a qualified compliment meaning he or she might not look much and isn't particularly elegant around a flock or herd, won't win prizes at trials .. but works and gets the job done. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,86</i>)

### breadiebut
 
 <i>n.</i> bread and butter [ca1935]

### break
 
 <i>v.</i> of sheep

>  <b>SCOTT</b> I could just see his expression when the wonder-dog shot off after rabbits and all the sheep broke. <i>(1953,Breakfast at Six,144</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [re lambing]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> He made his name during his first day on the place by his masterly handling of a "break" of lambs. Only those who have had experience of dealing with "breaking" Merino lambs at tailing time can appreciate the test for man and dog. <i>Ibid.</i> p. 153.Break : Lamb-break:... Great care must be exercised, as a false move by man or dog will have the lambs, ever on their toes, stampeding or breaking. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,148</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [wild pigs] to escape from bailers and holders.

>  <b>SWAN</b> Further up the hill John heard Osgood start to bail. Within seconds the pig broke but Osgood quickly stopped it and resumed his bail. Once again though the pig broke and this time Osgood was not heard again. <i>(1992,Grunter Hunters, The,22</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [stock,esp. lambs, being handled in a mob] to bolt.

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> The sheep broke repeatedly before we could get them in. <i>(Oct 25, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,202</i>) [NOTES: (1950)]

>  Break - break away, usually of lambs. Both as a noun and a verb. <i>(Jan 13, 1934,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),13</i>)

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> When lambs 'break' as this bolting is termed, they may run for miles before they stop ... <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,22</i>)

### break away
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [stock] to bolt from a mob.

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> Started after a mob of sheep that had broken away from the man that was keeping a boundary. <i>(Oct 6, 1864,Chudleigh Diary</i>) [NOTES: (1950)]

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> The wild brutes tried to break away in every direction. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,135</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to break free from a herd or mob.

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> The wild brutes tried to break away in every direction, and ... poor C. would have stood a small chance ... if we had not been there to help him in forcing them into a small wattle yard near at hand; ... <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,135</i>)

>  Had dinner and started after a [147] mob of sheep that had broken away from the man that was keeping a boundary. <i>(Oct 6, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,146,1950 ed.</i>)

### break back
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [stock in a mob] to rush in the opposite direction to which they are driven.

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> A mob broke back when we where [sic] crossing a small stream and ran over two miles before we could turn them in. <i>(Oct 13, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,148</i>) [NOTES: (1950)]

>  <b>JONES</b> If a mob .. broke back .. the rest of us would have to wait while the shepherd on whose beat the sheep had broken back, went for them. <i>(1933,Autobiography of an Early Settler,59</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to run or dash in a reverse direction (of a mob of sheep)

>  A mob broke back when we where [sic] crossing a small stream and [we] ran over two miles before we could turn them. <i>(Oct 13, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,148,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>JONES</b> If a mob ... broke back, ... the rest of us would have to wait while the shepherd on whose beat the sheep had broken back, went for them. <i>(1933,Autobiography,59</i>)

### break camp
 
 <i>phr.</i> (of sheep) to move from a camping ground

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> Got up fron the ground by daybreak and let the sheep break camp at once. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,211</i>)

>  <i>Break camp.</i> - Move away from camp; shift camp. Sheep which camp together are also said to `break camp' when they move off to feed at dawn. ([1874 Kennaway Crusts], p.113; Butler A First Year in Canterbury Settlement, p.54.) <i>(Sep 16, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> Next morning we had to be up early to reach the tops before the merinos broke camp and started back for the country off which they had been mustered. ... At this stage, it was essential to get around the sheep before they broke camp, for to muster them again after they had begun to scatter would take many hours. <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,18</i>) [NOTES: [f. c1869]]

### break down
 
 <i>v.</i> tone down, cease

>  Break down the swearing... The padre doesn't like it. <i>(Mar 15, 1943,NZEF Times,II/90 : 6</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Break down</i>. v To make lighter: 20C. NZ C. -2. See n2. <i>(1938,Dictionary of Slang,91</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to take the first cuts off a log to make it into a manageable size for sawing into timber (See [breaking-down saw](../B#breaking-down-saw))

>  Stripped of their branches they could be floated down the driving creeks with the ordinary logs, and if `broken down' or cut into suitable boards for wainscoting and furniture, would be highly appreciated for their striking beauty. <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/186</i>) [NOTES: (Firth)]

>  In the south, rough tramways are laid down in the bush, and the logs hauled on low carriages to the mills by horse- or bullock-teams. here the great round baulks of timber are `broken down' being cut lengthwise (by saws moving up and down vertically) into `flitches', which are passed over to the circular-saws to be ripped into boards and scantling. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,158</i>)

### break feeding
 
 <i>n.</i> the rationing of feed to stock by a temporary fence (usually electric), moved at frequent intervals (See [cf. break n](../B#break-n))

>  Break feeding: Feed is rationed by use of a temporary fence (usually electric) moved at frequent intervals. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,15</i>)

>  ... break-feeding of stock ... <i>(Jun 26, 1986,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Farm and Rural reporter]

>  <i>break feeding NZ</i> rationing of feed for grazing animals by division of pasture by means of a movable (usu. electric) fence. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,86</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### break fence
 
 <i>n.</i> [Check meaning: ?f. break(away) of sheep - i.e. to stop breaks; or is the reference to break off difficult or dangerous places like steep faces or gullies [(farming)]

>  <b>McLEOD</b> This was the repair of a `break fence' crossing a leading spur above the bush. <i>(1959,Tall Tussock,37</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Most of the paddock fences were renewed and attention was then directed to the hill; numerous `break' fences (short lengths of two or three barbs spanning gullies or running from existing fences up steep faces and into bluffs) were put in at strategic spots ... <i>(1975,Sixty Thousand on the Hoof,199</i>)

### break in
 
 <i>v.</i> [Is this the sense here; or is the reference to the topography of the land.]

>  <b>FEATON</b> The fore-ground is open fern land, partially broken and intersected by a fresh-water stream and one or two shallow gullies. <i>(1879,Waikato War,56</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to train to be milked

>  <b>FITTON</b> ... milch cows, and heifers ready to be broken in for milking. [fn] A friend tells me the expression `breaking in' requires explanation. ... A young cow [allowed to run wild] when first milked is generally `baled up', that is, her head is secured between upright posts so that she can raise or lower it, but cannot turn round to use her horns. ... A pint or less of milk is considered a satisfactory result of the first attempt to milk an `unbroken heifer'. When a cow has submitted to this process a few times without much struggling, she is considered to be `well broken in to milk'. <i>Ibid.</i> 242 A well broken cow for milking generally sells for nearly [pounds]20. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),218 and fn</i>)

>  <b>WIERZBICKA</b> They put the cow into the bail today. She seems ... uncommonly quiet for a cow that had not been broken in. <i>(1973,Wilson Family,91</i>) [NOTES: 1874 J.G.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 191]



 
 <i>v.</i> to clear and prepare virgin land for cultivation (See [cf. break up](../B#break-up))

>  Jim and Rose Henare set out to break in a farm at Motatau that people said would never pay its way. <i>(Aug 11, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> Considering the facility of breaking in land here, ... the clearing and burning off for the entry of the plough varying from ten to twenty shillings per acre ... <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),99</i>)

>  <b>WALLACE</b> <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,1/24</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> The fact is my dear Mrs. Ashwin, I am supposed to be here for the purpose of gaining `colonial experience,' as it is called. I am supposed to be ... receiving daily lessons in the rougher and more laborious branches of colonisation - here, where men are hourly engaged in breaking-in the wilderness, and all that sort of thing. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,222</i>) [NOTES: [?transf.]]

>  Imperceptibly, farming had progressed from a developmental phase when a high proportion of labour was utilized in `breaking in' new land ... <i>(1943,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,24/205A</i>)

>  The place is getting a good clean up, more bush being broken in. <i>(Jan 5, 1929,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/168,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>VAILE</b> I was breaking in some country about two and a half miles from the homestead. <i>(1939,Pioneering the Pumice,99</i>)

>  <i>(Feb 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,141/1</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> It isn't far to where they'll be breaking in this land you've heard so much about. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,87</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> But there were no cattle coming up to listen. ... They'd been in there fern crushing, hundreds of acres of bracken. Men'd just walked off that land in the slump but those cattle were breaking it all in again. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,46</i>)

>  <i>break in ... 5. Austral.</i> to bring new land under cultivation. <i>~ n. break-in. </i> <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,133</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 86; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 59]]

>  <b>REES</b> Breaking in country means clearing it, doesn't it? <i>(1927,Life's What You Make It,118</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to tame or develop [land] into pasture from bush or wasteland.

>  <b>WALLACE</b> Of the fern country, that upon a limestone formation is the best and least expensive to break in. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,230</i>)

>  The land is nearly all broken in, fenced and in grass. <i>(Feb 1894,NZ Farmer,64</i>)

>  The best routine for a settler breaking in pumice land is to sow a limited area in permanent grass ... <i>(Jul 16, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture</i>)

>  With two six-horse teams he worked for 12 years breaking the land in from tall tea-tree and flax. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,18</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> For nine years he has been breaking in the scrub country. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,54</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. some years ago an area of about 3,000 acres at the Ngamatea homestead was broken in and sown down ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,151</i>)

>  The farm is 150 acres but 25 are steep river bank and are not really usable although they are clearing and breaking in five of these which seem to have some potential. <i>(Oct 1974,NZ Journal of Agriculture,28</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> break in or bring in: to clear land of its orginal flora, cultivate it, and sow it down with something more productive such as a pasture of exotic grasses. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,10</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  <i>Sheep dog</i>.- Dog to work sheep; <i>collie</i> (q.v.).<i> S.ds</i>. run naturally either to bring sheep towards you or to drive them away; and when a puppy shows his preference he is broken in for that work. The dogs that bring sheep are called <i>heading ds.</i> (q.v.) and most of these can be taught to <i>lead</i> (q.v.) if desired. Dogs which take to driving sheep away are broken in to <i>huntaway</i> and are called<i> htwys</i> (q.v.). Some dogs will both <i>hd.</i> and <i>htwy</i> and are called <i>handy ds</i>. They are not generally high-class heading dogs but very useful about hand and sometimes very useful mustering dogs. Some men have a special <i>slewing d</i>., but a well-trained,<i> hdg.</i> dog or <i>htwy</i>. will usually <i>slew</i> (q.v.). (C.) <i>(Nov 25, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i> a bullock to harness

>  McD and Edwardes brought home young bullock to break in. <i>(Feb 5, 1860,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  I broke in young bullocks for working. <i>(1882,TrNZI,14/131</i>)

### break it down
 
 <i>exclam.</i> stop it! cut it out! be quiet! (usu. as a reprimand or expressing disbelief)

>  Break it down, gunner, how often have I told you to answer your name properly. <i>(1940,HM Transports 23 and 23A,16</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> Hey, break it down. <i>(1943,Passage to Tobruk,68</i>)

>  Break it down. There's still a few/ Thirty-niners left to check up on you. <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in the Middle East,19</i>)

>  <b>COLE</b> `Break it down!' Wood shouted from the telephone. `I can't hear a thing.' <i>(1949,It Was So Late,13</i>) [NOTES: (OEDS)]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The proprietor has him tightly held across the counter and he is getting angry. `Break it down', he says. `Break it down or you'll get turfed out of here. Turn it in or I'll get the cops.' <i>Ibid.</i> 202 Drink and be merry for tomorrow we die he yelled and the boys said break it down, cut out that sort of talk ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,167</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> In the dark, my [disturbed] mind cries out. ... `Break it down,' reproves Pilling. `Don't do your block.' <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,49</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> You expect too much. `Break it down - that job's too much for a new chum - he can't handle that until he's had a bit of practice.' <i>Ibid.</i> 112This story is exaggerated.`Ha Ha! Break it down - you don't expect us to believe that, do do you?' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],111</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> `... Getting the sack as usual,' ... `Break it down, you're worse than the wife's mother. But ... I did get the bullet in the end - through my brother, the silly cow. <i>(1964,Watersiders,108</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> `... I guess you'll have a whole fleet of nannies and nurses and governesses.' `Break it down,' I laughed. `None as like as not and we'll send 'em all to state schools. Better education anyway.' <i>(1971,Plekhov Place, The,148</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Steady on, Ted. Break it down, boy. Watch she doesn't stall.' <i>(1976,Pacific Star,52</i>)

>  <i>6. break it down. Austral. inf. a</i>. stop it.<i> b</i>. don't expect me to believe that; come off it. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,133</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 86]]

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Break it down!' I said. `I didn't think he meant that much.' <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,66</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>break it down</i> demand to stop unacceptable behaviour; eg `Break it down, will ya! Some of us are trying to sleep!' c. 1930s. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,21</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>exclam.</i> go easy [1940s]

### break out
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to tame [land] into pasture from scrub or bush.

>  There are in the Auckland province hundreds of thousands of acres of ploughable land yet to be broken out of fern and scrub. <i>(Oct 1, 1926,NZ Farmer,1425</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [shearing] to open out the fleece of a sheep as it is shorn up the neck.

>  <b>BOWEN</b> The next blow is from the off side of the brisket down to the opposite flank, and if possible, the belly wool is broken out. If this is found too difficult to break out, then leave it for the third blow. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,24</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> break out: a machine shearer 'goes up the neck' with his comb flat on the skin. The shorn fibre usually remains attached to the tips. He 'breaks out' with a movement of wrist and forearm as they follow the handpiece. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,10</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> My style of breaking out the the neck by turning the head and coming out behind the ears could not be applied on these loose, wrinkle-necked half-breds. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,97</i>)

### break up
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to convert land into pasture by turning with a plough.

>  <b>PAUL</b> Tutu soil is the most expensive to break up ... <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,97</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> .. the smell of the freshly-turned earth of a paddock near the house, which was in the process of being broken up for English grass ... <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,39</i>)

>  The extent of land broken up, but not under crop, was 216 acres against 635 for the previous year. <i>(May 12, 1880,Hawera Star,2</i>)

>  Where it is intended to break up new land for cultivation. <i>(Aug 1883,Northern New Zealand Settler,233</i>)

>  Extent of land broken up but not under crop, 210 acres ... <i>(Mar 10, 1888,Egmont Star,7</i>)

>  Since the little farm first passed into the hands of the first immigrant, the acres by the creek had never been broken up. <i>(Aug 1, 1927,NZ Farmer,1035</i>)

>  <b>CRAWFORD</b> .. there was no incentive to break up the land and grow English grasses. <i>(1949,Sheep and Sheepmen of Canterbury,66</i>)

>  Kerikeri 10 acres 5 broken up, partly fenced and in grass. <i>(Jul 3, 1875,Weekly News,3</i>)

>  Notwithstanding the oft repeated cry that large holders never cultivate, I have to note with pleasure that nearly 1,000 acres on Flaxmere and Tunanui is being broken up this season. <i>(Aug 1888,NZ Farmer,288</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> something or somebody who breaks you up (with emotion, esp. amusement); a laugh; a character.

>  <b>CARMAN</b> The idea of her on one of the Reomoana tough-nuts [i.e. horses] is a break up. <i>(1925,Broad Stairway, The,164</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [[Should this go in as an hist. use in NZ; or is it merely a common use of OED2 `disband' etc.]]

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,96</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> He'll vote against free immigration,/ Look after land sharks in the north,/ Break up every sheep run and Station,/ <i>(1889,Rhymes and Rambles,39</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to split (wood) (almost a technical term)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> A man can break up a cord to two cords a day. <i>(1925,Me and Gus,19,1938 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to divide land property.

>  <b>MCKEE WRIGHT</b> New fences climb the warm brown spurs to guard the scrubber ewes, Because the run is broken up for hungry cockatoos. <i>(1898,Station Ballads,46</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> one who is broken up, a wreck

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> A person who is a wreck, temporarily, or hopelessly. .in 4;`He's a breakup. He's never sober, seems to have given up altogether.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],116</i>)

### break water
 
 <i>v.</i> of a whale, to rise to the surface (after sounding). -->1838 Polack NZ 2/421 After the whale is pierced, they frequently remain from twenty to sixty minutes previous to rising again on the surface of the ocean, termed breaking water, ...

### break-fed
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of stock] contained by temporary fences within a paddock.

>  <b>TAIT</b> Some of the Pindel Jersey herd sold as in-calf heifers on break-fed pasture. <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol II,493</i>)

### break-feed
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to feed stock in a restricted area of a pasture or crop.

>  Then we're ready for the rest of the day feeding out to the beef cows, break-feeding the grazers, then docking and back to the shed about 4.30. <i>(Nov 9, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

### break-feeding
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the feeding of stock on part of a crop or new pasture with the use of temporary fences or breaks.

>  <b>TAIT</b> Breakfeeding by the use of an electric fence for spring and early summer grazing has played a large part in increasing production. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,85</i>)

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFIELD</b> Also called strip grazing, break feeding and close folding, rationed grazing is a still more intensive method of achieving controlled grazing. <i>(1961,Agriculture,304</i>)

>  Under very wet conditions it can be worth giving away breakfeeding and allowing the mob access to the whole paddock. <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,32</i>)

>  <b>BROSNAHAN</b> All the stock are on intensive feeding systems in the form of breakfeeding with electric fences during winter. <i>(1998,Glenaray 1898-1998,82</i>)

### break-fence
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to graze stock within break fences.

>  Oliver sets the blocks up before winter and doesn't break-fence. <i>(Apr 12, 2001,NZ Farmer,19</i>)

### break-graze
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to limit grazing to a strip or small area.

>  <b>CONNELL & HADFIELD</b> In the early spring the winter-stored, high quality feed is break-grazed and carefully rationed. <i>(1961,Agriculture,371</i>)

>  The system of farming .. is to stock heavily with breeding ewes and break-graze them with the electric fence. <i>(Sep 28, 1961,NZ Farmer,2</i>)

>  Break graze this whole mob round the entire farm on a 60-day cycle using two electric fences, one ahead and one behind. [break graze] <i>(Mar 1967,NZ Journal of Agriculture,35</i>)

### break-grazing
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the grazing of stock in restricted areas known as breaks.

>  October to December is the peak period for milk production, the start of round-the-farm break grazing of each paddock in turn ... <i>(Jan 15, 1953,NZ Journal of Agriculture,5</i>)

>  Where subdivision is inadequate, break grazing with the electric fence is essential. <i>(May 1957,NZ Journal of Agriculture,480</i>)

>  Fed from a plastic hose, the troughs are very handy for use with break-grazing of ASP so they can be shifted along with the herd. [break grazing] <i>(Nov 11, 1963,NZ Dairy Exporter,19</i>)

### break-up
 
 <i>n.</i> (of a school) [1740 RICHARDSON Pamela II. 364 When you break up next, my Dear, said he, if you're a good Girl, you shall make your new Aunt a Visit. a1855 C. BRONTE in Cornh. Mag. (1860) Apr. 495, I wrote..to the friends of my pupils, notifying the day when we break up.]

>  <b>BOWMAN</b> They were gala days for Port Chalmers and were usually fixed for the day prior to the "break-up" at the end of the year. <i>(1948,Port Chalmers,160</i>)

### breakaway
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep or beast that breaks from the mob.

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> But in driving the cattle to the yard there was an occasional breakaway. Bill had some wild-westing galloping after the breakways. <i>(1981,Sunrise on Hikurangi,76</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a panic rush of animals, often away from the main herd

>  <i>Break&#769;.a-way&#769;.</i> (br&amacr.k&#769;.&adot.-w&amacr.&#769;.), <i>n. 1. </i>A wild rush of sheep, cattle, horses, or camels (esp. at the smell or sight of water); a stampede. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2018</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> ?washout; ?landslip; a piece of land washed out by a stream or river in flood. -

>  Larger landholders [on the Hawke's Bay plain] supplied estimates of `breakaways' in their land as a result of the excessive rainfall that then took place. <i>(1897,TrNZI,515</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [droving or shepherding] a break in a mob of sheep or cattle.

>  Break. - Break away, usually of lambs. Both as noun and verb. <i>(Jan 13, 1934,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),13</i>)

>  <b>MCNAUGHTON</b> But once they were started on the downward journey, the danger of a breakaway was past, and all that remained was hard punching to keep them together. <i>(1970,Tat,99</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> an animal that breaks away from a herd [1968 [see cit. above]]

>  <b>ACLAND</b> Break away, usually of lambs. Both as noun and as verb. <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,Glossary 1.</i>)

>  <i>breakaway n</i>. panic rush of stock; bullock that leaves the mob. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),4</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> [sc. of sheep] It was a slow process, getting those sheep down. The hillside was benchy and rough. But once they were started on the downward journey, the danger of a breakaway was past, and all that remained was hard punching to keep them together. <i>(1970,Tat,99</i>)

>  <i>break away n.</i> a bullock that separates from the herd; <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,2</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Some of our boys [=? converts] returned from Taramai where they had been to visit their sick friends. [compare: 1830 Nov 11 <i>Ibid.</i> Two in my own family have died lately in a very happy state ; and Williams's occasional use of <i>youth</i> in this context.] <i>(Dec 22, 1826,Journal,31,1961 ed.</i>)

### breakbone
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We headed up a great herd of nellies (giant petrel or breakbones). <i>(Jan 30, 1890,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  As we came out [of the Snares Islands], flocks of sea-birds flew and swam around us, and we headed up a great herd of nellies (<i>Ossifraga gigantea</i>, giant petrel or breakbones), and chased them awhile. <i>(1891,TrNZI,23/492</i>)

### breakdown
 
 <i>n.</i> [sawmilling]

>  <b>MANDER</b> By day the whole bay vibrated with the whistle and screech of the circular saws, the tear of the break downs, the rasp of the drags, . <i>Ibid.</i> 197 They went past the the goose and circular saws and the moving platform of the of the breakdowns to the skids ... <i>(1920,Story of a NZ River, The,159,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MALFROY</b> The laying of the foundations of the breakdown should first be proceeded with. ... The logging-delivery tram, mill log-skids, engine, breakdown bench. <i>Ibid.</i> 28 The<i> breakdown benchman</i> should be able to keep his own saws, and break the logs down to suit the cutting at the sawyer's bench. <i>(1923,Small Sawmills,17</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> And sawdust in a shower/ The `break-downs' thump,/ And the logs go bump,/ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,70</i>)

>  <b>MANDER</b> They watched the progress of the log from the booms up the skids to the side of the breakdown platform... Halved and quartered, it was then levered ... on to greased rollers and rushed towards the big circulars, which turned it into flitches. Then the small circulars, the drag, and the goose completed its metamorphosis into the regulation strips ... <i>(1920,Story of a NZ River, The,198,1960 ed.</i>)

### breakdown bench
 
 <i>n.</i> [sawmilling]

>  There are break down benches and two large saws of five and a half feet and four and a half feet. <i>(May 24, 1921,Mataura Ensign</i>) [NOTES: 1921 May 24 Ensign (Mataura) in 1966 Barry In the Lee of the Hokonuis 144]

>  <b>MALFROY</b> ... advantage can be taken of placing the log-skids and breakdown [7] bench on a higher level ... <i>Ibid.</i> 19 For small operations where the expense of a Pacific carriage or travelling bench is too ambitious, a good ... breakdown bench can be built locally on the principle of the `Bullock' bench which has been used in some parts of New Zealand for the last fifty years. <i>(1923,Small Sawmills,6</i>)

### breakdown benchman
 
 <i>n.</i> the sawmill worker who operates the breakdown bench; breaker-down.

>  <b>MALFROY</b> <i>The <i>breakdown benchman</i> should be able to keep his own saws, and break the logs down to suit the cutting at the sawyer's bench. <i>(1923,Small Sawmills,28</i>)

### breakdown man
 
 <i>n.</i> a breakdown benchman.

>  <b>BENNETT</b> [The timber trade] has supplied a wide variety of occupational terms The cleared tracks carry logs to the mill, where [they are] handled by a <i>break-down man</i> (`to break down' in New Zealand means `to make lighter'). <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,85</i>)

### breaker
 
 <i>n.</i> a man employed to break in horses.

>  <b>BARKER</b> The most experienced breaker declined [the mare] with thanks. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,233</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> According to one story, the sailor was supposed to get horses from somewhere and sell them to those breakers. <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,9</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,105, 107</i>) [NOTES: [Notes p.2 and reverse]]

### breaker-down
 
 <i>n.</i> breakdown benchman

>  At the conclusion of the metalworks season I scored a job as a breaker-down at Wilson's Sawmill at Washdyke near Timaru. <i>(2001,Good Bastards: the larrikin's guide to success,75</i>)

>  BREAKER-DOWN. The head-sawyer of a breaking-down bench. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

### breakerout
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a worker who attaches logs to a winch-rope, or tractor

>  Experienced Breakerout wanted for our established Hauler Crew. Must pass a pre-employment drug test and be fit and willing. <i>(Oct 28, 2008,Wairarapa Midweek,26</i>)

>  The operator tried to get on the machine to try and stop it and the roll continued trapping a breakerout working below and crushing him. <i>(1998,Forestry Bulletin,124/1</i>)

>  Motu Logging Cable Forest Harvesting Crew .. has a position available for a breaker out/faller for a cable harvesting operation situated in the Houpoto Forest. <i>(May 23, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,10</i>)

>  <b>BIRD</b> The breaker-out and his hooker-on heaved the slack cable across to the chosen rimu log. <i>(1998,Viaducts against the Sky,50</i>)

>  The person who has the job of attaching the dropline to each log is called the breaker-out, and he needs his wits about him to avoid injury. <i>(Jul 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 64/103</i>)

>  <b>BIRD</b> "Ho,ho!" called the breaker-out, and the whistleboy gave two claps of the switch in his hand. <i>(1998,Viaducts against the Sky,49</i>)

>  Following after him was the breaker-out, hooking the fallen trees up to the hauler with heavy wire straps. <i>(Jan 9, 1992,Dominion,15</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> Other [timber-trade] terms ... are <i>breaker-out</i> (the man who clears a timber jam; pl <i>breakers-out</i>) <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,85</i>)

>  <b>R GIBERT</b> Next in importance to the bush-boss is the `breaker-out' in mills where steam haulers are still in use. He is responsible for directing the hauler-driver in the negotiation of obstacles. <i>(1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1951 R. Gilberd : Okaihau]

>  BREAKER-OUT The bushman who attaches the cable to trees or logs being hauled from stump to landing. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

### breakeven stumpage
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the price a grower has to receive in order to make a return from forestry.

>  <b>MAF</b> breakeven stumpage: the price the grower would have to receive in order to make a return from the planting which exactly equals the discount rate. <i>(1989,BLANK ENTRY,128</i>) [NOTES: Source: Economic Evaluation of Agroforestry in Otago]

### breaking
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Break : Lamb-break:... Great care must be exercised, as a false move by man or dog will have the lambs, ever on their toes, stampeding or breaking. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,148</i>)

### breaking in
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>J S MATTHEWS</b> The latter part I was engaged in attending to the breaking in of land, and planting corn and potatoes for native food. <i>(Jan 5, 1836,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1836 Jan 5 J.Matthews letter in 1940 Matthews Matthews of Kaitaia 80]

>  From 1902 onwards the breaking-in of the swamp was undertaken on a large scale ... <i>(1930,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/279</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> breaking in (land or a farm): clearing of its original vegetation and preparing for sowing or ploughing. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,298</i>) [NOTES: Pearson Glossary]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEVY</b> Another pest during the early breaking-in stage was foxglove, and for a time it seemed that this plant would be the ruination of the hills. <i>Ibid.</i> 250 Following the initial primary forest burn, secondary scrub fires and logging-up burns continue the breaking-in process. <i>Ibid.</i> 319 This is really a form of land development subsidy provided largely from farming profits in the course of breaking-in-operations until the block is subdivided by ballot for ownership. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,16</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a (usu. rough) introduction or familiarisation (with a job, place, etc).

>  <b>PINFOLD</b> The lodging places of the pioneer parson were many and various. ... His first `breaking in' was to be given a `shake-down' under the counter of the public house; a small rude wooden `shanty' that has been hurriedly erected. <i>(1930,Fifty Years In Maoriland,66</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COWAN</b> The late Mr. Alexander Turnbull ... searched out documents which threw additional light on events in New Zealand's `breaking-in' period. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,xv,1983 ed.</i>)

### breaking out
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>REED</b> The cost of filling and cross-cutting might be set down, say, at fourpence per hundred feet; breaking out (separating the logs), sniping and chuting into creeks, one-and-two per hundred. <i>(1955,Story of the Kauri, The,151</i>)

### breaking up
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the use of a plough or other implement to turn over or cultivate land.

>  <b>FULLER</b> The strongest implements .. are to be advised in breaking up waste land into which the plough has not previously entered. <i>(1859,Five Years' Residence in New Zealand,145</i>)

>  <b>HOOS</b> The breaking-up requires a strong plough ... <i>(1882,NZ Practical Agriculturist, The,16</i>)

>  <b>CRAWFORD</b> .. there was no incentive to break up the land and grow English grasses. The breaking up of the land, though it progressed slowly, had been started by the pre-Adamites. <i>(1949,Sheep and Sheepmen of Canterbury,66</i>)

>  A group of Cape Foulwind dairy farmers .. are evaluating flipping or breaking up the pakihi iron pan to bring free-draining soil to the top. [breaking-up] <i>(Oct 25, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,19</i>)

### breaking yard
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a yard used for taming horses or cattle.

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> That black whirling Stardust stallion in the breaking yard again. <i>(1981,Tales of the High Country,5</i>)

### breaking-down
 
 <i>n.</i> the conversion in a sawmill of logs to flitches

>  When the loco arrives at the bull skids, the logs are hauled off by two winches, cut to the required length by the fiddling saw, then kicked to the Pacific bench which does the "breaking down". <i>(Jul 25, 1928,NZ Free Lance,7</i>)

>  <b>332</b> Breaking-down is commonly effected by the circular-saw, and in the case of very large logs by two circular-saws, an upper and a lower working in the same vertical plane. <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook</i>) [NOTES: (T. Kirk)]

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> ... he would go among the men from the mills ... asking if there were any jobs going. `Benching? - any benching? How about breaking-down?' <i>(1965,Power of Joy,45</i>)

### breaking-down bench
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DE MAUNY</b> The scream of the bandsaws on the breaking-down bench ... <i>(1949,Huntsman in His Career, The,162</i>) [NOTES: [check]]

>  BREAKING-DOWN BENCH. A sawbench used for the purpose of reducing logs to flitches. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> The next morning we saw them thunder down skids to a breaking-down bench in the mill. <i>(1981,Black Beech & Honeydew,103</i>)

### breaking-down saw
 
 <i>n.</i> a saw used to take the first cuts (i.e. bark and slabs) off logs and to square them up for the smaller saws.

>  [Auckland mills] are fitted with vertical breaking-down saws, capable of dividing the largest logs into halves or flitches, as may be required; ... <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,332</i>) [NOTES: (T. Kirk)]

>  <b>CARR</b> Old fashioned `Breast' benches (where flitches from the breaking-down saw are cut into commercial sizes) are still used in many mills. <i>(1913,Country Work,40</i>)

>  <b>R GILBERD</b> A log comes off `the skids' ... and on to a travelling platform which carries it on to the `breaking-down' saw. The man who works this saw is the `breaker-down' and his offsider is `the slabby.' <i>(1951,Personal Communication</i>)

>  <b>GROVER</b> Breaking-down saws - most efficient amputators of arms and legs. <i>(1967,Another Man's Role,34</i>)

>  <b>JACKSON</b> [He] shouted greetings to men in black singlets ..., tailing out. He was answered by the ... drone of the breaking-down saw as it bit into a pine log. <i>(1988,Rainshadow,90</i>)

### breaking-in
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the converting of bush or scrubland to pasture.

>  The breaking-in of the country had its fair share of difficulty. <i>(Jun 1897,NZ Farmer,172</i>)

>  At the close of the preceding year the last of the clearing, stumping and breaking-in had been completed on the farm. <i>(Feb 20, 1930,NZ Journal of Agriculture,112</i>)

>  In many parts of the North Island, farm country still offers a stubborn resistance to breaking-in. <i>(Feb 15, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,141</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> During the breaking in of the three farms, many young Englishmen were employed ... <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol II,311</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Needless to add the breaking-in of this country is a costly business. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,57</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The breaking in of the Purerua scrub country has simple been a story of hard work and heavy machinery. [breaking in] <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,204</i>)

### breaking-out
 
 <i>n.</i> a logging operation

>  Breaking-out - attaching felled trees to a cable so they can be hauled to the skid for processing - is treacherous work. <i>(Jul 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 64/103</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the work of a breaker-out [1838 cit. belongs elsewhere]

>  <b>REED</b> ... breaking-out (separating the boys) <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,151</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> And what's breaking out? Oh, you just get the log ready and hook her on to the winch-rope when the tractor comes to swing(?) her out to the skids. <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,37</i>)

>  The Men employed cutting wood in the [67] Bush for building a Breakwind round the Try work. <i>(Feb 27, 1838,Piraki Log,66</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> pertaining to the hauling of logs from the bush

>  The roads bringing the logs out of the bush from the stump are called Breaking-Out Roads, and are really only bush tracks along which the bullocks haul the logs out to the main road. <i>(1951,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: >[ca1914] 1951 H.S. McCarroll The Days of the Kauri Bushmen [Radio Talk 2: TS]]

### breakman
 
 <i>n.</i> a safe-breaker

>  The old-style criminal activity such as `breakmen' and `tankmen' blowing safes and so on is starting to go. <i>(Jul 17, 1978,Dominion,4/3</i>)

### breakout
 
 <i>n.</i> the unloading of a wagon

>  <b>DAVIS</b> They've opened the VB [wagon] and she's crammed to the door. ... Only room for one joker to work. He hops up does the break-out <i>(1964,Watersiders,36</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a room set aside for refreshments in a meeting complex [Seen at Turnbull House 18/2/04 (DEH)]

### breaksea devil
 
 <i>n.</i> a sound of rushing air, attibuted to a phantom bird; or the bird species (hakuwai) that causes the noise (See [hakuwai](../H#hakuwai))

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> All the people frequenting this coast [sc. west coast of Otago] believe in the existence of an extraordinary bird, or phantom, which they can never see, but only hear rushing past them through the air with the rapidity of a falling rocket, and making a terrible rushing sound. The Maories declare that it is a bird possessing many joints in its wings. The whalers call them <i>break-sea-devils,</i> after the name of an island where this phenomenon is of most frequent occurrence. <i>(May 24, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 May 24 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 224]

>  <b>JENKIN</b> The strange birdcall [hakuwai] seems to have been limited to only some of the muttonbird islands in the South Cape area. These were Poutaima, Big Island, Solomons and the Green Island, though a similar strange bird, called by whalers the Breaksea Devil, was said to be heard on Breaksea Island. <i>(1970,NZ Mysteries,157</i>)

### breakwind
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FEATON</b> Breakwinds of titree and flax were erected ... <i>(1879,Waikato War,67</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

### bream
 
 <i>n.</i> ? snapper

>  The crew caught with lines an astonishing quantity of fine fish belonging to the species <i>Dorade unicolor</i>, ... It is the same fish that Cook calls `bream' ... <i>(1910,TrNZI,42/430</i>) [NOTES: (D'Urville hauraki Gulf 1827 Mar 1)]

### breast bench
 
 <i>n.</i> sawmilling: a saw-bench with reversible rollers for sawing flitches into planks.

>  <b>CARR</b> Old fashioned `Breast' benches (where flitches from the breaking-down saw are cut into commercial sizes) are still used in many mills. <i>(1913,Country Work,40</i>)

>  <b>MALFROY</b> BREAST BENCH. [heading] <i>(1923,Small Sawmills,22</i>)

>  BREAST-BENCH. A table-high sawbench with a single circular saw and with reversible rollers for feeding flitches in and out. The flitches are set against a fence and the whole rig is designed to give the maximum flexibility in sawing to grade. <i>(1952,NZ Forest Glossary</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> I went back to work on the `breast bench' and as the fragrant slabs of pine timber came screaming off the saw and I threw them up on to the truck I sang a little song ... <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,69</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a saw-bench with reversible rollers that cuts flitches into planks of timber.

>  <b>CARR</b> Old fashioned 'Breast' benches (where flitches from the breaking-down saw are cut into commercial sizes are still used in many mills. <i>(1913,Country Work,40</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> This was all kauri, which was pit-sawn into 9-inch flitches and milled through a breast bench. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,114</i>)

### breast plate
 
 <i>n.</i> a piece of timber placed in the ground to support the stay at a strainer, angle, or corner post; sometimes called stay foot.

>  <i>Breastplate</i>: Piece of timber placed in the ground that supports the stay at a strainer, angle, or corner post. Sometimes called stay foot. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,27</i>)

### breast rail
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a front end rail of a cow bail or stand where cows are milked.

>  The zig-zag breast rail means the cups are always in the right position. <i>(Sep 20, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,20</i>)

>  We are the specialists in cowshed wall and floor coatings .. Give new life to milk room floors, pit floors and walls, breast rail walls, shed entranceway ... <i>(Apr 11, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,18</i>)

### breast up to
 
 <i>phr.</i> to approach closely

>  <b>CHARLES</b> And the driver comes in and breasts up to the bar./ He says, `Give me a jar and a couple of crates,/ For I am running the cutter tonight for me mates!'/... `We are fresh out of beer,/So that's why I'm here,/ Running the cutter tonight./ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea</i>)

### breastfeeding a shovel
 
 <i>phr.</i> Of government roadworkers esp. leaning on a shovel [cf grey friar] [ca1920 p.c. Wendy Orsman .]

### breather
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  During the (1905 Rugby) tour the Daily Mail (London) published the following explanation of the players' pet names ... `Players not included in a particular team are called `Breathers'. <i>(1905,Daily Mail</i>) [NOTES: 1905 Daily Mail, London in 1953 Oct 31 Evening Post 18]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SARGESON</b> ... little dinghy [at Matakana, Northland] which was then rowed along water-roads that wound through mangroves, their roots spiking up through the mud (breathers as you heard them called), until you were in the clear deep water ... <i>(1977,Never Enough!,11</i>)

### breathers
 
 <i>n.</i> the lungs

>  <b>THOMSON</b> He had an insatiable desire to see what was inside any creature he came across, to find out how they were `propped up', as he put it, ... to locate their `pumper', and `breathers', and other `gear', as he called their organs. <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,13</i>)

### bred to the purple
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [stock sales] well-bred. (See [cf. born to the purple](../B#born-to-the-purple))

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Just the other day, I took out a nine month old huntaway bitch, who's bred to the purple. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,60</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> 'She's bred in the purple.' <i>(1990,Matawhero, Elsewhere and Other Things,28</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> They're bred in the purple. [bred in the purple] <i>(1996,Trouble with Stock Agents, The,98</i>)

### breeding bag
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the uterus of sheep.

>  <b>STEVENS</b> Eversion of the uterus or breeding bag. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,138</i>)

### breeding index/BI
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an assessment of an animal's breeding potential.

>  Breeding index is the term used to show the relative merit of a bull in comparison with the 'average' bull .. breeding indexes for bulls of different breeds should not be compared. <i>(Apr 1980,NZ Dairy Exporter,48</i>)

>  A philosophy has been emerging in recent years that high BI and low BI cows are two completely different animals. <i>(May 13, 1982,NZ Farmer,87</i>)

>  Have you got high Breeding Index cows? <i>(Sep 1985,NZ Dairy Exporter,77</i>)

>  .. many high BI cows in NZ herds will be expected to produce milk on a diet which is 75% fibre, like standing hay. <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,71</i>)

### breeding station
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large property where emphasis is on stock-breeding.

>  <b>MCCASKILL</b> When he purchased Molesworth he decided to establish a breeding station near the homestead. <i>(1969,Molesworth,152</i>)

>  Breeding station fetches $6.6m. <i>(Mar 9, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,3</i>)

### breeding unit
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a farm property where emphasis is on stock-breeding.

>  The property is run in conjunction with a 3500 acre breeding unit at Rangiwahia and serves as the finishing unit for the store stock produced on the Rangiwahia farm. <i>(Aug 4, 2001,Dominion,33</i>)

>  Te Whanga is a large and intensive property .. as a sheep and beef breeding unit, which includes an Angus stud. <i>(Nov 23, 2002,Dominion Post,C19</i>)

### breeding worth/BW
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a breeding value of farm stock, expressed in dollars.

>  A 50 BW Friesian calf will make $100 ... <i>(Aug 22, 1996,NZ Farmer,37</i>)

>  .. we used natural mating for 10 years on 180 cows and it went well. However, we only bought high BW bulls. <i>(Feb 6, 2001,Fencepost.com,Web discussion</i>)

>  Genetics providers used a figure called breeding worth to calculate benefits of genetics in dollar terms. BW is Breeding Worth, or worth for breeding, and is expressed in dollars. So a bull at 130 BW compared with another at 120 BW has $5/year/offspring more value for breeding, as only half is passed on from the bull. <i>(Sep 22, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,10,Farming 2000 ed.</i>)

>  The herd has a Breeding Worth of 90, ranking it in the top 5% in the country. <i>(May 24, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,13</i>)

>  In the dairy section a few good BW crossbred heifers came forward and sold relatively well. <i>(Apr 12, 2001,NZ Farmer,44</i>)

>  Top BW/PWs with 100% reliability and good cell counts ... In recent years the breeding worth has improved considerably. <i>(Apr 6, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,28</i>)

### breeding, feeding, and weeding
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [expressed in any order] three vital factors in successful stock production.

>  .. the three leading factors in building up and improving a dairy herd are breeding, feeding and weeding. <i>(Oct 1, 1926,NZ Farmer,1420</i>)

>  Of the three inseparables - weeding, breeding and feeding - the last-named is, perhaps, the most important. <i>(Sep 26, 1925,NZ Dairy Exporter,21</i>)

>  It is axiomatic that economic dairy production is chiefly dependent on three factors - feeding, breeding, weeding (or culling.) <i>(May 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,3</i>)

### Brent Garner saga
 
 <i>phr.</i> [Garner's was a bizarre criminal hoax of the 1990s]

>  More depravity than a night out with the Spice Girls, more twists and turns than a Brent Garner saga. <i>(Feb 2, 1997,Sunday Star Times,F4</i>)

### brew
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> BREW (n) Illegal, locally made alcoholic beverage. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,245</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a `cook up' of homebake drugs.

>  [...] he had heard about `this stuff you can make to get a buzz.' When the police came in he was just thinking about `making a brew.' He had got Coward to assist him because Coward knew what to do. <i>(May 24, 1985,Press, The,9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a drink of tea, brew up, noun and verb;as elsewhere, freq. for `(a brew of) tea', esp. made outdoors in a billy, a boil-up: also brew-up. (See [boil up](../B#boil-up))

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Tony sitting on a jerrycan stared moodily in front of him, his half-empty mug in his hand. `Last brew on Zaafran(?), sir', said Smithy. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,123</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> The workmen were having a spell ... The foreman nodded as we came near, squinting up at us from where he sat nursing a mug of black tea. `Goodday, mates. Like a mug of brew?' ... `Yeah, there's plenty in the billy.' <i>(1963,In the Sticks,84</i>)

>  <b>HELMER</b> He poured tea into the mugs lined up on the split-log bench, adding sugar and condensed milk. `Who's for a brew?' <i>(1964,Stag Party,80</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Chaffey swinging along the trail was invited into Flora Hut for a brew by a party weatherbound there in the early 1930s. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,93</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Suppose you could stand a brew too. <i>Ibid.</i> 130 `... Billy on?' `Yeah' `Good, I'm dying for a brew' <i>(1982,Wild Pig,55</i>)

### brew the billy
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Having brewed the billy, we made sure the fire was out and the tent securely fastened, ... <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,130</i>)

### brew up
 
 <i>v.</i> to make tea [brew the billy ca1946 N.C.C. Small (trampers')] (See [brew the billy](../B#brew-the-billy))

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> `It's time to brew up, I can hear them coming - they'll be ready for a good hot cup of tea.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],120</i>)

>  <i>(Jan 10, 1953,Evening Post,22</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> [The hunters] were brewing up when Henry and Bart arrived. <i>(1982,Wild Pig,40</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to burn out (as from a direct hit). -

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Another tank brewing up. But only one six-pounder left by the sound of it. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,317</i>)

>  <b>ARMSTRONG</b> One of the tanks received a direct hit and `brewed up.' <i>(1947,Life Without Ladies,10</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i> 3.to smoke (cigarettes) illicitly [1984 17 M E114Pakuranga Coll. 33 BREW UP]

### brew-billy
 
 <i>n.</i> a tea-billy cf brew up

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Someone suggested that we move to the edge of the bush and swing a brew-billy. <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,70</i>)

### brew-can
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [brew-billy](../B#brew-billy))

>  <b>SL,ATTER</b> ... and the black swing of the besmoked brew-can carried by the section leader for the Kiwis are in the dugouts brewing up the chai. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,235</i>)

### brew-up
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> `We'll take a breather here,' he announced. `Have a brew-up. ...' ... I collected some sticks and lit a fire. Then I filled the billy from a creek. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,206</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He is really getting on my nerves as he natters on and on, a lot of nonsense about getting out of B Ech and back into the sharp end with the boys. Plenty of room at the other end of the paddock. A bunch of D-Day Dodgers abd if they're going to hang around in their own half ... they might as well ... have a brew-up. <i>(1970,On the Ball,136</i>)

>  It seems that the well known trampers [sic] `brew-up' may become a thing of the past. <i>(1978,Manawatu Tramping Club Jubilee,61</i>)

>  <b>FULLARTON</b> The brew-up order was now at the end of the column. <i>(1946,We Walked Alone,97et passim</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Her picture was in his left breast pocket now, only a little crumpled at the corners, from being handled around an admiring circle of friends, over the "brew-up" fire in the desert. <i>(Sep 25, 1944,NZEF Times,IV/170 : 10</i>)

### brewer
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Others wanted [to play] `Prisoners' Base' or `Chivvy', `Brewer', `Egg-in-the hat', `Duck-stone', `Red Rover', or `Kingy-seeny'. The boys did not know where this last game came from. They did not know its proper spelling or pronunciation. `Kingy-seeny' it was to them and they did not care whether that had a meaning or not. This rough and tumble game was the most popular of all. <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,84</i>)

### brewer's asthma
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He's a moral to get potted - Shock treatment for the old brewers' asthma - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,164</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a fat belly, `pot' (associated with too much beer-drinking). -->ca1945 Wellington (but much earlier?)

### brewpub
 
 <i>n.</i> a bar and associated brewery

>  The brewpub is a neighbourly indoor-outdoor bar serving fresh naturally carbonated ale brewed in the glass-fronted copper and steel brewery they've built from scratch. <i>(Oct 16, 2004,Dominion Post,E 9</i>)

### briar
 
 <i>n.</i> occas. for <i>Rubus australis</i> (bush-lawyer)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> ... the prodigious intricacy of various climbers, briars, shrubs, and ferns which were interwoven throughout the forests, rendered the task of clearing the ground extremely fatiguing ... <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/127</i>)

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> For the first two hours I almost despaired ..., so impenetrable was the forest, with its labyrinth of briars and supple-jack, fallen trees ... continually interrupting my progress. ... Frequently I could only get through the briars by cutting a way with my knife. Nature be thanked, there is only one species of briar to punish invading man in the woods of New Zealand, but that one scratches cruelly. <i>(Apr 24, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Apr 24 and 25 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 212]

### briar rose/brier rose
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>rose, briar, brier [=] sweet brier [s]</i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,65</i>)

### brick veneer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The house is constructed of Roman brick veneer. <i>(Jan 19, 1956,Weekly News,18</i>)

>  In the single brick, or brick veneer, there is still a large quantity of timber that has to be placed in a house so constructed. <i>(Apr 2, 1952,Evening Post,6</i>)

### brick-fielder
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WEEKES</b> A `brickfielder' or South wind generally follows which blows violently and covers everything in dust. <i>(1841,Journal,74</i>)

>  <b>DOWSING</b> My door is never shut 'cept to a `brick fielder' ... <i>(1894,He,14</i>)

### brickie
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cit](../C#cit))

 <i>adj.</i> [1984 14 F E126 Wgton Girls C. 10 BRICK [sthg or someone useless].]

### bridal suite
 
 <i>n.</i>

With great reluctance two-man cells were officially approved. In Mt Eden they became known as `bridal suites' ... <i>Ibid.</i> 95 <i>bridal suite</i> cell containing two prisoners <i>(1980,While We Have Prisons,70</i>)

### bridal tree
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Pennantia corymbosa.</i> [[a1927] Ver. (A). =Vernacular names supplied by B.C. Aston (And) Pennantia corymbosa bridal tree]

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> <i>kaikomako</i>, a small tree (Pennantia corymbosa), bridal-tree, Maori fire. Sometimes kahikomako. The softer of the two woods used by the Maori for producing fire by friction: he can get the blaze in under a minute and a half. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/151</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The kaikomako, also known as CHEESEWOOD and BRIDAL TREE, commences life as a bushy shrub ... <i>(1961,Flora NZ,178,1929 ed.</i>)

### bride's bouquet
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Earina autumnalis</i> [ca1954 R. Mason `heard in Wellington']

### bride-cut
 
 <i>n.</i> first or virgin cut (in a whale's carcase)

>  <b>MALONE</b> One of the whale captains with a long-handled kind of sharp-edged spade, made the first, or as they called it, the bride-cut [in the whale's carcase]. <i>(1854,Three Years Cruise,70</i>) [NOTES: [Auckland Islands]]

### bridesdude
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Beefy Lucas Maxwell is blazing a brave new trail for blokes - by entering the bridesmaid category in tonight's Taranaki bride of the year competition. In recognition of his bold step, the category has been renamed as the gender-friendly "bride attendants" and Mr Maxwell's job title is "bridesdude". <i>(Jul 29, 2000,Evening Post,1</i>)

### bridge
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Bridge</i>. 3. In New Zealand post-G.W. C., a look, a glance. <i>(1938,Dictionary of Slang,92</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> (Also brige.) A look, a glance: NZ: 1932, Nelson Baylis, (private letter); extant. <i>(1949,Dictionary of the Underworld,72,1961 ed.</i>)

### Bridge to Nowhere
 
 <i>n.</i> a bridge in the backblocks of Wanganui leading to a later-abandoned WWI soldiers' settlement

>  [Hdg] Cross a Bridge ... to 'Somewhere' or 'Nowhere'. New Zealanders are skilled inventors of ingenious names, such as the 'Nippon Clip-on' for the extension lanes on the Auckland Harbour Bridge. Two other examples are the 'Bridge to Nowhere' and the 'Bridge to Somewhere', both of which have a somewhat similar history. <i>(Oct 2004,NZ Memories,No. 50/50</i>)

>  There are plenty of walking opportunities such as the mysterious Bridge to Nowhere, built by returned servicemen after World War I and abandoned as farmers fought a losing battle with excessive rainfall. <i>(Nov 21, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C12</i>)

>  If you've always wanted to see the Bridge to Nowhere but were put off by the thought of having to canoe or tramp three days to get there, try the four-hour scenic jetboat trip. <i>(Nov 5, 2001,Evening Post,16</i>)

### bridging
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  What is now known as "bridging". Whereby players steal the ball from the opposition despite being on their feet, remains illegal but was tolerated in the first few years of Super 12. <i>(Mar 14, 2001,Dominion,11</i>)

### bridle chain
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A short chain with a ring in the centre and a hook at each end was hooked onto both runners [of the catamaran]. This was called the Bridle Chain, and it was into this the drag chain was fastened for the team to haul by. <i>(1951,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: [ca1914] 1951 H.S. McCarroll The Days of the Kauri Bushmen [Radio Talk 4: TS]]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> BRIDLE CHAIN: Additional chains to prevent load slipping endways. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,185</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1920 A Teamster's Glossary in 1961 Henderson Friends In Chains 185]

### Bridle Path
 
 <i>n.</i> the (original) track over the hill from Lyttelton to Christhurch

>  <b>ADAMS</b> Two or three hundred yards in advance is the foot of the `bridlepath', a steep and narrow road leading directly over the range of hills at the back of [Lyttelton]. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,27</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> Two or three hundred yards in advance, is the foot of the `bridlepath', a steep and narrow road leading directly over the range of hills ... to the ferry across the Heathcote on the way to Christchurch. <i>(1853,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,27</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> `Dear me!' petulantly exclaimed a lady who had lately landed, and was being conducted over the bridle-path by a friend who had gone into port to meet her. <i>(1867,Twelve Years in Canterbury,7</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE</b> Some of them remained in the emigrant barracks at Port Cooper for a few days; but almost immediately the greater number started to walk over a recently made Bridle Path to the plains beyond. <i>(1930,NZ Memories,120</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> Although the Pilgrims could cross the hills to the Plain over the Bridle Path - and could carry their lighter belongings; all heavy luggage had to go to Christchurch by sea and then up the Avon River. <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,26</i>)

>  I went to the ferry instead, along the Bridle Path which finished so far. <i>(Jan 23, 1851,Journal of Edward Ward,115,1951 ed.</i>)

>  Moved by Mr. Shand ... That the bridle track be made practicable from the bridle-path to Christchurch. <i>(Apr 12, 1851,Lyttelton Times,3</i>)

### bridle path
 
 <i>n.</i> a track fit or designed only for horse rather than wheeled traffic.

>  I pay my shilling and commence a steep ascent on the "bridle-path" along the face of a hill with great care. <i>(May 1, 1852,ANZ Gazette,ns 42/156</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The other road, or rather `bridle path', commences at the Scotch village of Kaiwarawara, which is distant a mile from Wellington. <i>(1842,New Zealand (various authors/titles),78</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> At last we mounted and rode by a bridle-path among the hills ... <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,176</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Mr. Greenlaw was in charge of the road party, which ... had been sent up to form a bridle-path over the Pass, ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,119</i>)

>  <i> [Path]</i> is the word that is most often used by Englishmen for what we call a track, but its use in this sense survives here only in <i>bridle path</i>, and that has a rival in<i> bridle track.</i> <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,24</i>) [NOTES: 1958 Sep Ruth Mason `Track and Trail' in Tararua 24]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a primitive track accommodating a saddle or pack horse.

>  <b>PRATT</b> .. another party of men were employed in forming a shorter cut over the hills, since known as the 'bridle path.' <i>(1877,Colonial Experiences,217</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> .. the track resembled a broken rocky staircase more than a bridle-path. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,10</i>)

### bridle road
 
 <i>n.</i> a road fit or designed only for horse rather than wheeled traffic.

>  Colonel Wakefield is also about to direct a line or bridle road (the basis of the future road) to be cut. <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/43/224</i>) [NOTES: (Robert Stokes)]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> A bridle-road leads from Kaitaia for thirty-four miles through the forest; ... <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/220</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> A bridle-road has been marked out by the Company ... round the eastern spurs of Mount Egmont. ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,278</i>)
